# BOOK ONE

[Signature a ii]
<meta>Library Stamp: National Library Vittorio Emanuele, Rome</meta>

### BOOK ONE OF THE CAMALDOLESE DISPUTATIONS BY THE FLORENTINE CHRISTOPHORO LANDINO, ADDRESSED TO THE ILLUSTRIOUS FEDERICO, PRINCE OF URBINO. CONCERNING THE CONTEMPLATIVE AND ACTIVE LIFE. BEGINS HAPPILY.

**OF** ALL THE STUDIES, most illustrious Federico, through which the human race is exercised—varied and deeply different from one another as they are—this one especially is approved by the consensus of all in whom even a moderate prudence shines, and is preferred by the judgment of the wisest men as the best: that in which we investigate that final end of all things, which the Greeks call <term>the Goal</term> <note>original: "Telos" — the ultimate purpose or "supreme good" of human life.</note>. To this goal, as if arriving at the final marker of a racecourse, it is permitted for us to rest in safety and tranquility. Indeed, unless a certain and predetermined end were set before us by the highest God, I cannot find what I might imagine more miserable than the human condition. For if nature has established something ultimate and final for all other things—whether they have souls or lack them—whereby when they reach it they may rightly be called blessed, should we not think we have been dealt with most unfairly if man alone has nowhere to find that toward which he may direct his gravest and almost infinite labors, all his thoughts, and finally the course of his entire life? 

But truly, just as a target is set out in the distance for archers, at which they aim and direct their arrows, so too has this ultimate end—which I have spoken of—been set before man by that Nature which never fails in any thing. If he neglects this, man will always be miserable. But if all his ways of living tend toward that point, we shall achieve the highest happiness. Therefore, what could be imagined more foolish than we, who spend such constant vigils, endure such intolerable labors, and rush into such manifest dangers for things that will be of no benefit, and rather often do harm? Yet, of those things by which alone we might be armed against the various assaults of fortune, and by which we might know the difference between what is empty and shadowed and what is solid, distinct, and truly good—of those things we take not even the slightest care.

<margin>Leo B.</margin>
As I was often turning this matter over in my mind, it seemed not out of place if I were to commit to writing those conversations which I remember <term>Leon Battista Alberti</term> <note>1404–1472; the famous Renaissance "universal man," known here for his immense learning and eloquence.</note>—a man most practiced in every kind of learning of all those I have ever seen, and distinguished by the highest eloquence—held regarding the two-fold way of living which is proper to man, and concerning the ends of good and evil things, even drawing from the divine poetic allegories of <term>Maro</term> <note>The Roman poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro), whose works humanists often interpreted as philosophical allegories.</note>. Since it pleased me to honor our book with the authority of that prince who...

<summary>In this opening preface, Christophoro Landino addresses Federico da Montefeltro, arguing that the most important human study is the "Telos" (ultimate end). He introduces a dialogue featuring Leon Battista Alberti regarding the active and contemplative lives.</summary>
<keywords>Federico da Montefeltro, Leon Battista Alberti, Telos, supreme good, contemplative life, active life, Virgil, Humanism, philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dedication to Federico da Montefeltro, Duke of Urbino, following the introductory remarks on the ultimate goal of human life. The author, Cristoforo Landino, now moves from general philosophical principles to a direct appraisal of Federico’s character.</meta>

## On the active and contemplative life

Of all those whom our age has produced, who excelled so greatly in both those kinds <note>Referring to the active and contemplative lives mentioned in the title.</note>—whom, most illustrious Federico, could I compare to you? Even though I have long explored the whole of Italy in my mind and thoughts, I have found no one. For while some, endowed with a more elevated talent, have either so cultivated society and the state that—by advising prudently and acting rightly—they have been of use not only to themselves but to others as well; or those endowed with a greater mind, having left behind human affairs, have raised themselves to the contemplation of divine things: do we not recognize that both of these qualities are within you? It has not escaped you that those who have written about social and civil life have divided the whole matter into the study of peace and of war; they demonstrate that the former <note>Peace.</note> is to be sought for its own sake, <gloss>underlined: but the latter [war] is by no means sought for itself</gloss>. Rather, they show it must be undertaken so that one may return to peace. 

<margin><unclear>Beginning</unclear></margin> What virtue among those, then, was ever hidden from you? By these virtues, civic harmony and public leisure—the best foundations—were established in your state from the beginning and have been preserved thereafter. By these same virtues, we are also best guarded lest we fall into the hatred, envy, or contempt of the princes of foreign nations through our own fault. Indeed, when I recall from memory those things which you devised with a certain divine wisdom in governing your domain, and which you also administered through the highest justice; how much you have always valued faith and how much you wished it to be valued among all your people; and finally, when many examples offer themselves freely—by which your supreme religion toward the immortal God, your beneficial liberality, and your clemency (worthy of the best prince) are very well known to all Italy—I gladly recognize in you a second <term>Numa</term> <note>original: "Numam" — Numa Pompilius, the legendary second king of Rome, famous for his piety and for establishing religious and civil laws during a time of peace.</note>. 

In military matters, however, what can mortals desire from nature, or attain through industry, art, experience, or practice, that we do not see brought to perfection in you? We certainly consider it a marvelous thing, by the consensus of all nations—and one which appears very rarely in any century—that a man is found so distinguished by so many, such great, and such varied and difficult virtues, that in the gravest dangers, when the struggle is for the majesty of the empire, for liberty, for the common safety, and finally for life and blood, you may safely entrust to him the supreme command of the whole army. Regarding this matter, what should I specifically report about you? For I do not fear that I might seem to have said anything out of flattery, as these things are not only celebrated by all Italy but have also led many foreign peoples and nations into admiration. But by what logic can I now compress your achievements into the narrow confines of a preface, which could not be sufficiently explained according to their dignity even in the widest field of history? 

Because of the great fire of wars, by which nearly everything was long ago set ablaze, our age produced most excellent leaders in military affairs, with whom you necessarily had to contend at various times. But to omit many others, <margin><unclear>highest</unclear></margin> was it not the opinion of everyone regarding <term>Jacopo Piccinino</term> <note>original: "Iacobo Piccinino" — a famous Italian condottiero (mercenary general) and one of Federico's most formidable military rivals.</note> that he himself applied weightier counsel and no less audacity to his father's speed and cunning? And certainly in that war in which there was a struggle with such burning hatred for the <term>Kingdom of Apulia</term> <note>original: "Apuliæ regno" — refers to the Kingdom of Naples in Southern Italy, a frequent site of conflict between the houses of Anjou and Aragon.</note>, he conducted himself in such a way that we can name no one—besides you—by whose force and industry (I will not say by the benefit of fortune) he was pushed back. 

<margin><unclear>Malatesta</unclear></margin> <term>Sigismondo Malatesta</term> <note>The Lord of Rimini and Federico's lifelong arch-rival.</note> had blazed with immortal hatred toward you, and the enmities had proceeded so far that...

->A ii<-

He specifically highlights Federico’s military success against rivals like Jacopo Piccinino and his piety, comparing him to the Roman King Numa Pompilius.</summary>

# Book One
a iii

...did it not seem that either your side must perish, or yield to the victor? This struggle had raised the minds of all Italians: and although the greatest part of men hoped for victory for you because of the difference in your life and morals, the outcome of the matter appeared doubtful to everyone. For it was the common opinion that he <note>original: "illum"; referring to Sigismondo Pandolfo Malatesta (1417–1468), the Lord of Rimini and Federico's lifelong rival.</note> possessed the greatest power of gold; he certainly had a flourishing army. He himself was recognized as a distinguished leader through many virtues in warfare and long experience. Concerning you, however, I shall say nothing—lest while I pursue the truth, I might seem to disparage him who, now deceased, cannot clear himself of the charges. Yet, both the fair and the unfair must admit that you, provoked by the injury of your adversary, descended unwillingly into this contest; and driven by the most just causes, you undertook the task with great counsel and accomplished it with even greater spirit. 

Finally, you used victory so most mercifully <note>Federico was famous for his "clementia," or mercy, a key trait of the ideal Renaissance prince.</note> that all could now understand you preferred nothing more than peace—you who in war acted only with tranquility. You knew his two sons when they had come into your power: you consoled them with a long speech, raised them to a better hope, honored them with many gifts, and dismissed them unharmed, supported by many promises. Indeed, after their father’s death, you embraced the elder son with such affection that you even accepted him as your son-in-law; and whatever remained of his father's kingdom was defended and preserved for him under your leadership.

<margin>Rimini</margin>
For with what wisdom and what excellence of mind you liberated the city of Rimini <note>original: "Ariminum"</note> from a most difficult siege! You so overcame the strongest forces of the enemy that the greatest part of the soldiers were captured with their military standards, and all their baggage was plundered. 

<margin>Alexander<br/>Napoleone</margin>
At which time both Alexander Sforza <note>Lord of Pesaro and an ally of Federico.</note>—a leader distinguished in so many wars—and Napoleone Orsini—a man most celebrated among the leaders of soldiers—after they had resisted manfully for a long time, finally wounded, sought safety for themselves in flight. But these are a few examples out of many. For our age remembers in how many regions and in what diverse places and natures of terrain you fought with standards joined against various kinds of enemies; how many strong cities you besieged; how many besieged by enemies you liberated. 

<margin>Fabius<br/>Cato<br/>Sertorius</margin>
In these matters, who ever found wanting either your counsel in foresight or your maturity in acting? For what did either Fabius Maximus, or the two Catos, or Sertorius—

<margin>Hannibal<br/>Mithridates</margin>
—or even, to mention foreigners, Hannibal or Mithridates (the cunning of all of whom in conducting war is praised to the highest degree) ever devise more slyly, shrewdly, or cleverly against an enemy that escaped you in military affairs? Moreover, we have seen that endurance of labors has been the greatest praise for leaders in conducting business, when at the same time they perform the duty of both a supreme commander and a strenuous soldier. 

<margin>Jugurtha</margin>
Such a man, to omit others, history records Gaius Marius to have been among the Romans, and Jugurtha <margin>I</margin> among the Numidians. Moreover, who doubts that the strength and vigor of the body has had such power in a commander that they often restored a situation already deeply lost? 

<margin>Caesar<br/>Scipios</margin>
Thus we read of both Julius Caesar and those two Scipios who, in Spain, after most famously conducting affairs, fell manfully. 

<margin>Marcellus</margin>
Likewise Marcus Marcellus; 

<margin>Leonidas<br/>Themistocles</margin>
and among the Greeks, Leonidas the Spartan, Themistocles the Athenian, 

<margin>Epaminondas</margin>
and Epaminondas the Theban, who by their own example and by being the first to expose themselves to dangers—

<meta>This page concludes the introductory dedication to Federico da Montefeltro and begins the narrative frame of the "Disputationes Camaldulenses." Landino moves from Federico’s military successes to his intellectual life, describing how the Duke manages to balance the "active" life of a soldier with the "contemplative" life of a scholar. He then introduces the setting of the dialogue: the monastery of Camaldoli, where a group of Florentine elites, including Lorenzo de' Medici, have gathered to escape the summer heat.</meta>

# On the active & contemplative life
## 6
### iiii

...when you had led the army to that point where they could be moved by no prayers, no threats, and no kind of speech before. These two things <note>The "two things" refer to Federico’s military prowess and his subsequent mercy (clementia) toward his defeated enemies mentioned on the previous page.</note> not only those who favor you truly attribute to you, but your enemies too are forced to concede, even against their will. But what am I doing? For I did not set out at this time to pursue your praises. That would require more time and more leisure. Thus, so much for your actions. 

However, in the life of leisure <note>original: "ociosa uita"; here referring to the *vita contemplativa*, the noble pursuit of study and reflection rather than idleness.</note> and the study of literature, who does not know that you have been so engaged since your tender years? When you had drunk in childhood learning with a wonderful greed during a total holiday from other matters, no subsequent time was ever so busy for you that you did not daily subtract some part from your occupations and devote it to the study of various doctrines. Thus, since in your early days you could already interpret the poets excellently and had unrolled the records of all histories; and since you had not only made the precepts of speaking most familiar to yourself but had also diligently exercised yourself in them, you then turned all your care to philosophy itself. 

In this matter, you imitated the <margin>Al.</margin> examples of the greatest leaders. For you had read that Alexander the Great made use of Aristotle, and Scipio Africanus of Panaetius the Stoic, and that many other leaders made use of most learned men as teachers; you always keep men outstanding in every kind of learning generously and honorably with you. For whether you are at home, or abroad, or finally at war, you always have many around you who can discourse learnedly, elegantly, and ingeniously on the greatest matters, so that even amid the noise and tumult of great wars, the voices of those disputing were heard constantly. Thus, it can no longer seem a wonder to anyone if all proclaim you the most just in peace, the most brave in war, and the most wise in both; so too they judge you the most learned in the leisure of letters. 

To whom, therefore, ought I to dedicate this book—in which the two kinds of living are debated—rather than to you, who have so embraced both that you excel in both? But so that we may hear <term>Baptista</term> <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous Renaissance architect and polymath, who serves as the lead speaker in this dialogue.</note> himself disputing as soon as possible, first hear briefly where a conversation of this kind took its origin. 

When my brother Pietro and I had come into our Casentino territory, both to avoid the heat and to relax the mind, it pleased us the next day to ascend into the Camaldolese forest <note>Camaldoli is a famous monastery in the mountains of Tuscany, known for its extreme seclusion and natural beauty.</note>, a region most well-known to you and to all of Italy for its ancient religion. There, even when the <margin>Hom.</margin> <term>Dog Star</term> <note>original: "syrius"; the star Sirius, which the ancients believed caused the oppressive and sickly heat of mid-to-late summer.</note> rages violently—bringing diseases and fevers to mortals (as it says in **Homer**) —one may enjoy a springtime and exceptionally healthy climate with the greatest pleasure. 

And so, when we had first reached the <term>cenobites</term> <note>from the Greek *koinos bios*, "common life"; these are monks who live together in a community.</note> and then the <term>hermits</term> <note>from the Greek *eremites*, "person of the desert"; these are monks who live in total solitude.</note> (to use the Greek words), we learned that shortly before us, <term>Lorenzo de' Medici</term> <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" (1449–1492), the brilliant statesman and patron of the Florentine Renaissance.</note> had arrived there with his brother Giuliano. He had brought with him from our city Alamanno Rinuccini, Pietro and Donato Acciaiuoli, Marco Parenti, and Antonio Canigiani—men of the highest learning. These were men who, since they had achieved power and a richness of speaking through the most exact art and long exercise from their earliest years, had subsequently made the greatest progress in philosophy through vehement and long-lasting study. Therefore, we went directly to that little cell in which they were resting, exhausted by the difficulty of the journey, and we came upon them as they were sitting. When they...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the dedicatory preface to the narrative proper of the "Disputationes Camaldulenses." Having established the setting at the monastery of Camaldoli, Landino now introduces the arrival of the key participants: Leon Battista Alberti, Marsilio Ficino, and the Medici brothers, setting the stage for the philosophical debate on the active versus the contemplative life.</meta>

# Book One

...they looked at us, expecting no such thing: moved by sudden joy they immediately rose and after those words which the most intimate friends are accustomed to say at a first meeting:

"O Landino," Laurentius <note>Lorenzo de' Medici (1449–1492), the de facto ruler of Florence and a patron of the arts.</note> said, "nothing more desirable could have happened to us than for you to find us in this kind of solitude; for since for these several days—in which everything is scorched by the most intense heat—we had fled from urban cares and weariness into this pleasant place with the intention of enjoying the most temperate climate of these mountains and engaging in some pleasure of the mind, I hope that if I join you to these philosophers, we shall miss no kind of joy or sweetness."

<margin>Marsilio</margin> To which, when I had already begun to respond, a sudden event interrupted the speech. For it was announced that Leo BAPTISTA Alberti <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous Renaissance polymath, architect, and philosopher.</note> would arrive shortly after. For he had recently come from Rome; and when he had detoured on the Aretine way at Figline to visit Marsilio Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), the leader of the Platonic Academy in Florence and a central figure of the Renaissance.</note>, a man who is easily the prince among the Platonists of our time, they decided by common counsel not to go to Florence before they had avoided the entire dog days <note>original: "caniculam"; the "dog days" refer to the hottest period of summer when the Dog Star, Sirius, rises with the sun.</note> within the Casentino valley. These men, therefore, had now reached the monastery; and having sent away their horses, they were ascending to us with a slow step along with Mariotto the Camaldolese abbot <note>Mariotto di Alessio, the superior of the monastery at Camaldoli.</note>, a man proven in both religion and learning. At this news, everyone was moved by sudden joy, and at the same time, inflamed with the highest ardor for meeting and conversing, we went out to meet them; we came together, we greeted one another. The rest of that day—for the sun was already descending toward its setting—we spent in the conversations of Baptista. For he was a man most accomplished in every kind of humanity and every branch of the arts of all those whom many centuries have produced. For why should I speak of letters, when absolutely nothing exists that it is permitted for a man to know, in which he was not skillfully and prudently engaged? 

Therefore, on the following day, when we had all risen and attended the sacred rites <note>The morning Mass.</note>, it was decided for the sake of health and pleasure to walk through the upper woods reaching toward the mountain ridge; and little by little we came to that place where a spreading beech tree covered a clear spring with its outstretched branches in a flowery meadow.

<margin>Socratic</margin> Here Baptista said:
"Behold for you, best of men, the tree itself and the stream running down from the spring with a sweet murmur reflects the image of the plane tree and the Socratic water <note>A reference to the *Phaedrus*, a dialogue by Plato where Socrates and Phaedrus sit under a plane tree by the Ilissus river to discuss love and rhetoric.</note>. Moreover, the seats which you see on every side, begun by nature but slightly refined by pastoral labor, will receive us most comfortably, so that after this little walk through the sloping ground we may rest quite conveniently."

And after we sat down:
"Indeed," he said, "I think those men are very blessed who, since they are men of letters, even if not always, yet often—either by settling their public and private cares or putting them off to another time—betake themselves to some solitude in this way: so that just as among our theologians <margin>Martha</margin> Martha is left behind, and from those waves in which she always seethes, one rests in the safe and tranquil harbor of <margin>Mary</margin> Mary <note>In Christian tradition, the sisters Martha and Mary represent the "Active" and "Contemplative" lives respectively. Martha is busy with chores, while Mary listens to Christ.</note>. From her high rocks, as if from some tall watchtower, one may not only look down upon the lands and seas, but much more ardently look up at the heavens themselves. And like those other Platonists, having recovered, one may fly around this whole upper world like <margin>Zethus</margin> a second Zethus <note>In classical mythology, the brothers Zethus and Amphion represent the conflict between the practical/active life and the musical/contemplative life.</note>. And although this must often be done by all men of letters, it is most just that you, LORENZO and Giuliano, do it very often. For you see..."

<summary>This page describes the arrival of Leon Battista Alberti and Marsilio Ficino at the monastery of Camaldoli, where they join Lorenzo de' Medici and others. The group retreats to a scenic spring, sparking a discussion by Alberti on the necessity of retreating from public life to pursue contemplation, using the biblical figures of Martha and Mary as symbols.</summary>

# On the Active and Contemplative Life

<meta>catchword: ra</meta>
<meta>image quality: good</meta>

<margin>As-m</margin> ...the great weight of the state <note>original: "rei.p." / "rei publicae"; the Florentine Republic</note> must now be supported by your shoulders because of the worsening illness of your father <note>Piero "the Gouty" de' Medici (1416–1469), whose chronic health issues forced his sons into leadership early.</note>. In this matter, although your virtue, **LORENZO**, appears clearly—and it should be considered divine rather than human—the fact that in your adolescent years there is no matter so great or so difficult that you cannot conceive it with the wisdom of an elder <note>original: "senili prudentia"; Landino is praising Lorenzo’s maturity, which exceeds his young age.</note> and undertake it with an unconquered greatness of soul. Although you are flourishing in that age which is usually accustomed to seething with every kind of desire, and although you are placed in that fortune and that liberty of all things in which we often see great men—even those distinguished by long experience and remarkable virtues—shaken from their firmest standing, you have never crossed the boundaries of modesty. Nevertheless, I believe it is in the great interest of both yourself and the state that, since you are soon to undertake its administration—or rather, have already largely undertaken it—whatever leisure you can steal from public business, you should devote it all to this: that far from urban tumults, either by yourself or rather with most learned men who are also most fond of you, you should inquire into and achieve through discussion those things by which our souls are led into the knowledge of their own origin and divinity. For no one will rightly administer either himself or the state unless he has first purified his soul from every bodily stain with those virtues that amend life and conduct, and then, once it is purged, has so illuminated it with those virtues that provide knowledge of the greatest things, that he may rightly know what he himself is and what other men are, and for what purpose they were produced by the supreme God. 

<margin>q:</margin> Indeed, I think this reason moved that divine Plato so that—although in almost all other things he hardly dares to affirm anything—he nevertheless seems to feel this without any doubt: that those republics will finally be happy which either philosophers administer, or those who administer them begin to practice philosophy. 

Proceed then, excellent young men. For since it has been achieved by the diligence of your father and grandfather <note>Cosimo de' Medici (1389–1464), the founder of the Medici political dynasty.</note> that you have been so nurtured in literature from almost your very infancy that you seem to have sucked it in together with your nurse's milk; and since you have now progressed so far that there is no cause for regret; and finally, since a most sharp wit, a most mature judgment, and the benefit of nature and your own practice shine forth in you, you will easily be able to reach your goal. 

Then **LORENZO** said: "You advise us wisely indeed, so that I may also answer for my brother, and in an entirely fatherly way. Therefore, so that you may see how much I value your precepts, I shall ask of you—since you have made mention of this way of life which is placed in the investigation of great things—that you proceed, I pray, and explain the whole matter more broadly to me and my brother, who are most eager to know. For although every reason of human actions—whether you are instructing yourself, or caring for your family and estate, or finally touching upon public duties—is directed toward the standard of those virtues which we name concerning life and conduct <note>The "moral" or "cardinal" virtues of the active life.</note>, and we achieve those mostly by use and habit; nevertheless, since whatever is said by Plato now seems more true to me than any oracle when it comes from the mouth of Marsilio <note>Marsilio Ficino.</note>—who beyond everyone else possesses the mind of so great a philosopher—I most ardently desire to know what it is that you, following that author <note>Plato</note>, judged should be borrowed from those who are engaged in the investigation of truth by one who governs the state."

<summary>Landino encourages the young Lorenzo de' Medici to balance his political duties with philosophical study, citing Plato's ideal of the philosopher-king. Lorenzo responds by asking for a deeper explanation of how the contemplative pursuit of truth can guide a ruler's active life.</summary>

<header>Book One</header>

# Book One

Since you have always been approachable and generous toward those who seek something from you, do not now prove yourself difficult toward us, contrary to your nature and character. This is especially true since the beauty of this place, the hour of the day, and the breath of the sweetly cooling breeze not only allow for this but even demand it; moreover, the murmur of the gliding stream and the harmonizing voices of the various birds invite us quite charmingly. When Lorenzo had said these things, and all of us—admiring the young man’s <note>Lorenzo de' Medici was in his early twenties at the time of this fictionalized dialogue.</note> character—vehemently approved of the proposal, **BAPTISTA** <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the renowned architect, theorist, and "Renaissance Man."</note> smiled gently and said: "I would rather have less influence with you than have you demonstrate so suddenly how much you value me, especially in a matter that requires a much more careful investigation than this country retreat of ours—undertaken for the sake of leisure rather than debate—can provide. For this is a very difficult question: one that can neither be easily explained by the most learned men in an extemporaneous debate, nor by those less learned (among whom I allow myself to be numbered) except after long meditation." 

"But with what face could I now deny anything to one of your age, whom even those who are less known and more stern are compelled to favor because of your burning zeal for learning? And indeed, since I hear from many who come to us daily from Rome <note>Alberti spent much of his career in the papal bureaucracy in Rome.</note> a most constant report that you have long since made up your mind to honor all who excel in any kind of learning with every type of honor and reward, and that many have already experienced your effort and generosity, I would seem to be under the charge of being not only a harsh man but an ungrateful one if I allowed a man who has deserved so well of the literati to lack for anything. Add to this that since I encouraged you just a moment ago toward this kind of debate, I would be failing the duty of a good man and a friend if I allowed you to desire something so honorable in vain. Therefore, I will approach the matter, not so much raised up by the hope of completing it, as compelled by the shame of refusing. For I would rather have my wisdom questioned by these most learned men present while I comply with you, than be accused of a lack of humanity if I did the opposite. And since you ask it, I believe we must debate both types of life in such a way that I first pursue each one separately. Then, I shall compare them with each other so that, although in this life we live we believe that man to be most perfect who has joined both, it may nevertheless appear which of the two is primarily more excellent. And so that I may seem to have the wisdom of a philosopher in the sight of such great philosophers, let us take our beginning in this way."

<margin>
The soul is the principle of life in itself, insofar as it is the soul; but it also understands insofar as it <unclear>leans toward</unclear> its <unclear>higher</unclear> duties.
</margin>

That the soul <note>original: "Anima"</note> is the principle of life is something that not only the more learned prove with manifest arguments, but even all those who possess healthy senses can see with their own eyes. However, when we act with prudence, justice, fortitude, and temperance <note>These are the four "Cardinal Virtues" of classical philosophy.</note> regarding those things which pertain to our common life, or when we call the mind away from the senses to look upon and contemplate something divine and immortal, we no longer say the soul can do this by the simple fact that it is the "soul," but by the fact that it possesses the power to achieve such strengths. Therefore, since we are produced by nature for acting rightly and for investigating the truth, it has pleased the more learned that, just as there are these two duties of human life, so there are two [ways of living]...

<vocab>Laurentius: Lorenzo de' Medici; BAPTISTA: Leon Battista Alberti; Roma: Rome; anima: soul; vita: life; disputatio: debate/discourse; philosophus: philosopher; munera: duties/functions</vocab>

<summary>Leon Battista Alberti agrees to Lorenzo de' Medici's request to debate the merits of the active versus the contemplative life. Alberti begins the philosophical inquiry by defining the soul as the principle of life and identifying its two primary functions: moral action in society and the pursuit of divine truth.</summary>

->### On the Active and Contemplative Life<-

<margin>characteristic</margin> ...to set down the types of living. "These things," said **LORENZO**, "are most excellent. But if someone were now to ask me whether there are also other duties which the human soul performs besides these you were mentioning? I would answer that there are indeed several more." <margin>imagination</margin> "The soul performs many," said **BAPTISTA**, "while it is in the body. But it does not escape you that when we investigate the life of man, only that is required which is so much its own that it belongs to man alone, and to no one else. What, then, shall we say belongs to someone, unless it is that toward which he is most inclined and disposed? For they <note>Referring to the Peripatetic (Aristotelian) philosophers.</note> said a thing 'lives' by that which is both its own and toward which it moves of its own accord.

If we wish this to be so, we shall never say a man 'lives' for the reason that he nourishes his body, or raises it from a tiny size to its proper stature, or produces another like himself. For these duties of life are not his own or unique, but are shared with crops and trees; nor again because he either feels or moves.

<margin>of beasts / of an ox</margin>
For how does he differ in this from the beasts? But it is because he understands, which he surely has from the mind; once you depart from man, you will find no living creature that shares in it. Now, it belongs to the mind both to act with reason and to contemplate the truth. Therefore, once those other duties of life—which are no more ours than they are the other animals'—have been excluded, we shall say that the life of man, by which he is truly man, consists in action and contemplation. For as to the fact that some strive to add a third type—which depends on pleasure—to those two, they seem to be vehemently mistaken. For although

<margin>compared to beasts / animal</margin>
most mortals—either given over to sleep and the belly, or charmed by the enticements of Venus <note>A metaphor for sexual or carnal desire.</note>, and always face-down on the ground—behold nothing lofty, I do not see why they should be numbered among men rather than cattle. For as for the bodily pleasures by which the senses are tickled: since they are not from the mind but from the senses themselves, why should we think they ought to be ascribed to man rather than to the other living creatures?

Since, therefore, the mind alone is our own, unless we degenerate from our nature, we shall live with it as our guide: so that we direct all our pursuits either toward the necessities of life—and not for ourselves alone, but since we are born for community and society, let us consult the interests of parents, children, and all friends, and cultivate the just and the right—or, having either cast aside civil cares and actions or deferred them to another time, we may lift ourselves up to the contemplation of truth. Therefore, we either act or we meditate; nor does it disturb me that some, dealing with us too captiously, deny that contemplation is a kind of living. For they say that the name 'life' carries with it a certain motion, whereas contemplation itself is situated in rest rather than in motion. <margin>solution</margin> Even if I do not deny this—especially since it is written in the book titled *Wisdom*: **"Entering into my house, I shall rest with her"** <note>A quotation from the Wisdom of Solomon 8:16; the "her" refers to Wisdom herself.</note>—who nevertheless would not see that even if we must be free from external motions while we are engaged in the investigation of things, it nonetheless cannot be completed without some kind of motion? <note>Alberti argues that mental activity is itself a form of internal "motion" or life.</note> It remains, therefore, since we are inquiring into the life of man and not that of the other living creatures, that it consists either in doing things or in the knowledge of truth; and so in both, that if it is done by right reason, we both do something pleasing to the immortal God and deserve the very best from the human race. These things you [will find] among the ancient poets, whom in the ancient religion they called Theolo[gians]... <note>In the Renaissance, poets like Orpheus or Homer were often called "ancient theologians" because their myths were believed to hide sacred truths.</note>

<summary>Leon Battista Alberti defines the unique human life as consisting of either moral action or the contemplation of truth, rejecting purely biological or pleasure-seeking existences as sub-human. He argues that even though contemplation involves physical rest, it is an active movement of the mind.</summary>
<keywords>active life, contemplative life, Leon Battista Alberti, Lorenzo de' Medici, human nature, reason, virtue, Wisdom of Solomon, civic duty, philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Lorenzo de' Medici and Leon Battista Alberti. Alberti is expanding on the two modes of human life: the active and the contemplative, harmonizing classical poetry, biblical allegory, and Greek philosophy.</meta>

<header>Book One</header>

# Book One

<margin>Virgil</margin>
...it is agreed <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of the two paths of life.</note> that these two duties existed. This is approved among our own people with the firmest consensus of all. Indeed, to omit all others, does not Maro <note>The Roman poet Virgil, whose full name was Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> propose certain eternal rewards for those who have finished their lives? He puts forward those "who enriched life through the arts they discovered." <note>A quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid*, Book 6, line 663.</note> He praises, therefore, those who through long investigation devised various disciplines and sciences, or added significant improvements to those things others had already devised. But so as not to leave the other kind of life unhonored, he follows up with right actions: "Here are the bands who suffered wounds fighting for their country; and the priests who remained chaste while life lasted; and the pious poets who spoke words worthy of Phoebus." <note>Phoebus is another name for Apollo, the god of prophecy and poetry. Virgil here lists the heroes of the "active" life in the underworld.</note>

As for what pertains to Christians, the matter is so clear it needs no proof. For both in the ancient writings of the Hebrews under the name of 
<margin>Mary, Rachel. Martha, Leah.</margin>
Rachel, and in those teachings which flowed from Christ under the name of Mary, we see the contemplative life <term>speculatio|From the Latin 'speculari' (to observe or scout). In this context, it refers to the 'contemplative life'—the intellectual or spiritual pursuit of truth.</term> expressed. And again, in the former through Leah, and in the latter through Martha, we see the active life demonstrated. Both types of living, therefore, are so much our own that whether men excel in the one or the other, we follow their lives with much praise.

But since all things are best distinguished by their "end" <note>The Greek concept of *telos*, or the ultimate goal/purpose of a thing.</note>, who does not see that just as in acting we set before ourselves what is right and just, so all investigation is referred to the truth? Indeed, this investigation, ascending through many steps in its own order, gradually emerges to gaze upon the very <unclear>incorporeal</unclear> and divine essence of God. <note>The OCR reads 'corpoream' (corporeal), but the context of ascending from matter suggests Landino likely wrote 'incorpoream' (incorporeal) or was referring to the 'body' of divine truth.</note> For we see—though their number is small—some who are warned by certain shadows and images of those things which fall upon our senses. These men are inflamed with such an ardent love for celestial things that they leave behind all cares and business. Though they had previously perceived physical bodies through the senses and the likenesses of bodies through imagination, they thereafter perceive the nature of bodies by reason itself; then they perceive incorporeal but created spirits by the intellect; and finally, by the intelligence, they gaze upon that which is uncreated. This is an altogether marvelous progress, through which our soul—gradually withdrawing itself from the most humble prison of the body and raised toward higher things—ascends from the lowest dregs of matter to the very summit of divinity. Therefore, we shall truly conclude this whole topic if we say the office of contemplation is concerned with the truth, since by the truth itself the human mind is perfected and completed.

<margin>Doubt. Solution.</margin>
But you will say, perhaps: will this truth be found in just any kind of thing? It will indeed, but especially if we are occupied with the knowledge of God. For our investigation sets Him before itself as the ultimate finish line of its course, to which all human actions and thoughts are referred. When you have reached Him, there is nowhere further to go. For there we discover that final and ultimate—and also the "highest good"—which we all desire by nature’s guidance, but which few understand once the clouds of error and ignorance are removed. This is what not only Christians preach, but what the Aristotelians understood long before Christ was born, and the Platonists understood long before others.

For the divine Plato thinks that our souls—after they have fallen from the bosom of God into these lowest dregs, or rather were drawn down to adorn this lowest part of the world—remain for a rather long time from such an exalted and headlong fall...

---

<margin>†</margin> <insert>† Here: the highest good is in God, by the consensus of Plato, Aristotle, and the Christians.</insert>

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of the human soul through the active and contemplative lives, using Virgil's poetry and Biblical figures like Mary and Martha to illustrate the point. It describes the intellectual ascent from sensory perception to the contemplation of God, defined as the "Highest Good" (Summumm Bonum) recognized by both pagan philosophers and Christian doctrine.</summary>

->## On the Active and Contemplative Life<-

<meta>catchword: what</meta>

...astonished, and from the tumult of the forest <note>In Renaissance Neo-Platonism, the "forest" (Latin: *silva*) often represents the chaos of the material world or the confusion of the senses.</note> which they have entered, they remain stunned and as if drunk. This lasts until gradually the divinity within them, awakening itself, returns to some memory—however obscured—of divine things. From this memory, the soul is kindled with a supreme ardor and strives to recognize those things again. Supported by justice and religion, as if by two wings, it raises itself on high and, not without the greatest pleasure, gazes upon the light of God, insofar as the sharp edge of the mind <note>original: "animi acies"</note>—dulled by the contagion of the body—allows it. But indeed, when our souls have at last been stripped of all mortality and returned to their simple nature, only then will it be possible not just to soothe, but to utterly extinguish our long-standing thirst for knowing God.

But I return to man—that is, to that living being who, since he consists of both soul and body, is able by the strength of the soul to overcome the weakness of the body in some part. For by leading the mind away from the senses, uplifted by his own wisdom and equipped with many doctrines, he is gradually led to the knowledge of those things I mentioned a moment ago, where at last he may be nourished on ambrosia and nectar. <margin>Plato</margin> When Plato says this, what else does he mean except that the man enjoys the knowledge of God and the pleasure perceived therefrom? For although he attains this most especially through those virtues which belong to the mind—since **Intelligence** <term>intelligentia|In the Aristotelian tradition, the intuitive grasp of first principles that cannot be proven but are self-evident.</term> perceives the first principles of things, **Science** <term>scientia|The logical knowledge of effects and conclusions derived from those first principles.</term> perceives the progress and effects flowing from those principles, and finally **Wisdom** <term>sapientia|The highest intellectual virtue, combining both intuition (intelligence) and demonstrated knowledge (science) regarding the most honorable things.</term> perceives both—nevertheless, we shall attempt these things in vain unless we are free from every kind of disturbance. For how shall we be able to think of anything lofty or excellent if we are charmed by bodily pleasures, or crushed by greed, or swollen with ambition? 

Therefore, they judge that those virtues by which our souls are purged of all the filth of vice must also be applied. And they must be applied in such a way that we begin there. <margin>Plato</margin> For if (as it is found in Plato) it is a sacrilege for the impure to touch the pure, <margin>The Gospel</margin> and if in the Gospel it says: **"Blessed are the pure in heart: for they shall see God,"** <note>A quotation from the Beatitudes, Matthew 5:8.</note> then surely we shall attempt such an investigation in vain unless we bring to it a mind wiped clean of every stain. For it is a virtue to flee vice, and the first wisdom is to have been free from folly. Since these things were most rightly said by Horace, <margin>Horace</margin> the same Horace surely saw what many centuries before either the poet or the prophet <margin>David</margin> David had excellently expressed in a psalm. For he cites one who asks: **"Who shall ascend into the mountain of the Lord? Or who shall stand in his holy place?"** <note>Psalm 24:3.</note> To this questioner, he immediately responds: **"He that hath clean hands and a pure heart."** <note>Psalm 24:4.</note> Therefore, he who restrains not only his hand but also his mind from vices will be fit to ascend the mountain of the Lord. For that immovable rest <note>original: "quies"</note> by which alone we can be blessed is not found in the ascent itself, but where you have come to the summit by ascending. Therefore, rest is not granted unless you have first labored. Nor will you sit comfortably on the level ridge of the mountain, from where all things may be surveyed, unless you have first reached the top by crawling up through the steep rocks.

I see now what you are doing, says *LORENZO*. I perceive that you by no means place the highest good in those actions which we direct according to the rule of those virtues called "moral" or "active." But perhaps you will explain this more fully a little later. In the meantime, however, since we are dwelling on the power of contemplation <note>original: "speculandi ui"</note>, I would greatly desire—unless perhaps it seems otherwise to you—that your feelings on this whole matter be explained by you most diligently. For I see that not a little...

<summary>Alberti explains that the soul must be purified of vices and worldly passions to ascend to the contemplation of God, drawing parallels between Platonic philosophy, Horace's poetry, and the Biblical Psalms. Lorenzo de' Medici interrupts to ask for a deeper explanation of why the "highest good" lies in contemplation rather than active moral virtue.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 5, where Leon Battista Alberti (Baptista) is discussing the soul's ascent. The previous page ended with the idea that man enjoys the knowledge of God; this page transitions into a technical debate about whether that ultimate happiness is an act of the intellect or the will.</meta>

# Book One.

b

<insert>attains the end.</insert>

...the most learned men doubt whether this matter should be placed in the mind or rather in the will. <term>Indeed</term> <note>original: "Quin"</note>, **BAPTISTA** says, "there is such controversy and discord among them that they are led into a final struggle; often they engage in battle with banners flying, and finally depart with the outcome so doubtful that you cannot easily discern which way victory has leaned. For each side offers arguments that you might seem able to defend. If we propose Truth as the goal in speculation <note>speculation here refers to deep intellectual study or "looking into" divine things</note>, who does not know that truth is subject to the intellect alone? For this reason, those who know Hebrew interpret Rachel—whom they set as a symbol of speculation—as 'the vision of the beginning.' <margin>+</margin> <margin>The Mind and the Intellect</margin> On the other hand, since those who speculate strive with all their strength to know the truth—and all striving excited within us proceeds from the will—the matter seems placed no longer in the mind, but in the will. 

But there are those who, like honorary arbitrators, strive to settle such a great suit and resolve the controversy. And indeed, if you look at the essence of the action itself, you will without any doubt refer it to the intellect. But if you more diligently weigh that by which we are moved to action, you will judge that it should be referred to the will. For the Will moves not only the other powers of the soul, but the mind itself. For we are kindled toward the knowledge of truth by that appetite which obeys reason—which all philosophers call the 'will.' In this matter, it makes no difference whether this happens out of a desire for knowing, or whether the pleasure perceived from knowledge draws us to the same place. From here, thought arises; from here, meditation is excited. From here we contemplate; from here we wonder; and finally, from here we speculate with the greatest diligence. Although all these things tend toward the same goal, I will nonetheless explain more carefully what each one signifies. 

The mind, therefore, **thinks** <note>original: "Cogitat"</note> when it **gathers** <note>original: "cogit"; the author is using a wordplay: 'thinking' comes from 'gathering'</note> many things into one, so that soon, once the other false things are removed from there, it may finally choose the truth. Since many things offer themselves from every side—which, because they are similar to the truth, pull the soul in different directions—it is necessary that the wise man find a remedy for this kind of evil. For this reason, he first throws these things together as if into a heap, and then, by reasoning and gradually refuting the rest, he leaves only that which he has found to be true because it cannot be refuted. Do you see, therefore, that because these things are gathered into one, this kind of action of the mind is called 'thought' [cogitatio]? 

We say the same thing, by a similar reasoning, for 'to suppose' [putare]. For to suppose [putare] is 'to prune' [purgare]. Hence we speak of 'pruning trees' [putare arbores] because they are rendered pure when the superfluous parts are removed. Hence 'pure gold' is that which is cleansed. From this comes 'to dispute' [disputare], when we apply that kind of reason in which words are set forth that are pure and clear, so that nothing is rendered confused or obscured. But to return to the first point: we shall say that **thought** involves the inspection of many things. 

In **meditation**, however, one finds a certain progress of reason, a journey from those principles which reach toward the speculation of truth; in this, there is a certain exercise. For he who meditates, like a runner, races from the 'starting gates' <note>original: "carcere"; referring to the starting stalls in a Roman chariot race</note> of the principles themselves all the way to the 'goal posts' of the concluded arguments. That this word expresses 'exercise' among the Latins is testified by Maro <note>The poet Virgil</note>. For he says: *The heifer practiced [meditata] for the plague of Achaia.* <note>Reference to Virgil's Georgics 3.153</note> Juvenal is also a witness: *Having practiced [meditatus] Tuscan battles in a marble villa.* <note>Juvenal, Satires 7.128</note> For we see the word is derived from a Greek word: since they also use *melete* <margin>Meditation: *melete* [μελετη]</margin> in the same sense. 

**Contemplation**, however, I see defined by our people as a sharp-sighted and firm [gaze] of the soul upon the truth...

</insert>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 5, where Leon Battista Alberti (Baptista) is discussing the contemplative life with Lorenzo de' Medici (Laurentius). Having previously touched on the role of the will and the intellect, the text now moves into a technical and etymological exploration of what it means to "contemplate" and "speculate," drawing on Roman history, St. Augustine, and the mystical theology of Pseudo-Dionysius.</meta>

### On the active and contemplative life.

<margin>
<unclear>contemplation</unclear>
<unclear>active</unclear>
<unclear>... is</unclear>
<unclear>...ing</unclear>
</margin>
...invited to knowledge: if you seek the origin of this word, it will not escape you that the ancient Latins called a "temple" <note>original: "templum"</note> that space in the sky which the <term>augurs</term> <note>Ancient Roman religious officials who observed the flight of birds to determine the will of the gods</note> designated with a <term>lituus</term> <note>A curved staff used as a ritual tool</note> for the purpose of taking omens; they never turned their gaze away from this space until a bird entered it. For this reason, we say that those who remain fixed in investigating a matter are "contemplating" <note>original: "contemplari"</note>, based on this very firm gazing at the "temple." 

Furthermore, let us now pass to "admiration" <note>original: "admiratio," often translated as wonder or awe</note>, which is called a stupor arising from the perception of a thing that exceeds our faculty. For admiration is usually the companion of that investigation by which we discover something that hitherto strongly disagreed with our opinion. But enough regarding these remaining matters. 

If you listen to Saint Augustine, you will think that "speculation" <note>original: "speculari"</note> is derived from "mirror" <note>original: "speculo"</note>. For it happens to those desiring to know the truth that we look upon certain likenesses of truth in those effects which flow from causes, just as we look upon the images of bodies in a mirror. You have here the explanation of the words. 

However, I would not wish you to take it in such a way—when we call the action of speculating a "motion"—that these words seem spoken against those who place speculation not in motion, but in rest. For we say that speculation is indeed a motion, but only in that sense in which a motion of a thing is called an act already absolute and perfect. For since our minds find a way to attain those things which can be perceived by the mind alone through those things which are perceived by the senses—and the actions of the senses are not without motion—it pleased [the ancients] that these states, which are arrived at through the senses, should also be called "motion." 

I could report many things in this place concerning this "motion," especially those things which are most sharply said both by **Dionysius** <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century Christian mystic whose works on the soul's "motions" were foundational for Renaissance philosophy</note> and by many of the Christian theologians who followed him. But perhaps these things are less desired by you. Therefore, let us pursue the rest. 

"On the contrary," says **LAURENTIUS**, "those things are of primary importance. For what can be brought forward by **Dionysius**, who was cultivated in all human and divine doctrine, that should not be learned with the greatest industry?" 

"Let us then grant you your wish," says **BAPTISTA**. "And since we must speak of this kind of 
<margin>
rectilinear
circular
oblique
</margin>
motion, I see that there are three types attributed to our souls: the **rectilinear**, the **circular**, and the **oblique**, which consists of both of the others. 

There is **rectilinear** motion in us when, in actions regarding the mind, we proceed from one thing to another. But if we are moved in such a way that there is one and the same simple form of moving, they say it is **circular** motion. For it is the nature of a circle to be moved around a center, always remaining at the same distance. If, however, something is mixed into this kind of motion that also proceeds toward diverse things, since it degenerates from the prior form, it will no longer be circular, but **oblique**. 

Therefore, although the bodily motions which arise from those things which strike the soul from external speculation disturb rest, the motions 
<margin>
Doubt:
Solution.
</margin>
which I was just mentioning not only do not disturb it, but greatly increase it. But you will say: are these motions of our souls the same as those of the separate essences, whom our people call **angels**, while the ancients called them sometimes **demons** and sometimes **gods**? 

Not at all. For we have said that rectilinear motion in the human soul occurs when we proceed from those things which strike the senses externally to those things which are perceived by the mind alone. But an **angel** attains the truth with his mind neither from the variety of composite things, nor again because he proceeds little by little by reasoning. But whatever he is capable of, he does all of it with a simple—so to speak—[intellection]...

<margin>
...calls [those] essences ours:
angels: the ancients [called them] then demons,
gods, etc. intelligences.
</margin>

He distinguishes between human discursive reasoning, which moves from the senses to the mind, and the direct, simple intuition of angelic beings.</summary>

# Book One.

<sig>b ii</sig>
<meta>catchword: administra- (fragment at bottom of page)</meta>

<margin>☿</margin> ...it is effective through its gaze. For it gazes upon God for as long as it exists, in an assiduous and always identical manner, without any beginning and without any end. In this same form, a circle is always moved around its center; therefore, the soul knows God not by a straight path like a human, but by an orbital motion <note>original: "orbiculato motu"; this refers to a state of perfect, unchanging focus on the divine center.</note>. At the beginning, our souls are unable to be driven in such a circuit. For they do not arrive at that state where they might apply the same constant form to their own movements until both that "straight" motion and the motion that proceeds through the progression of reasoning have entirely ceased. When these have finally 
<margin>In the knowledge of principles, error can occur.</margin>ceased, they then cling to the knowledge of divine things with an immovable gaze, so that they are agitated by no other motion. In this state there is no error, just as 
<margin>Doubt? Solution.</margin>we do not err regarding the knowledge of first principles <note>Self-evident truths that do not require proof, such as "the whole is greater than the part."</note>, because we know those things by a simple intuition. Therefore, when stirred by this motion, we are made equal to the angels and find rest from those two lower types of motion. But you will say: does Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century theologian whose works on "Divine Names" described the soul's three motions: straight, oblique, and circular.</note> not also attribute those motions to the angels? Indeed he does, but in a certain other way. For in an angel, "straight" motion is not the name for reasoning that progresses from one thing to another; rather, it is the certain order of their providence by which a superior angel enlightens those below him with his light. We, however, first progress by straight motion toward those things which are around us. Then, from external things that strike the senses, we are raised up to know incorporeal things with our mind. Yet we do not meet with the angels even in "oblique" motion. For in angels, oblique motion occurs when they provide for 
<margin>All motion in a straight line...</margin>inferiors according to their contemplation of divine things. But of angels, we shall speak elsewhere. As for what pertains to us: if we 
<margin>All orbital motion...</margin>progress from those things which strike the senses to those things perceived by the mind, led by the reason innate within us, 
<margin>All oblique [motion]...</margin>we are carried by a straight motion. If, however, we are also irradiated by divine light, 
<margin>All motion below...</margin>we are no longer moved in a straight line, but obliquely. For regarding the orbital motion, no one doubts that 
<margin>All motion to the right...</margin>only immobility—if I may put it that way—pertains to God. There are likewise those who say that souls are 
<margin>All motion from the front...</margin>moved "upward" and "downward" when we move from a general category to specific forms, or vice versa. Likewise "to the right" and "to the left," if we progress from one of a pair of opposites to the other. Finally, we move "from the front" and "from the back" when we go from a cause to that which is produced by the cause, or back again from the effect to the cause. These things are generally said regarding that kind of life which is placed in the investigation of truth. 

But as for the life that is occupied with action: if it is undertaken by a man in whom a sharp intellect and mature counsel are apparent, and whose soul stands firm against all dangers and remains moderate against lust; who thinks of nothing except what is just and pious—this will surely be something excellent and truly worthy of a human being. Since we were not born for ourselves alone, but much more to serve human society, with what praises shall we then honor him who is so occupied with his family and domestic affairs that all things pertaining to food and lifestyle are present in abundance while maintaining modesty? He ensures that his children and the others he has under his protection are educated as free-born people <note>original: "ingenue erudiantur"; referring to a "liberal education" fit for a free citizen.</note> and are refined by all the good arts. He increases his patrimony with the greatest care and diligence, removing all suspicion of greed, so that—besides those whom he must necessarily support—he may also bring help to others, practicing liberality toward citizens in private and hospitality toward strangers. In public, however, he can be of use either through the magnificence of his works and the splendor of his gifts, or by the contributing of taxes. When, moreover, he moves toward the administration of the state...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the definition of the contemplative life to the defense of the active life. It describes the ideal statesman and uses the biblical figure of Leah as a symbol for the active life before ultimately subordinating action to the "immortal knowledge" of contemplation.</meta>

### On the Active and Contemplative Life.

...[that he] should apply himself to action, so refined in every kind of virtue and adorned with all eloquence: that he may always hold beneficial and honorable opinions concerning the **state** <note>original: "re publica"; referring to the common good or the commonwealth</note>, and eloquently and abundantly persuade others of what he feels. He should be one who fears neither the violence of enemies nor the anger of seditious citizens, but repels the attack of the former with all the strength of mind and body, and breaks the impious and furious attempts of the latter with the greatest freedom of spirit. Finally, he is one who takes care that religion is cultivated, that justice and equity are preserved, and that the entire city is kept within the bounds of moderation. And so that he may accomplish these things, he spares no labor, no dangers, and finally, not even life itself. Therefore, shall we not say that he who has lived and acted thus has both fulfilled the duty of a human and deserved most excellently of the mortal race?

At this point, I am forced to greatly wonder at those who hesitantly ask whether anyone can rightly administer these things without those virtues called the **moral virtues** <note>original: "de uita & moribus"; literally "concerning life and customs"</note>. If these are removed, what "right action" remains in us? Or what is there at all that can be called ours, and which is not instead common with the beasts? For what else do we seek when we seek a way of acting, except what sort of actions a person ought to perform in living a social life? Unless these are done justly, bravely, and temperately, I do not understand what they could even be. But the same logic applies to <term>prudence</term> <note>original: "prudentia"; the practical wisdom required to make good decisions in real-world affairs</note>: unless it sits at the helm like an excellent pilot and directs the course of life into a calm and safe harbor, we must necessarily be overwhelmed in the midst of the waves and storms of disturbances.

Moreover, I will dare to say this: no one will rightly administer either himself and his home or the state who is completely devoid of all learning. For in what way shall I know either what the highest human good is, or how it is acquired, if the nature of both man and the world is ignored? And who will rightly cultivate religion if they have in no part touched upon the knowledge of divine matters? Therefore, he who wishes to lead the state will not be devoid of these things.

I confess, however, that it is difficult for a man occupied with constant private and public business to have a completely exact knowledge of those matters. I am aware that this was the reason why they called <term>Leah</term>—who in the Hebrew Scriptures <note>original: "hebræorum litteris"; referring to the Old Testament</note> stands as the symbol of action—"weak-eyed" <note>In Genesis 29:17, Leah is described as having "weak" or "tender" eyes. In Renaissance thought, this symbolized that the active life cannot see divine truths as clearly as the contemplative life (symbolized by her sister Rachel).</note>. But she was altogether fruitful: because while she is distracted by many things, she is less able to look upward at higher truths. Yet because she has served many well, she binds many to herself through her kindness, as if they were her children.

You see, therefore, that the life spent in action is by no means to be despised. For it touches human nature most closely, and by its industry <margin>It excels from here</margin> and its labors, it binds the human race together with a sweet bond, and ensures that it cultivates justice and religion. 

But since our mind—which is the only thing that makes us human—is perfected not by mortal action but by immortal knowledge (in which resides that ultimate end to which all things are referred, for the sake of which all things are done, and which is sought for its own sake), who would not see that <term>speculation</term> <note>The act of deep, intellectual contemplation of divine or natural truths.</note> is to be preferred by far?

When <term>Battista</term> <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous Renaissance "Universal Man." He is a speaker in this dialogue.</note> had said these things in roughly this manner, he himself, with his eyes fixed in a steady gaze like one who was turning over far more in his mind than he had expressed in words, sat silent for a good while. And a stupor had seized us who were present from such great learning from so multifaceted a man, so that rather those things which we had heard thus far...

<summary>Leon Battista Alberti defends the active life of the statesman and citizen, using the biblical figure of Leah as a symbol for fruitful social action, but concludes that the contemplative life of "immortal knowledge" remains the highest human calling.</summary>
<keywords>active life, contemplative life, Leon Battista Alberti, Leah and Rachel, moral virtue, prudence, statesmanship, Renaissance humanism, social life</keywords>

# Book One.

-> b - iii <-

with us: and we repeated these things among ourselves: rather than asking anything further. **LORENZO** <note>Laurentius de' Medici, the "Magnificent," presented here as a young, eager student of Battista Alberti</note>, however, as he is of the sharpest intellect and most desirous of learning everything—not indeed to show himself off, but to more clearly draw out Battista's opinion on the whole matter by opposing his words—finally breaking our silence, followed Battista's speech in this way: "Although it seems to be a primary duty both of the modesty which my age warns me to show, and of the counsel which even moderate prudence can obey, that we should approve what you have said by the mere assent of such great men; nevertheless, I think it pertains to the glory of this **speculation** <note>original: "speculationis"; refers here to the philosophical inquiry or the contemplative life</note> of yours that everyone understands it has emerged victorious from a contest, and has triumphed over an adversary that did not leave it a bloodless victory. And truly, when I consider our nature with what strength of intellect I possess, the battle-line of arguments which **civil action** <note>original: "ciuilis actio"; the life of political and social engagement</note> brings into the struggle does not seem contemptible. Among those arguments, this one especially offers itself. For when we inquire concerning the life of a human, I believe no one is of such rude intellect who does not notice that we are not inquiring about him in such a way that we conceive in the mind either the soul apart from the body, or the body destitute of the soul. But just as when we speak of a **two-horse chariot** <note>original: "bigas"</note>, we do not understand only one of those horses which, being joined together, draw the car, but both at once; so when the account of a human begins to be considered, we propose to ourselves that whole which consists of soul and body. If you grant this, therefore, I shall persuade myself that that kind of life is to be preferred which preserves and perfects not just one of these, but both."

<margin>Action is to be preferred to speculation</margin>
"Moreover, it is proved that the way of living which is perfected by civil action rarely fails to provide this, because the virtues concerning life and conduct by which civil actions are directed preserve both the body and the soul unharmed. For by them, health, the strength of all limbs, and the integrity of the senses are preserved. At the same time, the soul is kept unpolluted by any stain of vice. Therefore, action, which provides this, is to be preferred to the investigation of truth, which is so occupied in caring for the mind alone that it neglects the care of all other things."

<margin>

<unclear>κό α</unclear>
<unclear>um</unclear>
</margin>
"Furthermore, who does not see that our best parent, Nature, produced us for the sake of attending assemblies and gatherings, and for preserving common society? But we can provide for this in no way unless we constitute a **city** <note>original: "ciuitatem"; meaning a organized political community or state</note>. For what other reason does all Greece extol the famous Socrates to the heavens with immortal praise, except that he was the first to call philosophy down from heaven to earth and introduce it into cities? When they say this, what else do they mean, except that this most innocent man saw through his divine wisdom that it was much more beneficial to the human race for leading life in safety and tranquility if—leaving aside the most difficult knowledge of divine things and the secrets of nature which are entirely obscure—those precepts were handed down to men in which our actions might be corrected to manage not only ourselves and our family affairs, but much more so the entire **commonwealth** <note>original: "rem publicam"</note>. And truly, if we wish to trace back our origin, if we consider for what purpose we were chiefly born, we will understand that we..."

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici argues that the active life is superior to the contemplative life because humans are a composite of body and soul, and social action preserves both while fulfilling our natural purpose as political beings. He cites Socrates as the prime example of shifting philosophy from abstract nature to human conduct and the state.</summary>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici's argument in defense of the active life. He is responding to Battista Alberti's praise of the contemplative life, using military and civic metaphors to argue that humans are born for social and political engagement.</meta>

On the active and contemplative life.
iii

# On the active and contemplative life.

[we were] sent by that Sovereign God into this lowest region of the world as if on a distant and difficult expedition, so that by fighting manfully against many difficulties we might overcome two most savage enemies: pain and pleasure. Once these were routed, we might enjoy perpetual peace. For since everyone is moved by nature's guidance toward what is just and honorable, you would not easily find a shameful man, except for one whom either the fear of hardship or the hope of pleasure has driven astray. For both the desire for commanding and for possessing—two evils which drive mortals to every wicked deed—what else do they demand for themselves except to avoid all hardships and enjoy every pleasure? 

Therefore, having been stationed in an army of this kind, since we are led forth daily into the battle-line—in which we must fight for life and blood, which is to say for the salvation of our souls <note>Lorenzo uses the metaphor of life as a military campaign where the soul's "salvation" depends on active struggle against vice.</note>—should not he who deserts his post there, and having betrayed his comrades, secretly withdraws from the battle to the camp, be considered a deserter? For I ask you: since we are gathered within the same city, since we repel hostile injuries with the same walls and the same arms, since by common counsel we ordain those laws by which we may live justly, be inflamed to all arduous tasks for the sake of protecting our dignity, and not exceed the prescribed bounds of modesty—must not each of us strive with his greatest strength so that this **civil life** <note>original: "ciuilis uita"; refers to the life of a citizen participating in the state.</note> may be free from all crime and embrace every virtue? And so that it may not be deterred from providing this by any pain or danger, nor weakened by any pleasure.

<margin>❧</margin> But if, on the contrary, there should be someone who, wasting away in leisure, neglects these things, does he not seem to abandon the duty entrusted to him? Truly, so that it may now appear clearer than the sun what the difference is between your man of leisure and our man of business, let us imagine some city fully built, in which there are public and private buildings of every kind, both sacred and profane, in abundance and magnificence; <margin>❧</margin> and let there be a most wise man who undertakes to fill it with inhabitants of such a kind that they make the city abound in all things. In this city—just as in a living body it is right that no part exist which does not serve the whole—he himself, sitting at the gates, would admit no future citizen until he had most diligently examined each person wishing to enter, and thoroughly learned what use each would bring to the city through his wisdom or skill. 

And so, some will answer that they are wise **lawgivers** <note>original: "legum latores"</note>. Others, prudent counselors; others, passionate orators; others, just judges. There will likewise be those who pledge medicine, those who promise to interpret the ambiguities of civil law, and those who profess they will perform military service. Architects will be present. Sculptors, potters, and painters will be present. Smiths of iron and wood will be present. And <margin>❧</margin> since, besides these **arts** <note>original: "artes"; specifically the Liberal Arts worthy of a free person</note>—which, acquired by intellect and industry, are worthy of a free man—various **crafts** <note>original: "opificia"</note> are also required in that multitude, which are gathered together not only for living rightly, but also for living safely and comfortably, [they] will offer themselves of their own accord...

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici argues that humans are divinely stationed in the world to fight vice and contribute to society, likening the state to a body where every citizen must serve a specific function. He portrays the contemplative life as a desertion of civic duty, using the metaphor of a well-ordered city to show the necessity of diverse professions.</summary>

<keywords>active life, civic duty, metaphor of the body politic, virtue vs. pleasure, lawgivers, liberal arts, crafts, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>
