<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici's argument against the life of pure contemplation, transitioning from military metaphors to the economic and social necessities of a city-state.</meta>

-># Book One<-

[...merchants] who practice banking <term>argentaria; the business of money-changing and credit which was the backbone of Florentine wealth</term>: those who, by exporting the goods we produce and importing foreign ones, render the people both well-supplied with all things and wealthy with 
<margin>3</margin> great sums of money. There will be those who make garments from various wools, and those who dye the finished cloth with various colors, so that we might use them not only to repel the force of cold and heat, but also to achieve a certain elegance and dignity. I pass over those of a more menial sort and those who are like mercenaries—whose labor we buy 
<margin>3</margin> rather than their skill—I pass over the weavers, fullers <note>Workers who cleaned and thickened cloth</note>, belt-makers, and the stitchers of clothes and shoes, and many others of that kind. And lest I must descend into the delicacies of the marketplace, I omit the gardeners, fish-curers, bakers, cooks, sausage-makers, butchers, and fishermen: those whom Gnatho <note>A famous "parasite" or sycophant character from the Roman playwright Terence's comedy *The Eunuch*</note> from Terence boasts he served whether their fortunes were safe or ruined. 

Therefore, since the one whose judgment determines who should be granted citizenship will have admired the talent and craftsmanship of those I first listed, and considered the work and labor of these latter ones necessary, he will surely admit them all. He will encourage each one to diligently perform the business they have already professed. 

Among these, if your "wise man" should be present—idle and yawning—and if he remains hidden alone in his own library, never departing, mixing with no one, 
<margin>3</margin> greeting no one, and offering no service either privately or publicly: what shall we say his role is in the republic? What "contribution" <note>original: "symbolum"; literally a token or a share of a meal, here meaning one's civic duty or "buy-in" to society</note> does he bring to human life? Where shall we place him? Where shall we direct him? Will there be anyone who thinks he should be counted among the number of men? Surely not; rather, everyone will be indignant at him, as if he were a lazy drone <note>original: "fucus"; a male bee that does no work but eats the honey</note> coming for the honey of others. 

"I enjoy peace," he says, "and through the highest leisure <term>ocium; in this context, the productive leisure used for study, though Lorenzo is using it disparagingly here</term>, I contemplate the force of nature and strive to find the truth in all things." 

Blessed you are, indeed! But beware lest you seem to have forgotten your own nature—you who serve yourself alone in such a way that you have absolutely no regard for the rest. But come now, allow me with your good grace to ask what I desire to know. If you were to board a fleet about to go against enemies with the intention that you would neither sit at the helm as a pilot, nor pull the oar as a rower, nor—running through the decks—give orders to those who must handle the rigging or the sails, nor obey the one who does give orders; and finally, if you were not to stand ready with arms to fight the enemy, but only brought your weight to the ship and occupied a place in idleness where someone else’s service might have been useful: if, I say, you boarded the ship with such a plan that you wished to help it neither with work nor with counsel, would not those in charge of the ships consider you useless and worthy of being thrown headlong into the sea? And perhaps, if they were a little more severe, they would throw you in. 

Or do you think the republic perhaps has less need of those who take hold of it than a ship does—especially since most heavy and constant dangers always threaten it from every side? It is constantly sought after either by the ambition and greed of many princes and peoples through arms and deceptions, or it is shaken by the factionalism of seditious citizens. Does anyone ignore that the republic [is like] a living being?

<summary>Lorenzo de’ Medici argues that a city relies on the active contributions of merchants, bankers, and laborers, comparing a citizen who only contemplates to a "drone" bee or a passenger who refuses to help a ship in a storm.</summary>
<keywords>banking, civic duty, active life vs contemplative life, Terence, Gnatho, ship of state, metaphor, social contract</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue's critique of the "withdrawn" scholar. Having previously listed the necessary trades of a city, the author now uses a physiological metaphor to argue that the most talented citizens (the "wise") have a biological and social obligation to lead rather than retreat into private study.</meta>

# On the active and contemplative life.
<meta>catchword: pea-</meta>

...to be most similar [to a body] in which each member performs its duty with a certain supreme harmony. The heart produces from itself that spirit <term>spiritum; in Renaissance medicine, the "vital spirits" believed to be a subtle substance that acted as the bridge between the soul and the body</term> which provides life to the living creature; the stomach performs certain tasks regarding nutrition, but the liver provides more. That a creature has sensation is from the brain; that it breathes is from the lungs. But lest I be too long regarding the remaining parts which are hidden—and for that reason less known to us—let us look at the external parts. 

The feet carry. The hands work. The eyes see. The ears hear. From all of these, a single harmony (as if from diverse voices) is created for the common good, by which the creature might propel away what is harmful to itself and welcome what is friendly and healthful. But if the feet refused to carry the mass of the entire body, or the eyes refused to look out for what pertains to the safety of the whole, or the nostrils neglected to take account of smells, or the taste made no distinction between the healthy and the unhealthy—in what way do you think that creature would be cared for, or how long would it last? 

But add this: that a loss of this kind becomes even more grave. For those who, having turned to the contemplation of things, desert their roles in the state, are those who surpass the rest in talent and counsel. It is necessary that the city be affected by a greater detriment because of this, since, being deserted by the wiser, it is left to the less prudent. 

For just as in the Greek army, if Thersites <note>In Homer’s *Iliad*, Thersites is depicted as a low-ranking, ugly, and cowardly soldier whose presence or absence means little to the war effort</note> should desert his place in the battle line, it brings no great momentum to the Trojans toward victory. Indeed, even when he fights with all his strength, he cannot strike much terror into the enemy. Conversely, if the brave Achilles, angry at Agamemnon, remains idle in his tent, the Greeks can easily be turned to flight. For he who can be of great benefit when present is the same one who can do the most harm when absent. So is the logic of the state: if he who excels the others in talent and prudence should leave the helm, the business of the state must necessarily be administered by those who are duller, to the greatest detriment. 

But here our investigator <note>Referring to the proponent of the contemplative life</note> will say: "Indeed, I do this so that in contemplating the nature of things I may discover what pertains to the safety of men; so that I may teach by what reasoning cities are to be established and by what laws they are to be formed. Likewise, I show what kind of leaders there ought to be among the people; to whom the magistracies <term>magistratus; public offices or civil authorities</term> should be entrusted; by what punishments each crime should be restrained; and with what honors the good should be distinguished." 

I admire this man as blessed, and because he prepares such treasures for himself, I praise him vehemently. But again, when I notice that he keeps those things stored away, and I do not see him turning such great riches toward the freedom of others, I compare him to a very wealthy man who, having his riches hidden from everyone and digging in the ground, will profit neither himself nor others. It is as if someone were to take care that his body be most swift and robust, but then did not exercise himself in any race or contest. For by the immortal God, what will it profit to possess the knowledge of medicine unless we turn it toward the health of the many? What use would Phidias <note>The most famous sculptor of ancient Greece, known for his massive statues of Athena and Zeus</note> bring us from his art unless he expressed it in ivory or gold? 

We have heard that Numa Pompilius <note>The legendary second king of Rome, credited with moving Rome away from its violent origins toward a legal and religious framework</note> was a wise man, the second king of the Romans. And I see his wisdom: for he led the people committed to his faith from being fierce and more warlike than was fitting toward leisure and pea- <note>original: "pacem" (peace)</note>

<summary>The author argues that a state functions like a human body, where the "eyes" and "brain" (the wise) must actively lead or the whole organism will perish. He uses the examples of Achilles' absence from battle and the hidden hoards of misers to illustrate that knowledge is useless—and even harmful—if not applied to the public good.</summary>
<keywords>active life vs. contemplative life, political philosophy, Achilles, Numa Pompilius, civic duty, body politic metaphor, Renaissance humanism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that great leaders throughout history combined profound learning with active service to the state. The previous page ended with a discussion of how a body fails if its members refuse to perform their duties; here, the author transitions to historical examples, beginning with the leaders of Rome and concluding with a contemporary example.</meta>

## Book One.

...peace and led them to cultivate justice and religion. He ensured that those who, just a short time before, were unable to defend themselves from their neighbors with horses and arms, were now safe through the authority of a single man. I pass over **Brutus** and **Publicola** <note>Lucius Junius Brutus and Publius Valerius Publicola were the legendary founders of the Roman Republic who overthrew the monarchy.</note> at the very beginnings of liberty; I pass over **Camillus** <note>Marcus Furius Camillus (c. 446–365 BCE), a Roman general honored as the "Second Founder of Rome" for restoring the city after the Gallic siege.</note> in the times that followed, who led the empire back to its former dignity after it had been cast down from its high station to the lowest depths. I say nothing of the **Catos**, the **Laelii**, or the **Scipios** <note>Famous Roman families known for producing both great generals and devoted scholars.</note>—all of whom we have learned were men of letters. I say nothing of the countless other men who, had they not governed the state with no less wisdom than spirit, but instead surrendered themselves solely to the pursuit of knowledge, would neither have been held in great value while living, nor would they still be "living" now after so many centuries have passed. 

<margin>Federico of Urbino</margin>
In our own time, we have **Federico da Montefeltro**, Prince of Urbino. I do not hesitate to compare him to the greatest commanders of past ages. There are many and altogether admirable virtues in this most excellent man: a very sharp intellect and a powerful drive in all things. Yet, he possesses such a great devotion to letters <term>litterarum studium; the study of the liberal arts, history, and philosophy</term> that no break from business ever occurs in which he does not transfer that leisure to scholarship. By reading much, hearing much, and debating a great deal, he has ensured that he is rightly counted among the most learned men. But suppose he had surrendered himself so entirely to these kinds of speculations that he completely neglected the government which he administers in such peace and prosperity? Or if he had never touched the art of war—in which he surpasses the leaders of his own age without dispute and yields to no one from antiquity—to what kind of man would such a great figure be reduced?

But I return to the ancients. **Hercules** was wise. But he was not wise for himself alone; rather, his wisdom profited almost all mortals. For, traveling through the greatest part of the world, he removed terrifying beasts and tamed destructive and savage monsters. He restrained the most cruel tyrants. He restored law and liberty to many peoples and nations. If he had stayed with his teacher **Atlas** and devoted such great effort to "idle wisdom," we would have a sophist <note>In this context, a "sophist" refers to someone who uses clever but empty arguments rather than performing useful actions.</note> instead of a Hercules; nor would anyone dare to call him the son of **Jupiter**. Indeed, not even Jupiter himself—

<margin>Jupiter as the interpreter of the world?</margin>
—if we interpret him as the "soul of the world" according to the opinion of the Platonists <note>The author is referencing Neoplatonic philosophy, which viewed the supreme deity or "World Soul" as an active force that keeps the universe in motion.</note>, ever ceases to act. If he were to cease, all things that are driven by constant motion would stop. The heavens would not carry themselves around with constant mobility. No rising or setting of the stars would appear. The elements themselves would not be stirred by mutual transformation; the rivers would not flow. The earth would bear nothing; the seasons would change nothing. The Fates would allot nothing. The Muses would sing nothing.

But I return to men. You see by what kind of men the name of the Christians has grown, and how great, manifold, and varied their learning was. But whom can you give me from among those leaders, by whose work and counsel our affairs were established, who so surrendered himself to letters that he never touched any action of life? To omit the rest for the sake of brevity: how much do you value **Paul the Apostle**? How many do you have whom you can compare to him? Look at his style of speaking: I cannot find anything that could be thought of as more full, or sharper for teaching, or more effective for moving the soul. As for the man's learning, what need is there for my testimony or anyone else's, when by the most constant consensus of all nations—past and present—he is rightly called the "Teacher"? Through contemplation, he ascended even to the "third heaven," <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 12:2, where St. Paul describes being "caught up to the third heaven" to receive divine revelations.</note> where he saw and learned things that are not permitted for men to bring back here.

<summary>The author argues that true greatness requires balancing intellectual study with public service, citing Roman heroes, the Duke of Urbino, Hercules, and St. Paul as examples. He contends that even the divine "World Soul" remains in constant action to sustain the universe.</summary>

# On the Active and Contemplative Life.

<margin>
<unclear>History...
Holy...
men...</unclear>
</margin>

Therefore, did that man proceed to meditate by locking himself away in his cell in such a way that he utterly neglected the salvation of others? Read, I pray, what this most modest man writes about himself, and what many equally holy men report: how many journeys and how many voyages he undertook despite his poor health. Into how many dangers and how many calamities did he fall, so that he might lead **Peter’s bark** <note>original: "Petri nauiculam." A traditional metaphor for the Christian Church, often depicted as a ship tossed on the sea.</note>, nearly overwhelmed by the most fierce waves and dashed against the sharpest rocks, back into a safe and calm harbor. I have mentioned only one man out of many. 

But remember how many most learned men we can list among the Latins and the Greeks, all of whom we see took up the high offices of the **papacy** <note>original: "pontificatus." While this can refer to the office of the Pope, in a broader classical sense it refers to high priesthood or leadership.</note> offered to them, not out of ambition, but out of the most burning love for others. What else should I believe they wanted, if not to be of use to as many as possible by advising prudently, warning most gently, correcting more sharply, and finally helping through work and deed—bringing forth for the use of others what they had learned with much sweat and industry, rather than hiding it like that **wicked servant** <note>A reference to the Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14-30), where a servant is punished for burying the money entrusted to him instead of investing it.</note>? 

But why do I persist so long in these arguments when we see that, by the most constant consensus of all nations, **active men** <term>negociosos: those engaged in "negotium" (business/public affairs)</term> are always preferred to **idle men** <term>ociosis: those dedicated to "otium" (leisure/study)</term>? Read, I pray, the statutes of various republics; you will see that the greatest rewards and most massive honors were always established not for the idle, but for the active. There were triumphs in greatest glory; there were trophies; there were **ovations** <note>A lesser form of a Roman triumph granted to a general for a victory that was less than a full-scale war.</note>. Various crowns were invented, and various titles devised. We see statues erected to many most excellent men—not only wearing the **toga** <note>The "statua togata" represented a man in civil life as a statesman or orator.</note>, but also on horseback and in chariots. We see magnificently constructed tombs and lands given publicly, so that through monuments of this kind, illustrious men who served the state well might be honored among mortals. 

Truly, if you search back through all antiquity, you will find that among the idle, almost none were honored with such rewards; but you will hardly be able to count the crowd of men from civil life. Why then are these things so? Surely so that you may understand from the wisest and most just princes—who tempered their states with the best institutions and laws—that this kind of man was more beloved, for they obeyed **nature** <note>The author follows the Stoic and humanist idea that humans are naturally social beings meant for society.</note>, which never errs. 

And so that you may understand how much force truth has, those very same people who preferred "idle letters" (private study) to civil actions more often pursue the praises of a commander than a philosopher in their own writings. For even if that knowledge of great things—which I, as is right, have always valued most highly—delighted them greatly, yet warned by the voice of nature to which we are all forced to obey, they eventually confessed what they often evade in those boastful disputes. 

But I see myself lingering longer than is necessary on a matter so clear, especially since the whole thing can be proved by a single precept of Him who produced us from nothing—who, after we were produced and had fallen into death by our own recklessness, led us back into life. The whole matter, I say, can be proved by a single precept of the most high God: Is it not this, that you should **love your neighbor as yourself**? 

And what is closer to you than the **republic** <term>res publica: the commonwealth or the community at large</term> itself? I do not see, therefore, in what way you can love it as yourself unless you lead it back to the path when it wanders; unless you offer right counsel when it asks in doubtful matters; unless you stand ready with help when it implores you in the greatest turmoil and danger. The state needs a citizen to administer magistracies prudently and justly; it needs a commander; it needs a soldier. By what reasoning, then, do you [perform] the charity you owe it...

<summary>The author argues that active service to the state and the Church is superior to private study, citing historical Roman honors and the biblical command to love one's neighbor as evidence. He emphasizes that even great scholars of the past recognized the necessity of public duty over the "idle" life of pure contemplation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where Lorenzo de' Medici (the speaker "Laurentio") has been arguing for the importance of the active, civic life. # Book One.

...you will say [to the state], unless you are always so constantly present for it that you not only endure all labors with an even mind, but even approach extreme dangers if the situation demands, and meet death without fear. But why say more? For since all philosophers who have ever lived wish us to be born for a social and communal life, a man should not be called a human if he is not a citizen, nor can he be called a citizen if he neglects the care of the city in which he was born. These, therefore, were the things which seemed to me ought to be said on behalf of the **civic life** <note>original: "vita civili." The "active life" of political and social duty, a central theme of Renaissance humanism.</note>—more in a declamatory style, as I am accustomed to practice at **Landino’s house** <note>original: "apud Landinum." This refers to the villa or circle of Cristoforo Landino, the author of this dialogue, where these intellectual discussions took place.</note>, than in a strictly philosophical one. I did this especially so that I might elicit your most weighty judgment on this matter through my speech more clearly than I might explain my own view. If I have abused your patience in listening to me longer than your dignity and my respect toward you required, you will grant pardon to my most burning love for learning. For since I am inflamed by a wondrous zeal for learning, and the opportunity to approach and consult you is given so rarely, I pray you endure it with an even mind if I weary you beyond what is fair. 

When these things had been said by Lorenzo, a great amazement seized the minds of all who were present. For although signs had long shown themselves in the young man such that they had already conceived the highest expectations of him, nevertheless they thought such spontaneous abundance, such ready arguments, and such varied examples could bring no small praise even to a more mature age. But **LEON BATTISTA** <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous Renaissance polymath, architect, and philosopher.</note>, since by his own nature he followed all studious people with the highest love, and loved Lorenzo's talent uniquely and like a father, said: 

"For a long time now, Lorenzo, I have been listening to you most attentively and with the greatest pleasure. I see that you have not only embraced all the virtues of the civic life—which itself might seem worthy of the highest admiration in such tender years—but have also already turned your mind to these things which are more divine. It is easily apparent that whatever you said a little while ago for the civic life all tends toward this: that you might stir me to strengthen those arguments by which the **investigation of truth** <note>original: "veri investigatio." This is the core of the "contemplative life."</note> is preferred to action. If I am not mistaken, this can be achieved without much difficulty. For even to one considering it moderately, the nobility of the matter offers itself, so that when you depart from the speculation and inquiry of truth, everything else seems almost unworthy of the labor of our divine intellect."

<margin>The mind and contemplation</margin>
"For who, looking more closely at the nature of man, would not see that nothing in us is more excellent than the mind? But the mind's function is not to act, but to **speculate** <note>original: "speculari." In this context, it means deep, theoretical contemplation or philosophical study.</note>—and indeed to speculate on those things which belong to it alone and are perceived by no physical sense. Therefore, since the investigation of truth belongs to that part of us which is most divine, and deals with things entirely incorporeal that no sense can grasp, should it not be preferred to outward actions? Add to this that if we are led by the duties of life as if by a path toward the highest good, then surely that which proceeds in a perpetual series and is never interrupted by any interval is superior to those things that are its opposite. In meditating on great things, we are occupied with a certain constant tenor, whereas in performing actions, we are distracted by many different things at almost the same moment. Therefore, while **Martha** is troubled about many things, **Mary** meanwhile sits constantly by the Lord. <note>A reference to the biblical story in Luke 10:38-42, traditionally used by theologians to show that Mary's "contemplation" of Jesus's words was better than Martha's "active" service in the kitchen.</note> Furthermore, that from which a greater pleasure of the soul is perceived should be preferred to that which brings less pleasure. And the inquiry of truth provides this for us..."

<margin>The seeker of truth is to be preferred to action</margin>

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici concludes his defense of the active life, prompting Leon Battista Alberti to begin a counter-argument. Alberti argues that the contemplative life is superior because the mind is the most divine part of man and finds its highest purpose in seeking truth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where Leon Battista Alberti begins his rebuttal to Lorenzo de' Medici's defense of the active life. The catchword "maiore" from the previous page's footer matches the final line of this transcription.</meta>

->## On the Active and Contemplative Life.<-

For Mary feasts <note>original: "epulatur." A reference to the biblical story of Mary and Martha (Luke 10:38–42), frequently used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance to symbolize the contemplative and active lives.</note>. Martha, however, is distracted by many things. But I would not wish to overlook this point: for who does not see that what is sought for its own sake must always be valued more than what we desire not for itself, but for the sake of something else? Now, I shall prove that speculation <note>or "contemplation"</note> is of this kind, both by your opinion and the judgment of all learned men; and I will easily demonstrate through the authority of the Psalm what everyone believes of their own accord. 

> For one thing, he said, I have asked of the Lord: this I will seek. 

Then he expresses what he sought. He adds: 

> that I may dwell in the house of the Lord all the days of my life: that I may see the will of the Lord. <note>original: "ut uideam uoluntatem domini." This is a slight variation of Psalm 27:4 (Vulgate 26:4), which usually concludes "that I may see the delight of the Lord."</note>

Add to this that we contemplate in leisure and in quiet. For which reason that saying is most correct: 

> be still and see that I am God. <note>original: "uacate & uidete quia sum deus." From Psalm 46:10 (Vulgate 45:11).</note>

We act, however, not without noise and tumult. For Mary sits idle; Martha, in great haste, surveys all things. And it is no wonder, since the latter does not depart from <term>matter</term> <note>original: "hyle," the Greek word for "wood" or "raw matter," used here in its philosophical sense to denote the physical world of change and decay.</note>, in which all things are in an uproar and seethe with various waves of disturbances. The other, with eyes fixed, gazes always upon divine things, which no disturbance ever touches. Mary alone performs her duty with the mind, which is immortal and subject to no contagion. Martha achieves nothing without the senses, which hang from the body and easily slip into corruption—senses which we hold in common not with God, but with the beasts. For which reason, this point is concluded most divinely by Divinity itself: that Mary has chosen the best part, which shall not be taken away from her. He said "the best" so that you might understand that action is also good, but is nevertheless far surpassed. For it is easily proven that it shall not be taken from her. For if our souls, created by God, are to return to God and will find rest in contemplating Him, who would not understand that human business must one day cease and utterly die away? We, however, shall find our happiest rest in gazing upon God. But since these things are brought forward by us in a logical rather than an oratorical manner, let us try another way to see what we can achieve. And since you accused those men of being idle who, having abandoned the administration of public affairs, give themselves over entirely to leisure, let us take someone from our city who, living in such a way, might properly defend his way of life. 

<margin>the physicist and mathematician</margin>
Do we want to consider for a moment an excellent physicist and mathematician, whom your grandfather **Cosimo** <note>Cosimo de' Medici (1389–1464), the de facto ruler of Florence and a great patron of learning.</note> dearly loved, both for his admirable doctrine and for a certain sweetness in his speech and disputations? **LAURENTIUS** said: That very man! For he is the only one of all whom I have known among us whom the knowledge of the highest things so delights that, provided he attains it, there is nothing else he cares for. Therefore, said Baptista <note>Leon Battista Alberti, the speaker and famous Renaissance polymath.</note>, though he is a born citizen, and all the public affairs of our city are common to him as they are to the rest of the citizens, he nevertheless flees all civil tumults and struggles, and occupies himself at home in the constant investigation of truth. If this man, then, were accused of being a deserter of the public safety and dignity, as if he were shirking public duties, I think he would defend himself thus: "I know well, men of Florence, that because I lead a life apart from you, I do you no injury. For I do not turn the wealth of the treasury nor of any private person to myself, either by stealth or by force. Nor am I of such character or life that I bring any mark of shame upon the republic, such that it might repent having produced me. Toward individuals, I do not behave rudely, nor proudly, nor greedily. Moreover, of the laws and institutions by which the republic can endure in safety, there is no one who [regards them with] greater..." <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page.</note>

<summary>Alberti uses biblical allegories and logical arguments to argue that the contemplative life (Mary) is superior to the active life (Martha). He introduces a hypothetical "physicist and mathematician" citizen to defend the choice of intellectual solitude over political involvement.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Leon Battista Alberti and Lorenzo de' Medici. Alberti is arguing that while public life and magistracies are highly honored, they require the foundation of "true knowledge" (contemplation) to be successful and that the preservation of a state depends more on the harmony of its citizens than on physical structures like walls or ports.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
# Book One.

...take care. I, however, am not unaware that power in the <term>state</term> <note>original: "res publica," referring to the commonwealth or public affairs.</note> and magistracies are held in the highest esteem among mortals, and that the greatest honors come to those who perform those duties—provided they have administered them with the highest virtue. But if, on the contrary, they fail, it is inevitable that they will be overwhelmed by their own disgrace. <margin>Anaxagoras</margin> Wherefore that wise man of Clazomenae, Anaxagoras, wisely said that "office reveals the man." For those who are placed in a lofty and elevated position find that neither their virtues nor their vices remain hidden from anyone; and the more one wanders from the right path, the more one is criticized. 

But if anyone, led either by false glory or moved by the highest love for their country, attempts to approach the administration of the state, let him first see and consider with himself for a long time by what skill, or with what instruments suited to that skill, he should approach so great a matter. For neither a painter nor a sculptor will complete a work correctly unless they both know the art perfectly beforehand and possess an abundance of those instruments by which the work, already conceived in the mind, can be conveniently finished—lest they be condemned for ignorance or be laughed at for struggling in their poverty of means. 

But if anyone should ask with what things he ought to be equipped to whom the state can be rightly entrusted, I will respond without any hesitation that he will undertake all labor in vain unless the **knowledge of the truth** is present. For just as light offers itself to the eyes of those of us entering into this life so that we may behold all physical bodies, so also certainty of things offers itself to our minds—unless we are to wander utterly from the right path. For by this alone is not only that final goal, which everyone desires to reach, shown to us, but also the shortest and most expeditious way leading there is made manifest. 

If anyone, neglecting this light, strives to reach the end I mentioned, he himself is like a **blind leader**, and those who follow him will rush headlong into the whirlpool of all hardships; once they have been swallowed by it, all hope of returning must necessarily perish. I, therefore, think that this kind of light, by which we know our nature and those things that are consistent with it, is to be valued far more than the light proceeding from the sun, which shows us only bodies subject to corruption. 

Truly, we shall never attain this knowledge by base and common arts. We shall not reach it while addicted to pleasures, nor when subject to greed, nor when bound to ambition; rather, we must be inflamed by a certain most ardent love of finding the truth. To reach this, I follow the guidance of reason and do not abandon the footprints of nature herself. 

And these things, indeed, I say regarding myself. But if you press me more sharply, I will cite someone from those who have greatly profited in investigating things, who will not fear to assert that in this kind of living [the contemplative], he alone is far more useful to the state than many of those together whom you see active daily in the forum and in the senate. And indeed, I would never agree with you that a man preserves the state simply because he maintains the ports, or dockyards, or walls, or temples, or porticos. For even if an enemy does not demolish all these with fire or sword, and even if the injury of the weather does not destroy them, they will certainly waste away in the progress of time—than which nothing is more voracious. 

But the only guardian and most faithful preserver of a state is the **concord of its citizens**. This concord is created when the actions proceeding from individuals come together into one, just as different voices coming from individual strings on a lyre harmonize so that the sweetest accord arises from them, which the Greeks call *harmony* <note>original: "harmoniam."</note>. Moreover, the best establishment of laws will produce this harmony; the virtue of those who use them will preserve the observance of those laws; reason perfected by diligent inquiry produces that virtue; and finally, only exercise concerning the truth perfects that reason. But finally...

<sig>c</sig>

<summary>Alberti argues that public office reveals a person's character and requires "true knowledge" (contemplation) to be effective. He concludes that a state's survival depends on the harmony and virtue of its citizens, produced by reason and laws, rather than physical infrastructure.</summary>
<keywords>Anaxagoras, statecraft, virtue, truth vs. corruption, blind leader metaphor, civic concord, harmony, philosophy of law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the foundations of a virtuous life and effective governance. Having previously argued that statecraft requires specific "instruments" or skills, the speaker now transitions into a philosophical defense of the contemplative life (the life of the mind) as the essential source for all practical, active virtues.</meta>

## On the active and contemplative life.

...we will acquire for ourselves by applying a certain long leisure to it <note>Referring to the mind or the pursuit of wisdom.</note>. For virtue uses no other instrument besides true reason: by which the mind is sharpened and roused to learn; to commit to memory what it has learned; and to use what it remembers. For by using reason, you will go astray as little as possible. Such, therefore, is the exercise of truth. Such is the art and power of reason, which we must strive with all our might to be adorned with. For she alone is the one who leads to the <term>highest good</term> <note>original: "summum bonum," the ultimate goal of human existence in classical philosophy.</note>. Do you see in what manner the man devoted to <term>speculation</term> <note>In this context, "speculation" refers to "contemplation" or the deep study of divine and natural truths.</note> protects himself?

But to those things which were both ingeniously thought out and elegantly explained by you, I think he will respond thus: first, you said that account must be taken of the human being so that we embrace both the body and the soul together. He will not deny that, since we are enclosed in the most troublesome prison of the body, the care of the body is not to be entirely omitted; but that account must be taken of both: it must be conceded in some way that we consist of soul and body. But if we investigate our nature more precisely, we will not seem to go astray if we call the mind alone "the man."

<margin>page</margin>
<margin>Body. Mind.</margin>
I see that this pleases not only Plato, that great man, but also the Christians, than whom you will find none more learned. What then is the body? I could say it is a bond to the soul, from which it is dissolved shortly after; I could call it a clay vessel that is soon broken; finally, I could call it a garment of such a kind that soon grows old and wears out. The mind, however, which is eternal, seeks not action but contemplation as its food and, as it were, its nourishment. But let us proceed "with a stouter Muse" <note>original: "pinguiori... Minerva," a Latin idiom meaning to speak plainly or with common sense rather than with subtle philosophical rigor.</note>, as they say; and let us concede that man consists not only of mind but also of body. What then? You will ask, must account also be taken of the body? Certainly, it must. For while we live this mortal life, it is necessary for the soul to use the body's help for many things, since it cannot come into its own knowledge—which is of immortal and eternal things—except through those things which fall into the senses, and which, circumscribed by places and times, we see constantly rising and falling. 

<margin>concerning the matter.</margin>
Wherefore, as for what you say regarding the civil virtues—which arise from character—caring for both soul and body at once: I confess that indeed, provided that you also understand that all those virtues take their origin from the power of knowing. For who will be able to live justly unless he has first investigated with his mind what is just, and that what is just is consistent with our nature, and that it must therefore be done? This reasoning is widely applicable both in courage and in temperance. Therefore, those things which are applied to actions cannot be perfected without the investigation of the mind. 

<margin>Mary Martha.</margin>
Martha and Mary show this clearly <note>A reference to the biblical sisters; Martha represents the active life of service, while Mary represents the contemplative life of listening to divine truth.</note>. For Mary does not call Martha to her aid; rather, Martha asks for Mary when she complains to the Lord that she has been left alone by her. She was therefore asking for help, because without the investigation of truth, action accomplishes almost nothing. The inquiry into truth will therefore be more excellent, because it both produces the virtues we said are engaged in action and helps them in doing things; and beyond that, it touches upon that divinity to which action is unable to aspire. Nor do I think it was for any other reason that it was brought about by the immortal God that the greatest leader of the Hebrews, Moses, should receive the Laws—by which the people might be instructed in right and honorable actions—not in cities and the crowds of men, nor in a valley closed in on all sides, nor in a flat and low-lying place: but on the peak of the highest mountain. For God, the architect of all things, wished to signify to us by this thing especially: that those things which will be useful for the administration of republics [come] only through the investigation of the highest things...

<summary>The author argues that while humans consist of both body and soul, the mind is the true essence of a person and the source of all virtue. He uses the biblical examples of Martha and Mary, and Moses receiving the law on a mountain, to demonstrate that practical action must be guided by divine contemplation.</summary>
<keywords>contemplative life, active life, soul vs body, Plato, Martha and Mary, Moses, reason, civil virtues, highest good</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the superiority of the contemplative life. The speaker had just begun to argue that the mind (or soul) is the true essence of a human being, and now explains how the virtues found through study are more enduring and broadly useful than those found in mere physical or political action.</meta>

->Book One.<-
->c ii<-

...that it can be thought out by men. **W**herefore, both for the preservation of our common society and for the overcoming of those two enemies of human tranquility <note>likely referring to fear and desire, or pain and death, common themes in Stoic and Platonic thought</note>, those virtues which are engaged in the knowledge of truth will benefit us much more effectively than those which labor in active affairs. **A**lthough even the society by which the human race is bound together is sought not because of the excellence of the soul, but because of the needs of the body; and those enemies which you, in your speech, brought so fiercely before our eyes as to strike us with horror, are to be buried along with the body. For when our souls, free from every bodily contagion, return to their own nature, society will no longer need to be sought, nor enemies feared. Action will cease, while in the meantime <term>speculation</term> <note>original: "speculatio," meaning deep contemplation or study of divine truths</note> will be more and more strengthened. For the pleasure which comes from truth endures eternally. For it was not in vain that He who does nothing in vain said: *Mary has chosen the best part, which shall not be taken away from her.* <note>A famous biblical reference to Luke 10:42, often used by medieval and Renaissance scholars to prove that the contemplative life (Mary) is superior to the active life (Martha).</note> **W**hen these things are said, we nevertheless do not cease to extol with the highest praise the other kind of life [the active life], if it is conducted with every virtue. But we think greater thanks should be given to those men who, through supreme <term>leisure</term> <note>original: "otium," which refers to time dedicated to intellectual and spiritual pursuits rather than mere idleness</note>, have thought out and discovered those things which your patrons use as a kind of standard for administering civil affairs. Add to this that the things discovered through leisure spread more widely and benefit far more people than those things done in business. The same man can serve as an example of both things. **F**or who can be thought of as more admirable in action than Marcus Cicero? In his consulship, he possessed such wisdom and strength of mind that he warded off fire from the city, slavery from the Roman people, and devastation from all of Italy <note>A reference to Cicero's suppression of the Catilinarian conspiracy in 63 BCE.</note>. Indeed, after the murder of Caesar, though unarmed and a private citizen, he so barred the armed Antony—even though he was consul—from the city, contrary to the opinions of many senators, that although he had not yet been judged an enemy by the Senate, <margin>M. T. <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero</note></margin> he was nevertheless treated as one. **T**hese things were performed by him while wearing the toga <note>the garment of peace and civil life, signifying he acted as a statesman rather than a soldier</note> in such a way that they are to be preferred to the greatest triumphs of the most illustrious generals. Shall we then deny that actions of this kind should be followed with immortal praise? Not at all. **W**hy so? Because with the greatest labors and the greatest dangers—and as the outcome showed, even with his own death—he attempted everything by which he might restore to the state its long-interrupted, or rather lost, liberty. But I ask you: when he was living his life far from the <term>republic</term> <note>original: "res publica," public affairs or the state</note>, having turned himself entirely to investigating great matters, was he so useless to his fatherland that he benefited it in no way? When, by scrutinizing all the records of the Greeks and surveying all things with the divinity of his own mind, he understood not only what could help his citizens who were then alive, but, having embraced all of nature, he observed what the end of man is, to which all things are referred, what are the obstacles of life, and what things lead there; and he discovered many things by which we might despise death, many by which we might despise the pains and tortures of the body, and many by which we might despise sickness and the other disturbances of the mind. Finally, in those divine books—both *On Laws* and *On the Republic*—did he not pursue the entire civil discipline: what kind of state there ought to be, if indeed it is to be happy; what kind of leaders and governors should be in it; what the duties of private citizens and of magistrates are; from what ranks and types of men the republic should be constructed; and with what laws it should be adorned? He demonstrates to all what must be done by the counselor, what by the commander, what by the soldier, what by the craftsman, and what by the farmer. **A**ll of which things, since he discovered them most sharply and ingeniously, and described them clearly, abundantly, seriously, and ornately, he not only teaches with ease, but carries the reader away with vehemence...

<summary>The author argues that the contemplative life (leisure for study) is superior to the active life because it provides the foundational principles for governance and helps the soul transcend bodily concerns. Marcus Tullius Cicero is presented as the perfect example of a man who served his country both through political action and his philosophical writings.</summary>
<keywords>Cicero, active life vs. contemplative life, otium, leisure, Mark Antony, Catilinarian conspiracy, political philosophy, Roman Republic, virtue, Mary and Martha</keywords>

# On the Active and Contemplative Life

...and delights with the greatest sweetness, will you deny that he has been of use to his fellow citizens and men? But see what difference there is between those most distinguished actions and these divine contemplations <note>original: "speculationes," referring to deep intellectual study</note>. For in those, he benefited a single city; but in these, he has provided instructions to all who know Latin. In those things which he did with counsel and prudence, he drove away the greatest dangers that were then pressing. However, those things which he committed to writing through investigation look forward to all time, so that he might consult the needs not only of those present and then living, but also those who have lived throughout so many centuries until now, and those who will be in the future, leaving behind instructions for living well and happily. And indeed, if you unroll the monuments of those at leisure <note>original: "otiosorum," referring to those who have the "holy leisure" to dedicate themselves to study rather than manual or political labor</note>, you will find that men have been made teachable and civilized from being dull and savage, and have been led into this prudence and humanity by their writings. For it was necessary for wise men—who even before cities were founded gathered mortals together who previously wandered scattered through fields and woods, and instructed those gathered with laws—to diligently investigate, before they attempted the task, those things which were both useful and most closely touched upon human nature. Therefore, from these points, the whole topic is thus concluded: Those who are engaged in actions are indeed useful, but either for the present or for a brief time. But those who bring to light for us the nature of things hidden in darkness will always be beneficial. For actions meet their end along with the men themselves. Contemplations, however, by overcoming all ages, endure immortally and are equal to eternity.

<margin>X</margin> Therefore, when that wise man of yours, sitting at the gate, considers those about to enter, he will admit the senator; he will admit the orator, the soldier, the lawyer, and then the rest of the crowd which you enumerated more distinctly a little while ago. Nor will he admit them without cause, since the city, like a body, needs all those as its members, without which it would be crippled and in some part useless. But when this wise man of ours approaches and, being asked what he professes for the use of common life, answers that he is one who indeed intends to cease from all business, so that he touches neither private nor public care, but instead to be so engaged in the investigation of lofty things that he may best investigate what is useful, what is honorable, and what is according to nature, and hand down those investigated things to writing—will he, I ask, exclude this man? Or rather, rising with reverence as if to some mortal god, will he escort him even to the middle of the city, and see to it that the foremost and more excellent house be given to him, and that he be supported at public expense? Indeed, he will propose him as a model for all citizens, from whom each may learn to fulfill the roles entrusted to them in the republic <note>original: "re publica," meaning the state or the "commonwealth"</note>, and receive answers as if from an oracle in their doubtful affairs. And will anyone deny that such a man brings any use to the city, when there is no one besides him who is able to rightly perform the duty entrusted to him unless he has first consulted this man? And so, I was laughing to myself a little while ago at that parade of your words when you were comparing the republic to a ship and to a living body; and when I noticed with what mind you said those things, I silently approved of your purpose. But (believe me) that ship will be best managed in which, besides those you mentioned a little while ago, our wise man is present. He who, free from all business, may only contemplate what is conducive to the navigation, and show to those seeking counsel what things are best to be done. In a living body, moreover, only then will the senses truly judge and the appetite never be moved blindly, if the mind is present; which itself, though vacant from actions, nevertheless helps those who seek it with counsel. For as to what you were saying, that the greatest harm arises for the republic from that source:

# Book One
<sig>c iii</sig>

...that because the more excellent minds are occupied with the knowledge of truth, the state itself might be ruled by the worse sort; surely the wise man will never cease to give right advice to those who consult him on difficult matters. Thus, if not with his physical labor, he will at least help with his counsel.

But believe me, those who can be alone with themselves, fleeing human society, will be very rare; for this belongs only to that man who, having already overcome human nature, has flown toward more divine things. Therefore, as I said, there will be very few altogether. For we see throughout so many centuries how wondrously few they are, and how rarely they appear "swimming in the vast abyss" <note>original: "nantes in gurgite uasto," a quote from Virgil’s Aeneid describing shipwrecked sailors, here used to mean how few true scholars exist in the world</note>, so that there is no longer any danger of the republic being abandoned by them.

Furthermore, even those who are thus at leisure <note>original: "otiosi," referring to the "holy leisure" of a life dedicated to study rather than business</note> offer the greatest utility to their citizens. For they are a very serious example to others, lest people give themselves over to sleep and the belly in such a way that they neglect all learning. Nor, however, if the city wishes to employ them as leaders for the proper management of the state, will they deny it their labor.

<margin>Plato</margin> For it is a precept of the divine Plato that if a wise man sees another wise man in charge of affairs, he will make use of his own leisure, since it is much sweeter to be ruled than to rule. But if those in charge are fools, he will attempt either to take their place himself or to make them better.

But if he is neither admitted to lead, nor will those in power endure to be made better, he will return to himself and benefit men in another way. Furthermore, you have enumerated many who, by their most distinguished deeds at home and in war, attained divine honors; indeed, I think them worthy of much greater honors still, and I do not doubt that they were the greatest and most admirable men. For when I say that some excel others, I do not mean that the others should be held as nothing among men.

<margin>Virgil</margin> He is a god among mortals who (as it is said in the works of Maro <note>Virgil's full name was Publius Vergilius Maro</note>) "has been able to know the causes of things." <note>original: "rerum potuit cognoscere causas," from Virgil's Georgics</note> But I judge him to be the greatest and most excellent man among humans who manages affairs rightly. Since he cares for the affairs of others rather than his own...

<margin>Terence</margin> ...although he who is a man will think "nothing human alien to himself." <note>original: "nihil humanum a se alienum," a famous line from the Roman playwright Terence suggesting universal empathy and social responsibility</note> But because he is moved from leisure to business <note>original: "ab ocio ad negocium"</note>, and from quiet to tumults and dangers—and while he consults the safety of others, he neglects his own—<margin>+</margin> it was not without justice that a reward of this kind was proposed for such great labors.

For a republic honors those whom it is in its greatest interest to honor. It is of the utmost interest to entice men of this kind to defend and increase the state, and to amplify its majesty, with every kind of honor. For even if the power of virtue is great, and its beauty shows itself to us from every side such that we are compelled to seek it for its own sake, nevertheless—whether it is the ignorance of man in knowing himself, or the depravity and perversion of his character—there would be very few altogether who would be willing to undertake some greater labor or more serious danger unless certain great and distinguished honors were offered. You would find far more who work to attain the insignia of virtue <note>such as titles, medals, or public recognition</note> than who love virtue when it is stripped of its rewards. Therefore, it was wisely established—since right reason does not prevail among all—that we should be kindled toward what is right and honorable by inducements of this kind.

These, then, were the reasons that persuaded me to prefer knowledge to action when the investigation of truth and the performance of right were sought separately. <margin>X</margin> But when I have looked around at all things more diligently, and since I see that man proceeds from the mind alone in such a way that the body must nevertheless not be neglected—since he is born so as to be bound to others by the "knot of charity" and to burn with the love of knowing things—I shall, in short, consider him to be the true man who, having the right regard for both lives, joins both together: who gives as much to actions as the necessity of mortal affairs and hu...

# On the Active and Contemplative Life

the bond of human society demands: as much as the love of country impels him. However, he will turn toward contemplation only insofar as he remembers he was born for it, unless our human weakness calls him away from it. He will investigate, therefore, so that he may attain the <term>highest good</term> <note>original: "summi boni," the ultimate goal of human existence in moral philosophy</note>: he will act so that he may avoid his own troubles and those of his people. He will perform both rightly, provided he spends as much time on each as is sufficient. For these two lives are not so opposed or so at odds with one another that they cannot meet in some way. For they are sisters: Mary and Martha dwell under the same roof. Both please God—Martha so that she may feed, Mary so that she may be fed. Both are good: but one is hardworking, the other at leisure; yet in such a way that neither the work is a disgrace nor the leisure a path to laziness. Therefore, we shall cling to Martha so that we do not abandon the duty of humanity. Yet we shall join ourselves much more to Mary, so that our mind may be fed on ambrosia and nectar. For by the latter, we gradually ascend to the knowledge of God; and I would easily believe that whoever is ignorant that the highest good consists in this knowledge is also ignorant of himself and his own origin. 

For when I consider the various tides and storms of our life, which is very much like a most turbulent sea, I think it is most difficult to reach the highest point unless we flee into that knowledge of truth I mentioned, as if into a most tranquil harbor. <margin>+</margin> And even if this has been sung of in the most weighty sentences of all the philosophers who are indeed worthy of that name, it has nevertheless been depicted so sharply to those looking closely in the beautiful fictions of the two wisest poets, Homer and Virgil [Maro], that their reading delights me vehemently for this reason especially. For what did the one wish to show us through Ulysses, and the other through Aeneas, except the highest good of man? Nor did they wish only to show it, but also to demonstrate the most certain path which leads to it without any error. 

But what are we doing? Have we forgotten that we came to these places not so much for the sake of debate as for the sake of our health? I think, therefore, since we have rested enough, that we should rise; and we should descend to the rooms <note>original: "cellulas," perhaps referring to their private quarters or study spaces at a villa</note> not by the way we came, but through this nearby hill, by a greater curve but a less steep one. 

Indeed, said Laurentius: but on this condition, if you please, that we may enjoy your company both tomorrow and on the remaining days we are here. And since the debate on both kinds of life has been conducted by you not only elegantly and copiously, but also distinctly and acutely, I beg that you proceed; and what you were saying just now about Virgil [Maro], you will teach us in such a way that you follow the matter in a joined series and a perpetual flow. And so that you may understand what I want: I have seen some who interpret different passages in this poet according to this kind of opinion. But when those things which are left in between cannot be brought to the same meaning, they do not achieve credibility in the things they had translated. But you, in your wisdom and your singular talent in interpreting this poet—which I hear of from many—will complete the continuous course of the discussion from start to finish <note>original: "a carcere ad calcem," literally "from the starting gate to the chalk line," a metaphor from Roman chariot racing</note> without any stumbling. 

For even if there is no kind of learning whose every point you could not explain clearly, eloquently, learnedly, and wisely by the testimony of these most learned men: what, however, could be more closely joined to the debate recently held by you? Or what could be more pleasant to the ears and minds of all these men who honor and observe you as the only one among all? Or—since I know from your kindness toward me that you desire it vehemently—what could be more suited to my studies and those of my brother than when you have finished debating on both kinds of living, you now...

# On the Highest Good: Book Two

...that such a great man <note>referring to the poet Virgil</note> might show us that he holds your same opinion through the wanderings of his Aeneas and his arrival in Italy." When **Lorenzo** had said this, and we supported his request with our words and expressions as best we could, **Baptista** <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), a famous Renaissance architect and philosopher</note> replied: "This has turned out far differently than I had planned. I came here for the greatest leisure to enjoy the countryside, yet now I am asked to explain matters that must be unearthed from the deepest reaches of philosophy. But the night (as the proverb says) will give us the best counsel. For now, let us return to our small rooms, either by the path I mentioned or by another if **Mariotto** <note>Mariotto Rinuccini, a Florentine nobleman and scholar</note> thinks it more convenient." "No, let us go by that way," said **Mariotto**, and leading the way, he brought us back with pleasant and varied conversation, which, as they say, serves as a carriage on the road <note>original: "pro uehiculo"; a reference to the Latin proverb *comes facundus in via pro vehiculo est*, meaning a pleasant companion makes a journey feel shorter</note>.

### THE SECOND BOOK OF THE CAMALDULENSIAN DISPUTATIONS BY CHRISTOPHER LANDINO OF FLORENCE, TO THE ILLUSTRIOUS FEDERICO, PRINCE OF URBINO. ON THE HIGHEST GOOD.

<margin>They complain about nature</margin>
Not entirely without cause, most illustrious **Federico**, do many seem to complain about the weakness of the human condition. For nature has produced us in such a way that she has implanted a great desire to know things, but she has granted the ability to attain what we desire to only a few—perhaps a modest amount to those of more excellent talent—while bestowing none at all upon the rest of mankind. For nature has not (as they say) poured out her gifts upon us liberally and kindly like a benevolent and indulgent mother. Instead, like an unfair and envious stepmother, after showing us that which would delight us most and setting us violently aflame with vain hope, she then leaves us ignorant of the entire path. We wander in the darkness of errors, unable to discern any certain road to lead us out.

For from the beginning, our mind is burdened by the weight of the body and is immersed in <term>matter</term> <note>original: "hyle," a Greek term for the fundamental "stuff" of the physical world</note>, than which nothing is worse. As if made drunk, the mind lies buried in a deep sleep for a long time, and nothing thrives within us except the senses which come from the body. Reason is only awakened late; and it arises at a time when the appetite—which stems from the senses—has already seized the command of our entire life like a most cruel tyrant, though that command was owed not to the appetite, but to reason.

And so, returning at last to its kingdom after a long exile, reason is recognized by only a few of its subjects and received by even fewer. Thus, it either fails to recover its kingdom at all, or it must rule only by the grace of others. For the soul, corrupted by the worst habits and stained by false opinions—since the senses occupied it before it could even look upon the light of reason—can only with great difficulty return to the right path or look forward toward the truth. 

Even if the soul finally glimpses the truth itself—not in clear light, but at least in a "spiteful and uncertain moon" <note>original: "maligna... et incertam... lunam"; a quote from Virgil's *Aeneid*, Book VI, describing the dim light in the Underworld</note>—as if it were the harbor of its own salvation, it is nevertheless tossed by various waves of disturbance. It is thrown back into the deep by the receding tide so that it completely loses sight of the truth it thought it had already grasped. To these difficulties is added the disadvantage of the brevity of life, which is no small trouble (as those same people say). For as **Democritus** used to say, nature has hidden truth in the depths <note>referring to the philosopher Democritus's famous saying that "truth lies at the bottom of a well"</note>; and because of this, truth is buried in such profound darkness that it can only be unearthed and brought into the light through many labors and a long span of time...

<summary>The group retires for the evening, and Landino begins Book II with a dedication to Federico da Montefeltro, reflecting on why human beings struggle to find truth and why reason is often enslaved by physical desires.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, Lorenzo de' Medici, Federico da Montefeltro, Virgil, human condition, reason vs. senses, allegory of the soul, Democritus, Camaldoli</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Landino’s dedicatory preface to Federico da Montefeltro, Duke of Urbino. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's discussion on whether human life is too short to achieve the "Highest Good."</meta>

# On the Highest Good.

<warning>Faded marginal note in red ink on the left. Google watermark at the bottom right.</warning>

...to bring it forth; we are extinguished in the midst of our very course before we are permitted to reach the finish line <note>original: "calcem", referring to the chalk line that marked the end of a race in ancient stadiums</note>. When they say these things, they find many who agree with them. For every man prefers to impiously accuse nature rather than candidly admit his own laziness. But the excellent intellects of the most famous men clearly prove that it is far otherwise. Though these men were born under no different condition than other mortals, nor composed of any different mold, yet by their own industry, much sweat, and many sleepless nights, they arrived at that place where those who aspire to it are rightly considered blessed. 

But though I might now set aside the many who, both in our own age and in previous centuries, seem to have approached nearest to divinity either by action or by contemplation—are you yourself, **Most Illustrious Federico**, not able to be the greatest example for us? If anyone were to recall your most famous deeds, he would think no time could have remained for you for the study of letters <note>humanistic studies including grammar, rhetoric, history, and philosophy</note>. On the other hand, when we consider what learning you are endowed with, and what long and diligent labor you have spent on that matter, who would not be astonished that the Muses <note>the nine goddesses of the arts and sciences</note> dared to come amidst the greatest tumults of war and the horrendous clashing of arms? We read that many leaders among the ancients were highly literate. But that should be considered the praise of the times rather than the men, for in those days it would have been a disgrace not to know what almost everyone else excelled in. Our own times, however, have produced not a few who excel in military matters; 

<margin><unclear>Ant: Trong</unclear></margin>

among all of them, you might perhaps find some who are lightly tinged with letters, but I have known no one besides you whom one could call truly learned. For this reason, your glory must be held as more illustrious: not content with those arts which themselves have always been held in the greatest honor, you decided that you should not follow Mars <note>the god of war</note> alone—where everything resounds with the noise of arms and the blaring of trumpets—but you proposed that Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, goddess of both wisdom and defensive war</note> should be worshipped above all others, she who is held to be the goddess not only of warfare but of all wisdom. 

Armed with her counsel like a second Ulysses <note>Odysseus, known for his cunning and wisdom</note>, you have never lacked for guidance in the most difficult times and uncertain affairs. Even in your greatest and almost infinite occupations, with her as your leader, you have attained those teachings which many in the greatest leisure and abundance of all things have scarcely been able to taste. For when could either the enemy’s maneuvers or the shouting of your soldiers ringing in your ears from all sides ever so turn your mind away that a whole day passed empty for you—one in which you did not either read something yourself, or listen most attentively to another reading or disputing? 

Therefore, let us not wonder in the least if learned men have always been held by you in the highest honor. For how many do you have at home whom you have always nourished with your wealth and rendered honored with great dignity? And how many throughout all Italy have you either relieved from want with your wonderful liberality, or—even if they could easily support themselves on their own inheritance—have you nonetheless bestowed rewards suitable to their learning? For this reason I shall say indeed—not to take anything away from the praise of other princes, but to give such thanks as I am able, if not such as I owe, for your immense merits toward the Muses—that of all the princes I have heard of in our time, **Federico** alone is he who is both a saving harbor and a unique refuge for scholars in distress, and who brings grace and ornament to those established in greater fortune. Therefore, by as much as many princes surpass you in the size of their borders and empire, by so much greater praises shall the Greek, Latin, and Florentine Muses [carry] your [name through the] 

<meta>catchword: ages (secula)</meta>

He argues that Federico’s intellectual achievements are even more impressive because he pursues them amidst the chaos of war.</summary>

<page-num>16</page-num>
Book Two.

->### Book Two.<-

The ages will raise your name to the heavens: for the Muses, excluded from the courts of others, are received by you with such generous hospitality. Indeed, they are no longer guests but have been granted citizenship, and are now placed in a shrine most adorned and filled with every kind of book. For you have dedicated a library—one that is healthy in location, ample and distinguished in form, and most noble in its abundance of books—to Pallas <note>the goddess of wisdom, Athena</note>, Apollo <note>god of music and poetry</note>, and the nine Muses. As a result, the state now owes no less to you for this distinguished, honorable, and useful gift than Athens once owed to Pisistratus, or Egyptian Alexandria to Ptolemy (whose surname was Philadelphus), or Rome once owed to Lucius Lucullus, Gaius Caesar, and finally Asinius Pollio. It is recorded in memory that all these men established libraries filled with various volumes of every kind. And truly, what equipment, what furnishings, or what treasures should a man who is worthy of the most sacred name of "Prince" acquire for himself rather than those which alone are the best provision <note>original: "viaticum," literally the supplies or money intended for a journey</note> for us to reach the Highest Good? For from these books, ambrosia and nectar are obtained, upon which a blessed life can be nourished with the greatest pleasure.

But since I hear that, out of the nearly infinite number of your books, you are currently reading most eagerly those which concern the boundaries of good and evil <note>original: "bonorum malorumque fines," a reference to Cicero’s famous work on ethics</note>, I desire my own nightly labors on the same subject to be known to you. This is not so that you might finally learn anything previously unknown to you—for I am not so shameless as to try to "teach Minerva" (as the Greeks say) <note>original: "sus Mineruam," literally "a pig teaching Minerva," a proverb for an ignorant person trying to instruct a wise one</note>. However, I will consider it a great benefit if, among the monuments of so many most learned men, my book is allowed to sit even in the back rows.

Therefore, just as we described the first day’s discourse delivered by <term>BAPTISTA</term> <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous polymath and architect</note> in the previous book, we have now entrusted this second book to your name, **Federico**, most invincible in war and unique protector of the Muses. It contains the discussion which that most divine man held on the day after.

Scarcely had the next day dawned when we were called to the sacred rites <note>the morning Mass</note>. Our host, <term>MARIOTTO</term> <note>Mariotto Rinuccini, a member of the Florentine circle</note>, a man most pleasant in all his generosity and courtesy, had seen to it that these were performed early in the morning so that a greater span of the day would remain for us to listen to <term>BAPTISTA</term>. When the rites were finished, he led us around the area. Many features produced by nature in those places for the greatest pleasure, and many also where the monuments of the holiness and austerity of the men of old still exist, presented themselves to us everywhere. There was such pleasure—both for the eyes from the beauty of the region, and for the mind and soul from the traces of ancient religion—that we reached the fountain before we even noticed we had stepped away from the threshold. 

And then, when we had sat down and everyone's faces were turned in great silence toward <term>BAPTISTA</term>, he said: "I see that I am being called upon. But see, <term>LORENZO</term> <note>Lorenzo de' Medici (1449–1492), the 'Magnificent' ruler of Florence</note>, how generous the night has made me toward you. For I do not intend only to pay what I owe you from my promises, but I have something extra I wish to spend for free. You certainly asked for something worthy of a free man's knowledge, pleasant to hear, and not at all useless for many things. But since the discussion has been led to this point, it will not be out of place—before I touch on the matter specifically, and yet with no more words than necessity requires—to dispute concerning the boundaries of good and evil. You will say: 'Is this not almost the same thing as the previous talk?' But it is by another method. For then, we were seeking which path would most conveniently lead us; now, however, we seek what that thing is toward which we desire to be led."
