->### Concerning the Highest Good.<-

I believe this must be investigated; therefore, if we explain the opinions of nearly all the philosophers on this matter, and add to them many things which belong to the Christians, I hope that once these are viewed and understood, those things which we seek in Virgil <note>original: "Marone," referring to the poet Publius Vergilius Maro</note> will be entirely easier. Here, since we all strongly approved of the man's plan and showed ourselves most ready to listen, I remember that he used a beginning of this sort: Since it is manifest from those things which were disputed by us on the previous day <note>original: "externa die"</note> that man was born by chance neither for the most sluggish idleness nor for idle pleasure, but was produced by the immortal God either for the knowledge of the greatest and divine things or for certain most noble actions beneficial to the human race, that discussion always seems to me especially useful in which it is best explained what the end <note>term: "finis," in philosophy, the ultimate goal or purpose of human life</note> is—what is the extreme, what is the ultimate—to which all plans for both living rightly and thinking truly are to be referred. Since we see that concerning this matter there was no small controversy among the ancient writers, I do not doubt that, if the opinions of each are brought into the open and compared among themselves, it will be easy even for men endowed with moderate talent and learning to select from them all what exists as either the truest or the most likely. They, therefore, did not only 
<margin>Note!</margin> agree on the name—for with no one Refusing they called it the highest good <note>original: "summum bonum"</note>—but they also perceived that the thing itself was of such a kind that they affirmed all other things were to be sought for its sake, while it was sought *for its sake alone.*
But when they sought what that thing chiefly was, then such great dissension arose among them and such a sharp struggle was excited, that they seem to be desirous not of finding the truth, but of tenaciously holding onto that which they once snatched up. For this reason, although this entire part of philosophy which concerns life and morals flowed from Socrates alone as if from its own source, we nevertheless see that which ought to have been one thing split and derived into many parts by the diversity of opinions and the contentions of men. For while that most innocent man, with the sharpest edge of intellect for choosing and a certain firm constancy of mind for persevering in what he had chosen, always followed the same thing, nevertheless, by a 
<margin>Plato:</margin> wonderful irony <note>Socrates was famous for his "Socratic irony," pretending ignorance to reveal the flaws in others' logic</note> disputing about all things in both directions, since he did not always seem to feel the same way but was thought to bring forward different things in different places, he gave, as it were, certain handles to those who were delighted by the man’s teaching, so that each followed that as if defended by him which was most consistent with his own genius and nature. This is the reason why we see just as many families and sects of philosophers among the Greeks as there are opinions extant concerning the ends of goods and evils. For if we believe Marcus Varro and Marcus Cicero, most weighty authors, we shall think that no sect of philosophy should be so named 
<margin>it differs</margin> which, even if it *differs from the others in the rest, feels the same about the ends of goods and evils;* since indeed there is no cause for a man to philosophize except that he may be happy, nor can he be happy unless *the end of goods has been perceived and acquired.*
Therefore, that which follows no end of goods is to be called no sect. But I return to the ancients, some of whom were of such a dull and low intellect that, although we consist of soul and body, they could not follow the divinity of the human mind with their own mind. And so, noticing nothing else besides the body and the senses which hang from the body, and thinking themselves to be only that, in those things which these...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "Highest Good" (summum bonum) from Book Two. Having established the necessity of finding a life's purpose, the speaker now surveys the schools of thought that identify this purpose with pleasure (voluptas).</meta>

# Book Two.

<margin>Aristippus</margin>
...they established the **highest good** <note>original: "summum bonum"</note>. Therefore, since that pleasure which soothes the senses moves every living creature to desire it with the greatest effort, it has had many supporters who would grant it the primary place. The chief of all these is said to be **Aristippus**. He was born in Cyrene, Libya, and educated in Athens under **Socrates**. Although he attributed everything to pleasure, he noticed that many times arise in which men cannot enjoy any pleasure. Lest he fall into distress from a lack of things—just as he perceived pleasure from an abundance of them—he wonderfully trained his mind so that he could easily enjoy things when present and despise them when absent. For this reason, he was the first of the Socratic followers to teach philosophy for a fee; he also very carefully cultivated the court of the tyrant **Dionysius** so that he might conveniently acquire the wealth needed to procure every kind of pleasure. Yet he endured being looked down upon or suffering loss with a very calm mind—so much so that when a friend wondered why he bore being spat upon by Dionysius so mildly, he replied: "To catch a gudgeon <note>original: "gobium," a small, common fish</note>, a lowly little fish, fishermen allow themselves to be drenched by much seawater; shall I not endure a spray of spit so that I may feast on a sea-bass?" <note>original: "bleno," likely referring to a blenny or similar prized fish</note>. I pass over many things which he underwent voluntarily through the greatest indignity, lest he lose that pleasure by which alone he thought himself happy. For this reason, we see him not unjustly called a "royal dog" by **Diogenes**.

<margin>Eudoxus.<br/>Dionysius.</margin>
**Nicomachus** (the son of Aristotle) recorded that **Eudoxus of Cnidus** held the same opinion—a man otherwise distinguished and famous in mathematics and medicine, and a legislator of no small wisdom. **Dionysius of Heraclea** felt the same. Although he had learned to be somewhat brave from **Zeno of Citium** (the leader of the **Stoics**), and had heard from his mouth that he should cultivate virtue as the only good and shudder at vice as the only evil, he finally succumbed to pain when he fell into a disease of the eyes. Deserting the "Porch" <note>term: "Porticum," referring to the Stoa, the porch in Athens where the Stoics taught</note>, he defected to the **Cyrenaics**. He no longer feared to do anything involving pleasure, even in the eyes of everyone; indeed, he wrote a very precise book on pleasure in which he commends it as the highest good.

<margin>Epicurus</margin>
Truly, regarding **Epicurus of Athens**, whom many believe was the most spirited patron in defending pleasure, I do not know what to say. For in his "Principal Doctrines" <note>original: "ratis opinionibus," referring to the *Kyriai Doxai*</note>, when he speaks of the highest good, he affirms he has nothing by which he can understand "the good" if those pleasures which come from flavors, or sexual matters, or those which charm through beauty of form, are taken away from him. Yet, in a letter to **Hermarchus**, he values virtue so highly that even in the greatest physical torments (in which he himself places the "highest evil"), he dares to say he is nevertheless most happy because of a conscience of right deeds. Sometimes, however, he speaks of pleasure in such a way that he seems to mean the pleasure of the mind rather than that which comes from the senses. At other times, he places not pleasure, but **freedom from pain** <note>original: "indolentiam," referring to the Greek "aponia"</note> among the goods. But if you look at the life and character of the man, you will find many things to be pursued with the highest praise. Indeed, if he had thought rightly about God, you would find nothing in his actions that could not be justly praised. For he never did anything, nor persuaded others to do anything, that was far from justice or equity. He could be cast down from the highest level of constancy by no pains or tortures. There was such great loyalty and such great charity in the man that he even nobly nourished and educated the children of his deceased friends himself; and while dying, he provided in his will for them to be educated and fed by others. For in those pleasures which he extols in time, how continent and rather...

# On the Highest Good.

How temperate he was is easy to recognize: since he rarely drank wine, and rarely added anything to his bread besides a small piece of cheese. For this reason, he seems not to have shared the views of the common crowd of men; for most mortals, even when they live most basely, nevertheless present an appearance of the greatest restraint and severity in their expressions and words. For, as it is said in the satirist <note>The author is quoting Juvenal’s Satires (2.8): "What street does not abound with gloomy, obscene men?"</note>, **what street does not abound with gloomy, obscene men?** This man, however, although he says things that even a profligate <note>original: "asotae"</note> would not utter without blushing, nevertheless does not yield even to **Socrates** in his life and character. But enough about **Epicurus** for now. <margin>In <unclear>Rhodes</unclear> regarding pleasure</margin> 

**Hieronymus of Rhodes**, a man truly learned and pleasant—but one who was unable to maintain the gravity of the **Peripatetics** <note>Followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy.</note> in whose bosom he was nourished—judged the highest good to be not pleasure, but the **absence of pain**. <margin>Periander.</margin> In this, he followed, as many believe, the authority of **Periander**, the tyrant of the Corinthians, whom they claim was the originator of this kind of opinion. <margin>Leucippus.</margin> Truly, **Leucippus of Abdera** felt the same; <margin>Theodor.</margin> so did **Theodorus**, whom they nicknamed "the Atheist" <note>original: "Atheon"</note> because he denied the existence of the gods. 

For all the more learned men suspect something greater concerning **Democritus**. Since he was a man of outstanding intellect, when he says the highest good is <term>Euthymia</term> <note>From the Greek word for "well-being" or "cheerfulness."</note>, he does not only mean the absence of those pains which are in the body, but a right and quiet state of the soul, which the removal of all disturbances produces. <margin>In the peace of Italy</margin> Therefore, I believe that what this philosopher calls <term>Euthymia</term>, our people call **peace** in the Holy Scriptures. In this peace, the Hebrew poet places the highest good.

> For he says: Praise the Lord, O Jerusalem; praise your God, O Zion. For he has strengthened the bars of your gates; he has blessed your children within you. He has set your borders in peace. <note>A translation of Psalm 147:12–14.</note>

For when the bars of the gates are strengthened, no one shall go out from it, nor shall anyone enter. This is just as if he were to say that with nothing—neither external nor internal—moving the appetite beyond the bounds of reason, there will be the greatest tranquility in the soul. If we should say this is the highest good while we live, we shall never go astray. But more on this a little later. <margin>or the lack of pain or pleasure.</margin> 

We have now briefly shown, unless I am mistaken, those who believed the ends of good things consist in pleasure and those who believed they consist in the absence of pain. Besides these, however, I see there were those who believed neither of these things alone, but both joined together, create a happy life; I would more willingly number **Epicurus** among these than among those who cling to pleasure alone. 

There were likewise those who thought that not even these joined together could provide what we seek, but judged that all things called the **primary things of nature** <note>original: "prima naturae," a Stoic term referring to basic instincts like self-preservation, health, and strength.</note> must be summoned to complete so great a matter. Among these things, although many include pleasure and the absence of pain, there are far more things—both in the soul and in the body—that may be called "primary things of nature." But these will be discussed more distinctly and abundantly a little later in a more convenient place. 

Therefore, if you weigh the opinions of all these men correctly, you will see that they perceived nothing that can be considered excellent; they could neither call the mind away from the senses, nor understand anything above the senses. We may most rightly despise them without doing them an injustice. For who would not mock that "philosopher" who, in searching for the highest good of man, maintains that he has found it in a thing that either belongs to no part of a man, or is the smallest and worst part of a man? Such a man no longer deserves to be called a <term>philosopher</term> <note>A lover of wisdom.</note>, but rightly a <term>philosarchus</term> <note>A lover of the flesh.</note>. This error was detected by those in whom there was both gravity and...

The sharpness of their minds and the excellence of their talent were so great, and they so admired the divinity of the mind, that they believed nothing else besides the mind truly belonged to a human being. Therefore, they placed the "highest good"—the ultimate goal for which all other things are done—in the contemplation of divine and human affairs and in the uncovering of the secrets of nature. They say that the leaders of this opinion were **Thales of Miletus** <note>One of the "Seven Sages" of Greece, often called the first philosopher.</note> (one of the Seven Sages) and **Bias of Priene**, who was held in the same number. They also include **Anaxagoras of Clazomenae** and **Euclid the Socratic** <note>Euclid of Megara, a pupil of Socrates, not to be confused with Euclid the mathematician.</note>. If we were to count **Aristotle** among these, we would not seem ignorant of what he relates in his tenth book *On Ethics* <note>Referring to the Nicomachean Ethics, where Aristotle argues that the highest happiness is found in contemplation (theoria).</note>. Truly, since the entire family of the **Peripatetics** <note>Followers of Aristotle’s school.</note> takes its origin from this man, we shall explain shortly what the specific opinion of that sect is.

<margin>
Pittacus
Menedemus
Bion
Socratics
Pythagoreans
Stoics
Cynics
</margin>
There existed, moreover, another kind of philosopher. While they did not cling entirely to this sort of contemplation, they nevertheless embraced virtue alone so firmly in the conduct of life and the management of affairs that they dared to call nothing "good" except virtue, nor anything "evil" except vice. We read that the first among the Greeks to hold this view were **Pittacus of Mitylene**, **Menedemus of Lampsacus**, and **Bion of Borysthenes**. But the **Socratics** and **Pythagoreans** also seem to affirm the same. Truly, as for those who established the whole matter with the most numerous and serious arguments, we can put forward the **Stoics**. These were truly masculine philosophers, in whom you may find such strength of mind in every kind of danger, and such temperance against all pleasures, that they seem unable to be cast down from the step of highest tranquility by any snares of Fortune. If you were to join the **Cynics** to them, you would be doing so rightly; for they share the same opinion and the same rigor of mind, such that if you removed their outward dress <note>The Cynics were famous for their distinctive rough cloaks and staff.</note>, you would judge them to be almost the same.

<margin>
<unclear>i. f.t</unclear> it ends as.
Peripatetics
</margin>
You see, therefore, what the **Stoics** attribute to virtue. The **Peripatetics**, however, and the **Old Academics** <note>The early followers of Plato’s Academy.</note> thought that the origin of the "highest good" <note>original: "summi boni"</note> must be sought from nature. For since all things produced by nature only then live well and blessedly if they live (if I may put it this way) consistently and in harmony with their own nature, they deny that the ultimate end of man can be known if his nature is ignored.

<margin>
Marcus Varro
Primary things
</margin>
**Marcus Varro**—a man whom no learned person ever doubted should be compared in the magnitude and variety of his learning to the greatest of the Greeks—investigates the "ends" or goals in this way: since the highest good of man must be sought, he thinks one must first ask what this thing is that we call "man." Does he consist of mind and body? In this union, since the mind is far more excellent than the body, it could easily be doubted whether the mind alone is the man, while the body is no part of him. In that case, the body would be present for the mind like a horse for a knight. For a "knight" is not called such because he consists of a man and a horse, but though he is a man alone, he is called a knight because he has a certain relationship with a horse. Or is the body alone the man, yet standing in some relationship to the soul, in the same way that a cup stands to a drink? For a bowl and the drink it contains are not together called "the cup," but the bowl alone; yet it would never be called by that name unless it were suitable for containing the drink. Finally, since neither of these views is pleasing—and he does not think man is either the body alone or the mind alone—he investigates whether man should be called both together, just as we call a "team" <note>original: "bigas," referring to a two-horse chariot team.</note> not this horse or that horse, but both joined together to the yoke. Since this great man clings firmly to this latter opinion, he thinks that the highest good itself, by which we shall be happy, consists of the goods of the mind and the body together. Persuaded by this kind of reasoning, he considered the "primary things of nature" <note>term: primary things of nature (prima naturae). These are basic natural instincts like health, safety, and sound senses that the Peripatetics considered essential components of the good life.</note> to be desirable for their own sake. <meta>This page continues the discussion of philosophical schools regarding the ultimate goal of life. ## ## On the Highest Good

<margin> { </margin>
...since a living being <note>original: "animal"; the OCR is missing the start of the word</note> is, by nature, highly commended to itself, it acts with all possible care to preserve itself. For our "best parent" <note>Nature</note> first instilled in us this appetite: the desire to be safe and to be so disposed that we might be in the best possible state according to our own nature. For this reason, a person loves their own safety; they love the preservation of all their parts—health, the integrity of the senses, the removal of all pain, as well as strength and beauty, and finally other things of this kind. All of these are called the "primary things of nature" <term>prima naturae</term> <note>These are the basic physical and mental needs nature prompts us to seek before we develop reason.</note> in the body. Likewise, one loves those things in the mind that are similar: certain sparks and seeds of virtue, such as teachability and memory, which are both called by the single name of "talent" <note>original: "ingenii"</note>. However, virtue itself—which education implants and which they have become accustomed to calling the "art of living"—is something we do not recognize until late in life, even if it is the most excellent of all the goods found in the mind.

<margin> Plato. </margin>
For this reason, Plato rightly calls that man "blessed" to whom it happens, even in old age, that he attains wisdom and true opinions. Therefore, when virtue of this kind (which is the art of conducting life) has received those natural gifts—which were present even when education was absent—it seeks them for their own sake. It does this so that it may delight in and fully enjoy all of them, some more and some less, according to whether they are greater or smaller. Virtue, therefore, rejoices in all of them, yet does so with such judgment that if necessity requires it, it can easily despise certain smaller things in order to acquire greater ones or to preserve those already acquired. Therefore, virtue places nothing before itself among the remaining goods, whether of the mind or the body. Indeed, as the wisest leader or commander, virtue knows how to use both itself and those other things that make a person blessed in the most correct way. But if virtue should relinquish its command over life, and the other things do not obey it, those things must necessarily perish; nor can they be "goods" in any way for one who uses them badly.

<margin>
<unclear>so that the mind in...</unclear>
</margin>
<margin>
life
blessed
virtue
</margin>
**Therefore, the blessed life will consist first of virtue, and furthermore of those goods of mind and body without which virtue could in no way exist.** If, however, many of those goods are added—which, even if absent, do not take away virtue—the life is changed from being "blessed" to "more blessed." Finally, if not just many, as I just said, but all are added, what will forbid such a life, with its accumulation of all goods, from being called "most blessed"? Furthermore, it is easy to recognize which goods are those without which virtue cannot stand, and which are those whose absence cannot remove it.

<margin> [symbol] </margin>
**For the integrity of the senses, memory, and reason are of such a kind that without them virtue cannot exist.** Through these we attain the learning without which virtue is not perceived. However, speed, strength, beauty, nobility, and things similar to these can exist apart from virtue; and yet they are considered "goods" by the Peripatetics <note>The followers of Aristotle.</note>. Virtue loves these for their own sake, and the wise man will enjoy them rightly, as becomes him. Furthermore, they maintain that a blessed life of this kind must also be a social one.

Therefore, the wise man will love the goods of his friends for their own sake just as he loves his own. He will desire for his friend the same things he desires for himself, not for his own sake, but for the sake of the friend. He will, however, maintain a diligent choice among the human race, so that he understands best in what place his country, his children, his parents, his relatives, his other intimates, his fellow citizens, and finally the rest of mortals are to be held in their proper order, until he embraces the entire human race. I have touched upon, I believe, the entire opinion of the Peripatetics concerning the "highest good" <term>summum bonum</term>. If I have seemed to you to be briefer in this matter than you would wish, consider that no loss has been made by it.

<margin>
<unclear>Acciaiolos</unclear>
<unclear>Alamannu</unclear>
</margin>
For you have these men, the Acciaiuoli and Alamanno <note>Likely referring to Donato Acciaiuoli and Alamanno Rinuccini, prominent 15th-century Florentine scholars who translated and commented on Aristotle's Ethics.</note>, and the others here, who, given their learning and eloquence, can discuss all these things both solemnly and at length...

<summary>This page explains the Peripatetic view of the "highest good," arguing that while virtue is the most important component of a blessed life, it requires certain physical and mental foundations (like memory and health) to exist and flourish.</summary>

## ## Book Two

-> [Signature d ii] <-

...so that they may be able to dispute when you wish. And I wish our Oliverio Arduini <note>A contemporary philosopher and associate of the Medici circle.</note> were present: whom I value so highly in all philosophy that, in my opinion, he holds a most honorable place among the Aristotelians. 

"It is as you say," said Donato, "that man is distinguished by much learning; and if his own duties had allowed it, he would not have failed to be a companion to Lorenzo <note>Lorenzo de' Medici, the "Magnificent."</note> in these sorts of rural retreats. As for us, since you speak so honorably, whether you do so truly or rather out of excessive friendship, others will have to judge. Yet, one does not at all despise being praised by such a great and distinguished man. For even if we have always held that virtue itself should be sought for its own sake, even with no reward offered, we have not yet—to tell the truth—progressed so far that, if a certain 'little glory' follows it like a shadow follows a body, we are strong enough to flee from it. But I wonder why, while you count us among the Aristotelians, you passed over Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino, the famous Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> in silence?" 

At this, Battista <note>Leon Battista Alberti, the polymath architect and philosopher.</note>, having looked at Marsilio with a smile, said: "What else do you think, O Donato, except that I was prevented by envy from doing this? For even if I do not doubt that he has drunk abundantly from those waters which flowed from the spring of the Lyceum <note>original: "e lycii fonte"; the Lyceum was Aristotle's school in Athens.</note>, yet since we all acknowledge him as the truest interpreter of those enigmas which must be solved from the oracle of the Academy <note>Plato’s school.</note>, do you think I can be forced to grant him both?"

Here Alamanno said: "You are joking, Battista; and you wanted to sprinkle the gravity of your discussion with some wit, as is your custom. But to speak seriously, I wish others suffered from the same kind of 'envy' as you. For then these bitter rivalries would not be stirred up daily among learned men. Instead, embracing each other with mutual goodwill, each would extol without deceit the good things he saw in another; and if there were something that caused offense, he would suppress it all in a friendly manner. 

I cannot sufficiently wonder at this—whether one should call it cruelty or stupidity or both—of some men who, casting off the nature proper to a human being, think they gain as much praise as they have taken from others by disparaging them. They trust they can only prove their own learning to others once they have argued for the ignorance of everyone else. I shudder at their savage characters as if they were portents of nature. For literature <note>original: "litterae"; refers to a humanist education in the liberal arts.</note>, unless it is born in some monstrous beast, usually performs the same service for a man as they say water does for lupins <note>A type of legume known for being bitter unless soaked in water.</note>. For just as those, once soaked, lay aside all bitterness and offer themselves as very sweet to the taste, so the human mind, once refined by literature, lays aside not only whatever is crude and inept, but also whatever remains in it that is harsh or inhuman. 

But perhaps we have wandered too far from what was proposed. And so, if it seems right, return now to the beginning." 

"On the contrary, O Alamanno," said Battista, "a digression of this kind was most welcome. For I highly approve of the things you said, and I gladly recognize that you philosophize not only with words (as many are accustomed to do) but also with your life and character. But so that the speech may eventually return to where I left off: the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle, named for his habit of walking while teaching.</note> are accustomed to dispute about the 'ends of goods' <term>de finibus bonorum</term> <note>A technical term for the ultimate goal or purpose of human life.</note> according to this formula. Indeed, Marcus Varro affirms that this was also the opinion of the Old Academics,

<margin>
Antiochus
<unclear>my [teacher?]</unclear>
</margin>

on the authority of Antiochus <note>Antiochus of Ascalon, who sought to reconcile the Stoic, Peripatetic, and Academic schools.</note>: whom we read was the teacher to him and to Cicero—although Cicero contends that in many matters Antiochus was more of a Stoic than an Old Academic. You see, therefore, how varied are the opinions concerning the highest good. But truly, if we look more closely at our nature, and consider in what a miserable condition we live in this mortal life, and what good we can achieve in it as if reaching the summit—which nonetheless so embraces the whole man...

They begin to define the "highest good" (summum bonum) by referencing ancient authorities like Antiochus of Ascalon and Marcus Varro.</summary>

# ## On the Highest Good

-> [Signature b] <-

<margin>¶</margin>
...so that he might take account of both the body and the mind according to the dignity of each, those do not seem to have gone astray who place virtue first in such a way that they also join the remaining goods to it in the manner we have already demonstrated. For we must admit that as long as the soul—which is like a fiery and most swift horse—is joined to the body—which is like a dull and stupid donkey—the care of neither is to be neglected <note>A classic metaphor in Renaissance philosophy: the "horse" represents the rational soul, while the "donkey" represents the heavy, material body.</note>. For this reason, we consider this opinion the most perfect of all those discussed so far. Those who followed, wishing to appear as if they were bringing something new, took this "complete and whole body" of thought and broke off a small piece of it. 

For Herillus <note>A Stoic philosopher and pupil of Zeno.</note>, a student of Zeno the Stoic, seeing that knowledge was often praised for its own sake by Aristotle and Theophrastus, contended that knowledge alone was the highest good. Aristo <note>Aristo of Chios, another Stoic, who argued that everything except virtue and vice is "indifferent."</note>, however, delighted by the magnificence and appearance of words and driven by a love of the virtues, read much that had been written by the ancients about despising human affairs; he dared to affirm that once one has distinguished between virtue and vice, nothing else remains that should be sought or avoided. But because he so neglected those things that fall in the middle between virtue and vice—believing that no choice at all should be made between them and making all things equal—he judged there to be no difference between extreme poverty and the greatest wealth, nor between perfect health and the most serious disease. By doing so, he utterly destroys virtue itself, which he loves so much; for the most characteristic quality of virtue is to make a choice among those things that are according to nature. He thus removes the path to finding one's duty. Indeed, if duty were removed from life, I do not see what would remain to distinguish us from cattle. 

But Hieronymus of Rhodes <note>A Peripatetic philosopher who broke from the school to argue that the absence of pain was the goal of life.</note> was even more diminished, deviating greatly from his own teacher; although he saw many things counted among the "goods of nature," he selected only "freedom from pain" <note>original: "indolentiam"</note> (as we have already said) from all of them to hold the sovereignty of the highest good. Calipho and Diodorus were a bit more generous. For while one valued pleasure and the other valued that "freedom from pain" I just mentioned, neither of them excluded moral honor. Polemo <note>A head of the Platonic Academy.</note>, however, content with the primary things of nature, so neglected virtue that he thought a happy life could be achieved by those natural things alone without moral honor. Therefore, when we observe the opinions of all these men, we understand that they are like limbs torn away from that body which the Peripatetics <note>The followers of Aristotle, known for walking (peripateō) while teaching.</note> had expressed to us as a whole and intact.

Truly, since we are subject to so many, so varied, and so horrifying accidents of fortune, we shall never establish a man as "happy" unless virtue is present as a most fortified citadel into which he may retreat while cruel enemies rage and rush in from every side. For the struggle of appetite against reason is so fierce and constant, and such horrifying and frequent storms rise up from the various winds of fortune—exciting so many disturbances—

<margin>☞</margin>
that unless a certain true and almost divine wisdom is present—which, having removed the cloud of error (as it says in Juvenal <note>A reference to the Roman satirist Juvenal’s tenth Satire: "In all the lands... few can distinguish true goods from their opposites, once the mist of error is removed."</note>), can distinguish true goods—how shall we be able to seek or flee anything by reason? 

<margin>¶</margin>
For it is Wisdom which, having cut away the boundless desires and removed vain terrors, alone knows the truth; and, awakening Justice, she weighs and dispenses all things with an equal scale. When dangers are imminent or already pressing, she opposes them with Fortitude, as if carrying an adamantine and unbreakable shield; and with the spurs of magnanimity and endurance, she impels the soul to undergo every hardship for the sake of grace and moral honor. 

<margin>¶</margin>
On the other hand, lest the soul fall into excessive lusts and pleasures through too much wantonness, she restrains it with the bridle of Temperance. From this it happens that, like a most firm rock...

<summary>The author critiques various philosophical schools for focusing on "fragments" of the highest good, ultimately arguing that the Peripatetic view—balancing soul and body—is the most complete. He personifies the four cardinal virtues (Wisdom, Justice, Fortitude, and Temperance) as essential defenses against the storms of fortune.</summary>

## Book Second

-> d iii <-

...as a rock remains motionless in the midst of the waves, and is moved from its place by no tides or tempests: so should our mind, having broken all the waves of disturbances by its own constancy, dwell in the highest tranquility. Let it enjoy that peace—not the kind which the human condition provides, but that which God, as if in his final testament <note>The Latin *extremis codicillis* refers to a legal codicil or a final will and testament, suggesting peace is a divine inheritance.</note>, bequeathed to his own to enjoy forever. As far as our human weakness allows, let it live blessedly. And truly, what could harm that man who never admires those things which the common crowd considers greatest? <margin>Ch</margin> For such a man would hardly place poverty, exile, disease, the loss of children, or even death itself in the category of evils. Yet, even if these things are endured in such a way, Christians nevertheless deny that men can be called truly happy while we are occupied in this life. For if you say that virtue alone is the good, do you not leave the body—the companion of the soul and a small part of ourselves—without any good at all? If you think the body pertains nothing to us, I might easily concede the point; however, if diseases of this kind arise in the body that the senses are corrupted, and memory and judgment are taken away, where, I ask, will virtue remain? Or by what means will it be acquired? But if we call the comforts of the body "goods," then its discomforts will be "evils." And these can indeed happen so severely that they either turn us away from all the duties of life, or reduce us to such a state that death is to be preferred to life. Therefore, they would force us to take away from ourselves that without which we are not human—since the first voice of nature is that a man should be reconciled to himself and therefore flee his own destruction. We shall not, therefore, be happy while we wander in this mortal life, except by the sole hope of that life which is to come and is immortal. <margin>Paul</margin> Whence comes that saying in the Epistle of Paul: *For in hope we were saved* <note>original: "spe enim salui facti sumus" — a reference to Romans 8:24.</note>. For they say that God is the highest good: a thing which it is well known that Plato also sensed even before Christ was born as a man. You will easily be able to learn his opinion whenever you wish, either from those commentaries which Marsilio <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), the leader of the Platonic Academy in Florence and a character in this dialogue.</note> wrote on Plato’s *Philebus* <note>Plato's dialogue concerning the relationship between pleasure, intelligence, and the Good.</note>—which are not only most weighty but also exceptionally clear and detailed—<margin>Phi</margin> or, if you cannot endure to wait any longer, let him take over for me now while I catch my breath for a moment, and let him satisfy your desire.

I certainly, said **LORENZO**, since we are in full stride, would least of all wish to stop here, as if we had stumbled on a bumpy road. Therefore (provided the matter is conducted with "Greek faith" <note>original: "græca fide" — an ironic Roman expression usually implying dishonesty or "cash on the spot," but here used to mean sincerity or perhaps direct intellectual exchange.</note>), it makes no difference to me whether I receive the answer from you or from Marsilio. For neither of you should fear "changing soil" <note>original: "solum uertere" — a legal term for going into exile to avoid punishment.</note> or "shifting the debt" <note>original: "uerfuram facere" — to take out a new loan to pay off an old one.</note> in a small payment, since so much is stored away in the chests of both of you that your credit should not fail even in a much grander transaction. I wonder, however, since you have led the disputation to this point, that you have tried to substitute a proxy neither in yesterday’s nor today’s talk. Why is it that you now wish to interrupt a speech that has been led forth in a continuous thread until now?

Especially, said Marsilio, since in the sight of so many most distinguished Aristotelians, he has himself so narrated the entire opinion of that school that he did not even try to be relieved of a part of such a great burden by anyone.

There are indeed many present, as you say, said Baptista <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous polymath and architect.</note>, who could have most excellently performed this service for me; and if they had performed it, they would have done something very pleasing and delightful. Why then, you will ask, did I not seek it? Because I saw that since many could provide it, as long as one person deferred the matter to another, everyone would end up refusing. But there was no such fear regarding you. For although neither the Academy is forbidden to these men, nor the "thread" <note>original: "licio" — perhaps referring to the thread of the argument or a specific academic badge.</note> to you, I prefer to call these men Aristotelians and you a Platonist. Since I see you standing here alone, how, I pray, could you be a match for me—who am in such thin health—if we must clash in single combat? Nor is it a matter where age counts for much...

<summary>Alberti transitions the debate from Stoic and Peripatetic views to a Christian-Platonic synthesis, arguing that true happiness exists only in the hope of the afterlife. He then invites Marsilio Ficino to speak on Plato's concept of the Good, using metaphors of debt and credit to describe their intellectual exchange.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Cristoforo Landino’s Disputationes Camaldulenses. The speakers are Leon Battista Alberti (referred to as Leo), Marsilio Ficino (Marsilius), and Lorenzo de' Medici (Laurentius). They are transitioning from a playful debate about who should speak next to a serious philosophical investigation into the nature of the "Highest Good."</meta>

# On the Highest Good

...battles. For that you do not do so, the fight of Dares and Entellus <note>Dares and Entellus are two legendary boxers from Virgil’s *Aeneid*; the older Entellus eventually defeats the younger, more boastful Dares.</note> can serve as an example. Therefore, if you are wise, you will surrender <note>The Latin *dabis manus* literally means "you will give your hands," an idiom for admitting defeat.</note> and you will make the burden imposed on you lighter by your patience. When these things had been said most urbanely as a joke by Leo, Marsilio said: "Indeed, I hold out the grass <note>The idiom *herbam porrigere* (to hold out the grass) was an ancient sign of surrendering in a contest.</note> and I yield with a most calm mind. For when I have been pardoned for the strength of my rashness by all who are present, and have obtained their agreement to free an obedient student from the charge of impudence, what reason is there for me to refuse to speak? Especially since I can do so both for my own profit and without any loss to Lorenzo. For if there happen to be things which are expressed correctly by me, I will have made no small gain, since I shall henceforth use them as if they were beyond doubt, having sensed they were approved by you—something I have hitherto hardly dared to do, lacking confidence in myself. If, however, anything should be mistaken, what could be of greater use or pleasure to me than to return to the right path by your correcting my error? But lest a longer introduction than is proper be woven into what will be quite brief, let us now get to the matter at hand."

As Leo was saying just a moment ago, the divine Plato thought that God is the highest good; he not only said that God is one, but also taught this with the weightiest arguments. But this is not the question in the present dispute. However, he proves what the highest good is by a reason of this kind, if I am not mistaken: by the consensus of all philosophers, that is called "highest" which we seek for its own sake before it is acquired, and in which we rest once it is obtained, so that nothing else is desired beyond it. Since there are three types of goods, which the greatest part of men agree upon, let us see more carefully which of these primarily provides us with what we seek. 

<margin>Good.</margin>

Is it those things called "external"? Not at all. For those are sought not for themselves but for the sake of the body. For whatever things are found in the fickleness of fortune—riches, honors, and similar things—are for the pleasure or use of the body; therefore they are better called "useful" than "good." But neither do those things belonging to the body seem to be desirable for their own sake. For we require them for the sake of the soul. Add to this that they do not even seem worthy of being called "goods" themselves. For if we call "good" that which is by its own nature always the same for everyone, how can these be goods which are always bad for the one who uses them badly? For what does beauty of the body bring to the lustful? What do riches bring to the prodigal? What do power and strength confer upon the gladiator, the assassin, or the robber? Only that they become the worst out of the bad. For when instruments of this kind for their lusts have offered themselves to a mind that is already sick, what a window to every kind of wickedness do you think is opened?

But what is more miserable: take those same men to be good and especially frugal—have not many, although they used with the greatest prudence and temperance those things which fools believe to be not only goods but even the greatest goods, nevertheless experienced extreme ruin? In doing so, they ran into the hatred, envy, cruelty, and greed of evil men, and finally the lust of unchaste women. But orators pursue these themes more broadly. For us, it will be enough to have shown that what we seek cannot be found in the goods of fortune nor in those of the body. 

It will therefore be either in the goods of the soul, or nowhere. But the latter is absurd; therefore the first will be true. Yet the final end we seek will not be in those goods which are from nature—in which category we list talent, memory, boldness, and the rest which the <term>Aristotelians</term> <note>The "Aristotelians" or Peripatetics followed the philosophy of Aristotle, who classified certain natural capacities as "first things of nature."</note> call the "first things of nature." For we do not stop in those as if at the final goal of the race; but supported by them, we...

<summary>Marsilio Ficino accepts the challenge to lead the philosophical discussion, defining the Highest Good (summum bonum) as something sought for itself that provides total rest. He systematically dismisses external wealth and physical health as the Highest Good, arguing they are merely "useful" and can even harm the soul if misused.</summary>

<keywords>Highest Good, summum bonum, Plato, Marsilio Ficino, Leon Battista Alberti, Lorenzo de' Medici, external goods, goods of the body, goods of the soul, Aristotelians, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse of Marsilio Ficino as he defines the nature of the "Highest Good" (Summum Bonum). Having transitioned from the introductory banter on the previous page, Ficino now addresses the group on the relationship between virtue, the soul's restlessness, and the divine nature of truth.</meta>

# Book Two

...we proceed toward greater things. For this reason, the virtues acquired from there will be able to provide us with what we seek: yet not those which are spoken of regarding life and morals, for these are laborious and are applied like certain atonements <note>The Latin *piacula* refers to ritual purifications or sacrifices. Ficino suggests that moral virtues are a way of "cleaning" the soul of worldly stains rather than the ultimate goal itself.</note> to purge our minds. Rather, it is those virtues by which we rightly speculate. But you will say that this also occurs in the knowledge of lesser things: that knowledge indeed strives to make us happy, but yet it lacks the power to satisfy our minds—than which nothing is more greedy—which never rest and never lay aside the anxiety of investigation unless they reach that highest incorporeal good, unbegotten and dependent on nothing, upon which all things depend. This we call the cause, the origin, the fountain, and the principle of all things. Since the knowledge of this offers itself to our minds as something obscure, involved, ambiguous, and finally full of worry while we are shut up in the blind prison of the body, Plato denies that we can be happy unless we have returned, free, into our own nature after being released from earthly chains. For where there is no clear knowledge of things nor rest for the mind, how shall the highest good be found there?

<margin>The Good is placed in one God</margin>
Little by little, unless I am mistaken, the matter has been brought to this point: that it is proven that the highest good must be placed in one God. Even if this is clear and manifest to those looking more sharply, nevertheless the Platonists were accustomed to prove it with many and most powerful arguments.

<margin>Plato and Aristotle!</margin>
But since I notice that Baptista <note>Leon Battista Alberti.</note> is now proceeding on the path he began, and will a little later leave the philosophers to approach the Christian theologians, I shall omit all of those arguments. For whatever he applies from their reasoning to explain the ends of goods, all of those things have flowed from this one fountain. For I think the opinion and the approval of that opinion regarding this kind of matter is almost the same for both the Platonists and the Christians. For who among all the Greeks felt more divinely concerning the divine power? For this reason, while they everywhere gave him the title <term>thion</term> <note>Original Greek: *theion* (θεῖον), meaning "divine."</note>, they call Aristotle not <term>thion</term> but <term>daemonion</term> <note>Original Greek: *daimonion* (δαιμόνιον), meaning "supernatural" or "spirit-like." Ficino is contrasting the "divine" Plato with the "brilliant but earthly" Aristotle.</note>, because he was most sharply occupied in the study of physics. Moreover, Saint Augustine says he chose the Platonists above all others for this primary reason: that he judged them to be more illustrious than the others the more rightly they thought about God. Therefore, our souls will only then be most happy when, free from all corporeal contagion, they behold God alone.

<margin>CPS</margin>
Wherefore, when he spoke in that book titled the <term>Phaedrus</term> <note>One of Plato’s famous dialogues, which uses the allegory of a winged chariot to describe the soul’s journey.</note> concerning the blessed life which souls enjoy in the heavens, he affirms that they are nourished by the contemplation of truth, from which they perceive a certain incredible joy. He thinks that truth is like a fertile and rich field, and if it is well cultivated, speculation itself bears the best fruits. From these two, he says, the joy by which the souls are nourished flows continuously. For he says there is much effort to behold where the "Field of Truth" is. For the suitable food of the soul arises from that place, and the nature of the wings by which the soul is raised up is nourished by this. For he thinks our souls return to the heavens by a double kind of virtues—both those which consist in acting and those in investigating—as if by two wings. To these souls, once they have returned, two rewards are given: one is the contemplation of truth like the sweetest ambrosia, and the other is a joy perfected in all its parts, by which we are drenched as if by nectar. For his words in that same book, unless our memory fails us, are of this kind:

<margin>Plato.</margin>
> But when the charioteer has returned, bringing the team to the manger, 
> he throws ambrosia before the horses and gives them nectar to drink.

Do you see how, on the authority of such a great philosopher, our souls will finally be happy only when they return to heaven...

<summary>Marsilio Ficino argues that the "Highest Good" can only be found in the contemplation of God, asserting that the soul remains restless until it escapes the "prison" of the body. He aligns Platonic philosophy with Christian theology, using the allegory of the soul's wings and the "Field of Truth" from Plato's Phaedrus.</summary>
<keywords>Highest Good, Summum Bonum, Plato, Aristotle, St. Augustine, Phaedrus, contemplation, divinity, virtue, immortality of the soul</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s exploration of the "Highest Good," moving from the necessity of God as the ultimate source of happiness to a technical debate on whether this happiness consists in the "vision" (intellect) or the "joy" (will) of God.</meta>

# Concerning the Highest Good

<margin><unclear>Object</unclear></margin>
In these things, it will be permitted to enjoy God: yet not to enjoy Him in the same way the soul enjoys itself and its own body, which is joined to it not only by physical location but by its very essence. Nor, again, does it enjoy Him as a friend enjoys a friend. For although in true friendship the highest and perpetual concord flourishes, and the same will appears in both, nevertheless the friends are not the same person, nor are they circumscribed within the same place. The soul, however, adheres to God in such a way that, although it is not of the same essence, it is nevertheless separated from Him by no interval. <note>Ficino is describing a "mystical union" where the soul and God remain distinct in their being (essence) but are so perfectly joined in experience that there is no "space" or distance between them.</note> For this reason, just as the eye enjoys light—by which it is permitted to perceive all things—and enjoys it in such a way that, although the eye is something other than the light, it is separated from it by no gap; so too our souls enjoy God, always clinging to Him. But since we both see God and, at the same time, perceive from this kind of vision (if I may call it that) that joy which I said a little while ago was the greatest of all, many have doubted which of these contributes more to the <term>blessed life</term> <note>original: "uitam beatam"; a technical term for the state of ultimate happiness or salvation</note>. However, it pleases the more excellent <term>Platonists</term> <note>followers of Plato's philosophy, particularly those in Ficino’s circle who blended it with Christian theology</note> that vision exceeds joy. For who doubts that the ultimate end always corresponds to the "first mover"? Who, again, does not see that the first moving force in the soul is the intelligence? For it is by the intelligence that the <term>will</term> <note>original: "uoluntas"; the faculty of choice and desire</note> is moved. Therefore, the <term>highest good</term> <note>original: "summum bonum"</note> will consist in the intellect rather than in joy.

<margin><unclear>Note that</unclear></margin>
Added to this is the fact that when the highest good of man is sought, it should be placed in that part of the soul which we have in common with the incorporeal and divine minds, rather than in that part which makes us almost equal to the other living creatures. Now, <term>reason</term> <note>original: "ratio"</note> renders us most similar to the angels, whereas in our appetite we differ not at all from the beasts. Therefore, the highest good must be placed not in the will, but in reason. Add this also, if you please: since every motion is toward a good, and since our nature is carried toward a future good, it should find rest in that which is already present and which it has obtained, as if having found its proper place. Just as motion relates to a future good, so rest relates to that which is already present. However, we do not call something "good" simply because our nature is carried toward it.

<margin><unclear>Note</unclear></margin>
But on the contrary, nature is carried toward it because it is already established that the thing is good. For neither the object, nor the end, nor the mover depends on the power that is being moved; rather, that power depends on the mover. The same reasoning applies to what is present. For who would be of such dull judgment as to conclude: "This is good because one finds rest in it"? Rather, one should say: "Because right reason has already decreed this to be good, therefore the mind itself rests in it peacefully and with pleasure." Fire by its own nature flies upward; having reached that region, it rests, because it has attained its own good. It is not its good because it is carried toward it; rather, because it is its good, its nature is therefore moved to attain it. All these things tend toward this: that it may be concluded that neither rest itself, nor what you might prefer to call joy or pleasure, is the highest good; but rather it is something else which, because it is good, moves the soul to claim it for itself, and finally, when it has been reached, provides rest to the soul. The Platonists dispute concerning the highest good in roughly this form, though with far more arguments. But since some are prone to doubt—since the whole matter is placed in such a sublime citadel—whether mortals can ascend to such great heights, that same <term>Plato</term> makes this entire question very easy. For he proves that nothing is more consistent with nature than for all things perfected by it to pour themselves out and to make others participants in themselves, as far as they are able to receive it. For water does not turn air into itself so that the air might perish. <note>Ficino uses this analogy to show that God (the perfect) naturally shares His nature with us, and this union completes rather than destroys our human nature.</note>

<summary>Ficino argues that the "Highest Good" is found in the intellectual vision of God rather than the resulting joy, because reason is what distinguishes humans from animals and aligns them with the divine. He asserts that we desire God because He is inherently good, rather than He being good simply because we desire Him.</summary>
<keywords>Highest Good, Platonism, Intellect vs Will, Vision of God, Reason, Marsilio Ficino, Divine Union, Theology</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue on the Highest Good, transitioning from a philosophical argument about God’s nature as the ultimate source of goodness to a specific debate within Christian scholasticism regarding whether happiness consists in the intellect or the will.</meta>

<header>Book Two</header>

# Book Two

For Nature would not be the best, as everyone admits, but rather the most wicked, if she were to plan the destruction of things. Her goal is not to destroy, but to draw all things toward herself and take the form of water <note>This likely refers to the "fluidity" or generative capacity of nature, or a specific cosmological metaphor common in Neoplatonism.</note>. Since it is the property of nature to spread herself as widely as possible and fill everything with herself, it will certainly be the duty of a good person to follow nature as a guide and make others participate in what they possess. <margin>Aristotle</margin> For this reason, Aristotle rightly, when he had praised the "good," considered that while it is fine when it happens to even one person, it is more beautiful and divine when it reaches a whole city, or rather, the human race. Indeed, what shows this to us more clearly than the gifts of the virtues themselves? For what else does **Prudence** want but to provide for as many as possible? What does **Justice** demand, but that everything be shared for common use? Does not **Fortitude** exhort us to certain death for the safety of others? Finally, does not **Temperance** deter us from using lust to the injury of anyone?

Conclude, therefore: what nature intends most must be considered most "according to nature." Since nature desires to make her power available to others, this will be most suited to nature; if you grant this, you will also grant that since the first duty of a good person is to follow nature—which never errs—it is primarily the duty of a good person to offer themselves widely to all. If this is characteristic of the good, it will be more characteristic of the "better," and most of all of the "best"; and **God** is the best of all. 

You see now what follows: moreover, whatever qualities one shows to others, one must possess those qualities in oneself. For you can never give to another what you do not have yourself. God gave this desire to everyone to pour themselves out for others; therefore, He must have it in Himself, and all the more so because He excels all other things He has created. Since He excels infinitely, this "disposition" <note>original: "condictio"; referring to God's nature or state of being</note> (if I may call it that) in Him is infinite. 

These were the points that—since neither the weakness of my mind nor the shortness of time allowed for a broader and more extensive discussion—seemed to me to be touched upon rather than fully explained. Therefore, since many things were omitted and left behind by me, and you see that even these things I have said were narrated more meagerly and obscurely than the dignity of the subject and your most patient ears, O **Lorenzo**, required. If you think so, ask **Leo** <note>Likely a reference to a member of the circle or a potential authority; in the context of the Medici, this could be a nod to the future Pope Leo X.</note> not to allow you to lack anything in such a great matter. 

"On the contrary," said **Battista**, "these matters have been discussed by you both clearly and distinctly, **Marsilio**; and as far as a speech of this kind, woven from many diverse things, required, it has proceeded in its proper order. Therefore, unless Lorenzo thinks otherwise, let us return to the **Christians**." 

"Precisely that," said **Lorenzo**. "For Marsilio has satisfied me with his clear and transparent style of speaking, and has delighted me greatly with his brevity."

"The unanimous opinion of Christians, then," said Battista, "is that God is the ultimate and final end of all good things. However, a certain controversy arises there, <margin>Albert, Thomas --- Scotus, Ockham, Alexander, Nicholas</margin> because there are those who wish the 'ends of the good' to consist in divine <term>speculation</term> <note>original: "speculatione"; in this context, the intellectual "vision" or contemplation of God.</note>. It is easy to see that their leaders are **Albert**, surnamed 'the Great,' and **Thomas Aquinas**. There are likewise those who wish the matter to be completed not in speculation, but in the **love** that is perceived from it. 

Moreover, **Scotus** (whom they call 'the Subtle') and **Ockham**, likewise **Alexander** [of Hales] and **Nicholas of Lyra**, think we are made <term>blessed</term> <note>original: "beatos"; referring to "beatitude," the state of eternal happiness in God.</note> by that joy which comes to us from the perception of truth. But lest such a noble matter be reported by us perhaps too meagerly, I will repeat certain things from a bit deeper back, but briefly, so that the matter appears clearly enough, and yet the boredom of a longer dispute is avoided. First, therefore, they affirm that God is not only good..."

<summary>Marsilio Ficino argues that nature’s goodness compels the sharing of gifts, a trait perfected in God. The dialogue then shifts to a Scholastic debate over whether human ultimate happiness (beatitude) is found in the intellect's vision of God or the will's love and joy.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Lorenzo de' Medici, Battista Alberti, Thomas Aquinas, Albertus Magnus, Duns Scotus, William of Ockham, beatitude, divine speculation, virtues, highest good</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry from the previous page, moving from the general duty of a good person to follow nature, to a specific ontological argument: that God is the "Good of every good" and the only being who is good by His own essence.</meta>

-># On the Highest Good<-

Truly, He is also the good of every good. They prove this by the following reasoning, if I am not mistaken: the perfection of each thing can be called nothing other than its **goodness**. For if you should ask what virtue itself is, I would answer correctly, I think, that <term>virtue</term> <note>original: *virtus*; in this context, it refers to the "excellence" or "functional perfection" of a thing, not just moral character</note> is that by which the thing in which it resides is rendered complete and perfect. For who is unaware that the virtue of a horse consists in this: that it makes the horse good and ensures it performs its work perfectly? Therefore, the virtue of a horse will be the perfection and completion of the horse. For we call that "perfect" and "complete" which has already attained its own proper virtue.

For this reason, whatever we call "good," we consider worthy of that name precisely because it is complete <term>in all its parts</term> <note>original: *per omnes suos numeros*; a Latin idiom meaning "in every respect" or "perfectly"</note>. Since God, however, is so perfect that by His own perfection He encompasses the perfections of all things, He will also encompass all other "goodnesses" (if I may speak this way) by His own goodness. Therefore, He is the good of every good.

Furthermore, when we say that some thing is of such a quality, we say it (if I am not mistaken) because it reflects some likeness of that which is such a quality by its very **essence**. I ask: what kind of wall is this? You will answer: "white." Why so? Surely because it possesses something similar to that essence which we call "whiteness." Once you have granted this, you must also grant that God alone is He who is good by His own **essence**; other things, truly, are called good not by their own essence, but insofar as they reflect some likeness of the divine essence. Therefore, if we conclude this point truly, we shall call nothing at all among all things "good" except by the fact that it possesses something similar to the divine goodness. God, then, will be the good of every good.

To this is added another point: if each thing is worthy of being sought for no other reason than its **end**, and if the definition of "the good" is contained solely in that it is worthy of being sought, then whatever is called good is called so either because it is an end, or because it is directed toward a established end. If it happens that He is not only an end, but the *ultimate* end, He will surely be the one from whom all other things receive the character of being good. That this is God, I shall show shortly. God, therefore, is the good of every good.

For this reason, when God promised He would show Himself to Moses, He said: <margin>AA</margin> "I will show you all good" <note>Exodus 33:19. The author uses this to bridge scripture and Greek logic.</note>. If these things are accepted by you as true, I shall show by the same reasoning that He is the **Highest Good**. For the good that extends itself to the entire universe surpasses the good which reaches only some single part. It has already been demonstrated that God is such a good. Therefore, God is the highest good.

Add to this also that what is said to be something by its essence is said more truly than if it is said to be so not by its essence but by a certain (so to speak) <term>participation</term> <note>a central Platonic and Scholastic concept where created things "participate" in or share a portion of a quality that God possesses fully</note>. You will find nothing, once you have departed from God, that can be called good by its own essence.

I will say one thing more: for that which is greatest in any category is rightly said to be the cause of the others which are lesser in that same category. For the cause must be more powerful than that which is produced by the cause. But who would deny—as has been proved just now without difficulty by clear arguments—who, I say, would deny that all things, whatever they are, derive their character of being "good" from God?

I have now led our discussion to the point where it easily appears that God is the highest good for man. Therefore, although it seemed that more things should be explained in this same matter through a continuous thread of disputation, it pleases me now—provided you are willing—to return by a brief digression to those things which, by the popular opinion of various men, have occupied the place of the "highest good." <margin>AA</margin> Once these have been refuted and driven out by <term>legal challenge</term> <note>original: *manum ex iure conferendo*; a technical term from Roman law regarding a formal claim to property</note> from the possession which they had claimed for themselves not by right, but had invaded through the greatest injustice, we shall resume the discussion from which I now digress.

<summary>This page argues that God is the "Good of every good" and the only being whose essence is goodness itself, using analogies of a horse's virtue and a wall's whiteness. It concludes by proposing a digression to refute common, mistaken beliefs about what constitutes the "highest good."</summary>
<keywords>Highest Good, divine essence, virtue, perfection, Moses, participation, ontological argument, causality</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the "Highest Good." Having established in the previous section that God is the source of all goodness, the author now turns to the common classification of "goods" (body, fortune, and soul) to demonstrate why physical pleasure and external status cannot be considered the true Good.</meta>

->## Book Two<-

TRULY, lest there perhaps seem to be more goods than there actually are, I shall use that division which is on everyone's lips. For all things that have been called "goods" are said to be either **goods of the body**, such as strength, health, pleasure, and beauty; or **goods of fortune**, since they are placed in her randomness—in which number are held nobility, riches, honors, and glory; or finally, **goods of the soul**, which is virtue itself. Therefore, in refuting the first two kinds, we have not only Christian theologians to follow, but every serious philosopher fights on our side. For if we begin with pleasure—by whose allurements the senses are so soothed that judgment is corrupted in most people—what school would you find in philosophy (except for a few, and those not of the <term>higher ranks</term> <note>original: *maiorum gentium*; a Roman idiom referring to the most ancient and noble families of gods or senators, here applied to philosophical schools</note> but entirely <term>plebeian</term> <note>original: *plebeia*; meaning common or low-class schools like the Epicureans</note>) that does not declare war against it as if against a most destructive plague? <margin>pleasure</margin> With what thunderbolts does **Plato** cast her down, like some second child of the Titans, into the depths of Tartarus! On his lips was always that saying: we must remember that pleasures are brief and leave nothing behind but regret; nor can something be a "good" when it is held in common with the beasts, and happens to the wicked more than to the upright; for a "good" must be stable, whereas pleasure is fluid and in constant motion. He adds to this that one good never hinders another good. Yet there is nothing that stands in the way of the virtues more than pleasure. For it brings the greatest loss to men, giving birth to ignorance, restlessness, folly, and insolence. But whom will you find so lost that they would think pleasure worthy of any praise or veneration? No one was ever so dedicated to pleasures, nor loved them so much, that they did not use every industry to hide them. But why is this? Because even in the judgment of <term>profligates</term> <note>original: *asotarum*; refers to those who are hopelessly dissipated or "beyond saving"</note> they are shameful and disgraceful. But you will find nothing more praiseworthy or more honorable than the "good"; therefore, pleasures are not the good. For that which is "good" extends by its very nature to the common benefit of the human race—as **Marsilio** <note>likely referring to the Renaissance philosopher Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), who sought to harmonize Platonism with Christianity</note> showed a little earlier to be true regarding the virtues. Pleasure, however, applauding only itself and the senses, wishes to undergo no inconvenience whatsoever for the sake of other men.

But I ask you, can we rightly call anything a "good" for us which is neither implanted in our nature nor produced by our own industry? Which of these, then, will you say happens to pleasure? Certainly neither. For man is nothing else—if you believe anything of the arguments on which we rely—man, I say, on the authority of **Plato**, is nothing besides **mind and reason**. You ask, what of <margin>good / Body / Senses</margin> the body? It is the prison and the chain of the soul. Its senses are the most cruel guards; with these ambushes they seek us, and they stretch out the snares by which the immortal god [within us] is entangled, preventing our return to heaven, our ancient fatherland. Pleasure, indeed, is neither acquired by our industry nor does it proceed from reason; rather, having arisen from the weakness of the senses (which I showed just now to be our most savage enemies), it always opposes the highest good. And since it is the primary quality of a "good" that it wishes the thing to which it belongs to be preserved whole and safe in its own nature—for we see the virtues, situated in our character, keep our souls pure from every harmful thing and raise them toward certain greater and more divine things—this is exactly what the virtues, placed in the knowledge of truth, provide. Pleasure harms both. Therefore, pleasure is to be placed in no category of "goods." Most wisely, **Socrates** showed that the nature of goods and evils is so contrary that we slide easily into evil. <margin>Terence</margin> For as that character Simo in **Terence** says: *It is the nature of all men to be inclined away from labor and toward lust.* The path of virtue, however, is difficult, arduous, hidden, and full of labors.

<vocab>pleasure, virtue, Plato, Marsilio, Socrates, Terence, body, soul, good</vocab>

<summary>This page argues against the classification of physical pleasure as a "good," citing Plato and Socrates to define the body as a prison and pleasure as a temporary, shameful distraction from the soul's true rational nature. It contrasts the fleeting, selfish nature of pleasure with the stable, communal, and arduous nature of virtue.</summary>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical refutation of physical pleasure as the "Highest Good." While the previous page established the instability of pleasure, the author here uses the internal conflicts of vices and the biological purpose of pleasure to further his argument, drawing on Plato, Aristotle, and the Stoic concept of temperance.</meta>

# On the Highest Good

Therefore, good would be entirely extinguished by evil, unless there were a remedy for such a destructive plague. For evils are always at odds with one another; they never unite and never agree. For fear persuades one way, while rashness and audacity persuade another. What the **spendthrift** pursues, the **miser** utterly abhors. However, there is the highest harmony among all virtues: for where **prudence** leads, **justice** encourages, **fortitude** impels, and **temperance** persuades.

Evils, therefore, are dissonant; goods are consonant. In which category, then, will you place **pleasures**? You will find nothing more prone to internal conflict. Add to this: causes and their effects ought to be related; but from pleasures arise many disturbances which, like <term>instruments of torture</term> <note>original: *carnicifinæ*; literally "flesh-makers" or executioners, used here to describe agonizing mental distress</note>, continually rack and consume the soul. Therefore, pleasures are not good. On the contrary, they are the worst. For since they violently corrupt judgment, they give birth to **ignorance**; and no one should be considered more unlearned than he who does not realize that ignorance is the origin of all evils. For men ensnared by these pleasures remain fixed as if by a nail. Nor, blinded by such great darkness, are they able to escape what they know to be evil. <margin>~</margin> This is what the raging **Medea** of **Ovid** seeks not without wit: for she grieves when she "sees and approves the better, yet is forced to follow the worse" <note>This refers to a famous line from Ovid’s *Metamorphoses* (7.20-21) where Medea struggles between her reason and her passion for Jason</note>.

These, then, are a few of the many and very strong arguments of **Plato** that seemed worth collecting at this time. But the **Aristotelians** and many **Christian theologians** argue thus: first, they propose that pleasure was invented by nature not for its own sake, but for the sake of the actions from which the pleasure arises. The thirsty man drinks with pleasure; the hungry man feasts with pleasure. Nor is the work of procreation <note>original: *liberis opera*; literally "work for children"</note> given without pleasure. This is lest, if pleasure were removed from either those first needs or this last one, we might become too negligent and perish ourselves, leaving no successor to follow us.

Therefore, they argue: if the action—for whose sake nature instituted pleasure—is sought not for itself but for something else, much less will that pleasure be sought for its own sake. But feasts and cups, and similarly the performance of sexual acts, were instituted by nature not for themselves, but for something else. For the one was devised so that the human race might be preserved; the other so that the one who feasts might remain healthy. Therefore, pleasure is not sought for itself; therefore it is not the **Highest Good**.

Add also this: when we seek the "end of goods" <note>The *finis bonorum*, or the ultimate goal of human life</note> for man, it must surely be placed in that which belongs to man in such a way that it does not belong to other living creatures. But pleasure is common to all things that possess sensation. Truly, I think we call that the "Highest Good" which is entirely the most perfect and excellent thing in the nature of man. If this is so, it should by no means be placed in that which joins us to lower and far inferior beings; since the "end" must, without any controversy, be more excellent than the thing which exists for the sake of that end.

But who does not know that pleasures come from the senses? Who likewise does not know that we are joined to the cattle by our senses? Truly, hear something that could move even a stupid person: that which is not "good" unless it is bounded by certain limits of **temperance** is not good by its own nature; rather, it receives its "goodness" from that which moderates it. No one approves of pleasures unless they are moderate. Therefore, though **Epicurus** grants the primacy to pleasure, he does not dare to call it the highest good unless all the virtues assist them like <term>handmaidens and servants</term> <note>original: *pedissequæ ministræq;*; terms for low-ranking female attendants</note> and, acting as fine teachers of living, cut away certain infinite desires that are neither necessary nor natural. For he saw that if pleasures proceeded too licentiously...

<margin>M.</margin>

<summary>This page argues that pleasure cannot be the Highest Good because it is a biological tool for survival rather than an end in itself, and it requires the supervision of virtue to be considered 'good' at all.</summary>
<keywords>Highest Good, Plato, Aristotle, Epicurus, Medea, Ovid, temperance, biological purpose, virtue, pleasure vs. pain</keywords>
