<meta>This page continues a dialogue between Renaissance intellectuals regarding the nature of the "Highest Good." Having previously argued that physical pleasure is unstable and leads to ruin, the speakers now transition from refuting "Pleasure" to refuting "Honor" as the ultimate purpose of life.</meta>

# Book Two

...nor would there be any limit; the result would be that they would lead us into diseases, into the greatest losses, into the greatest disgrace, into the gravest dangers, and finally into destruction. Therefore, **pleasures** are not a good by their own nature. But the **highest good** <note>original: *summum bonum*</note> ought to be of such a kind: for that which is good of its own accord far surpasses all other goods which are not good through themselves, but through something else. But what do you think, **Lorenzo**? Do you not think that what has been said so far has enough strength to refute pleasures?

"Enough indeed," said **Lorenzo**. "For nothing is more powerful than those arguments for defeating the opinion of the **Cyrenaics** <note>An ancient Greek school of philosophy that taught that immediate physical pleasure is the highest good.</note> and the **Epicureans** <note>Followers of Epicurus who sought "ataraxia" (freedom from fear and pain), often misinterpreted by later critics as simple hedonism.</note>. Moreover, I recently read through **Marsilio’s** <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), a central figure of the Italian Renaissance who translated Plato into Latin.</note> book on pleasure, which he wrote while still a young man to **Antonio Canisiano**; in which (unless I am mistaken) there is nothing missing from those things which are usually brought together for this dispute."

<margin>Petrarch: Wise thoughts in youth.</margin> <note>original: *pensier can giovenil est*; a reference to Petrarch’s idea that wisdom—"gray hair of the mind"—can exist in a young person.</note>

"Indeed," said **Antonio**, "it was written in his youth but with ancient learning and elderly wisdom. For in the manner of the **Academics** <note>Followers of the "New Academy," a skeptical school of philosophy that practiced arguing both sides of any issue to find the truth.</note>, the question is proposed and then argued so sharply on both sides that, with the standards of battle joined, a most fierce fight is waged between the patrons of pleasure and the rest of the philosophers who disagree with them. And although at times, for the **Platonists**, the **Aristotelians**, and all those who support the **Porch** <note>A reference to the Stoics, who met at the *Stoa Poikile* or "Painted Porch" in Athens.</note>, matters seem to fall back to the <term>veterans</term> <note>original: *triarios*; the *triarii* were the elite third line of the Roman legion, used only as a last resort in desperate situations.</note>, in the end they throw the enemy’s ranks into confusion and finally force them to move from their position and turn their backs."

"Therefore," said **Battista**, "it is not necessary to draw up the battle line again against those already defeated. But with pleasure routed in a skirmish, we shall overcome **honors**. For who does not understand that something which is good for the sake of something else, and sought for the sake of something else, cannot be the highest good? Truly, as for honor—since everyone admits it is the reward of virtue and owed only to the good—no one seeks it for itself. But whether men are good or evil, they nonetheless wish to be called and considered good. Because they wish this, they marvelously rejoice and are glad to attain honors, so that they may produce them as if they were witnesses of their own virtues. This is indeed the reason why we especially desire to be treated with honor by those who are of some account and are considered wise. For this reason, **Naevius’s Hector** does not rejoice in being praised, but 'in being praised by a praised man.' <note>A famous quote from the Roman playwright Naevius: *Laetus sum laudari me abs te, pater, a laudato viro*.</note>

Add to this that it is likely the highest good was so established by nature that it is placed within **our own power**. For nothing could be imagined more wretched than man, or more envious than nature, if we were produced in such a way that, even when we strive for it with all our strength, we are not allowed to be happy. But no one doubts that honors are placed in the judgment of those who are going to bestow them, not in those who are going to receive them. But remember this also: that which is called the highest good must certainly be most absolute and accumulated from all its parts; therefore, it admits no evil. But to a man who has no evil present in him, a "bad" man cannot exist. Yet very often, the more wicked a person is, the greater the honor with which he is treated. For how many **tyrants** do we see—when they have imposed the harshest yoke of slavery on the necks of their fatherland—honored with those titles and those insignias which are owed to the authors of liberty? How many citizens have attained those magistracies through the most turbulent seditions, when they would have deserved to be affected by the highest punishment if the laws had not been oppressed by arms? For how did **Sulla**, or how did **Marius** deserve so well of the republic that the one, after his sixth consulship (in which he had exercised every kind of cruelty against the citizens and every impiety against the fatherland), should even attain a seventh? While the other, truly, when he had raved like a most monstrous beast against every rank...

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the "Highest Good." Having previously dismissed physical pleasure as the ultimate goal of life, the speaker now examines and rejects social honors, political power, wealth, and physical health, moving toward a discussion of the virtues of the soul.</meta>

# On the Highest Good

...a dictator might be created. I would be too long-winded if I were to now recount all those leaders whom they called **Caesars**; who, although they had not only cast aside all humanity, but had even been transformed into certain most monstrous and hitherto unheard-of portents by some accursed **Circe** <note>In Greek mythology, the enchantress Circe turned men into animals; here used as a metaphor for how absolute power corrupts human nature into something bestial.</note>, nevertheless attained divine honors in addition to human ones. By these examples, it is easy to see how far **honors** are from that which we seek. 

<margin>Glory.</margin> Because if honor is not the **highest good** <note>original: *summum bonum*</note>, then **glory** will not be the highest good either. For glory is frequent fame accompanied by praise. Why, then, do we desire to move in celebrated fame—that is, to be known by as many as possible, or rather by everyone if it can be done—unless to attain honors? But men do not see that it is more worthy of praise to **know** than to **be known**. For nothing holds so low and humble a rank among things—whether it be animate or inanimate—that it cannot be known. To know, however, belongs only to a more noble kind of living being. Therefore, glory will not be the highest good, especially since we do not seek it for its own sake, but so that we may obtain praise from it. For in those things that lack praise, no one wishes to stand out, but rather to remain hidden. 

<margin>Riches.</margin> If these things are not "goods," in what way shall we call **wealth** a good? For unless you put it to use, what reason is there for it to be desired? Therefore, it is sought not for itself, but for its use. Furthermore, the nature of the highest good requires that it be preserved as much as possible and not permitted to slip away. But as for wealth, unless it is divided for your own use, the use of your family, and for generosity toward others with a certain careful choice, I do not see what it contributes. Nor is there any reason why we should labor greatly at this time to refute either wealth in the state or kingdoms and empires; 

<margin><unclear>in .</unclear></margin> which indeed, placed in the recklessness of **Fortune** <note>The Roman goddess Fortuna, representing the unpredictable nature of luck and fate.</note>, are always being tossed about. They are acquired through sedition, fraud, and slaughter, and are snatched away by those same arts continually. But suppose they are acquired justly and administered justly: are they not full of anxiety, labors, and dangers? So much so that no one of sound mind would fail to recognize that it is much sweeter to be well-governed than to govern well. 

<margin>Goods of the body.</margin> There remain what are called the **goods of the body**, such as health, strength, speed, beauty, and similar things found among them. Since these are momentary and liable to many calamities, I cannot understand how they could possess anything solid in which that **tranquility** <note>original: *tranquillitas*; the state of mental calm prized by ancient philosophers.</note> we seek might properly reside. Indeed, is it not true that whatever strength or dignity the body has, it borrows it all from the **soul**? You will think, therefore, that the goods of the mind should be preferred to them. But by what reason can these bodily traits be called "ours," which are not only common to us and other living creatures, but in which we are surpassed by many individual animals? The **eagle** sees more sharply; the same power is in the **lynx**. The **boar** hears further. The **dog** smells more keenly; the **vulture** more keenly still. For he who does not know that he is surpassed by many beasts in strength, speed, and physical stature surely does not know himself. When you reflect on all these things more carefully with yourself, there is nothing you would think is further from the highest good. 

But let us now come to the **goods of the soul**—which was the third class established by us—now that the others have been refuted. We shall clearly see what these are if we demonstrate that the power of the human mind is threefold. For there is a power by which it **makes**; there is likewise one by which it **acts**; and finally, there is one by which it **contemplates**. In the making of things, therefore, **art** <note>original: *ars*; here referring to technical skill or craftsmanship.</note> is employed. For that is the "right reason of things to be made." In our actions, however, **prudence** <note>original: *prudentia*; practical wisdom in conduct.</note> is the leader, from which **justice**, **fortitude**, and **temperance** learn their duties. But in the investigation of truth, if we seek the principles...

</margin>

He transitions to the "goods of the soul," dividing human mental power into making, acting, and contemplating.</summary>

<meta>Continuing the dialogue from the previous page, the speakers have moved from dismissing external goods like wealth and honors to examining whether intellectual skills and moral virtues constitute the ultimate goal of human life.</meta>

## Book Two

<sig>c ii</sig>

<margin>
3 Aristotle
5 Virgil
</margin>

Understanding is our guide. But if those things flow from the principles of science, they are knowledge. Finally, **wisdom**, even if it embraces both, is nevertheless a third kind of thing. If, therefore, we consider **art** <note>In this context, *ars* refers to any skilled craft or technical knowledge, not just fine arts.</note>, it will by no means be the ultimate end. For how can something be the end when its knowledge is directed toward a further goal? We say the "end" of an art is the work produced by that art. For the end of the art possessed by the architect **Daedalus** <note>The mythical Greek inventor famous for building the Labyrinth of Crete.</note> is the **labyrinth**. I do not understand how works of this kind can be the ends of our lives, since all those things are manufactured for various uses in human life; nature did not produce man for the sake of these objects, but rather the opposite. 

Nevertheless, you will not find the "highest good" even in those virtues of the soul that pertain to life and character. For if virtues of this kind were the ends, they would surely be sought for their own sake and would not be established for any other purpose. This is far from the case. Take the most prominent among them, and from there you may judge the rest. Do you desire **fortitude**—specifically that which is practiced in war and on the battle-line? Do men seek it for its own sake? Ask, I pray, our <term>Federico of Montefeltro</term> <note>original: *feretranum nostrum Federicum*; Federico da Montefeltro (1422–1482) was the Duke of Urbino, a famous condottiero (mercenary leader) and a great patron of the arts.</note>; ask the commanders of his wings; ask whoever leads the ranks in his army; ask, finally, every individual horseman and foot-soldier whom he leads into battle. No one will say he seeks fortitude because it is to be desired for itself, but rather so that they may obtain **victory** over the enemy—so that, having subdued them, they may live safely in leisure. 

What about **justice**? What about **temperance**? Do they not act so that we may live in peace with ourselves and with other men? It is manifest, therefore, that these are not sought for themselves, but so that they may return the soul to a state purged of all vice. But not even **prudence** can bring us the highest good, since it is not occupied with the investigation of truth, but with the management of civil actions. 

When <term>Baptista</term> <note>Leon Battista Alberti, the famous Renaissance polymath and architect who appears as a character in this dialogue.</note> had said these things, <term>Laurentius</term> <note>Lorenzo de' Medici ("The Magnificent"), the ruler of Florence and interlocutor in this debate.</note> said: "I have been listening to you most attentively for a long time now. For I see that you have not only refuted those things which have long been refuted by the most excellent philosophers, but you have also denied that we are made happy by those things which the most serious **Stoics** <note>An ancient Greek school of philosophy that taught that virtue is the only good and is sufficient for happiness.</note> placed not only as the highest good, but as the *only* good. Now, therefore, since you have overthrown the goods of fortune and of the body, and you do not even entrust so great a matter to those virtues concerning life and character, proceed, I beg you: explain at last where our mind may come to rest, as if at the final goal."

"Let the dispute concerning the <term>highest good</term> <note>original: *summum bonum*</note> be taken up again from where it diverged," said Baptista. "Indeed, since it is now established that **God** is the highest good, the remaining things will be more easily explained. And this first of all: since God is the highest good, what trouble is it to prove that He is also the highest good for man? It has long been demonstrated that whatever anyone does, they do it for the sake of some good. You will allow me, therefore, to 'act what has already been acted' <note>A Latin proverb meaning to repeat a point already settled or to do work already done.</note>."

"I will certainly allow it," said Laurentius.

"No one doubts," said Baptista, "that a good of this kind is the end of that thing. For everything that exists and acts tends toward its end by that very action. For we say that is the **end** by which, once acquired, the appetite is so fulfilled that it seeks nothing further. Every appetite is filled by 'the good.' Therefore, if it is correctly concluded that something is rightly called the 'end' if it is desired when absent, and enjoyed and rested in when present, it is easy to see that the end and the **perfection** of any thing are the same. For whatever lacks its perfection is carried toward it—either by the impulse of never-erring nature or by its own sense and reason—until it attains it; and once attained, it rests there of its own accord. I think I have <unclear>shown</unclear> clearly enough..."

<margin>
The highest good is in God.
The End.
</margin>

<summary>Baptista argues that neither technical skills nor moral virtues like fortitude and justice are the ultimate "highest good," as they are all means to other ends. He begins to demonstrate that God is the true final goal where human desire finds rest and perfection.</summary>

<meta>Continuing the discussion on the nature of the "highest good," the speakers now transition from earthly virtues to the theological concept of God as the ultimate end toward which all things strive. Baptista uses a medical analogy to explain how intermediate goals lead to a final purpose.</meta>

# On the Highest Good

->ii<-

...that everything seeks its own end, and that this end is also the good.
"Indeed, you have shown this," said **Laurentius**. "And from this, I clearly understand what you wish to conclude. For if the good and the end are the same, who does not see that the highest good is, above all, the ultimate end?"
"You conclude correctly," said **Baptista**. "But accept this as well: since there are many and varied ends in the same matter, it is necessary that all the others be referred to the one which is the final end. To make this appear more clearly, I will demonstrate it with a brief example.
**Philip the physician** <note>Philip of Acarnania, the personal physician and friend of Alexander the Great.</note> sets before himself the health of the ailing **Alexander**. This, then, will be like the ultimate goal to which all other things are referred: that the king may be well. Therefore, he reads the precepts handed down by **Hippocrates** <note>The famous ancient Greek physician, active in the 5th century BCE.</note>. But to what purpose? Surely, so that he may find which potion he should especially provide. Once found, it is given, but with the intent that the overflowing **bile** <term>bile</term> <note>original: *bilis*; in the ancient medical system of "humors," an excess of yellow bile was thought to cause "acute" diseases like fevers.</note> in the king may be purged. And for what reason is this done? Unless it is so that, by removing the bile, the heat of the fever may be extinguished.
Yet he does not even do this for its own sake, but rather so that Alexander's former health may return. When the physician has provided this, he has finally reached that which he sought for no other reason than itself, and in that he rests. Do you see how all the other lower ends proceed into this one as if into the ultimate?
By the same reasoning, then, the various and manifold good—which is channeled through many streams, as it were, by its own steps—flows back into **God**, the highest good, as if into the fountain and origin of all goods. It does not escape me that those who pursue these matters by disputing more precisely are accustomed to prove this point with many arguments. But in an extemporaneous discussion, we are not able to pursue everything.
However, I will not omit this:
¶ For since we establish God as the ultimate end of all things, it is of great importance how this is understood. For if we call God the ultimate end as if He were the first of all things that act, we shall not go astray. For other ends, because they are established through the action of that which acts, cannot be the first of all those that act; rather, they are that which is produced from it.

<margin>
The end is not produced, but pre-existing.
</margin>

For this reason, it is wrong to speak of Him as an end in the sense of something "constituted" or "established," but rather as something **pre-existing**. 
Furthermore, if anyone acts for the sake of something that already exists, so that something may be established by that action, it follows that something is acquired for the thing for which the action is performed. For **soldiers**, who fight fiercely at the command of their general to achieve victory, produce that victory for the general. But nothing is acquired for God by our efforts, nor by those of anyone else. Indeed, He is so absolute in every respect that nothing can be added to Him. 
For it is necessary that by the same reasoning an agent acts toward an end, the work itself obtains its end. But God—whom it is established is already the first of all things that act—does not act in such a way as to acquire anything for Himself by His action, but rather to bestow it upon others. 
From all these points, the place is truly concluded that all things that exist are carried toward God as if toward their own end—not, indeed, so that they add anything to God (which is impossible), but so that they may attain Him as the ultimate end of all things. 
If, therefore, whatever thing has been produced by God is rendered similar to God by the same reasoning through which it acquires divine goodness, it will be the final purpose of all things to render themselves similar to God. But since that which has the nature of an "end" must be good, each thing will strive to make itself similar to God specifically in that respect by which He is good. 
But to what purpose is all this? Surely, so that you may understand that if everything created flows back toward God as its origin, it is necessary that the **mind** also strive for this same truth in a certain more excellent way according to its powers. And that way is that it should **understand Him** through its own action. For by this path, the essence of God is reached in some manner.

<margin>
<unclear>illegible</unclear>
</margin>

It identifies God as the pre-existing source and end of all goods, toward which the human mind specifically strives through the act of understanding.</summary>

->c iii<-

# Book Two

...if anything of it falls into our knowledge. Indeed, there are those present who might prove what I have said with this sort of reasoning: Every being has its own end and its own proper action. To **understand** is the proper action of the human mind. You see what follows: if that which is most perfect exists in that action, it will be the ultimate end. 

Furthermore, since operations receive their character from their objects, the nobler the subject of the operation, the nobler the operation itself will be. Nothing among those things which come into the mind is nobler than God. Therefore, the **highest good** <term>summum bonum</term> <note>original: *summum bonum*; the ultimate goal or supreme good that provides the basis for ethics.</note> consists in the knowledge of God. 

But hear this as well—which I had shown a little while ago—that everything tends toward a divine likeness as if toward its own proper end. The human mind is made most like God through its intelligence. There is nothing among all things more greatly desired than the highest good. Yet the human mind is delighted by nothing so much as the knowledge of divine things. For although our little minds <note>original: *ingeniola nostra*; a diminutive form suggesting human intellectual humility.</note> are like **owls** <note>This is a famous reference to Aristotle’s *Metaphysics*, suggesting that as an owl is blinded by the sun, the human intellect is overwhelmed by the "light" of divine truth.</note> facing the sun when they are turned toward this light, nevertheless, that very bit of knowledge we possess, however small it may be, is valued much more highly by us than if we held a completely exact knowledge of all other things. For this we desire, this we love, and this we enjoy. 

Indeed, if even things lacking reason—and even sense—tend toward a likeness of God as their highest good, then surely the more a thing is made like God, the more it reaches its own end. But nothing in created things is more like God than the mind; therefore, through the mind, we are made like God. The mind reflects a certain likeness of God, not merely because it has the natural capacity to understand, but because it actually **understands**. 

<margin>Wisdom belongs to the blessed:</margin>
If you gather all these points correctly, they lead to this: that the highest good of man is the supreme action of the mind—that is, **wisdom** <term>sapientia</term>—by which one knows God as much as is permitted. 

Gradually and by its own steps, unless I err, our inquiry concerning the ends of goods has been led from a humble and lowly place up to heaven itself, where it can finally rest, having been placed in its own nature. For we leave **Aristippus**, **Eudoxus**, and **Epicurus** <note>These are ancient Greek philosophers associated with Hedonism, the belief that pleasure is the highest good.</note> prostrate at the very bottom, nor do we find **Hieronymus of Rhodes** <note>A Peripatetic philosopher who defined the highest good as "freedom from pain."</note> or the others much higher—those who loved either a lack of pain or the "first things of nature" without virtue. 

As for the **Peripatetics** <note>Followers of Aristotle.</note>, the **Stoics**, and the **Cynics**, I would not dare to say anything except what is honorable. For they handed down teachings which, if they cannot rescue a man from the many and greatest calamities to keep him from being miserable, nevertheless instruct us so that, bearing misery with an unconquered soul, even if we have not reached the summit, we have nonetheless progressed a long way. 

But let us also dismiss these men, since they are not strong enough to lead us where we desire to go. This can be done without danger, for we have now found the most certain guides. Following them, we have arrived at a point where we may rest in the "citadel of nature"—that is, in God Himself. 

Yet, because we do not only reach the divine essence by understanding, but we also desire and love it, and through loving it, we enjoy it—all of which are placed in the **will**—it has been rightly debated among theologians whether such a great thing should be placed in **knowledge** itself or rather in the **will**. 

<margin><unclear>Whether in</unclear> knowledge or rather in [will]</margin>
From this, a multiple and varied battle is stirred up, and it is fought on both sides with such strength that they seem at last to depart the field with the battle's outcome in doubt. But those who believe the matter is placed in the will, or in love, attempt to defend their cause in roughly this manner: Since the object of the will is the "good" (which we have shown to be the same as the "end"), we should say that the "truth" presented to the mind is the end not because it is true, but because it is **good**. Thus, it will now be ready for us to reach the final...

<summary>Baptista argues that the highest human good is the knowledge of God, comparing human intellect to an owl blinded by the sun's brilliance. He dismisses hedonistic philosophies and transitions into a Scholastic debate over whether ultimate happiness is found in the intellect (knowing God) or the will (loving God).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the "highest good." Having previously established that the human mind seeks a divine likeness, the text now explores the Scholastic debate over whether this ultimate fulfillment is found in the Will (voluntas) or the Intellect (intellectus).</meta>

-># On the Highest Good<-

<meta>Catchword: rit</meta>

<margin>
That the highest good resides in the will.
</margin>

...[that we] attain the ultimate end not through the industry of the mind, but through the labor of the **will**. Furthermore, we say that pleasure is that which most fully perfects and completes an operation, just as we say a discovery is perfected by its beauty. Now, a completed operation is rightly called an "end"; and we attain it more because we **will** it than because we understand it. They also add this: the ultimate end is sought by a certain silent consensus of all people, rather than by some learned doctrine. Therefore, it is sought with nature as our guide. Since more people desire pleasure than wisdom, the end seems better called **pleasure** <note>original: *voluptas*; here used in the philosophical sense of the satisfaction or "delight" found in reaching a goal, rather than mere physical indulgence.</note> rather than knowledge.

Likewise, no one doubts that among all the powers of the soul, that one is most likely the end which is the most excellent and outstanding of all. That the **will** is more excellent is most apparent from this: that by its work, the intellect is moved toward its end. For then at last the intellect considers in actual practice those things which it had previously held only as a potentiality, once it has already desired something. Therefore, the **will is nobler**. Likewise, everyone confesses that the highest good must be placed in that thing by whose goodness the whole human being is said to be "simply good." And it is by the goodness [of the will] that a person is called simply good. Added to this is the fact that the highest good seems to consist mostly in our **enjoyment** of that highest good itself.

<margin>
Augustine says...
</margin>

Wherefore **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose writings heavily influenced how medieval thinkers viewed the relationship between love/will and knowledge.</note>, in the book he titled *On Christian Religion*, says that the highest reward is that we should enjoy God. Now, "to enjoy" is said to occur not because we understand, but because we **will**. Those who grant the primacy of the highest good to the will rely on these sorts of reasons and many more; and those who attribute it to **knowledge** bring forth their own arguments against them:

<margin>
On natural and rational matters.
</margin>

But first this: since we are seeking the "end of goods" for that form which is the mind, we shall certainly think it to be that which is most **proper** <note>original: *proprium*; meaning a characteristic that is unique to a specific species and defines its nature.</note> to it. By what reasoning shall we say that **desire** <term>appetitus</term> <note>original: *appetitus*; a broad term in scholastic philosophy for the natural tendency of a thing toward its perfection or good.</note> is proper to our mind, since the opinion of all learned men is now established that desire exists in all things—not in one certain way, but in various ways—seeing as not all things know in the same way? For those things which completely lack knowledge are said to be carried by that desire which is called **natural**. By this, all heavy things are carried downward and light things upward. But in those things that use senses, desire arises from the senses alone. For by this, every living creature both adopts what is healthy for it and flees what is harmful. However, in those beings where reason also flourishes besides sense, desire obeys reason (unless it wishes to be wicked); and this we call the **will**. 

Since the **will** is a certain kind of desire, how can it be "proper" to the human mind? The **intellect**, however, is by its very nature proper to the human mind; therefore, the highest good of the human mind is in the **intellect**. Furthermore, among all the powers of the soul which are moved by the objects presented to them, these objects must be prior by nature to any action of such powers. A body flecked with color is presented to the eye; therefore, such a color is prior to the vision produced in the eye. For the mover is prior by nature to the thing that is to be moved. The will is a power of this kind. For that which is worthy of being sought moves the desire; therefore, that which is presented to the will is prior to the action of the will. If you grant this, you must also grant that the act of the will cannot be that which we first desire. But that which we first desire is the **highest good**. Therefore, the will shall not be the highest good. Likewise, in all powers that can reflect upon their own acts, their act is first directed toward the object presented, and then returns to itself. For in what way will our mind understand that it understands, unless it has first understood something?

<margin>
That the highest good resides in the intellect.
</margin>

<summary>This page outlines a classic Scholastic debate: whether the highest human good (summum bonum) is found in the Will—through the love and enjoyment of God—or in the Intellect, through the unique human capacity for reason and knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>highest good, Will vs Intellect, Augustine, appetitus, scholasticism, human nature, desire, logic</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholastic debate from page 37. While the previous page presented arguments for the superiority of the Will in achieving the "highest good," this page shifts to arguments favoring the Intellect, before introducing a third, middle-ground perspective.</meta>

# ->Book Two<-

For only then do I understand that I understand you are my friend, when I have first understood that you are my friend. For the act of understanding that the intellect understands belongs to some object; therefore, it is necessary to arrive at that first act of understanding. This is not itself the act of understanding, but rather something that can be understood. By the same reasoning, that first thing which we will is not the act of willing itself, but some other good. Now, the first thing that a rational nature wills is the <term>highest good</term> <note>original: *summum bonum*; the ultimate goal of human existence.</note>. For we will whatever else we desire for the sake of that highest good. Therefore, the highest good does not reside in the act of the will. There is also another of their arguments: Everything possesses the power of its nature from those things which constitute its essence. In what other way does a human corpse differ from a living person, except that the corpse lacks those things which constitute the human essence? Yet true <term>happiness</term> <note>original: *beatitudo*; a state of supreme blessedness or fulfillment.</note> does not differ from false happiness in terms of the will. The will does not behave differently when desiring, loving, or enjoying something it *thinks* is good—even if it is false—than if it were entirely true. However, whether a thing is true or false is a matter for the intellect, not the will. Therefore, the highest good consists more in its essence within the intellect than in the will. 

They also propose it this way: <margin>Will and desire</margin> If the will brings us the highest good, it will be either through desire, love, or joy; for the will is divided into these three, and there can be nothing else besides them. But how can we call desire the "end" of all goods? For desire is for a thing that is absent, which we vehemently wish to be present; desire is directed toward that which you have not yet attained. For this reason, just as I would never call a man the victor in a chariot race who has not yet reached the finish line—even if he is leading the race—so too will that man not be happy in whom there is the greatest ardor for possession but who has not yet <gloss>attained his wish</gloss>. 

But love will make us no more happy than desire. For we often love those things which are absent. Indeed, desire is summoned by love to possess that which you do not yet possess. And as for joy, how shall we place it there? For the "end" must be such that all other things are referred to it, but it is referred to nothing else. Yet we see that joy is excited by nature for the sake of actions themselves, just as we showed in the previous section when <note>original: *in voluptate confutanda*</note> refuting the idea of pleasure as the highest goal. Add to this, if you please, that just as desire seems to drive one toward attaining a thing, so joy renders you quiet and tranquil, so that you rest in what you have already attained. We see that heavy objects are carried toward the lowest point by their own nature, in which they do not refuse to rest; but they [the philosophers] would have it that this kind of rest is not the "end" itself, but something that *participates* in the end. 

I would be more long-winded than the nature of this discourse allows if I were now to recount all the arguments in their proper order, by which these men strive to transfer the peak of the matter to the will for the good, and those men to the knowledge of the truth. <margin>The highest good in both will and intellect</margin> But there was a third group who would commit the whole matter neither to cognition alone, nor again to the will alone, since they believe neither can provide it on its own. Instead, they wish for both joined together to make us most abundantly happy. Their primary reasoning is offered as follows: They say that in acts which relate in an equal way (so to speak) to that which makes us happy, happiness must also be established in an equal way. The intellect and the will are of this sort. For as much as we know of God, that much we love; therefore, both together will constitute the highest good. They likewise add that if enjoying God is the highest good, knowledge must appear within it. For as **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian who often wrote on the relationship between knowing and loving God.</note> testifies, those things which we enjoy...

<summary>This page weighs the roles of the Intellect and the Will in reaching the "Highest Good," concluding that neither desire nor joy alone suffices. It introduces a conciliatory view that ultimate happiness requires both knowing and loving the divine.</summary>
<keywords>highest good, intellect, will, happiness, desire, love, joy, Augustine, scholasticism, essence</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholastic investigation into the "highest good," shifting from the arguments for the Will (presented on the previous page) to the arguments for the primacy of the Intellect. It utilizes classical references to illustrate how the mind remains pure even when contemplating evil, whereas the will is corrupted by what it desires.</meta>

->## On the Highest Good<-

...unless they are already known: in which things the <term>will</term><note>Latin: *uoluntas*; the faculty of choice and desire.</note>, having been delighted, then finds rest. Likewise, we shall then call ourselves blessed when our mind is joined to God as far as it is possible for it to be. Who does not see that a union of this kind occurs by both the intellect and the will? For by the latter, we are joined to God as He is the essence of the highest goodness; by the former, as He is the essence of the highest truth. Finally, if you ask from where the highest good comes to us, they will answer with the consensus of the most learned that it comes from the fact that we were created in the likeness and image of God. They will affirm that we reach such an image not by one of these faculties alone, but by both.

<margin>Is it the mind?</margin>
Although all these things are presented in this way, there are nevertheless those who dare to strive against them. Therefore, although they admit such equality of the mind and will, they nevertheless think that because the <term>mind</term><note>Latin: *mens*; often used interchangeably here with the intellect or the rational part of the soul.</note> is more noble, the highest good is to be placed primarily in it as the leading faculty. They think it is more noble because if these two joined things—the mind and the will—could be separated, the mind itself would remain as a perfect act and proper to the rational form. But the will, if deprived of the intellect, what else will remain but a certain desire common to all living things? Who would not notice that the act of the will depends on the mind? For we never want anything unless we have first either known it or thought we knew it to be good. Added to this is the fact that the act of the mind is purer. For it contracts no stain from that which is presented to it. Nor does the soul commit a crime in knowing evil. For <term>Davus</term><note>A clever, scheming servant character from the Roman plays of Terence.</note> does not say correctly of the old man: <note>original: "mala mens malus animus"</note> "bad mind, bad heart," even though the master knows the servant's fraud. The will, on the contrary, is easily defiled. Wherefore <term>Simo</term><note>The master/old man character from the same plays, often the victim of Davus's schemes.</note> says correctly of Davus: "bad mind, bad heart." For the servant’s effect is bad—not because he understands how perversely things are being done, but because he *wants* to act perversely.

And no wonder: for while we either love or will anything, the soul itself is moved toward the thing loved. Therefore, the lover is in some way received by the beloved. But whatever is received is received not according to its own standard, but according to the standard of that which receives it. Wherefore, while we will filthy and wicked things, the will is transformed into the nature of the filthy thing and becomes basely foul. We cannot say the same of the mind. For when anything is perceived by the mind, the soul is not moved toward the thing, but the thing toward the soul. This ensures that the mind does not become base through the lowliness of things; but rather those things which are lower are ennobled by the mind. For they take on a certain spiritual essence in the intellect itself. They also grant this: that power which extends itself to more reasons is more excellent and noble than all the others that reach fewer. Therefore, the mind, which extends not only to the nature of the true but also to the nature of the good, will far surpass the will, which touches nothing except insofar as it is good.

Likewise, that power in us will be stronger which, since we use reason, divides us from those things that lack reason. For this is properly suited to us. But who does not know that we are divided from those lacking reason not by the will, but by the mind? Likewise, it is necessary that the power which more closely touches that act by which we are made blessed is more noble than all others. This is the property of the mind. For since not simply the good, but the *known* good is presented to the will, what will ever be accomplished unless knowledge intercedes? Although they say all these things most sharply, there are still those who try to overthrow them, whose arguments it would be long to relate. But here at last, if I gather correctly, they end up: they say that when something...

<summary>This page argues for the superiority of the Intellect over the Will in achieving the highest good, claiming the mind remains pure when perceiving evil while the will is corrupted by what it desires. It asserts that the mind is the faculty that truly distinguishes humans from irrational animals.</summary>
<keywords>Intellect, Will, Highest Good, Davus, Simo, Morality, Rationality, Perception, Corruption of the Will</keywords>

## Book Two

...it is simply stated that it must be understood in this way: that which is entirely perfect in its kind. For if someone mentions an "animal," we do not understand a dead or painted one, but a living animal. By that same reasoning, if someone states that a soldier went over to the enemy by deliberate <term>will</term><note>Latin: *uoluntate*; here meaning a conscious, rational choice.</note>, that will must be understood as one which, bound by certain rules, obeys reason; an act of this kind will indeed be more perfect than an act of the intellect. But what are we doing? For I see that while I linger too much on individual details, I have almost fallen away from my intended purpose. It should have been enough for that purpose to report what the <term>highest good</term><note>Latin: *summum bonum*; the ultimate goal of human existence.</note> was. Since I have shown through clear arguments that this is indeed God, whether we attain Him by the mind, or by the will, or by both, matters not at all to those who wish to grasp the reality. For one must progress by the same path and rely on the same reasons. Therefore, let us leave this entire question of what or of what kind it is to be investigated by those who have an abundance not only of learning, but also of leisure. For matters of this sort are such that they can be argued with probability on either side by those who are most lettered and strong in intellect. As for me, even if I were taken as an honorary arbiter in such a great matter, I would never interpose my own decree; rather, I shall release myself by a <note>original: *Virgiliano iudicio*</note> Virgilian judgment and pronounce "each worthy of the heifer." <note>A reference to Virgil's *Eclogues* (3.109), where a singing contest between two shepherds is declared a draw: "Both are worthy of the heifer." It means the debate is a tie.</note> For since we have now reached the point where I recognize that God is the end of all our goods, I will hold that we—that is, the human essence—are made blessed by God.

When he had said these things, and we followed his speech with admiration and then applause, **LAURENTIUS** said: I could not be more fully satisfied than I have been by you. For you have led me over to your opinion in such a way that if anyone now wished to bring any argument against it, I would stop up my ears with that <note>original: *cera illa Vlyxea*</note> wax of Ulysses <note>In Homer's *Odyssey*, Ulysses (Odysseus) used wax to plug his sailors' ears so they would not be lured by the Sirens' song.</note> so that I might not be forced to listen against my will. For I have it clearly settled in my mind what the highest good is. Only one thing remains—unless perhaps I shall be a burden to you, exhausted as you are by this long discussion—which, once I have understood it, I shall feel I depart a happy man.

On the contrary, **BAPTISTA** replied: as long as I can explain what you desire to know, I will let you lack for nothing.

Hear then, said **LAURENTIUS**, what I wish to be made plain to me. I have often been present when the ends of "goods" were being discussed, and I noted that when a matter was brought forward according to the opinion of some philosopher, these two things could easily be perceived together: so that when you understood what "end of goods" he proposed, you would immediately and by the same reasoning recognize the "end of evils." Epicurus says the highest good is the pleasure of the body; therefore, without any trouble, we shall call pain the highest evil. Another places everything in riches, another in honors. Therefore, one will assert that the end of evils is disgrace, and the other, poverty. Nor will the Stoics hesitate, since they have called virtue the only good, to affirm that vice is the only evil. The reasoning for the rest will be the same. But now, since you have refuted all others and called God the highest good, I clearly understand what you mean. And I not only understand, but I also agree.

<margin>The Highest Evil</margin>
But when, a little later, I inquire more diligently into what I should oppose to this highest good as its absolute contrary, nothing occurs to me that could justifiably be called the "highest evil" in the same way that this is called the "highest good." For this reason, you would do a very pleasing and delightful thing if you would explain what this "highest" thing is and what kind it might be.

You are right to doubt, said **BAPTISTA**. For a matter of this kind is such that it lacks neither difficulty, if you wish to investigate it, nor utility, if you abound in a vigorous mind regarding it. Indeed, I have seen many people stumbling over it. For when philosophers...

-># On the Highest Good<-

...if you scan them all, you will find no one, as you were saying, who does not posit a highest point for both of two opposites. However, when you turn to the Christian theologians, they indeed posit a highest and ultimate point for goods, but they do not posit one for evils. Yet, unless I am mistaken, we shall easily settle this controversy between them if we investigate the very thing we call "evil" in such a way that its nature becomes perfectly clear. To attain this knowledge, we must trace certain matters from a bit deeper in the subject—unless perhaps you think it enough if I show you briefly only *what* they think, and not also *why* they think it.

On the contrary, both indeed! said **LAURENTIUS**. For although there is nothing that I do not judge should be granted to the authority of such great men, you nonetheless know the greed of our minds, which is so great in the pursuit of knowledge that they never rest until the underlying reason is perceived.

I shall therefore do as you wish, said **BAPTISTA**. But you will endure it with a calm mind if, while I comply with you, I employ a somewhat lengthy discourse. 

There is, then, nothing among all those things that act which does not propose some end for itself in its acting. For those who plow, those who serve as soldiers, those who sail, and those who build do not do these things just to be doing them; rather, they endure all their labors so that from these actions they may reach that goal which they proposed. Moreover, they call that the <term>end</term><note>Latin: *finis*; in philosophy, this refers to the purpose or "final cause" for which something is done.</note> toward which those who act are carried. For we would undergo so many labors, so many difficulties, and so many inconveniences in vain unless something were proposed for the sake of which those things were undertaken.

For this reason, when <term>Maro</term><note>The poet Virgil (Publius Virgilius Maro).</note> commands that "in early spring, when icy moisture melts from the snowy mountains and the crumbling clod unbinds itself at the west wind's touch, let the bull then begin to groan under the deep-pressed plow, and the blade shine, worn by the furrow," <note>original: *vere nouo gelidus canis cum mōtibus humor liquitur...* This is a quote from Virgil’s Georgics, Book 1, lines 43-46, describing the start of the farming year.</note> he does not command this so that the oxen should exhaust themselves by plowing, but so that from the diligent cultivation of the field they may finally receive a more bounteous harvest. Therefore, in all our actions, that which is sought for its own sake must be found. All other things, however, are sought for the sake of that one thing, so that when we have arrived at it, there is no further to go. For unless a certain end were prescribed, one would have to proceed through things that are entirely infinite. Since there would be no power to reach these, we would cease from all action; for no one is moved toward that which he utterly despairs of attaining. 

For this reason, whatever **Cicero** <margin><unclear>2</unclear></margin> did in pleading cases, he did for the sake of persuading; and our own **Federico of Urbino** <note>Federico da Montefeltro (1422–1482), the Duke of Urbino and a famous patron of the arts and mercenary commander.</note> in this recent war against **Bartolomeo of Bergamo** <note>Bartolomeo Colleoni (1400–1475), a celebrated Venetian commander.</note>—whatever he did either in drawing up his soldiers or in scouting the forces of his adversaries—he aimed at this: that he might gain the victory; and once it was won, he would rest.

To what point, then, do we revolve back so many times? Surely to establish that whatever things are done, they are done for the sake of some thing which is sought for itself. This, moreover, must be a "good." For if in our actions we always propose something to ourselves as certain, we will deem that thing suitable for us and agreeable to our nature. For whatever nature has produced, it has so recommended to itself that it always seeks what is useful to it, and by the same reasoning, it flees from whatever is harmful. 

We seek, therefore, the good. Thus, we say the "end" and the "good" are the same thing. Because of this, it is rightly concluded that whatever humans do, they do for the sake of that which is good. From these facts, this much remains: if whoever acts proposes a good for himself, surely if he wanders at all, that error—which is <term>sin</term><note>Latin: *peccatum*; can also mean "mistake" or "fault" in a non-theological sense.</note> and evil—happens beside the purpose. Nor would one easily distinguish a sin unless all actions tended toward their proper end. For how could you say an archer wanders from the mark unless a target...

<summary>In this dialogue, Baptista explains to Laurentius that all human actions are directed toward a specific "end" or "good." Using examples from farming, law, and war, he argues that evil is not a goal in itself but rather an error or failure to hit the intended mark of the good.</summary>

### Book Two

...at which he has proposed to direct his arrows. For this reason, even a physician **errs** <note>Latin: *peccat*; in this context, the author uses the word to mean a "miss" or "failure" to achieve a technical goal, rather than a moral sin.</note> when, intending to cure a disease for a sick man, he instead increases it. You may observe the same thing in nature: for nature wills that a human be produced from human seed; but if an offspring is born with a bull’s head or horse’s legs, that will be called an **error of nature**, since it has strayed from the end it proposed for itself. Since all these things are so, it will be manifest that evil occurs in things outside the intention of the one who acts. For whatever arises in actions that is different from what the agent had proposed for himself must be admitted to have happened outside his intention. Moreover, everyone knows that evil is far different from good; therefore, it happens outside the intention of the one who acts. I will leave aside in this place those things which are led by the force of nature, for which the reasoning is the same. But let us look at human actions, of which our mind is the principle. There is a certain power in us in whose possession is the judgment of right and wrong. This power apprehends the forms of things and is carried toward that which it has already proposed to itself as an end. But if, deceived by some false appearance of a thing, it should tend toward something else, then an error is born which no one would deny is outside the intention. <margin>sa</margin> <note>Likely an abbreviation for Scaevola.</note> This power in **Mucius** <note>Gaius Mucius Scaevola, a legendary Roman hero who attempted to assassinate the Etruscan king Porsena during a siege of Rome.</note> decides that **Porsena**, the king of the Clusians, must be slaughtered because he is a most unjust enemy to the Romans. In a slaughter of this kind, therefore, Mucius is carried as if toward a good. But, deceived by the similarity of dress and appearance, he kills the king’s scribe instead of the king. This happened to him outside his intention, no differently than if someone, about to receive gold owed to him, were to take **orichalcum** <note>A gold-colored alloy of copper and zinc, often mistaken for gold in antiquity.</note> instead of gold, having been deceived by the color of the metal. Indeed, this point, which is not at all obscure, may be concluded thus: since we do nothing except by that reason by which we have already judged it to be good, whatever evil arises from it will be outside our intention.

These things which you have said so far, said **LAURENTIUS**, are very pleasing to me. For everything seems to proceed in its own order, and at the same time they are corroborated by the weightiest arguments. There is one thing, however, that I desire to be made clearer to me. For when you were saying that evil happens outside the intention of the one who acts, the reasons you set forth persuaded me that it was so. But on the other hand, when I weighed the matter more diligently, it occurred to me that whatever happens outside of intention usually happens by luck and chance, and therefore only rarely. But how shall we say that evil happens either by chance and fortune, or rarely? For, to omit natural things for the moment—in which <term>privation</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "privation" is the absence of a quality that should naturally be present.</note> is joined not rarely or by chance, but always—would you not find far more vices than virtues in the life and character of men? And those very things are committed by our own will. Pay closer attention, I pray, to those things done by all the men who are known to you. For how few will you meet whose almost infinite errors you do not discover? No one doubts that these are undertaken not only by will, but also by choice. For I will now omit the authority of **Aristotle**, which could move anyone on account of the man's supreme learning; nor will I now recount the reasons of so great a philosopher by which he proves that vices are voluntary. Never in well-administered public affairs would those who brought the laws wisely and justly have prepared such numerous and heavy punishments against the wicked, unless they clearly understood that men sin of their own accord and not because they are compelled. Since, indeed, in that branch of philosophy by which life is purified, virtues and vices are weighed not by what someone has done, but by the mind with which he did it. Therefore, since I observe that vices happen frequently in human actions and are committed by us willingly, I do not understand...

<summary>Baptista argues that evil is an accidental error or "missed target" using examples like Mucius Scaevola’s mistaken assassination attempt. Laurentius counters by questioning how evil can be accidental if it is so common and legally punished as a voluntary act.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Baptista (Leon Battista Alberti) and Laurentius (Lorenzo de' Medici). Baptista is responding to Laurentius's previous point about how evil actions can be considered "outside of intention."</meta>

# On the Highest Good

...in what way they can exist outside the purpose of the one by whom they are committed.

"You ask these things rightly indeed, **Laurentius**," said **Baptista**. "It is not easy to solve the things you have said; nevertheless, I will try to see if I can satisfy you in this. So that this may be accomplished more conveniently, let it not be hidden from you that evil is discussed in two parts: we consider it either in a certain **essence** <term>essentia: the fundamental being or "what-ness" of a thing</term> or in the action of an essence. 

We shall say, then, that evil exists in an essence when something is missing from it which, by its own nature, it not only can have, but which it must have unless it is to be defective. Therefore, it will not be an evil for you if you do not have winged sandals (such as **Mercury** <note>The Roman messenger god, often depicted with winged shoes called *talaria*.</note> has). For the nature of a human is such that he cannot obtain winged sandals. Nor, again, should it be thought an evil if you do not have blonde hair. For even if this kind of color can happen to human citizens, it is possible for a man to lack it without any personal loss. 

But no one can lack a hand without his own detriment. For wise nature observed, when she produced such an excellent animal as man, that he could not exactly practice the arts she was going to attribute to him without the help of hands. Therefore, if someone should lack hands, he will lack that instrument without which he cannot perform the things necessary for the use of life.

<margin>Evil</margin>

From these things, indeed, we are led to the point that we call 'evil' that which is a **privation** <term>privatio: the absence or stripping away of a quality that naturally belongs to something</term> of a thing which nature granted to man, and without which you cannot lead a good and convenient life.

Now, as for matter, which the Greeks call **hyle** <note>original: *hylen*; a philosophical term for the primary, unshaped 'stuff' of the universe.</note>, even if it can by its own natural power receive all forms, no single form is so 'owed' to it that it could be called more perfect with this one rather than that one. And yet, you will find no form which is not owed to some of those things which consist of matter and form; for neither could water be 'gilded' without the proper form of water, nor fire without the form of fire. Therefore, a privation of this kind of form, if it is compared to the 'forest' <note>original: *syluam*; Alberti uses the Latin word for 'forest' or 'timber' as a direct translation of the Greek *hyle* to mean raw matter.</note> itself, will be no evil to nature, since the matter is no more inclined to this form than to that. 

But if it is compared to that thing whose form it is, it will be entirely an evil. For if fire is stripped of its form, who does not see that this is now an evil for the fire? These, therefore, are the kinds of evils which are said to exist in a certain essence. 

In the doing of things, however, evil is said to happen if the actions themselves lack their proper order and their proper **measure** <term>modus: the limit, boundary, or correct proportion of an action</term>; for there is no action to which a certain measure and a certain order are not owed. You now have what the philosophers think is evil in essence and what, again, is evil in action. 

As for that argument of yours, by which you thought to prove that whatever happens outside the purpose of the one who acts is from fortune and chance, we shall easily refute it. For you will never persuade anyone that things of this kind are from fortune, which—although they happened outside your purpose—nevertheless follow of their own accord from that which you proposed to do. 

I will demonstrate this with a brief example to make it clearer: a thirsty glutton proposes to himself that it is useful to enjoy the great sweetness of Cretan wine; but while he holds to that, he becomes drunk. Drunkenness, therefore, comes to him outside his purpose, yet not by chance, since drunkenness accompanies frequent cups of that wine either constantly or very often. 

You will find the same reasoning in natural things: in which, even if the generator proposes no such thing for himself, **corruption** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, 'corruption' is the natural process of a thing losing its form so a new thing can be born.</note> always follows. For air is never born from water unless the form of water perishes. But if it should happen rarely, that could then be from fortune and chance—as if a **monster** <note>original: *monstrum*; here meaning a biological abnormality or birth defect that deviates from the natural 'blueprint'.</note> were born from human seed. For this does not necessarily follow from what nature had proposed for herself to do. Rather...

<summary>Baptista defines evil as a "privation" or lack of a necessary natural quality (like a hand) rather than a mere absence of a non-essential one (like wings). He further distinguishes between unintended consequences that are "chance" (rare mutations) and those that are "natural" (drunkenness from drinking or the decay of one element to create another).</summary>
<keywords>essence, privation, matter and form, nature, chance vs. necessity, drunkenness, corruption, hyle, Leon Battista Alberti</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Baptista (Leon Battista Alberti) and Laurentius (Lorenzo de' Medici) regarding the nature of evil. Baptista is explaining that evil is not a substance in itself, but a failure or absence of good that occurs during an action or within a being.</meta>

## Second Book

...nature opposes it, since she desires to generate what is perfect. However, in the categories of nature, evil arises then: when the power that acts fails in the acting. Even though this is outside the intention of that power, it will not be said to happen by chance if it occurs frequently—only if it happens rarely. But regarding nature, we shall speak elsewhere. 

Now, to return to our human actions, which are the subject of our talk: our entire purpose is directed toward the good—not toward that universal good which is not contained by any specific boundary (for universals do not move us to action), but toward some predefined good circumscribed by place and time. If, therefore, a good of this kind has a **privation** <term>privatio: the absence of a good that should naturally be present, like blindness is the privation of sight</term> of good—that is, an evil—joined to it which it drags along with it, we shall never blame either fortune or chance. 

For example, you propose to yourself that it is a good to take sexual pleasure from a woman who is married to another. Here, you are not seeking "adultery," but "intercourse"; yet the latter cannot exist without the adultery. Nevertheless, the adultery was not committed by chance, even though it was outside your specific purpose, because it cannot be separated from that intercourse. 

In the same way, we would say it happens by chance if someone fighting in a battle line, while striving to drive the enemy from their position, happens to strike a citizen whom he desires to protect. For that striking of a citizen is not so attached to the fight waged against the enemy that it cannot be separated from it. 

Wherefore, it will appear even to those looking with only moderate attention that the vices committed in the course of doing things—even if they are outside the original purpose—are nevertheless voluntary. This is not because we want them for their own sake, but because, just as a shadow always follows a body, so do those vices always accompany that which we want for its own sake. For the word "will" is also rightly used for those things which we want for the sake of something else, even if we would not want them for their own sake or simply on their own. 

For sailors caught in a great storm set safety as their goal. But when they cannot achieve it with a loaded ship, they willingly throw out their goods—even precious ones. Therefore, they cause the loss of their own accord; yet not simply for the sake of the loss, but for the sake of safety. 

If you hold all these things correctly, you will also understand that absolutely nothing among all things that exist is evil. **Evil** <margin>Evil</margin> itself is rightly called a privation, which is not an **essence** <term>essentia: the fundamental "being" or reality of a thing</term> at all, but a negation within an essence. 

That everything which exists is good is clear from this: whatever things exist, they desire nothing more than that they *be*. Now, if "being" were not a good, they would in no way desire it. Therefore, no essence is evil. No evil, therefore, will be an essence. 

Add to this, if you please, the following: whatever exists is said to be so either in **act** <term>actus: the state of being fully realized or "doing"</term> or in **faculty** <term>facultas: the potential or power to do something</term>. You will find nothing else by which a thing can exist. Actuality, by the very reason that it is the realization of a thing, will be a good. For all things are called "perfect" when they exist in act. But the faculty itself must also be good. For through it, one arrives at the act; it clings to the act through a certain analogy, and it is in no way contrary to it. It is, likewise, of the same kind. Therefore, whatever exists, by whatever reasoning it comes to be from that which is good, is itself good. Therefore, evil has no essence. 

Furthermore, it is established among the **metaphysicians** <term>metaphysica: philosophers who study the fundamental nature of reality and being</term> that whatever exists, its entire being is from God. But since God is the highest good, no one of sound mind would say that evil was produced by Him. This sentiment fits perfectly with the passage from **Genesis** <note>Referencing Genesis 1:31: "And God saw every thing that he had made, and, behold, it was very good."</note>: "God saw all the things that He had made, and they were very good." 

I think it has been clearly demonstrated to you now that no essence is evil. 

"Indeed, it is clear," said **LAURENTIUS**, "and by those arguments which not only compel the unwilling but also lead the willing. Thus, when I hear them, I surrender myself entirely to them. Yet again, when I reflect on the matter with myself, I do not see, if it is truly stated that 'there is evil in things,' why it should not also be truly said that 'evil is an essence.' Or I beseech—"

->f<-

<summary>Baptista argues that evil has no independent existence or "essence," but is merely a privation of good that occurs as a side effect of seeking a specific good. He uses examples of adultery and sailors in a storm to show how unwanted outcomes are "voluntary" consequences of our goals, and concludes with a theological defense of the goodness of all creation.</summary>
<keywords>Baptista, Laurentius, evil as privation, essence, voluntary action, metaphysics, Genesis, act and potency, problem of evil</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Baptista (Leon Battista Alberti) and Laurentius (Lorenzo de' Medici). Having established that evil is a privation (a lack of something), they are now debating how we can speak of evil "existing" if it is merely an absence, and how a "good" cause can produce an "evil" result.</meta>

# On the Highest Good

<margin>himself</margin>

<note>original: "se"</note> I ask you, would it not be incorrectly said that Appius Claudius is blind? <note>Appius Claudius Caecus was a famous Roman statesman known for his blindness; the speaker is using a famous historical figure as a logical example.</note> But if it is said correctly, is it not also correctly said that blindness is "something"—even though we all understand it to be a **privation**? <term>privatio: the absence of a quality that should naturally be present, such as blindness being the absence of sight.</term> BAPTISTA replied: "Correct indeed, but not in a simple way, nor in one and the same sense. For we say something 'is' in two ways: first, in the way that the **essence** <term>essentia: the fundamental 'what-ness' or substance of a thing.</term> of a thing is signified (which is divided into those ten categories that the **logicians** <term>dialectici: practitioners of dialectic or formal logic.</term> call the **predicaments** <term>praedicamenta: the Aristotelian categories used to classify everything that exists, such as substance, quality, or quantity.</term>); and second, in the way that we express the truth of a particular statement. Therefore, with the approval of all learned men, you may say in this second way that evil and privation are an essence, since it is most truly said that something is deprived by the privation itself; however, in the first way, it is not allowed at all. 

But enough regarding the nature of evil. Now, it will perhaps seem absurd to some to say that the cause of evil is not evil, but good. Yet this is so true that it could not be any other way. For if you say that evil is the cause of evil, you must remember what has already been demonstrated: that no evil acts except by the power of the good itself. For this reason, we shall call the primary cause of evil 'good.' But does this not also carry great weight in the matter? For if evil is nothing, how can that which is nothing be a cause? What then shall we say? This, if I am not mistaken: if anything is the cause of evil, that cause is good—yet not because of the goal it sets for itself. For things that are opposites will never be the cause of one another; rather, it happens from that which occurs outside the intended goal. 

We see, for example, that cold is the cause of heat. From this comes the saying: *and the piercing cold of the north wind burns.* <note>A reference to Virgil’s Georgics; the idea is that extreme cold can produce a sensation or effect similar to burning.</note> This is not because it is the inherent power of cold to heat things, but by some other reason. Therefore, evil draws its origin from good—not from the nature of the good, but from its own **weakness** <term>imbecillitate: a failure of power, fragility, or inability to complete a natural action.</term>, of which we can take a clear example from nature. There is a certain power in a living being to which nature has assigned this task: to **digest** <term>decoquere: literally 'to cook down,' the term used for the stomach's process of breaking down food.</term> the food that has already fallen into the stomach. This, therefore, is what the power of digestion proposes to do. But if, having become weak, it is unable to fulfill its duty, that digestion is left imperfect, and the raw **chyle** <term>chilus: the semi-fluid mass of partly digested food.</term> remains in the stomach. Thus, the power errs while it acts—not because it wants to, but out of its own weakness. For the power itself, in acting, does not measure its action by its weakness; rather, although it intends to bring the matter to a finished result, it fails during the acting because of that weakness. 

It also happens sometimes that a power is strong and sufficient for acting, but because it encounters an unsuitable instrument to use, it cannot complete the task. For the power by which the movement of the legs is stirred into a straight gait may be strong; however, if the legs, which it uses as instruments to form the gait, are crooked, it will never produce a straight walk. For Horatius Cocles <note>A legendary Roman hero who defended the Sublician bridge; tradition says he was left with a permanent limp from his wounds.</note> did not lack the power to form a straight step, but one leg, ruined by a wound, would not obey him. I think you understand how certain corruptions happen to actions, both from the infirmity by which the acting power is weakened, and from the defect of the instrument, which makes it unfit. 

Truly, just as corruption comes to an action, so too can that which arises from the action fall into evil. At that time, either the **matter** or the **form** will be at fault. <term>materia and forma: the two components of all things in Aristotelian philosophy; matter is the 'stuff' and form is the 'design' or 'soul' that makes it what it is.</term> There is a power in the male seed so that it can form a human being from the female blood. But if it finds the matter—upon which the human form must be impressed—to be resistant and entirely stubborn, it will not shape a man, but some **deformity** <term>portentum: a 'portent' or 'monster'; a biological anomaly that deviates from the natural pattern.</term>. Who would not admit that this failure comes from the matter? Furthermore, it is necessary that the privation of another form be joined to the form itself. Therefore, when nature strives to create air from water, it proposes to itself the form of air; however, the privation of that form which is water occurs outside its intention...

<summary>Baptista explains that evil exists only as a logical statement (privation) rather than a physical substance, and argues that evil is caused by "good" agents failing due to internal weakness or faulty "instruments," like a wounded hero limping or a stomach failing to digest food.</summary>
<keywords>privation, essence, logic, categories, digestion, Horatius Cocles, matter and form, deformity, nature of evil</keywords>
