<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of evil. Having previously established that evil is a privation of good, the speakers now shift the focus from abstract nature to human morality and the mechanics of the "Will."</meta>

<page-num>f ii</page-num>
<header>Book Two</header>

...comes. You may perceive the same reasoning in the arts as well. *For art always imitates nature as far as it is able.* <note>original: "Ars enim naturam quoad ualet semper imitatur." This is a classic Aristotelian principle suggesting that human creativity follows the patterns found in the natural world.</note> But I do not know how it happens that when we set out to investigate the evil committed in life and character, I slide back a second time into nature. Therefore, leaving this aside, let us finally return to that point; and let us establish first, following all philosophers and Christian authors, that [evil] lies not in what proceeds from the action, but in reason alone. For they do not notice what was done, but with what mind it was done. For this reason, they set down four principles, as it were:

<margin>
Thing
Knowledge
Will
Motion
</margin>

When something offers itself to us, a certain power of our minds is moved by the thing thus presented so that it may know it; and finally, it decides that one thing is good and another, by contrast, is bad. Therefore, when the thing has already presented itself, and when, in the second place, a judgment has been made about it, the **will** is excited in the third place, so that we may follow this or flee that. With this will commanding, motions finally arise in our bodies, so that the limbs carry out what the will previously decreed. Neither from those first two principles nor from this last one is vice perceived, but from the **will**, which we placed third in the order. 

For **Verres** <note>Gaius Verres was a Roman magistrate notorious for his corruption and the plunder of Sicily; Cicero famously prosecuted him.</note> did not sin because the paintings, statues, and the rest of the Sicilians' most precious furnishings offered themselves to him. Nor, again, because he judged that it would be useful for him to abound in such decorations; but because he **willed** to seize them. Since the matter depends so entirely on the will alone, even if he did not seize them, yet because he willed to seize them, the crime has been committed. For it is not whether he killed or did not kill, but whether he **willed** to kill that constitutes the guilt. Strength failed **P. Clodius** <note>Publius Clodius Pulcher, a populist Roman politician and rival of Milo.</note> so that he could not kill **Annius Milo** <note>Titus Annius Milo, who was eventually tried for the murder of Clodius; the speaker is referencing their famous feud.</note>. In this matter, if you look for a defect of nature, that power failed which did not fulfill the mind’s purpose; but if you turn to morality, it is not the act of bodily motion but the act of the will that conceives the crime. And Clodius will rightly be called a murderer because he willed to kill Milo. 

But suppose he had killed him when he least willed to do so: he would immediately be acquitted of the crime. For those who commit some deed not out of will, but either from a weakness of strength (as I just mentioned) or out of ignorance, not only are they free of blame, but they are also very often considered worthy of pity. For who, when reading about **Cephalus and Procris** <note>A Greek myth where Cephalus accidentally kills his wife Procris with a spear, thinking she was a wild animal rustling in the bushes.</note>—even if they think it a fable—does not only free him of the crime but also follows him with the greatest compassion? For they observe a man who, out of ignorance, while thinking he was wounding a wild beast, struck his most chaste wife, into whose death he would shortly thereafter fall into the deepest sorrow and mourning. 

You see, therefore, that vice in character flows from the act of the will as if from its own origin. But since it is now established that the weakness of an action proceeds from the infirmity of the primary agent, I believe this matter must be explained more plainly. Let us see, then, in what the will fails before the crime is committed. Indeed, this defect will not be from nature (for then it would always cling to us and we would always sin), nor again is it by chance or fortune (for then it would be outside of us). It is, therefore, **voluntary**. 

But so that you may see where the error comes from, take this: the force of a thing that acts is perfected by that agent which is above it. For as long as that which acts in the second place persists in the order of the primary agent, it performs its task perfectly. But if it should decline from that, there is no longer any remedy; it fails either immediately or shortly after. A circle is turned in a ring that is twisted by a human hand. This same circle, if the hand declines, will cease from motion. Therefore, to return to the matter I was just speaking of: there were two principles that preceded the will: the Thing which offe[rs itself]...

<summary>This page argues that moral vice resides solely in the human will rather than in the external object or the physical act. Using examples like Verres's theft and the myth of Cephalus, the author demonstrates that intent is the only true measure of sin or crime.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of how the "Will" is moved by reason and perception, building on the four principles (Thing, Knowledge, Will, Motion) established on the previous page. It addresses why evil cannot exist independently of good.</meta>

# ->Concerning the Supreme Good<-

<note>original: "De summo bono"</note>

...[occur]: and there is a certain power that receives those things offered to it. But since everything of this kind—insofar as it can be moved—must have something specific by which it is moved, not every power of perception will move every appetite. For that which perceives sensible things <note>Things that can be experienced through the five senses, like heat or color.</note> is only able to move the appetite which arises from the senses. **Reason**, however, properly moves the **will**. Again, since reason can perceive various kinds of goods, and each has its own proper end <note>The "end" or *telos* refers to the ultimate purpose or goal of a thing.</note>, there will also be a proper end for the will; and the first thing by which the will is moved will not be just any good, but something certain and predetermined. 

<margin><unclear>virtue reason</unclear></margin> If, therefore, our mind and will are moved by the perception of that reason which holds a right judgment of good and evil, a right action will arise from it. But if it arises from those things which are decreed to be good by the false judgment of the senses—when they are not good at all—the will immediately errs in life and character. A perversion, therefore, of the order which is directed toward reason and toward its proper end produces sin in action. 

Regarding reason, this happens when the will is carried away by a sudden sensory perception toward something that, if you were to look closely, is not good; but because the sense has charmed it with its allurements, it is judged good by them. There is also a case when reason itself is not at all deceived and decrees that something is good which can truly be called good. Yet at this time or in this manner, it is denied to be good. The will, however, is carried toward it, having no regard for the order. A perversion of this kind of order is therefore voluntary, and for that reason, it is not free from vice. 

I have perhaps been more talkative than is proper regarding the nature of evil. I will add, however, from those arguments by which it was demonstrated that **evil has no essence** <note>In philosophy, an "essence" is the underlying reality of what a thing is. By saying evil has no essence, the author means evil is not a "thing" in itself, but a lack of something else.</note>—and for that reason cannot exist by itself—that it can easily be noticed that it must always exist in some good. 

But the same thing is proved by this reasoning: when we speak of evil, we speak of a **privation** <note>A "privation" is the absence of a quality that should normally be present, like blindness being the absence of sight.</note>; for I have already established this. Now, the privation itself and the **form** <note>The "form" is the defining characteristic or "blueprint" of a thing.</note> of which the thing is deprived are in the same subject. That which is subject to the form is of such a kind that, by its own native faculty, it is able to receive the form. But who will deny that this is a good, since the faculty or power itself and the act that comes from it belong entirely to the same category? 

Furthermore, evil is called evil only for the reason that it harms. But it does not harm evil. For it would be a good thing if it brought destruction to evil. Therefore, it harms the good. Nor would it harm—if we speak of the form of the thing—unless it were within it. For what blindness will harm **Polyphemus** <note>Polyphemus is the giant Cyclops from Homer’s *Odyssey* who was famously blinded by Odysseus. He is used here as a classic example: blindness is only "bad" because it exists within a living creature that ought to have sight.</note> unless the blindness is in Polyphemus? 

But since evil is opposed to good, how can both have the same subject? For one of two opposites drives out the other. If you say this, I will answer you thus: Whatever can be called a **being** <note>original: "ens"</note> is also called **good**. Now, it is not absurd for a "non-being" to be in a "being"; for every privation is in some essence, which is a being, and yet it is not in the being that is its own opposite. For if I speak of blindness, this "non-being" is certainly not so general as to remove sight everywhere. Therefore, it is not in the sight as if in its own subject, but in the living creature. 

All these things tend toward this: that a **supreme evil** cannot be found in the same way that the **supreme good** is found. For that which would be the supreme evil would have to exist without any fellowship with any good. But you will find no evil entirely separated from the good. Since, as we showed a little while ago, evil has struck its roots in the good, and in it has laid its own—so to speak—foundation. 

<margin>evil in the thing</margin> Furthermore, if you grant me that there is something that is a supreme evil, it would be evil by its very essence, just as we see that the supreme good is good by its essence. But it has already been demonstrated that evil has no essence. Thus, that which is a first principle <note>A fundamental starting point or origin of something.</note> can have no cause; for it would not be a first principle...

<summary>This page argues that evil is a voluntary perversion of the will's natural order and a "privation" that lacks its own essence. The author concludes that because evil must exist within a "good" subject to cause harm, a "supreme evil" is logically impossible.</summary>

[the beginning of the word is missing]...principle, if it were a cause on which it depended; but we have said that the cause of evil is good. Add this also: for a cause which is called a cause *per se* <note>original: "per se"; meaning a cause that acts by its own nature or intention.</note> is always prior to that which is called a cause *per accidens* <note>original: "per accidens"; an "accidental" or incidental cause, where the result is not the primary intent of the agent.</note>. But evil is not a cause except by accident. Therefore, a "supreme evil" is not found. These are the things which, out of many far more excellent points, Leon Battista <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous Renaissance "universal man," architect, and philosopher.</note> discussed from memory, clearly and abundantly, in the assembly of such great men, as far as I was able to remember. When Lorenzo <note>Lorenzo de' Medici ("The Magnificent"), the de facto ruler of Florence.</note> had been abundantly satisfied by these things, and the sun had now reached midday, we all rose at the urging of our most liberal host, Mariotto; and following him, we departed to refresh our bodies.

## THE THIRD BOOK OF THE CAMALDVLENSIAN DISPUTATIONS OF CHRISTOFORO LANDINO OF FLORENCE, TO THE ILLVSTRIOVS FEDERICO, PRINCE OF VRBINO, ON THE ALLEGORIES OF P. VIRGILIVS MARO.

<margin>Poet</margin>
<margin>Argument</margin>
<margin>Poet</margin>
When I had decided, most illustrious Federico <note>Federico da Montefeltro, the Duke of Urbino, a celebrated patron of the arts and a renowned military commander.</note>, to commit to writing that discourse which Leon Battista Alberti delivered—not without the greatest admiration and amazement of all who were present—concerning those fictions in which P. Virgilius <note>The Roman poet Virgil, author of the *Aeneid*.</note> hides that most profound knowledge, by which he divinely describes the supreme good of man and wonderfully expresses the way by which we may reach it, I feared lest I might fall under the censure of some men. These are people who, measuring everything by the weakness of their own wit, believe Maro <note>A common name for Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note> himself composed nothing but little stories to delight the idle ears of listeners, and think that we have invented everything we say according to our own whim.

Indeed, if they would reflect upon what poets are, how ancient their origin appears, and if they would consider how great and varied the learning is in which many have flourished in that art, they would surely recognize that which we see confirmed by the judgment of the gravest philosophers: that there has been no kind of writers who have been equal to poets either in the greatness of eloquence or the divinity of wisdom. 

For which reason, we should believe that Aristotle—a man of excellent genius and, after Plato, of altogether singular learning—was moved to affirm that in ancient times, theologians and poets were the same. And indeed, if we look more closely at what poetry itself is, it will be easy to know that it is not just one of those arts which our ancestors called "liberal" <note>The Liberal Arts: the core curriculum of classical and medieval education, including grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy.</note> because they are more excellent than the rest (in which, if any have flourished in one or the other, they have always been held in the highest esteem). Rather, poetry is something more divine. It embraces all those arts, bound by certain rhythms and proceeding by certain poetic feet, and is distinguished by various "lights" and "flowers" <note>Metaphors for rhetorical ornaments and stylistic beauty.</note>. Whatever men have done, known, or contemplated, poetry adorns these things with wondrous fictions and translates them into other forms. Thus, while poets seem to be telling something much lower and humbler, or are believed to play at mere stories to delight the ears of those at rest, they then most especially bring forth lofty things hidden in the very fountain of divinity. By which most welcome error, the listener, having finally noticed the truth, might not only arrive at the highest knowledge of things but also be drenched in wondrous pleasure from the fiction. This matter, which I consider divine rather than human...

<summary>This page concludes a philosophical debate on the nature of evil and introduces a new book dedicated to Federico da Montefeltro, defending poetry as a divine medium for hidden wisdom.</summary>

### ON THE ALLEGORIES OF P. VIRGILIUS MARO

<margin>Plato.</margin>
Whom should I believe more than Plato? For he says in the *Ion* <note>Plato’s dialogue *Ion* discusses the nature of poetic inspiration as a divine gift rather than a learned skill.</note> that poetry is not handed down by human art, but creeps into our minds by divine frenzy <note>original: "divino furore"; the Platonic concept of poetic inspiration or "madness."</note>. Moreover, in the work titled *Phaedrus*, after he had explained three other types of divine frenzy, he expressed the fourth frenzy—which he intends to be poetic—with an opinion of this sort (if I am not mistaken). For he relates that while our souls were dwelling in the celestial seats, they were participants in that harmony which exists in the eternal mind of God, and in that which is produced by the movements of the heavens. But when they were later weighed down by the desire for mortal things, and for that reason, having already fallen to lower regions, they were enclosed in bodies: then, hindered by earthly limbs and dying members, they could scarcely perceive with their ears those harmonies which are compared to human artifice. Even if these [human songs] are far removed from celestial harmony, nevertheless, because they are like certain semblances and images of it, they lead us into a silent recollection of heavenly things and inflame us with a most burning desire to fly back to our ancient fatherland, so that we may recognize true music itself, of which this is a sketched image. Meanwhile, however, as far as we are permitted in this most troublesome prison of the body, we strive to imitate that [divine music] with our own. We do this not with the modulations of voices as certain common and more frivolous musicians are accustomed—whom I would not deny can soothe the sense of hearing, but I do not concede they can provide anything beyond that. Instead, imitating divine harmony with a certain weightier judgment, they express the deep and innermost feelings of the mind in elegant song; and stirred by divine frenzy, they often bring forth things so marvelous and so far above human strength with such a grand spirit that when that frenzy has subsided a little later, they admire themselves and are struck with amazement. Therefore, they do not only flatter the ears, but soothe minds with sweet nectar and divine ambrosia. These, therefore,

<margin>Ennius.</margin>
are divine <term>vates: a Latin term for a prophet or divinely inspired poet</term> and sacred priests of the Muses; these are rightly called "holy" by Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius (c. 239–169 BC), an early Roman poet whom later writers like Virgil and Landino revered as a foundational figure.</note>. To these alone it has been granted by divine power that they may snatch the listener wherever they wish: now with a song gliding pleasantly and sweetly, now rising gravely and loftily, now rushing with a vehement impulse, now flowing in the manner of a calmed river, sometimes overflowing copiously, and sometimes proceeding briefly and concisely. For this reason, since a more divine and more vehement spirit rises within them, they are called *vates* from this kind of vehemence. The Greeks, however, called them "poets" <note>original: "poetae"</note> because among them *poiein* <note>original: "ποιειν"; the Greek verb for "to make" or "to create."</note> signifies "to make." But you will perhaps say: can not other writers of their own books also be rightly called "poets," that is, "makers"? Indeed they can. But since these [poets] alone far surpass all others both in speaking and in understanding, they have claimed for themselves as their own property that name which ought to have been common to all writers. And truly, whichever bards were worthy of this name, they seemed to possess something above human power. To this fact those ancient men can bear witness, whom it is established were poets. For among the Hebrews, Moses—a man unconquered in war, who liberated both the Egyptians from the Ethiopians <note>This refers to a tradition, found in the historian Josephus, that Moses served as a general for the Egyptians against Ethiopia before the Exodus.</note> and the Hebrews from the Egyptians—did he not encompass all divinity in his verses? For he composed a volume in verse. He was a man so ancient that when he was already eighty years old and leading the Jews out of slavery, Cecrops <note>The legendary first king and founder of Athens.</note> was reigning in Athens. For I think it is hidden from none of those Christians who are considered somewhat more learned what those things are which the Idumean Job <note>Job is often identified as being from the land of Uz/Edom (Idumea).</note> committed to his songs. But he, as may be conjectured from his book, was born in the third generation after Israel [Jacob]. Nor will I now pursue how great or of what sort are the things contained in the songs of King David, those of his son Solomon, those of Deuteronomy, or the song of Isaiah. It is altogether an excellent discovery, and

### Book Three

<margin>c u</margin>
...retained a continuously unbroken series <note>Landino is arguing that there is an ancient, unbroken tradition of using allegory to hide truth.</note>: so that not only poets, but also all other writers who committed any great matter to letters, would obscure it with various fictions and various coverings of figures <note>original: "figurarum integumentis"; this refers to the "integumentum" or "veil" of allegory used to hide philosophical meaning.</note>. For they thought it would be the case that if they rendered the task more difficult, the things they wrote would possess greater dignity and authority; and those who perceived them—attaining them not without labor and industry—would value them more highly. They would derive a greater pleasure from them if the things they held were not shared at all with the unlearned. By this reasoning, therefore, they kept the profane away from holy and sacred things. They were moved not by envy, but so that some distinction might appear between the skillful and the [dull] mind, since the idle man would not achieve the same as the studious one. For in this way, the rewards owed to the learned were set before them alone, and others were greatly encouraged to strive for those same arts when they saw they were not making progress in the remaining ones. For the wits of mortals are sharpened by the difficulty and scarcity of a thing; and **tireless labor conquers all, and poverty pressing in hard circumstances** <note>Landino is quoting Virgil’s *Georgics* (1.145–6): "Labor omnia vicit improbus et duris urgens in rebus egestas."</note>. The Greeks also followed this method of writing, among whom we have heard that Orpheus the Thracian, Musaeus the Athenian, and Linus the Theban were the most ancient. Indeed, scarcely any traces of Linus and Musaeus remain; but it is easy to recognize that the poems of Orpheus—in which many things concerning divine power and not a few concerning the nature of things are contained—were written in that form which we have described. Concerning the rest who flourished thereafter, I shall say nothing. For the fictions of the myths—by which they express the nature of the gods, or of things, or those matters which pertain to life and morals, somewhat obscurely indeed, but with the greatest dignity—make the matter manifest. Wherefore, to whom should it seem a wonder if every age, every nation, and all who have anywhere excelled in any learning, have always held poets in the highest esteem? For, to omit the others for the present, how many and how 
<margin>Aristotle</margin>
great passages in philosophy does Aristotle, so great a man, strengthen by the testimony of poets! Indeed, if he had not attributed so much to them, he would never have written two books on poets, nor three books on the <term>Art of Poetry</term> <note>Aristotle’s *Poetics*, a foundational text of literary theory.</note> with such great accuracy. Moreover, how highly 
<margin>Plato</margin>
Plato values this class of men, he himself easily shows in the book on the *Republic*, since he affirms that nothing penetrates the innermost parts of the mind more vehemently than poetry. But someone will say: does not the same Plato, in the book on the *Laws*, judge that poetry ought to be rejected? He does this nowhere. Rather, he advises that those parts should be rejected which, in the tragic manner, imitate disturbed souls; but that which sings the praises of the gods, describes the ancestral institutions, teaches morals, extols honorable men, and casts down the wicked, he commands to be accepted. Finally, in some places, you may find a certain kind of poet disparaged by this philosopher. But when does he not extol poetry itself as divine? When those who condemn our plan have carefully considered these things with themselves, I believe they will change their opinion. However, if they still wish to pluck at us, let them rather argue us of rashness—because we have attempted things which are beyond our strength—rather than think that we have seen something which Maro <note>P. Virgilius Maro, known commonly as Virgil.</note> did not see. For myself, although I do not claim so much for myself that I can explain the divinity of this poet sufficiently for his dignity, I nevertheless thought it would not be useless if our industry, however small it may be, might rouse more learned men to demonstrate greater things concerning the *Aeneid*. For they, understanding that we could not do everything, might be incited either by a not entirely unjust indignation against us, or by a beneficial co...

He argues that Virgil’s Aeneid contains deep divine mysteries and hopes his commentary will inspire others to study the poet more deeply.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dedicatory preface of Book 3. Landino is addressing Federico da Montefeltro, Duke of Urbino, praising his patronage of the arts before transitioning into the recorded dialogue featuring Leon Battista Alberti and Lorenzo de' Medici.</meta>

-># On the Allegories of Publius Virgilius Maro<-

...nature toward those eager for this kind of learning might be stirred; and let them correct our errors and add whatever may be missing. In which matter, I will not only bear being corrected with a calm mind, but I even now earnestly beg all who are able to do so to do it. For since I think it most characteristic of a man both to freely show others what he has seen and to zealously learn what he does not know—since in the former he benefits others and in the latter himself—I have so ordered my life that I may both pour out most liberally whatever is in me and not disdain to receive from any mortal those things I lack. 

To whom, however, should I rather write these things of ours, such as they are, than to you, most Illustrious Federico? You have always been, beyond all others, most devoted to Maro <note>original: "Maronis"; referring to Virgil, whose full name was Publius Virgilius Maro.</note>. And while the other princes of Italy place all their industry in acquiring the greatest possible treasures for themselves, so that their heaps of gold and silver grow more and more every day, you most liberally pour out the greatest part of your wealth for the ornament of the Muses <note>The Muses were the Greek goddesses of inspiration in literature, science, and the arts.</note> and of those who worship the Muses. 

Thus, just as the Homeric Agamemnon <note>The legendary leader of the Greeks in the Trojan War.</note> was confident that if ten other Nestors <note>The aged and wise counselor of the Greeks, known for his eloquence.</note> were present with him, Troy would soon be captured; so we may hold it as certain that if the Italian peoples had—I will not say ten, as he did—but just two more Federicos, all Italy would shortly become a second Athens. 

But of other matters elsewhere; for we have not come to this discourse at this time to criticize anyone, but to honor you with the greatest praise we can, you who have so deserved well of men of letters. For although you alone of all who are established in power uphold these parts, nevertheless your liberality toward all learned men is so wide-reaching that no fewer poets, orators, and writers of all things will come forth from it than those whom that Pope Nicholas V <note>Pope Nicholas V (reigned 1447–1455) was a great patron of the Renaissance and founder of the Vatican Library.</note> (whom we saw) once provoked with his most beautiful gifts and greatest rewards. These men, indeed, stirred to study by your benefit, will claim glory for themselves through their learning and eloquence; and they will keep you—such a patron of the Muses—alive in eternal and ever-fresh memory, even then when the fame of many princes (who live now or will rule in the future) lies buried. 

Truly, since these things are clearer than any light, I do not think they need to be pursued further, especially as the matter itself now demands that I recount the divine discourse of the most learned man, Battista <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous architect and philosopher who is the primary speaker in Landino's dialogue.</note>, in its proper order, as much as my memory can recall. For when we had come early in the morning to the accustomed place, and were hanging upon his words in the greatest silence, inflamed with a wondrous desire for hearing, he began the disputation with a beginning of this sort:

"Since you desire, Lorenzo <note>Lorenzo de' Medici (the Magnificent), the ruler of Florence and a close friend of Landino.</note>, that the mind of that poet be opened to you—who, whether from all the regions whose inhabitants history knows, or from all the centuries that have reached our memory through the benefit of writers, stands if not as the first, then certainly as an equal and peer to the first—I cannot but be deeply disturbed at the start of my speech by the magnitude of such a great matter. No one imbued with even moderate learning has ever doubted that this man surpasses eloquence itself (if I may speak so) in the power and abundance of his speech. 

For since there are three <margin>three [styles]</margin> figures or characters in speech <note>These are the three levels of style in classical rhetoric: the humble (plain), the middle (intermediate), and the grand (high) style.</note>, in any one of which if someone excels, he attains the greatest glory. Who does not see that he [Virgil] has not only fulfilled each one in his individual volumes, but has often so fused and mixed them all in a few verses that, by a certain wondrous temperament, just as if different voices in a harmony..."

<summary>Landino dedicates this book to Federico da Montefeltro, praising him as a peerless patron of the arts compared to other Italian princes. The text then transitions into a dialogue where Leon Battista Alberti begins a lecture on Virgil's supreme mastery of poetic styles.</summary>

-># Book Three<-

<margin>Style</margin>
...produce the most <note>The text begins mid-sentence, completing a thought on Virgil's literary composition.</note> noble result, it yields incredible pleasure to the ears. From four types of speaking, he has so woven his work that neither abundance is lacking for leisure, nor brevity for business. You will see some parts please by their very dryness and austerity, while others truly delight as if illuminated and distinguished by little flowers. In short, all things are constructed with such artistry that you will find no other writing from which you might better take examples for every kind of <term>elocution</term> <note>The art of stylistic delivery and word choice in rhetoric.</note> than from here. Add to this his knowledge of history; add how diligent an investigator he was of antiquity—not only of our own Roman affairs, but also of the Greeks and of all nations. Consider how observant he was of ancient words, how elegantly he formed certain new ones himself, and how appropriately he grasped the power of them all. I pass over **civil law**; I omit **pontifical law**; I say nothing of the **law of the augurs** <note>original: "ius civile... ius pontificium... iure augurum"; Virgil was famously well-versed in the various branches of Roman law and religious ritual.</note>—all of which he held in such mastery that he seems not to have received them from others, but to have established them himself.

Therefore, if you were to ask me to explain these and similar things to you, and if you were to contend that I should more diligently open up each individual passage in the poet, you would be imposing a task upon me that is both laborious and difficult. For who would not judge those things to be most beautiful, excellent, and hidden with the highest artistry? Yet, they have nevertheless been made clear by many equally learned men. 

But what you ask for is something much more divine: it lies more hidden in the shadows, and to my knowledge, no one has yet laid it open in its proper sequence. It is something that neither the grammarian nor the rhetorician would know, but must be unearhed from the <margin>Opinion</margin> innermost secrets of **philosophy**. For you wish to know what <term>Maro</term> <note>The family name of the poet Virgil.</note> intended through those enigmas of his regarding the **wanderings of Aeneas**, and regarding that man's journey into Italy. 

To be sure, there are some who, while they admire those things I mentioned a moment ago as the greatest merits and think the poet's highest and most absolute praise is contained within them, suspect nothing greater in the seer. Yet if you ask these people what Virgil wished to accomplish in this matter, they will assert that he proposed to himself the imitation of **Homer**. They will add that neither talent nor learning was lacking to him to prevent him from excelling in that. Once they have granted us this, they must utterly yield. For we have (to omit the almost infinite crowd of grammarians) many of the most serious philosophers who contend that Homer drank in all **Egyptian learning**, and shadowed it forth in his usual way with various fictions. <margin>Basil</margin> If Aristotle had not been of this opinion, he would never have written six books on the ambiguities of Homer. Indeed, what **Basil** <note>Saint Basil the Great (c. 330–379 AD), a Church Father who wrote on the usefulness of Greek literature for Christians.</note>—called "the Great" for both the magnitude of his learning and the sanctity of his character—thought of the man is easy to judge; he said that the whole of Homer's poetry consists of the praise of virtue. He followed, as I believe, <margin>Anaxagoras & Archesilas</margin> **Anaxagoras of Clazomenae**, who indeed affirmed the same regarding this poet. **Archesilas**, truly the founder of the Middle Academy, attributed so much to Homer that he never withdrew to his bed without first reading something from him; he even used to say he was "going to his lover" when he went to read him, so that he might have a greater abundance of his reading.

<margin>Plato</margin> But why should I collect the rest now, when there is nothing that cannot be proven by the testimony of **Plato** alone? He, therefore, in that volume which he wrote concerning the **Highest Good** <note>original: "summo bono"; likely referring to the *Republic* or the *Philebus*.</note>, affirms that all arts, whether they be divine or human, have flowed into the single poem of Homer as if into their own proper receptacle. Therefore, Maro, noticing that the learning of this man (drawn from the springs of the Egyptian priests) held a very similar logic to the **Platonists**—of whom Virgil was a most zealous student—admired it to such an extent that he wished to accomplish the same in his own **Aeneas** that the other had previously fashioned in **Ulysses**. For this reason, he shaped for us, through most beautiful poetic fictions, a man who, having been gradually purified of many and great vices, and thereafter...

<summary>Landino argues that Virgil’s Aeneid is not merely a masterpiece of style and law, but a profound philosophical allegory inspired by Homer and Plato. He suggests that Aeneas represents a man being purified of vice, mirroring the hidden "Egyptian wisdom" found in Homeric epic.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Book 3 of Landino’s Disputationes Camaldulenses. Having previously praised Virgil’s command of language and law, Landino now shifts to the philosophical core of his argument: that the Aeneid is an allegory for the soul’s journey toward the "highest good."</meta>

-># On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro<-

## On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro

...distinguished by wondrous virtues, he might nevertheless attain that which is the **highest good** <note>original: "summum bonum"; the ultimate goal of human existence in ancient philosophy.</note> for man, which none but the wise can achieve. But since he had learned from Plato that this consists in the contemplation of divine things, he also learned that one cannot arrive there until our minds are thoroughly purified by those virtues concerning life and morals.

<margin>Socrates.</margin>
Since Socrates himself says it is not right for the pure to be touched by the impure: For this reason, Virgil has not only wondrously expressed the goals of the "goods" for us, but he has also demonstrated by what path or by what method a man may finally succeed in escaping to that place. He did this so that no part of that philosophy—which the Greeks call **ethics** <term>ethics</term> <note>original: "ethicen"; derived from the Greek "ethos," meaning character or custom.</note> and we call "concerning life and morals"—should be overlooked. For in this study, we seek nothing other than, first, the goals of good and evil, and second, the **duties** <note>original: "officia"; moral obligations or appropriate actions.</note> by which we are led to them as if by a certain path. This is a truly laborious task and full of every difficulty; yet it is divine, and by it alone a man is made both happy and wise, and is joined to God. For it is permitted only to the wise man to be joined to God to such an extent that nothing can intervene to separate them. For God is **Truth**. Who, then, would not know that he who does not reach the truth with his mind cannot possibly be wise? Now, since there are four things we must investigate to uncover the writer’s mind, I think it will serve our purpose to define certain boundaries now, which we must not overstep in interpreting the poet.

<margin>History</margin>
The first is when we seek what was actually done, which they call **history**; as when we read in Maro <note>A common name for the poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note> that not far from there, "swift chariots had torn Mettius asunder." <note>A reference to Aeneid 8.642, describing the punishment of Mettius Fufetius for treachery.</note>

<margin>Etymology.</margin>
We likewise seek not what was done, but for what reason it was done; as in the line: **"But you, Alban, should have stood by your words."** <note>original: "At tu dictis albane maneres"; Aeneid 8.643. Landino is arguing that the reason for the punishment (the etymology of the event) is found in the broken oath.</note> For in that place, he demonstrates that the king of the Albans was torn apart by chariots for the reason that he had not remained faithful to his word. This, the Greeks call **etymology**.

<margin>Analogy.</margin>
In the third place, we ask whether the things being said are in conflict with one another. For instance, in one place Christ says the Father is greater than He; in another, "I and the Father are one." Therefore, when we interpret these things in such a way as to show they do not disagree in the least, we are following **analogy**.

<margin>Allegory.</margin>
Finally, something is interpreted through **allegory**, which occurs when we do not understand exactly what the words signify on the surface, but rather something else obscured under a figure. Poets write that stones were moved by **Amphion’s lyre** <note>In Greek mythology, Amphion's music was so beautiful it moved stones to build the walls of Thebes.</note> so that they came together of their own accord to form the structure of the walls of Thebes. Through this fiction, what else do we understand but that it was achieved by the eloquence of a most wise man? By his speech, the people of Boeotia—who until then were as stupid as stones toward all reason and most hardened against all humanity—came out of the woods and dens into the city, and finally, of their own accord, subjected themselves to the laws that had been established for the common good. We, therefore, will omit the other three types at this time and will concern ourselves with **allegory** alone, so that we may see what he intends by **Troy**, what by **Aeneas**, what by **Italy**, and other things of this sort.

<margin>We understand the way <unclear>which you know</unclear>.</margin>
**Aeneas**, therefore, originates from **Troy**; by which city we rightly understand, I believe, the first age of man. In this age, since all reason is still asleep, the **senses** alone reign. And mortals themselves—because at that age they do not even suspect the existence of wisdom—propose for themselves only those things which philosophers call the "primary things of nature." For since every living creature is commended to itself by nature, it loves itself first; then, it desires to have all parts of its body so whole and strong that they are at once useful and beautiful to it. But above all, it is soothed by **pleasures**; and although it understands itself to be both soul and body at once, and desires both to be safe, nevertheless, in those things which are to be sought in the soul, since...

<summary>Landino defines four methods of interpretation—History, Etymology, Analogy, and Allegory—and announces he will focus on Allegory to explain the Aeneid. He begins by identifying Troy as a symbol for the first stage of human life, dominated by the senses rather than reason.</summary>
<keywords>highest good, ethics, Socrates, Plato, four senses of interpretation, allegory, Aeneas, Troy, Amphion, human development</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Book 3 of Landino’s Camaldolese Disputations. The previous page established that Virgil’s Aeneid is an allegory for the soul's journey toward the "highest good" through the purification of the mind and the practice of ethics. Here, the dialogue shifts to the pivotal moment when a human transition from childhood instinct to adult reason.</meta>

-># Book Three<-

[The soul] does not yet clearly know those higher things and labors less for them; rather, it anxiously seeks those things that benefit the body and bodily pleasures. For these are most familiar to it from its very birth. Therefore, since in this stage of life we are pulled by the force of nature rather than being masters of our own actions, we leave very little or even no room at all for virtue or vice, because what we do is neither voluntary nor done with any true choice. Thus, no one would say there is virtue in a child.

But when, with the passage of time, our mind begins to be illuminated by some light of reason, only then does enough judgment appear in us that we are able to discern right from wrong. For now, one has arrived at that "crossroads of the Pythagorean letter" <note>original: "pythagoricæ litteræ biuium"; the philosopher Pythagoras used the Greek letter 'Y' as a symbol for the crossroads of life, where the stem represents childhood and the two branches represent the paths of Virtue and Vice.</note> and that "error ignorant of life," as it says in the works of Persius: *It led trembling minds into the branching crossroads.* <note>Persius, Satire 3, line 56. This refers to the moment a young person must choose their moral direction.</note> From that point, once we depart, we must either proceed straight ahead or turn off to the left. For whatever we do from then on—since we do it with a certain degree of reason—is credited to virtue if it is right, or to vice if it is otherwise.

Thus, both Aeneas and Paris are found at Troy together. But the latter, because he deemed it necessary to put pleasure (Venus) before virtue (Pallas), had to perish along with Troy. The former, however, with Venus as his guide and mother, extricates himself from the entire conflagration. What else should we understand by this, except that those who are inflamed by a great love and are driven toward the knowledge of truth are able to achieve all things easily? Therefore, we shall rightly interpret "Venus" here as **divine love**.

> But you, **LORENZO**, seem to have wanted to say something for some time now.

<margin>Justly</margin>

> "I do indeed desire to," said **LORENZO**, "did I not fear to break the continuous thread of your discourse."

> "On the contrary," said **BAPTISTA** <note>Leon Battista Alberti, the famous Renaissance architect and polymath, who serves as a primary speaker in this dialogue.</note>: "For since this entire conversation was not established by us to show off our wit, nor to acquire glory, but so that I might comply with your most honorable wish—and if there is any learning in me, I will pour it out gladly—ask, interrupt, object, and refute at your own judgment. For by this path, the truth which we seek will appear more clearly."

> "I will use the judgment you permit me," said **LORENZO**, "though for the sake of my own education, not for refuting you. I wonder, therefore, why you interpret 'Venus' as love—especially that love which is not only chaste, but even divine. For I see Venus used in this way not only among the poets, but also among other writers, as meaning nothing other than the union of male and female. Hence that line of Terence: *Without Ceres and Bacchus, Venus grows cold* <note>A common ancient proverb meaning that without food (Ceres) and wine (Bacchus), lust (Venus) cools down.</note>. And Virgil himself says in the Eclogues: *Gifts are ready for my Venus* <note>Referring to a lover.</note>. Therefore, if you define Venus as this kind of union, everything you have said so far will seem to contradict itself.

> "But there is also something else that I cannot explain at all unless you make it clear to me. How does it happen that since Aeneas and Paris are both men, one must perish with Troy because he puts Venus before Pallas, while the other escapes all danger unharmed because he obeys the instructions of Venus? I do not see why, if Venus is 'good,' she should harm Paris, or if she is 'bad,' she should benefit Aeneas. While I think on these things, I am drawn instead to the opinion of those who refer this whole matter to the star called by that name, and to history itself. For they think—and I am sure this has not escaped you—that at the hour Aeneas set out from Troy toward Italy, the sign of Libra, which is the 'house' of Venus, had risen in our hemisphere. And that Venus herself was joined with Jupiter in the middle of the sky."

<margin>
An opinion
</margin>

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici questions the allegorical interpretation of Venus as "divine love," noting that classical authors usually use her name to represent physical lust or sexual union. He further challenges the internal logic of the allegory, asking why Venus leads one man to ruin and another to safety, suggesting an astrological explanation instead.</summary>

We now move into a discussion of the astrological and allegorical significance of the gods—specifically Jupiter and Saturn—and how the poet Virgil balances historical tradition with allegorical meaning.</meta>

<header>On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro</header>

# On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro

<margin>The Moon especially governs life.</margin>
By all these things, happiness was portended for him, and it was signaled that kingship would come to him through a woman. For not only does Homer signify that Jupiter presides over kingdoms—since kings <gloss>seem to be</gloss> <note>original: "videntur"</note> as he writes, "nourished by Jove"—but the mathematicians <term>mathematicians</term> <note>original: "mathematici"; in this period, the term usually referred to astrologers who calculated planetary positions.</note> say the same thing. For Jupiter is an entirely healthy star: because it is placed between the cold of Saturn and the heat of Mars, it possesses the best temperament; and for that reason, it is favorable for all outcomes. For since the Sun and Moon especially govern our life, Jupiter is the most healthful for us because it is joined to the Sun in all numbers, and to the Moon in the most. They also report that at the beginning of the world’s construction, Jupiter was in Aries, which was the ascending sign at that time. They wish for him to represent laws, chastity, and mercy toward the needy and those oppressed by calamity. He makes men truthful and true friends without fraud or deceit; he breaks the cruelty of Saturn and whatever evils that planet brings; Jupiter either removes or diminishes them. Therefore, Persius wisely wrote: *And we break heavy Saturn together with our Jupiter.* <note>Persius, Satire 5, line 50. The poet suggests that one's personal "Jupiter" (reason or a good nature) can overcome the "Saturnine" influence of a harsh fate.</note> Finally, if Jupiter is well-positioned at one’s birth, it makes that person fortunate.

"These things do not displease me at all," said BAPTISTA <note>Baptista Alberti, one of the participants in the dialogue.</note>. "For they are drawn from the highest learning and are strongly suited to the history. But since our entire discussion does not concern the logic of history, but rather attempts to express that which I so often name by the Greek word <term>allegory</term> <note>original: "allegoriam"; a narrative where characters and events symbolize deeper moral, spiritual, or physical truths.</note>, I do not see why the interpretation I have applied cannot be rightfully accepted. For if, omitting what ancient writers handed down about Aeneas and the other Trojans, the poet had been permitted not only to invent but also to subvert, add, and subtract at his will—if, finally, having no regard for history, he only attempted to show how he might shape a man through Aeneas who would eventually be wise and blessed—he might have used not Venus but perhaps Cupid or some other divinity. But since he had decided to follow a poetic fiction in such a way as to not depart from history—since he had read that Venus was Aeneas’s mother and showed herself as a leader of the exile to her seafaring son—he had to complete the task from the elements that were present, not by inventing names. For this is more work for the poet than for others who wish to obscure a matter with a different fiction. For those other poets are free from all history and devise things at will which better fit the promotion of their subject."

To make this clearer, I think one example out of many should be put forward. It pleased the first author of this fable to show that those things which are born from matter in time all fall into destruction, except for the four elements which are the principles for generating all things. Therefore, he invented two gods, Saturn and Ops <margin>manuscript</margin> <note>The marginal "ms" likely refers to a textual variant in the manuscript the printer was using.</note>, and intended for Saturn to represent a symbol of Time, as the Greek name indicates. For *Cronos*, which is Saturn, is derived by removing the aspiration [the 'h'] from what they call *Chronos*. <note>The author is using a common Renaissance etymology: Saturn (Latin) is Cronus (Greek), which sounds like Chronos (the Greek word for Time).</note> And who does not know that "time" is called *chronos* in Greek? Through Saturn, therefore, he understands Time; through Ops (or Rhea), he understands the Earth. He then adds that Saturn devoured all the children he had received from Rhea except for Jupiter, Juno, Neptune, and Pluto. By this fable, he expresses that all things made of matter are worn away by time and led to destruction, except for the four elements themselves. <note>In this physical allegory, Jupiter represents Air, Juno represents Air/Earth, Neptune represents Water, and Pluto represents Fire or Earth.</note> To what end is this, so that I don't pursue the rest of the story? Namely, so you may understand that it would have been permitted for this man to invent whatever he wanted at will so he could conveniently express what he felt about the procreation of things, since he had proposed nothing for himself other than a small part of <term>physics</term> <note>original: "phyſices"; here meaning natural philosophy or the study of the physical world.</note>. But the logic of Maro <note>Publius Virgilius Maro, known as Virgil.</note> is far different, who, when he takes up the deeds of Aeneas for praise...

<summary>This page explains the astrological benefits of Jupiter as a counter to Saturn's cruelty and shifts into a discussion on the nature of allegory. It uses the myth of Saturn devouring his children to illustrate how time consumes all material things except the four eternal elements.</summary>
<keywords>Jupiter, Saturn, astrology, allegory, Virgil, Aeneas, Chronos, four elements, Baptista Alberti, Persius</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of Virgil’s Aeneid. While the previous page focused on the astrological significance of Jupiter as a symbol of law and mercy, we now shift to the figure of Venus. The author explains that Virgil does not merely follow historical tradition by making Venus the mother of Aeneas, but uses her to represent different levels of Platonic love and the soul's journey toward divine beauty.</meta>

### Book Three

...to adorn Augustus: and he took it upon himself to illuminate the book itself with all the lights of poetry—not those which he chose by his own whim, but those which history provides—he achieves this supreme praise of his own genius. A truly marvelous man who weaves his work not from what he wished for, but from what was given to him: so that while he does not abandon history in the least, he nevertheless expresses human happiness through that incredible covering <note>original: "integumento"; a common Renaissance term for allegory, literally a "covering" or "veil" that hides deeper truths.</note>. You have (I believe) the reason why he was forced to represent Venus as divine love. This proceeds so correctly that it cannot be criticized except by the unfair. For we see that Plato, in that discourse which he calls the *Phaedrus*, makes Aphrodite—whom we call Venus—preside not only over lustful love but also over divine love. Indeed, we will easily understand which Venus the Platonic poet <note>The author refers to Virgil as a "Platonic poet," believing the Aeneid contains hidden Platonic philosophy.</note> intended to be the mother of Aeneas if we trace certain things a little more deeply from Plato himself. Pausanias, therefore, in the *Symposium*, commemorates two Venuses: one celestial, the other vulgar. He relates that the first was born from heaven and has no mother. By this fiction, he signifies that Intelligence which is placed in the angelic mind, which is seized by an inborn love to understand the beauty of God; because she is far from all companionship with matter, he says she was produced without a mother. He attributes the second Venus, however, to the Soul of the World <term>Soul of the World</term> <note>original: "anima mundi"; the vital principle that animates the physical universe in Platonic thought.</note>: writing that she was born from Jove as father and Dione as mother. For she flows from that power which is in the soul of the world, and she creates the power which generates all these lower things and enters the forest of the world <note>original: "mundi syluam"; "sylva" (forest) was a common philosophical term for primeval matter (Greek: "hyle").</note>. Therefore, both are seized by an inborn love: that celestial one toward the contemplation of God's beauty, and this second one so that she may shape that same beauty out of the "forest" of matter. 

But these things are of little use unless applied: our soul, since it also possesses certain similar powers of understanding and generating, is likewise said to have two Venuses, who are accompanied by twin Cupids. For when bodily beauty is presented to our eyes, our mind (which is the first Venus) admires and loves it—not because it is bodily, but because it is an image of divine beauty; and by this, it is raised up to the heavens as if by a certain path. However, the power of generating (which is the second Venus) desires to create a form similar to this beauty. Therefore, each love is rightly described: the one as a desire for contemplating beauty, the other for generating it. No one, therefore, unless they are devoid of all reason, will dare to condemn these two loves, since each is necessary to human nature. For the race of mortals could not exist for long without the propagation of offspring, nor could it exist *well* without the investigation of truth. Therefore, with that more excellent Venus as his guide, Aeneas was able to reach Italy.

<margin>objection</margin>
But you will say: why did this second Venus, if she is good, harm Paris? It is because he used her badly. For a man more greedy for generating than right reason dictates—and occupied in that matter more than is proper—is 

<margin>Sign</margin>
drowned in bodily pleasures alone. Thus it happens that he entirely abandons the first Venus, who leads to the highest good, and most wickedly abuses the second. Because of this, he falls into all the perturbations of the soul, and having despaired of truth and become miserable, he descends into every indignity. Divine love is, as I have said if we believe Plato, the desire to return from bodily beauty to the contemplation of the divine. Yet, when we desire the divine, we do not despise that beauty which is perceived by the eyes. For whoever seeks something must necessarily be delighted by the image of that thing which he seeks. But there are some of such dull wit that they can in no way call the mind away from the senses: these do not know true beauty. This kind of love is therefore adulterous and degenerates from the truth; it is what we call wantonness and impudence...

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic allegory of the "Two Venuses"—Celestial and Vulgar—arguing that Virgil uses Venus to represent the soul's dual drive toward divine contemplation and virtuous procreation.</summary>
<keywords>Venus, Plato, Symposium, Aeneid, allegory, Divine Love, Vulgar Love, Anima Mundi, Virgil, Paris, beauty</keywords>

### On the Allegories of P. Virgil M. <note>original: "In.P.Virg.M.Allego."; abbreviation for "In Publius Virgilius Maro Allegoriae"</note>

<insert><note>Institutional stamp: NATIONAL LIBRARY</note></insert>

always accompanies: which they define as the desire for that pleasure which is perceived from bodily form. They are correct who call it the heat of a soul dead in its own body and living in another's; following this, a certain poet said as much. Plato truly says that this love is born from human ailments and is full of anxiety. For who would not see that it possesses neither counsel nor any limit within itself? In it, there are injuries, suspicions, and all those other plagues which the faithful servant wisely pointed out to Phaedria in Terence <note>Reference to the character Parmeno in Terence’s play *The Eunuch*, who warns his master Phaedria about the irrationality of love.</note>. You have (as I think) a twofold love: that true and divine one of which I spoke a little while ago, and this false and adulterous one. This latter love is to the true one as a flatterer is to a friend, or as a cook is to a doctor. Indeed, when Paris gave himself entirely to this love, he perished together with Troy. Aeneas, however, with that heavenly leader <note>Referring to the celestial Venus, or Divine Love.</note> gradually extricating himself from the Trojan fire—that is, from the heat of bodily pleasures—even if not by a direct voyage (for it is either never or rarely granted to the human condition to cast off folly and become wise at the same time), nevertheless, after many wanderings, he reached Italy, which signifies true wisdom.

No one will ever fully complete this voyage—since it is most full of sweat and labor—unless, kindled by the highest love of that wisdom, he is prepared to endure every difficulty. For true Love, as the speech of Eryximachus shows in the same Plato <note>In Plato's *Symposium*, the physician Eryximachus argues that Love is a universal force governing nature and harmony.</note>, is the creator and preserver of all natural things; for by it, all similar things are drawn to those which are like themselves in eternal concord. It is likewise the master of all the greatest arts. For no one either invents an art or learns one invented by another unless the delight of investigation and the desire for learning incite them. Our Virgil signifies this very thing—if not openly, then somewhat obscurely, as is the custom of poets. For when in the <term>georgics</term> <note>Virgil's "Georgics" is a poem about agriculture, but here interpreted as an allegory for the cultivation of the mind.</note> he says that he prefers the knowledge of truth above all other things, he demonstrates with these words that he will overcome difficulty itself by the supreme power of love:

> But as for me, first, may the sweet Muses, above all things,
> Whose sacred symbols I bear, struck by a great love,
> Receive me. <note>original: "Me uero primum dulces ante omnia musæ : Quarum sacra fero ingenti perculsus amore Accipiant." A quote from Virgil's Georgics, Book 2, lines 475-477.</note>

Therefore, he affirms that by a great love he will bear the greatest labors which must be undergone in the rites of the Muses—that is, in the acquisition of knowledge. For Divine Love meditates on nothing else, strives for nothing else, labors in no other thing, attempts nothing, and endeavors nothing, except that, having been stirred by the sight of bodily beauty, it may snatch us away toward divine beauty. For as long as our souls are submerged in bodily darkness, they do not recognize divine things except through certain shadows and likenesses which present themselves to our senses. Not only did the ancient philosophers of Greece express this—among whom I can number Pythagoras, Empedocles, and Heraclitus, but Plato far above the others—but even the Christians do not deviate from this opinion in the least. For both Paul and he who was a listener of Paul, Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A judge of the Athenian Areopagus converted by St. Paul; in the Middle Ages and Renaissance, he was believed to be the author of influential mystical texts regarding the "Celestial Hierarchy."</note>, wish for celestial and divine things (which do not fall within the reach of the senses) to be discerned through those things which are perceived by the senses. This, therefore, is that true Venus who raises our mind to divine things. Who does not know that Aeneas was born from her as his mother? His name is derived from that which is *ainos* <note>original: "æneos"; the author is using a Greek etymology, linking Aeneas to αἶνος (ainos), meaning "praise" or "fame."</note>, that is, from "praise." For who would not pursue with the highest praise a man born for all great and exalted things? Yet he himself, smoothed over by his own will, labors for a time around the defense of Troy. For Troy represents the first stages of nature, in which bodily pleasures are most vigorous...

He argues that both ancient Greek philosophers and Christian theologians agree that physical beauty is intended to lead the mind toward higher, invisible truths.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Landino’s Neoplatonic interpretation of the Aeneid. Having just discussed the two types of love—earthly and divine—the text now explains how Aeneas (representing the human soul) transitions from a life of youthful pleasure to one of seeking truth, guided by the "celestial Venus."</meta>

### Book Three
g ii

one may interpret it thus: for in the first stage of life, since reason itself cannot yet rouse itself and unfold its powers, even those who are destined to be great and admirable men are soothed by pleasure and admire the first things of nature as if they were the highest good. Since they do not yet know what divine things are, they think that life is most blessed which allows one to enjoy pleasure. These people, therefore, have not yet discovered what the highest good is. But when they are inflamed with the greatest ardor to acquire it, they gradually notice that all these things I called the "first things of nature" are fleeting and momentary. For true love has this power, as I said a little while ago: it vehemently sharpens the mind and becomes its master in discovering things, so that nothing can remain hidden from it. For this reason, <note>Reference to Virgil, Aeneid 4.296: "Who could deceive a lover?"</note> "who could deceive that excellent lover?" Then, since the lover thinks nothing is difficult as long as the beloved thing is attained, he endures all labors and overcomes all difficulties. 

This is that Venus—not the common one which, mixed with matter, has the power of procreation—but the celestial one, removed from all matter, which belongs to our mind. She rouses the mind itself and reveals her light to us (previously unknown to us) in the "night"—for that "night" is our ignorance. She confesses herself to be a goddess, for by revealing herself she demonstrates her divinity. She warns that Troy—that is, the origin of the body—cannot be saved, for it must perish. This same Venus shows that bodily pleasures are corrupted not only by the <unclear>Lacaena</unclear> <note>Likely referring to Helen of Troy, "the Spartan woman," symbolizing destructive physical beauty.</note>—that is, by themselves, as we said in yesterday’s disputation—but also by Juno, Pallas, and the other gods. For who does not know that the gods destroy Troy? Indeed, all divine things are opposed to pleasures. 

But Pallas is foremost in this. For she holds the symbol of wisdom. Wisdom not only despises pleasures but utterly dreads them. Nor should anyone doubt Juno; although she is considered the goddess of kingdoms and therefore seems more inclined toward these fleeting and mortal things, nevertheless, when men (through the desire for command) refuse to endure no labor, they declare war on all pleasures, provided they might reach the point where they can rule over others. 

Aeneas, however, does not see the gods at all while he fights for pleasure. For all his vision is snatched away by the darkness of clouds. Our souls were created by God in such a way that by their own nature they might easily attain all truth. But from bodily matter, which philosophers call <term>sylva</term> <note>Latin for "wood" or "forest," used here to translate the Greek "hyle," referring to the chaotic primordial matter that traps the soul.</note>, all evils come to us. For matter slows, dulls, and perturbs our minds, obscuring them with darkness. If all vices proceed from ignorance, then—as Chrysippus and the rest of the Stoics say—all perturbations of the soul arise from false opinions. This was perceived long ago by that Mercury whom the Greeks call <term>Trimaximum</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus ("Thrice-Greatest"), the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with hermetic philosophy and alchemy.</note> because of his divine genius. If, therefore, all vices proceed from ignorance, and ignorance comes from bodily gloom (as Plato thinks), then all vices come from the body. I would say this was the chief reason why the Mercury I named a moment ago calls <term>sylva</term> "malignity"; but a more convenient place for discussing matter <note>original: "sylva"</note> will be given a little later. 

Aeneas, therefore, fights for a life of pleasure, and being submerged in it, he cannot see the gods. But when, in such misery, the love of investigating the truth is not lacking, that love itself is able to rouse the mind so that, collecting itself, it may scatter the darkness and see by which divine powers Troy is being overturned. Led by that same love, he takes a safe path through the middle of flames and enemies. And truly, for one wishing to advance toward arduous things, there is a certain wonderful pleasure here: on one side is the desire for the things to be let go, like a certain flame, and on the other, the difficulty of the labors...

<summary>Landino provides a Neoplatonic allegory where Troy represents the physical body and Aeneas represents the soul. He explains that "Celestial Venus" (divine love) helps the soul recognize that matter (sylva) and ignorance prevent us from seeing divine truth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic commentary on Virgil’s Aeneid. Having established that "divine Venus" (the desire for truth) has appeared to Aeneas, the text now explains his departure from Troy (the body) and the internal struggle between reason and the physical senses, personified by his father, Anchises.</meta>

### In the Allegories of P. Virgil M.

...and the terror of being cultivated <note>original: "cultatumq; terror"—likely referring to the frightening labor of self-improvement or the fear of being overcome by base instincts</note> which constantly fights against honor, set themselves in opposition. All these things yield to Aeneas under the guidance of Venus. For unless love were present, we would neither be able to despise such alluring pleasures nor overcome such harsh difficulties. He comes home, therefore, so that he may put his entire household in order and then depart from the city. For the mind returns into itself and arranges all its various powers and virtues in their proper order for the departure—which is, in fact, the journey toward the knowledge of truth, which he could never attain in Troy. All things would have succeeded according to his prayer if his father were willing to follow his son. But Anchises says he will not depart from Troy.

<margin>
Anchises refuses <unclear>to go</unclear>
</margin>

Indeed, if you ask me what this signifies, I believe it is this: Aeneas is born of parents such that Venus is a goddess and Anchises is a mortal. For a human being consists of a mind, which is immortal and divine, and a body, which is mortal and liable to fall quickly into ruin. The Mind, therefore, always looks up to its origin, and desiring to return to it, most eagerly abandons Troy <note>In this allegory, Troy represents the material body or the state of being trapped in physical existence.</note>. The Senses, however, which belong to the body, prefer bodily things to the incorporeal. From this, therefore, arises that constant and fierce struggle—the **spirit against the flesh**, as our people <note>A reference to the Christian scriptures, specifically St. Paul in Galatians 5:17.</note> say—whenever the mind tries to draw the whole man toward divine things and desires to bring the senses under its power and make them obedient to itself. On the contrary, the senses, drunk on the dreg-filled drink of the elements and weighed down by Lethean <term>Lethean: relating to the river Lethe in the underworld, which caused forgetfulness</term> forgetfulness, desire nothing but what is fleeting and earthly.

Anchises, therefore—that is, the earthly father and that which is called **sensuality** by Christians using a somewhat common term—refuses to leave Troy. For the senses would rather perish than be deprived of pleasure. Soon, however, when he hears that his son and his whole house—that is, the entire man—is about to perish, and when he is warned of better things by heavenly omens, he changes his mind. He is then lifted up and carried out by Aeneas. For this softest and most weakened part of the soul never carries itself toward the **Highest Good**, but is rather carried there by the mind. This much regarding Anchises. But Aeneas, when he had now escaped the dangers of fire and arms and had exited the city unharmed,

<margin>
Aeneas [finds] a huge number of companions
</margin>

found to his wonder that a huge number of new companions had flocked together. They had indeed gathered from all sides, ready in spirit and resources for whatever lands he wished to lead them to across the sea. And rightly so. For when the mind is finally liberated from the slavery and smoke of the fire of pleasures, and is now eager to find the right and the true, then the many powers of the soul—which until now lay numb with laziness—are vehemently awakened; they follow the well-instructed mind wherever it calls. At that very time, so that Aeneas might not wander from the right path: *Now the morning star was rising over the ridges of highest Ida, leading the day.* <note>Quoting Virgil, Aeneid 2.801–802.</note> For **Lucifer** is the star of Venus. Setting aside the sun and moon, it is the brightest of all the five stars which our people call "wandering" and the Greeks call **planets**. It completes the zodiac in three hundred and forty-eight days, never departing further from the sun than forty-six degrees of a single sign. Truly, because it sometimes precedes and sometimes follows the sun, the ancients believed it was not the same star but two. They say Pythagoras was the first among the Greeks to discover the truth of it. Therefore, when it precedes the sun, it is called **Lucifer** (the light-bringer); but when it follows, it is called **Vesperus** (the evening star).

Correctly, then, Lucifer is the precursor to the sun. For the star of Venus—which is the **love of finding the truth**—rises for him who now abandons a life subject to pleasure. It leads the day, for such love awakens Reason, by whose light we are enabled to know the truth. Moreover, it appears from Mount Ida, which is to say, from **Beauty**. For among the Greeks, *Idos* <gloss>εἶδος - form or appearance</gloss>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of Aeneas's departure. Having left Troy (the material body), Aeneas now enters the "high sea," which the commentator interprets as the turbulent realm of human emotions and desires.</meta>

### Third Book
->g iii<-

signifies form. Furthermore, Love is defined by Plato as the desire for beauty. <margin>Love</margin> Therefore, within this love, shame calls us away from shameful acts, while the desire for excellence hurries us toward all things honorable. Thus Aeneas, as an exile, is carried onto the high sea with his leader, uncertain where the fates may carry him or where he may be allowed to settle. All these things are placed here by the poet with the greatest wisdom: for he easily recognizes that Troy must be abandoned, and that the sovereignty of the highest good must by no means be handed over to pleasure. However, he does not yet know in what thing the highest good consists. He is, therefore, rightly called an exile. For he has been cast out from what he once held, and he has not yet found that which he might possess as his own. He is carried on the sea because, whatever way our minds are moved, they are moved by no other thing than "appetite" <term>appetite: from the Latin 'appetitus,' referring here to the soul's natural drive or inclination toward a goal</term>. I will reveal shortly how similar this is to the sea, if I first say a few things about appetite. There is, then, a certain sense and power in our minds which they call "thinking," to which nature has entrusted the judgment of good and evil. Sometimes, however, this power judges in such a way that it looks at nothing beyond the physical senses; attracted as if by their charms and corrupted by the offered reward of pleasure, it decides that what is good for a beast is the good for a man. If, however, this same power of thinking is illuminated by the healthy light of reason and directed by its standard, it does not judge that to be good which merely soothes the senses, but rather what right reason dictates: that which is the true and simple good, which can neither perish nor be corrupted. When, therefore, this power has decreed that this thing is good and that thing is evil, another power is awakened within us that rises up to embrace the good and turn away from the evil. Everyone calls this appetite. But it must also be twofold: one which always depends on the judgment made by the senses alone and seeks nothing through reason; the other which follows nothing at all except what reason has first commanded. We call the first one lust <note>original: 'libidinem'</note> and the second pleasure <note>original: 'uoluptatem' — used here in the philosophical sense of a higher, rational joy</note>. <margin>Why? <unclear>E</unclear></margin> Therefore, the appetite by which the minds of men are moved to embrace the good and turn away from the evil will be "right" if it is moved by reason, and conversely "wrong" if moved by the senses. Thus, with a most beautiful riddle <note>This refers to the famous Chariot Allegory in Plato's dialogue, the *Phaedrus*</note>, the divine Plato, having described our soul as if it were a chariot, assigns to it a charioteer and two horses. <margin>Plato's symbol</margin> For just as a chariot is pulled by horses, so the mind is led by appetite. But the horses carry it not by their own will, but by the command of the charioteer by whom they are ruled; in the same way, appetite decides nothing to be done on its own, but follows what has already been decreed by another power. Why he fashioned one of the horses as white and most beautiful, desiring honor and ruled no less by encouragement and reason than by force, while the other he made black, ignoble, and stubborn, is clear from what I said a moment ago regarding the twofold appetite. For through the good horse he expressed the rational appetite, and through the bad horse the irrational appetite by which the mind is carried. So much for appetite; but who would deny that it is very much like the sea? For we see that if the sea is struck by no winds, it remains calm and tranquil. But if it is beaten by diverse winds, it rises up into the most heavy and turbulent storms. You can perceive these same things in appetite: make it free from disturbances, and it will desire nothing but what is right; make it again violently troubled by them, and what waves or what storms you will then witness! This is why [the poet writes] most elegantly: "it fluctuates with a varied tide of passions" <note>original: 'uarioq; irarum fluctuat estu' — Virgil's description of Aeneas's internal conflict</note>. However, it may perhaps occur to you that something seems not to cohere well with what we have said: for if Aeneas is carried by rational appetite, if already his life of pleasure...

The author distinguishes between rational appetite (the white horse) and irrational lust (the black horse), comparing the turbulence of human emotions to a stormy sea.</summary>
