<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Aeneas's journey, focusing on his emotional state upon leaving "Troy" (symbolizing the life of the senses) and the philosophical distinction between different levels of self-control.</meta>

# On the Allegory of P. Virgilius Maro

<margin>Injurious</margin>
...condemned as injurious: hence now he leaves the shores and harbor of his fatherland weeping. For that which we have already begun to hate, we are accustomed to flee not weeping, but joyfully. But Virgil wanted to demonstrate the first departure from pleasure toward **virtue** <term>virtue: from the Latin 'virtus,' here referring to moral excellence and the strength of character</term>. In this stage, since we are not yet temperate but merely **continent** <term>continent: from 'continentia,' describing a person who does the right thing but must still struggle against their desires</term>, we perform the action indeed; but because we have long been accustomed to pleasure and are soothed by its charms, we are torn away from it only with difficulty. Let us imitate the Trojan elders: when Helen had climbed the walls to watch the battle between the Greeks and Trojans, they marveled at the woman's beauty with awe and were greatly delighted by it; yet, noticing that she was the cause of such great evils, they said, "Let Helen go, rather than have **Troy** perish on her account."

To understand this more clearly: just as **fortitude** is the virtue by which we bear all hard and harsh things with an unconquered spirit, so we are armed by **temperance** against pleasures. Because we have already formed a habit in this virtue, we accomplish the task without any difficulty or trouble. But if the habit has not yet been formed, and yet we attempt to achieve that same thing and finally do achieve it, we have attained something neighboring and very close to virtue; though we are not yet made temperate, we nevertheless abstain, albeit with difficulty and not without a struggle. This is called **continence**, and if we exercise ourselves in it for a long time, we gradually acquire temperance; thus that which until now was not virtue becomes virtue, or rather, the entrance to virtue. This, therefore, is the difference between **temperance** and **continence**. For although both provide the same result, the continent person is less perfect because he abstains with pain and is not firm enough against pleasures. The temperate person, however, abstains willingly and joyfully. 

If you investigate **incontinence** and **intemperance** in the same way, it is easy to see that just as temperance surpasses continence, so the **intemperate** person is more dangerous than the **incontinent** one. For the incontinent person, because he is not yet in a habit of vice, discerns reason and holds onto its principle; he fights against evil, but finally, conquered by the magnitude of desire and the weakness of his own mind, he is snatched away into slavery like a captive. But so that these things I outline in words may be made clearer by an example, we say that **Dido** was continent at the beginning; although she was held by her love for **Aeneas**, she nevertheless resisted so manfully that she preferred to die rather than violate her modesty. 
<margin>Dido</margin>
Later, however, she is rendered incontinent when, conquered by her sister's speech, she released her modesty. For at first, still somewhat strong, she fought in such a way that she might emerge victorious. Then, completely weakened by the struggle, she succumbed. For the incontinent person fights, but is overcome. The **intemperate** person, however, being established in a habit of vice, has lost all reason; he does not fight against his desires, but rather obeys them willingly and joyfully, precisely because his judgment is so depraved that he calls what is evil, good. 

But to return to our purpose: **Aeneas** was not yet fortified by temperance. For that reason had only recently dawned in the man, enabling him to look upon the filth of pleasures; yet he was neither temperate nor incontinent, for he would not have freed himself from there otherwise. But while the charms of pleasures pulled from one side, and the beauty of an honorable man raised him toward all high things from the other, he was indeed soothed by pleasure and judged it most sweet to the senses, and he could not be torn from it without grief. For pleasure is a flattering flatterer and applauds the senses so vehemently that even those of a more noble mind are captured by her. She creeps into us so sweetly that she gradually occupies us entirely. Therefore, Aeneas rises up against her, and though weeping, he nevertheless leaves the Trojan shores.

The previous page established that Aeneas's departure from Troy represents the difficult transition from a life of pleasure to a life of virtue, characterized by "continence" (doing right while still desiring wrong) rather than "temperance" (naturally desiring the good).</meta>

# Book Three

...he leaves it: because you are **continent** <term>continent: from 'continentia,' a state where a person acts rightly but must still struggle against their internal desires</term>. But if he had attained true **temperance** <term>temperance: a higher state of virtue where a person’s desires are naturally aligned with reason, acting rightly without internal conflict</term>, he would have left not with tears, but with joy. For the poet does not represent him as wise from the very beginning, nor adorned with true virtue, but as one who—desiring to claim his soul from its disturbances—gradually redeems himself from vices and, after various errors, arrives in <margin>Italy</margin> **Italy**, which represents true **wisdom**. For you should understand that what we have said regarding self-control and the lack of it—in which the senses and reason fight, alternately winning and being defeated—applies equally to the rest of the vices and virtues. Between these are the "middle affections," not yet firm in any **habit** <term>habit: from 'habitus,' a settled or regular tendency or practice, especially one that is hard to give up</term>, but which drive a person now toward these things and now toward those. Someone might perhaps call these "virtues" in the context of civic life, where you find things tending toward the good that are begun rather than perfected. 

But let our **Aeneas** <note>The hero of Virgil's epic, here representing the human soul on a journey.</note> now set forth. But where, pray tell, shall he be carried as an exile across the deep? To be sure, to the region of **Thrace**, a land neighboring his fatherland and consecrated to **Mars** <note>The Roman god of war.</note>. In this land, **Polynestor** recently killed his guest **Polydorus** to gain possession of his gold. **Thrace**, then, will be a symbol of **avarice** <term>avarice: extreme greed for wealth or material gain</term>. For the poet himself says a little later: *Flee the greedy shore* <note>original: "Fuge littus auarum"</note>. 

Truly, there are two kinds of greed. There is the greedy person who snatches from where it is least appropriate, and there is the one who gives nothing to those to whom he ought to give. That first kind is expressed through **Thrace**. For if **Mars** is worshipped there, who does not know that most wars are waged by mortals out of a desire for possession? Neither **Polynestor**—the murderer of his guest and the robber of his goods—could denote anything more clearly than greed and plunder. Why then is **Aeneas's** first voyage to **Thrace**? Because when we have departed from **pleasure** <note>represented by Troy</note> and have not yet contracted the habit of true virtue, we easily fall from that into another desire, and the lust for possessing rises up. For many people think they have attained the most blessed life if they surpass other mortals in great resources and wealth. Because they are inflamed by desire, they do not hesitate to undertake wars that are not only wicked, but also full of labors and dangers. This is surely a great folly, proceeding from a mind that—even if it has despised pleasures—cannot yet look up to anything high. 

For greed possesses a devotion to money, which no wise man has ever desired. Nothing is more fickle than money; nothing is more subject to the recklessness of fortune. <margin>Sallust</margin> Therefore **Sallust** <note>A Roman historian (86–35 BC) known for his social criticism.</note> rightly said that greed is so imbued with evil poisons that it *weakens both the manly mind and body* <note>original: "ut animum corpusque virile effœminet"</note>. Indeed, it forces one to descend to every kind of humility and the lowest filth, and to rise up in every kind of cruelty for the sake of the most worthless thing. It teaches treachery and perjury; it equips the heart with frauds, the tongue with lies, and the hand with poisons and the sword for the destruction of others. What can be holy to greed, when even guests—as the poet shows by the example of **Polydorus**—are not safe? 

Yet no one should wonder if **Aeneas**, who is indeed desirous of wisdom but not yet wise, has slipped into a vice of this sort. We see many things in human life that, although they are fleeting and momentary, mortals nevertheless admire as the greatest things. Since they see that all these things are for sale, they are devoted to money above all else. For how few people can be found who do not think that "Queen Money" <note>original: "regina pecunia"</note> grants both noble birth and beauty? Who is not moved when they hear that *persuasion and Venus grace the well-moneyed man*? <note>original: "et bene nummatum decorat suadela Venusque"</note> But he who is led by **Venus** <note>Here representing a higher love or the love of great things rather than mere lust.</note> and is incensed by the love of great things, gradually recognizes his error. Abominating the vice, he flees the most greedy shore of **Thrace**. And when he has now been deceived a second time...

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of Aeneas's arrival in Thrace, viewing it as the soul's first encounter with the vice of greed (avarice) after leaving behind a life of physical pleasure. The author uses the myth of Polydorus and quotes from Sallust to illustrate how greed corrupts the mind and leads to war and treachery.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Virgil's Aeneid. Having discussed Aeneas's departure from the "Troy" of vice, the text now follows him to the island of Delos, where he seeks the oracle of Apollo. The author interprets this journey as the soul's pursuit of Wisdom.</meta>

### On the Allegory of P. Virgilius Maro
<meta>catchword: your</meta>

...he judges it most shameful and wretched to be deceived henceforth. He decides that **Apollo** must be consulted, for he has heard that his oracles are most true. For he thinks that if he establishes his life according to the commands of that god, it will come to pass that he cannot be wretched. For this reason, he undertakes a voyage to **Delos**. By **Apollo**, however—who is the **Sun**—what else shall we understand but **Wisdom**? For even if I omit the fact that just as all things that fall under the senses are illuminated by the sun, so a soul illuminated by wisdom is able to foresee all things, let us look at the rest of the nature of that planet. But this first:

<margin><unclear>illegible</unclear></margin>
<margin>Heraclitus. Cicero, in *The Dream of Scipio*:</margin>
For **Heraclitus** calls him the "fountain of heavenly light." **Cicero**, indeed, called him the "leader of the other lights" for this reason: because he precedes them with the majesty of his own light. He likewise called him the "prince" and the "moderator." For he stands out so much that, because he alone <note>original: "solus"</note> appears in this way, he is called the **Sun** <note>original: "Sol"; the author is making a traditional etymological connection between *Sol* (Sun) and *solus* (alone/only).</note>; and he himself moderates the courses and returns of the others. For there is a fixed definition of space to which each wandering star <term>erratica stella: a planet</term>, when receding from the sun, arrives as if it were forbidden to approach further, and is driven back. Then again, when it has touched a certain part in its receding, it is recalled to the accustomed path of its direct course. For this reason, he was not wrongly called both the **mind of the world** and the **heart of the sky** by the ancients. Do not all these things fit **Wisdom**? Does not wisdom precede the other powers of the soul? Does it not moderate them? Furthermore, if you observe the power of this star more diligently, it is rightly said to be given to wisdom. For just as we receive the power of reasoning from **Saturn**, the power of acting from **Jupiter**, the heat and vehemence of minds from **Mars**, the motion of desire from **Venus**, and that which we speak and interpret is from **Mercury**, and finally that which the Greeks call *phyticon* <term>phyticon: the "vegetative" power of the soul, responsible for nutrition, growth, and reproduction</term>—that is, the power of begetting and increasing—we have from the **Moon**; so the **Sun** himself provides us with that which we know and that which we opine. 

But enough of **Apollo**. The name of **Delos** itself also brings something to the matter; for in Greek it signifies "manifest" or "clear." For the places over which wisdom presides are always bright and manifest. Moreover, the fact that **Anius** rules the whole island—who is both a king of men and a priest of the gods—is not without reason. For **Wisdom** contains the knowledge of human affairs. For this reason, nothing "new" can happen to the wise man, since he has already perceived all things; this very fact the name of the king demonstrates. For **Anius** is named as if he were <gloss>without newness</gloss> <note>original: "ἄνεὺ νέου" (aneu neou). A Greek-based etymological pun suggesting the wise man is never surprised by "new" events.</note>. He, therefore, receives **Aeneas** with hospitality; and indeed our souls are nourished by wisdom itself. Furthermore, he venerates the temples, and specifically those temples built of ancient stone. For what, I ask you, is found to be either more stable and immovable, or more ancient than **Wisdom** itself? Which **Solomon**, that wisest of all the Hebrews, most truly says was created from the beginning and before the ages. 

But why do you look at me smiling, O **LAURENTIUS**? 

**LAURENTIUS** said: I cannot help but admire the geniuses of the most learned men, and I joyfully repeat to myself what you have said from the beginning concerning both history and allegory. For who would not be stunned by the plan of this poet? He had read in **Cloatius Verius** that there was an altar of **Apollo Genitor** in **Delos** upon which no animal is sacrificed, which they say **Pythagoras** adored as if it were inviolate; he had also read in **Epaphus** that **Delos** was never troubled by earthquakes either before or after, but always remained on the same rock; and he had read in **Thucydides** that it is no wonder if the island is always safe by the protection of religion, since a reverence for the place has come upon it...

<summary>The author provides an astrological and etymological allegory of Aeneas's arrival at Delos, identifying Apollo with Wisdom and King Anius with the "lack of newness" found in a wise soul. He uses planetary influences to describe the various faculties of the human mind, all governed by the Sun/Wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Aeneid allegory, Apollo as Wisdom, Anius etymology, planetary powers, Solomon, Laurentius, Delos, ancient stone, humanistic commentary</keywords>

# Book Three
### Page 40

...with the continuous firmness of that same stone. Therefore, when he had read these things, he wrote in such a way that he might extract history from antiquity at the same time: for he posits that Aeneas venerates the god with the prayers of the sun, and that the temples were constructed of ancient stone; and when he posits these things, he simultaneously brings forward those things which agree with the allegory of wisdom. You will ask: what about the rest? The same thing. But I know not how this place, in which history is hidden no less than allegory, has moved me much more. But go on, I beseech you: for I do not wish to bring trouble upon myself—who am most eager to listen—by a delay caused by my own interruption.

An oracle is therefore given by Apollo, says **BAPTISTA**:

> Hardy descendants of Dardanus, the same land
> Which first bore you from the stock of your ancestors
> Shall receive you back into her joyful breast;
> Seek out your ancient mother:
> Here the house of Aeneas shall rule over every shore,
> Both the children of his children, and those who shall be born from them.
<note>This is a quotation from Virgil’s Aeneid, Book 3, lines 94–98. The "ancient mother" is a riddle referring to the land of their ancestors.</note>

I do not find what could be thought out more divine than this oracle. For what is more salutary for a man, what is more beneficial, than to know his own origin? For only when he is able to return to it will he finally be most blessed. Therefore, **Maro** <note>original: "Maro"; the poet Virgil's cognomen or family name.</note> said in many words—lest he depart from the poet's style—what the Greeks express in only two words, they who wish the greatest of all oracles attributed to Apollo to be <term>nosce te ipsum: Know thyself</term>.

But so that these things may be explained more clearly by us: All philosophers, whoever have ever written about the highest good <note>original: "summo bono"</note>, agree in this, if not in words then at least in fact: that to live well and blessedly is to live aptly and consistently with nature. Truly, once one arrives at the point where the nature of man must be defined, then innumerable and most destructive errors flow forth: when, the power of our souls being ignored, more than is right is attributed to the body. For since man is said to consist of soul and body, and the one is brute, fleeting, and prone to destruction;

<margin>dual origin</margin> the other is incorruptible, immortal, and divine; very few indeed so separate the mind from the senses that they recognize themselves as immortal by the nobility of the soul, and judge that the body should be held as almost no part of them. Therefore, the Trojans are commanded to return to that place from which they draw their origin. Moreover, they have a dual origin. For **Teucer**, the son of a certain Scamander, having set out from the island of **Crete**, came into Phrygia and reigned together with **Dardanus**. Dardanus, however, had also come into Phrygia earlier, yet not from **Crete**, as the former did, but from **Italy**; nor was he born of a mortal father, but from the god **Jove** <note>Jupiter</note>. 

They both come into Phrygia—that is, into life—and the first age which we have signified by **Troy**; but one from heaven, the other from a mortal. For in the composition of this living being which we call man, the soul comes from a heavenly father, the body from a mortal one. Wherefore, when Apollo—whose oracle is "Know thyself"—commands us to seek our first origin, he does not command us to investigate what the body is, or what is beneficial to it. But he mandates that we must inquire what the soul is and in what way we can be happy by living according to the nature of the soul. 

Indeed, so that he might signify this thing more expressly, he called it "ancient." For the soul is prior—if not in time, as the **Platonists** <note>Followers of Plato who believed in the pre-existence of the soul.</note> believe, then certainly in its dignity and excellence. Therefore, it is a most excellent oracle. But what does it profit if **Anchises** interprets it badly? This mortal parent of Aeneas, referring all things to the senses, judges that their seats should be placed where the first origin of the body occurs, as if the first things of nature to be regarded were not of the soul but of the body. For which reason he proposes that they must sail not to **Italy**, but to **Crete**, on which island the Trojans are destined to undergo many evils. For since the highest good consists not in those things which belong to the soul, but those which...

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of Apollo’s oracle to the Trojans, equating the command to "seek your ancient mother" with the philosophical precept "Know thyself." It contrasts the soul's divine origin in Italy/Heaven (Dardanus) with the body's mortal origin in Crete (Teucer), explaining Anchises' error in seeking a physical rather than a spiritual home.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Virgil's Aeneid, Book 3. It explores the symbolic meaning of the Trojans' failed attempt to settle in Crete and their eventual redirection toward Italy, representing the soul's journey from the material body toward divine reason.</meta>

# On the Allegories of P. Virgil Maro
### Interpretation of the Soul and Body

If we look only to the body and ignore our true nature, placing our hopes there, it is inevitable—since the body must soon fall into plague and finally into destruction—that we shall not be blessed, but miserable. Therefore, the Trojans paid the price for their folly; though commanded to sail to Italy, they sought out Crete instead. For if the Trojans return to Italy—that is, to the **origin of the soul** <note>The commentator interprets "Italy" not just as a geographical location, but as the spiritual source from which the human soul originates.</note>—they perceive the knowledge of divine things, in which alone they find stable and lasting seats.

As the poet says: **"Here the house of Aeneas shall rule over every shore, both the children of his children, and those who shall be born from them."** <note>Aeneid 3.97–98. The commentator argues this "dominion" refers to the mind's rule over the universe.</note>

In Crete, however, Aeneas has no empire. For the body moves neither itself nor anything else; rather, it lies inert and dull, without sensation, and has no power without the aid of the soul. But in "Italy," the empire extends far and wide. The soul is the master and ruler of the body, and it falls into no slavery unless it does so willingly. It subjects all things to its own understanding: it sees itself through itself, and when it attempts to know God, it is raised to higher things by the sharp edge of its own mind. By considering all things, it observes them; it searches out hidden secrets. It sees what is absent, and in a brief moment of time, it encompasses all the shores of the world. It descends to the depths; it ascends to heaven. It clings to God, in whom is its true fatherland. And since the soul is immortal, it does these things forever; wherefore its empire is eternal.

At the beginning, the Trojans could not understand these things, however much they were warned by divine precepts. Now, however, having experienced calamities, they learn—like **Epimetheus**, they are wise too late. <note>original: "Epimetheo quidem ferius." Epimetheus was the brother of Prometheus in Greek mythology; his name means "afterthought." He represents someone who only understands a mistake after suffering its consequences.</note>

But see, I pray, with what admirable genius the poet follows the rest: while the Trojans were suffering from the plague, Anchises condemns his own interpretation of the oracle. For after a man has raved for a long time, obeying his senses and placing all hope in fleeting things, he is finally made more learned by use and experience. He notices that Apollo did not urge them toward Crete—that is, that men can never be blessed by those things which are mortal. He decides, therefore, that happiness must be sought elsewhere; but as of yet, he does not have enough mental sharpness to discern in what thing that happiness consists.

<margin>The sign of the prophet</margin>

For when the soul is prostrate on the ground, even if it strives to rise, it is overwhelmed by the body and cannot discern those things placed on high except after a long time. But the **Penates** <note>The Penates were Roman household gods. Here, they are allegorized as the higher faculties of the human mind.</note> will say the same things that Apollo was going to say. For the ancients thought the Penates were the souls of their ancestors who had been famous and illustrious for many outstanding virtues, acting as domestic gods. Therefore, we shall interpret these as the more excellent powers of our souls, such as **reason, intellect, and intelligence.**

Until now, while the senses were raging and filling everything with tumult, these powers could judge nothing sanely. But now, after man has experienced by his own heavy loss that the judgment of the senses is false, he casts them down from the tribunal they had tumultuously occupied. He permits the power of pronouncing judgment to those faculties I named a moment ago. Since these faculties now employ more obedient senses—which, confused by shame, dare nothing further—and since long use and experience have refuted the judgment of the senses, they have decreed that the senses should obey rather than command. They collect themselves from the tumult and rouse themselves; with the greatest effort of justice, they put to flight the clouds with their own light. Having rescued the mind from the most unjust judgment of the senses, they release it from the "Cretan" dwelling, so that they might decree the journey to Italy by their own mandate.

They add, moreover, that their opinion must be stood by, since they command the same things that Apollo himself—by whom they are sent—would say. And indeed, our mind, made more learned by the experience of many things, knows many things from itself which wisdom is accustomed to teach. Nor should it move anyone if Anchises is persuaded by the speech of the household gods.

<summary>This page provides an allegorical reading of Aeneas's journey, casting Crete as the physical body and Italy as the soul's divine origin. It explains how human reason (symbolized by the Penates) eventually triumphs over the misleading physical senses after suffering and experience.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of Book 3 of the Aeneid. Having left the "body" (Crete), the soul (Aeneas) is now being guided by divine reason (the Penates/household gods) toward the "origin" (Italy), but is hampered by the senses (represented by the pilot Palinurus).</meta>

# Book Three

For when reason begins to prevail, the appetite is subjected to it. The household gods <note>The *Penates*, or Trojan household gods, are interpreted here as divine truths or insights that dwell within the soul.</note> have now made much progress; they have clearly unraveled what Apollo had signified more obscurely. They teach that one must sail to Italy for the knowledge of divine things. Aeneas would be truly blessed if he stood by these decrees—if he grasped what he knew to be good so firmly that nothing could tear him away. For then he would not be thrown off the right course so often. But he is not yet a man who has hardened himself in a constant habit, nor is he entirely purified from the desires for mortal things through the highest temperance. Instead, wavering between the various fruits of self-control and lack of control, often when he has proceeded for some distance with a favorable wind, the ship is suddenly thrown off the right course. For the pilot holding the tiller is not one who is able to overcome even the harshest storm with the highest skill of sailing; rather, it is **Palinurus**. After "a dark storm cloud stood over his head, bringing night and storm," after "the wave grew rough with shadows," after "the winds continually roll the sea and great waters rise" <note>original: "ceruleus supra caput astitit imber... postquam inhorruit unda tenebris... continuouenti uoluũt mare." Virgil, Aeneid 3.194–197.</note>, and what follows, he himself "denies that he can distinguish day from night in the sky, or remember the way."

We said from the beginning that the soul is moved by appetite alone. We said likewise that appetite is twofold: one which is excited by the senses alone and is opposed to reason, which is called **lust**; the other which obeys reason, and is rightly called **will**. If this "will" had indeed been in charge of the ship, it could have held a straight path even against opposing winds. But **Palinurus** was in charge; for he is the one who obeys the senses alone, and he interprets his strength according to the winds. <margin>S br</margin> <gloss>**Palinurus** in Greek is said to mean "back" and "winds" <note>original: "retro & uentis". A creative etymology from *palin* (again/back) and *ouros* (wind/fair wind).</note>, as if it were he who carries one back in the opposite direction.</gloss> Therefore, when disturbances rise up and stronger desires envelop the soul like darkness, since he himself is illuminated by no light of reason, he "denies he can discern day from nights"—that is, he cannot tell truth from falsehood. Truly, great is the ignorance of men and greatest is the disturbance of the senses, which so oppose reason that although reason often rises up to snatch the soul away from their wicked tyranny and slavery—and like a most just queen wishes to bring in true liberty—yet because she has not yet recovered her strength, having recently returned from a long exile and being recognized by only a few of her citizens, she is often repelled by those senses before she can recover possession of the kingdom which is her legal due. Indeed, those senses, having held tyranny for many years, have corrupted the appetite with every kind of bribe and have soothed it so much that it prefers to live luxuriously in slavery rather than to labor honorably in freedom.

For this reason, since frequent battles are fought between them, Reason often departs defeated. Because Palinurus refuses to obey her, he perceives nothing healthy. Through his folly and recklessness, the course which the household gods had commanded is abandoned, and the ship turns aside to the **Strophades** islands. This place, if I am not mistaken, we rightly interpret as the vice of **Avarice** <note>Greed or hoarding.</note>. Yet it is not that kind of greed by which we snatch things from where it is least appropriate—for Thrace <note>Referring to the earlier episode in Thrace where Polydorus was murdered for gold.</note> designated that for us—but rather another kind. This occurs when we do not help those whom nature, right, and the bond of human society demand we help, using the things we have already produced. For Aeneas, now taught by the fragility of human affairs, was being carried toward divine things as reason demanded. But his appetite, still stubborn against reason, could not stand by the decrees. Turning back again toward those things which the common crowd admires, he desires riches. But since much of his former wildness had been diminished, he does not strive to acquire them for himself through force or robbery, but...

<summary>The author explains that Aeneas's pilot, Palinurus, represents the sensory appetite which lacks the light of reason, leading the soul away from "Italy" (divine truth) and toward the "Strophades" (the vice of avarice).</summary>
<keywords>Aeneas, Palinurus, Reason, Appetite, Will, Italy, Strophades, Avarice, Allegory, Virgil</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Aeneas’s journey. Having left Crete (representing the physical body), Aeneas now encounters the Harpies, who symbolize the vice of avarice (greed).</meta>

->### On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro<-

<margin>Σ.</margin>
<margin>living
<unclear>the same</unclear></margin>
Through the greatest filth, saving more than is fair from what he has earned, and fleeing from every duty of generosity, the greedy man is beneficial neither to himself nor to his kin. Even while he does these things, he declares himself to be merely thrifty rather than greedy; he puts on the appearance of a good man even though he is among the worst. For this reason, it is not without justice that the Harpies themselves are imagined with the faces of young women. For they simulate shame, modesty, and sobriety, and they display every virtue. Yet the discharge of their bellies is most foul. For who could fully explain the filth of avarice? Who could sufficiently relate how shameful is the life of a man who is both wealthy and yet a hoarder? Since it is an undeniable madness—a manifest frenzy—to live as a beggar so that you may die rich. What wonder is it, then, if their faces are always pale and gaunt from hunger? For we rightly compare men of this sort to **Tantalus**, who, placed among waters and various fruits, is nevertheless consumed by thirst and hunger. For as the heap of riches grows, he meanwhile defrauds his own "genius" <note>In Roman belief, the "Genius" was a personal spirit; "defrauding one's genius" meant denying oneself pleasure or basic needs.</note>—just like **Geta** in the plays of Terence <note>Geta is a character in Terence's comedy *Phormio* who speaks of self-denial.</note>—abstaining from what he has acquired and fearing to use it. 

The fact that they are imagined with hooked hands is not without reason: for the greedy never let go of what they have once seized. Indeed, the hooks of avarice are so destructive that they drag man—who was once born for divine things—down from the highest cares to these inferior things, thrusting him as if from heaven to earth, and from the bright stars into the deepest pits of Tartarus. For the desire for possession turns us away and calls us back from the knowledge of those things through which alone the soul can be happy. Wisely, therefore, the poet adds: *No monster more grim than these, no fiercer plague and wrath of the gods ever rose from the Stygian waves.* <note>original: "Tristius haud illis monstrum nec saevior ulla pestis: & ira deū stygiis sese extulit undis." Virgil, Aeneid 3.214-215.</note> 

Furthermore, it was no foolish reasoning that moved the poets to mythologize that the Harpies were born from **Thaumas** as father and **Electra**, daughter of Ocean, as mother. We rightly call Thaumas "Admiration," for the Greeks use the word <gloss>to wonder</gloss> <note>original: θαυμάζειν (*thaumazein*)</note> for "to admire." Since, out of extreme foolishness, we think riches are the greatest good—when they are either not good at all or the very least of goods—and because we admire them, it follows that the desire for possessing them inflames us out of that admiration. For no one desires those things which he neglects or despises. Moreover, they are born from a mother who is the daughter of Ocean; for if anyone considers the nature of riches more diligently, he will see that everything in them is most similar to the sea. Just as the sea is in constant motion, and all things in it are easily mixed and disturbed, so you will find nothing more fluid than riches and wealth; from them, many tumults and the most savage wars are stirred up. 

These Harpies, therefore, as they graze everywhere upon the herds and flocks, take nothing from them for their own necessity, nor do they permit others to take anything. But the man who is desirous of acquiring the "Highest Good" eventually disentangles himself from this kind of avarice as well. He had previously left behind pleasures; he had fallen into robberies <note>Referring to the episode at Thrace/Polydorus.</note>; and having condemned those as well, he consults the oracle. From it, he receives the command: *Know thyself*. In this, **Anchises** errs when he refers those things to the body which were actually commanded concerning the soul. Having been made cautious by his own loss, he recognizes his error, changes his plan, and having left Crete, he head towards Latium. Yet, the soul, troubled again by disturbances, flows back toward riches—not, however, to those riches gained through robbery as before, but to those acquired through excessively sordid stinginess. But recognizing and condemning this too as a vice, he sets out through his enemies toward **Helenus** <note>Helenus is a seer who provides further spiritual guidance to Aeneas.</note>. 

You have here the reason why the poet imagined Aeneas being driven to the island of the Harpies. The fact that he was prohibited by them from eating the banquet already prepared signifies this: avarice has such power that even those who are the wealthiest would rather perish from hunger than diminish even the smallest particle of their heap. Nevertheless, the Trojans drove them off with arms; for since avarice is born from weakness and humility of soul—which is the reason why both old men and women—

<summary>This page provides an allegorical reading of the Harpies as personifications of avarice. The author explains their physical traits and parentage as metaphors for the deceptive nature of wealth and the soul's struggle to overcome worldly greed during its journey toward divine truth.</summary>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical discussion of Aeneas's encounter with the Harpies. The previous page established that the Harpies represent the filth and hypocrisy of avarice (greed). This page focuses on the fear of poverty and the contrast between the Greek philosopher Diogenes and Alexander the Great.</meta>

# Book Three

...souls that are smaller always show themselves to be greedier; but greed is easily driven away if we take up a brave and noble spirit. Aeneas departs from the Strophades <note>The "Islands of Turning," where the Harpies lived.</note>, but not before he receives a grim oracle from Celaeno <note>The leader of the Harpies; her "grim oracle" was the prophecy that the Trojans would be so hungry they would eventually eat their own tables.</note>. She is a completely lying prophetess and especially deceitful; she is the type who wants to strike horror through a din of words where nothing is actually to be feared. 

But truly, the greedy suffer from this disease: for if ever some honorable and true reason raises them to explore divine things—and therefore persuades them that these human and mortal things should be neglected—immediately a fear arises out of their avarice. They fear that if they care for their family estate too carelessly, they will perish of hunger. But these most foolish men do not know with how little nature is satisfied, how easily and with how little expense her riches are obtained. 

Hunger is only to be feared by those who are empty, who have set before themselves infinite desires that are neither necessary nor natural. Their gullet is so wide and so deep that it cannot be filled by any force of gold, nor by any pile of gems and precious stones. However, those who have so organized their lives that they consider themselves blessed by virtue alone, and who propose to enrich the soul rather than the body—for these people, all things will always be present in abundance. 

To make this matter clearer to you, and indeed to set it before your very eyes, imagine two men of very different fortune but of nearly the same age: <term>Alexander the Great, King of the Macedonians</term> <note>The world-conquering king, representing the height of worldly wealth and ambition.</note> and <term>Diogenes the Cynic</term> <note>A philosopher who lived in extreme poverty in Athens, representing the height of self-sufficiency.</note>. Which will you judge to be the wealthier? Consider what you say. Alexander possessed the greatest treasures; many very powerful armies fought for him; he held a vast empire. Almost innumerable nations and peoples from Europe and Asia paid him tribute. 

But what can be imagined more meager than Diogenes? Besides that cracked vessel received from a potter <note>Diogenes famously lived in a large ceramic jar (often called a "tub" in later stories) to show his independence from material needs.</note>, into which he withdrew to be safe from the cold and heat, he did not even have a small hut. You would see him covered in the same rag during both the summer and winter solstices. His raw vegetables, even sprinkled with no salt, would surpass the banquets of the wealthiest kings. 

Which of these, then, will you judge to be wealthier, Lawrence? 

**LAWRENCE** said: If I depart from that most depraved habit—which is almost a second nature in us—and if I hand the whole matter over to be understood by reason (excluding the judgment of the senses), I will not hesitate to declare Diogenes the most blessed and Alexander the most miserable. For I strongly agree with those who believe that in weighing riches, one should not notice how much a person has, but how abundantly what they have will suffice for them. For if a man is rich whose desires are so satisfied that he misses nothing further, who is wealthier than Diogenes? When he saw a shepherd or a plowman scooping water from a spring with cupped hands to drink, he decided that the clay cup he carried for the same purpose should be thrown away as superfluous. 

Who, on the other hand, was poorer than Alexander? After he had heard from <term>Democritus</term> <note>A pre-Socratic philosopher who theorized that the universe was made of atoms and that there were infinite worlds.</note>—I believe it was that philosopher—that there are many worlds, he did not cease to weep, as if he could by no means be made rich unless he had first added those worlds to his empire. 

You judge correctly regarding both of them, Lawrence, said **BAPTISTA**. Therefore, when that same king, moved by the tranquility of mind he had recognized in the Cynic, announced: "I would wish to be Diogenes, if I were not Alexander," he revealed his own foolishness in large part. He preferred to suffer in the midst of the greatest wealth rather than to grow rich in the midst of the greatest poverty. Therefore, let men learn with how little nature is satisfied. When they have learned this, they will easily laugh at the oracle delivered by Celaeno, even though she herself tries to make her speech believable.

<sig>h</sig>

<summary>This page uses the dialogue between Lawrence and Baptista to argue that true wealth comes from virtue and self-sufficiency rather than material possessions. It highlights this through a comparison between Alexander the Great's insatiable greed and Diogenes the Cynic's contentment with nothing.</summary>

Having just discussed the nature of the Harpies as symbols of avarice and the fear of poverty, the text now transitions to Aeneas's arrival at Buthrotum to consult the prophet Helenus, interpreted here as the soul seeking the light of reason.</meta>

# On the Allegories of Publius Virgilius Maro

<margin>A.</margin>
...[She] says that she proclaims what the Almighty Father <note>original: "pater omnipotens"; referring to Jupiter, the supreme deity.</note> foretold to Phoebus, and what Phoebus Apollo foretold to her. For those who are greedy always try to cover the vice from which they suffer with **false masks of virtue**. For what is pure avarice, they call "thrift"; and they try to persuade us that hunger is to be feared by citing the authority of the gods or of the greatest men. This is surely a deceitful desire, which often deceives even those we consider to be quite prudent. Against its frauds, the only remedy is to know that those things which the most foolish human desire considers necessary for living life not only provide no benefit, but are actually the cause of all our evils.

Leaving the island of the Harpies, Aeneas therefore sails toward **Helenus**. Helenus is both a prophet and a fellow citizen. For this reason, we correctly call him the <term>innate reason</term> <note>original: "ingenitam rationem"; the natural spark of logic or moral conscience born within every human being.</note> and the **light of truth** that shines within us by nature. We, who are deceived by false goods, consult him so that he might lead us back from our errors onto the right path. 

The prophet himself can foretell the truth, but it is difficult to reach him, since the journey must be held through the midst of enemies. For both all our senses and the physical appetite that obeys the senses are always opposed to us when we wish to descend into true judgment. Indeed, they make such a noise against us as we deliberate that we can hardly approach reason or distinguish true goods from false ones. 

But when we have reached Helenus, *it is a joy to have escaped so many Greek cities and to have held our flight through the midst of enemies.* For once the <term>disturbances</term> <note>original: "perturbationibus"; a Stoic term for the irrational passions—such as fear, lust, and greed—that agitate the mind.</note> have been overcome, the mind is left tranquil and quiet; in this state, the light of reason, stirring itself, shows us the truth. Having been made more learned, the mind then recognizes "Italy"—which it used to think was nearby—is actually divided from it by a long, impassable way, and that much of the sea must be circled and a descent to the underworld must be made before it can establish a quiet home in Italy. 

How much reason there is in all these sayings is easier to grasp with the mind than to express with words. For after the soul—I will not say has utterly defeated, but has largely repressed its vices—descends into the light of its own radiance through the midst of enemies (as we have said), only then does it perceive the **Supreme Good**. It used to think this good was placed nearby, but now realizes it is far off, and that we must sail a wide circuit through seas beset by monsters. For between contemplation itself and this life we live, there lies that which I have already so many times called "appetite," like a most turbulent sea, which the most destructive monsters, **Scylla** and **Charybdis**, make dangerous. And yet, we must sail through these places if we wish to reach Italy. 

For we said from the beginning, if I remember correctly, that the soul is moved by no other force than appetite. But since the poet speaks of these two monsters, it is easy to guess from the myths themselves what he intends. Since tradition records that these were the most grasping of women, shall we not say that the excessive desires of the soul are aptly expressed through them? No one doubts that the leaders of these desires are **Lust** and **Greed**. 

**Scylla**, then, who was loved by Glaucus, expresses <term>lustful pleasures</term> <note>original: "venereas uoluptates"; specifically sexual or romantic desires.</note>, which flourish most when our affairs are prosperous. As for her lower parts being turned into barking dogs—it is easy to see how that relates to the matter at hand <note>The author implies that lust turns the human into a loud, animalistic creature.</note>. 

But who does not understand that **Charybdis**—who once dared to steal even the oxen of Hercules—represents for us the image of **Avarice** (Greed)? We are not urged on by her as much when things are succeeding happily as we are by lust; rather, it happens more when we seem to be hemmed in by too narrow limits of wealth, and for that reason, our own things please us very little.

<summary>This page interprets Aeneas's arrival at Helenus as the soul's discovery of innate reason, which allows it to see that the "Italy" of the Supreme Good is a long moral journey away. The monsters Scylla and Charybdis are identified as allegories for Lust and Greed, the primary obstacles to a tranquil mind.</summary>

<keywords>Aeneas, Helenus, Allegory, Reason, Scylla, Charybdis, Lust, Greed, Avarice, Supreme Good, Virgil, Philosophy</keywords>

They are interpreting Book Three of Virgil’s Aeneid, specifically the transition from overcoming basic vices like avarice to the higher spiritual preparation required to reach "Italy," which represents divine wisdom.</meta>

### Book Three

...and we consider them unhappy. For that reason, Maro <note>The Roman poet Virgil, whose full name was Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> said that the right side is occupied by **Scylla** and the left by **Charybdis**: because the one presses upon us too much in those things we consider adverse, while the other affects us in those things in which we take great delight. When Baptista had said these things and fell silent for a moment to catch his breath, Laurentius said:

— I marvel at the divine nature of such great and hidden learning. Yet, the more these things delight me, the more I desire that not even the slightest doubt be left in my mind concerning the whole matter. Therefore, I shall use the freedom—or rather the license—which you have granted me. To help you understand what it is I want made clearer: you had shown at the start that Virgil established that Aeneas left Troy because the man had already come to despise bodily pleasures. Furthermore, you argued that through Thrace and the Strophades islands, both types of avarice were expressed. Why then, if Aeneas had already cast off such vices, is he now warned by **Helenus** to avoid them again?

— Your question delights me, Lorenzo, — said **BAPTISTA**. — For it shows that you have considered what I said with a judgment greater than is usual for your age. But so that everything may be clear to you, remember that the Aeneas presented by Virgil is not a man in whom the habit of virtue is already perfected. Instead, he is one who so fights against vices for the sake of virtue that he overcomes them through <term>continence</term> <note>original: "continentiam"; in Renaissance philosophy, this refers to a state where a person still feels bad desires but uses their will to resist them.</note> not without great difficulty, and sometimes is even overcome as if he lacked self-control. 

Truly, for one who is about to come to **Italy**—which signifies the pursuit of divine things—it is not enough to be merely "continent." For although continence keeps one from desires, yet because it is engaged in constant struggle, it does not provide that tranquility to our minds which is necessary for understanding such lofty things. Therefore, he has need of that virtue of <term>temperance</term> <note>original: "temperantia"; unlike continence, temperance is a state of "ordered" character where the soul is so balanced that it no longer even feels the pull of lower desires.</note> which is absolute in every respect and solidified in nearly the third order of virtues. Those who are equipped with this not only overcome all desires, but even utterly forget them. 

No mortal should trust that he will be solidified in this habit unless he has first exercised himself with many constant actions to achieve it. This cannot be accomplished without a long interval of time. Virgil expressed this delay in a poetic but excellent way when he said: *It is better to round the goals of Trinacrian Pachynus, and to circle about on long courses.* <note>original: "Præstat trinacrii mœtas lustrare pachinni. Cessantem longos/& circunflectere cursus"; Virgil, Aeneid 3.429. Pachynus is the southeastern cape of Sicily. Allegorically, this "long way around" represents the time needed for spiritual discipline.</note> 

That he warns Aeneas to confirm himself in the habit I mentioned can be proven by a single verse. For he adds: *than to have once seen misshapen Scylla in her vast cave.* <note>original: "quam semel informem uasto uidisse sub antro scyllam"; Aeneid 3.431.</note> Therefore, we may most correctly conclude this whole passage by saying that no one can reach divinity itself unless, through a long interval beforehand, he has acquired that habit I spoke of, so that he is not only not snatched away by Scylla, but does not even see her once. 

What else does this mean, except that he should so forget all desires that they never return into the sight of his mind? 

— All these things are very beautiful and appropriate, — said **LAURENTIUS**. — But what you wished to explain a little earlier regarding that **triple order of virtues**, I do not fully understand. 

— The matter is of this sort, — said **BAPTISTA**, — which will often be of the greatest use to you elsewhere and is particularly necessary for our present conversation. For the divine **Plato**, although he had established the same virtues regarding life and morals as others, finally distinguished them into different orders or kinds, showing that they are cultivated in one way by those who love societies and cities...

h ii

<summary>Baptista explains to Lorenzo that Aeneas must move beyond mere self-control (continence) to a state of total inner peace (temperance) before he can reach Italy (divine wisdom). He uses the "long way around" Sicily as an allegory for the time and practice required to reach this higher state of virtue.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Baptista (Leon Battista Alberti) and Laurentius (Lorenzo de’ Medici), where Baptista explains the Neoplatonic hierarchy of virtues as applied to the character of Aeneas in Virgil's Aeneid. He distinguishes between the life of a citizen and the life of a spiritual seeker.</meta>

<header>On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro</header>

<margin>3. By Virtue</margin> ...those who, desiring to unlearn all mortality and moved by a hatred of human affairs, are raised up toward knowing divine things alone. Finally, there are those virtues held by people who, having already been purified of all contagion, dwell only in divine matters. He therefore called the first type **civic**, the second **purifying**, and the third those of **the soul already purified**. <note>Baptista is referencing a Neoplatonic classification of virtues (likely from Plotinus or Macrobius) which categorizes moral progress into distinct stages.</note> For there is a threefold order of men who live rightly and according to reason.

The lowest of these three is the order of those who, living in social and civic life, undertake the administration of republics. Next to these, but established on a higher step, are those who transfer themselves from public actions—as if from stormy and tempestuous seas where the recklessness of fortune dominates everything—into the harbor of tranquility. Withdrawing from the crowd into leisure, they lead a quiet life, yet not so completely that there does not still remain something against which they must struggle. In the highest place, however, you will see those who are entirely removed from the rushing and tumult of human affairs, committing nothing for which they might feel regret.

It is common to all these orders that, with **virtue** as their guide, they direct everything toward the standard of the good and the right. However, because in the civic life everything is in turmoil due to desires and disturbances, and these are resisted only with difficulty, they say that in that class of men, the virtues are begun rather than perfected. Therefore, in those men, **Prudence** <term>Prudentia: the ability to discern the right course of action</term> strives so that nothing is done for which at least a probable reason cannot be given. **Fortitude**, indeed, carries the soul above all danger and fear, and warns that nothing is to be feared except shameful things. **Temperance**, meanwhile, shows that only honorable things are to be sought, that the law of moderation must never be exceeded, and that all desires must be brought under the yoke of reason. Finally, the parts of **Justice** are to give to each person their due, so that all may live under equal law.

In that second class of men, however—those who reclaim themselves from business for leisure so that they might more freely devote themselves to the contemplation of divine things—**Prudence** will perform its duty if, having despised all mortal things and holding them as nothing in comparison to the celestial, it turns every care and every thought toward the divine. **Temperance**, since it allows us only those things without which life cannot be preserved, will pronounce that all other things are to be despised and fled from with the most severe judgment. Nor will **Fortitude** be absent, which constantly commands that we should dread no discomfort, no labor, and no danger that might hinder the soul from tending in a straight and perpetual course (so to speak) toward the heavens and toward its own origin. You will ask: what of **Justice**? This will certainly take it upon itself to bend the consensus of the remaining virtues toward a purpose of this kind. These men, like those above them, fight against disturbances, but they overcome them more easily and gradually render themselves purified. For this reason, the virtues themselves in those men are called **purifying** <note>original: "purgatoriae"</note>.

But hear now that third class of those whose souls are far removed from every stain of vice. These men, therefore, exercise **Prudence** in this way: not so that they prefer divine things to earthly ones by making a choice, but so that they know the divine alone, and gaze upon it as if nothing else exists. They employ **Temperance** not to restrain desires, but to be entirely ignorant of them. The same logic applies to **Fortitude**. For it does not conquer disturbances, but is unaware of them; indeed, it will desire that hard and horrifying things be offered to it, not so that it may achieve a victory, but so that it may endure in the forgetting of them with perpetual firmness.

If you examine all these things diligently, you will easily recognize the warning given by **Helenus** <note>A Trojan seer and son of King Priam who gives Aeneas instructions on how to reach Italy.</note> to **Aeneas**: that he cannot establish quiet and stable seats in **Italy** <note>In this allegory, "Italy" represents the state of divine wisdom or the goal of the soul's journey.</note> unless he first arrives at this third class of virtues. What then of his progress so far? Had he not already deserted **Troy**? And had he not left **Thrace** and the **Strophades**? He had indeed deserted them; but even if he had fled from those vices, he was not yet able to "unlearn" them. For it was not merely as a pleasure...

<summary>Baptista explains the three levels of virtue—Civic, Purifying, and Purified—defining how the four cardinal virtues function in each stage of the soul's ascent. He argues that Aeneas must reach the highest level of virtue before he can truly reach the peace of "Italy."</summary>
<keywords>Virtues, Civic Virtues, Purifying Virtues, Purified Soul, Prudence, Fortitude, Temperance, Justice, Aeneas, Helenus, Troy, Thrace, Strophades, Allegory, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Virgil’s Aeneid. Having established the hierarchy of virtues—civic, purifying, and purified—the speaker now focuses on the specific psychological dangers that even a noble soul like Aeneas must overcome, particularly the transition from a desire for glory to the danger of tyranny.</meta>

### Book Three

...and should cast aside avarice: which he had already done: but in such a way that he deletes it from the mind; he should commit it to perpetual oblivion so that the memory of such things never steals upon him. Since, however, the poet <note>original: "diuinus poeta," referring to Virgil</note> advises above all things that **Juno** must be appeased again and again—for as long as she is an adversary, he shall never take possession of Italy—is this not the greatest proof to us that among the innumerable and various vices, there is none from which even those who are raised to the highest excellence are more reluctantly freed than from the **desire for honors and power**? For it is easy to despise pleasures for one who has already conceived greater things in his mind. Riches, truly, even if they show themselves to us in the beginning under the appearance of the greatest goods, are nevertheless eventually neglected by an excellent soul. But in truth, honors, magistracies, and empires—because they seem to contain within themselves something excellent and eminent—deceive even an exalted mind with the appearance of grace and magnificence. For since that mind desires to render itself as close to God as possible, and notices that we are like God in no other thing more than in **granting benefits**, and that these cannot be provided by men unless they are established in the highest power of affairs, it is ignited by a certain violent desire to surpass the rest. For it is implanted in us by nature that we always desire to emerge as superiors in all things, while we think it most shameful to yield or succumb. 

Indeed, unless this natural desire is tempered by right reason, it hurries us into ambition and finally into **tyranny**, in which we commit many cruel, foul, and wicked acts against humanity. And although nature herself, unless she be depraved, raises us toward **magnanimity**, we seize upon everything for the sake of pride and domination. From this, frauds; from this, murders; from this, the remaining monstrous crimes arise. Stripped of humanity itself by these things, we are turned into the most savage monsters. It is not, therefore, without the highest wisdom that the divine poet leads Aeneas to the shores of the **Cyclopes**: so that he may show that those who seek certain great and exalted things, when no certain reason rules the mind, are deceived and fall from greatness of soul into savagery. But Aeneas, warned, escapes these places of misery as quickly as possible. For what else can depict for us more expressly, and (so to speak) 

<margin>Cy</margin>

place before our very eyes, that ambitious and tyrannical life—detestable for its extreme ferocity—than the Cyclops **Polyphemus**? He, far from all human fellowship, feeds upon human flesh and leads a lonely life among the dens of wild beasts. For should we not rightly call those men <term>Andropophagi</term> <note>Greek for "man-eaters"</note>—for so the Greeks call those who feed on human flesh? We speak not of those who turn bodies already lacking a soul into their feasts (for that is much more tolerable), but those who most cruelly devour the living with every torture. These are men who, so that they may either acquire tyranny for themselves or protect it once acquired, most savagely murder every best man and every lover of justice, equity, and liberty. That they may become masters of their most wicked vow, they slaughter not only individual men, but entire cities; and not only entire cities, but they ravage whole nations with sword, fire, and famine. They subject all things to military lust. They are men who fear neither to despoil the cultivators of the fields nor to drive away the plunder of men and cattle; who snatch boys and tender virgins from the embrace of their parents either for death or for lust; who violate the chastity of pure matrons; who utterly overturn public and private, sacred and profane buildings; and those who just now, in a most flourishing republic, held the most ample rank of dignity with the highest glory, they now lead away into most miserable slavery, despoiled of all their fortunes.

<summary>This page discusses the allegorical danger of political ambition, arguing that the desire for power can deceive even noble minds into becoming "man-eating" tyrants, symbolized by the Cyclops Polyphemus.</summary>
<keywords>Aeneas, Juno, Ambition, Tyranny, Magnanimity, Polyphemus, Cyclopes, Andropophagi, Humanitas, Virgil</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of Virgil’s Aeneid. Following the previous discussion on the desire for glory, the author now interprets the monsters of Book Three and the geography of Sicily and Italy as stages of the soul’s journey from the lower "active" reason toward "contemplative" divinity.</meta>

-># On the Allegories of P. Virgil M.<-

Which **Cyclopes**, then, or which **Laestrygonians** <note>mythological man-eating giants</note> shall we compare with such immense savagery? Therefore, a man desirous of the highest good—who previously, through an improperly regulated greatness of spirit, strove by any path toward honors and power—now at last detests such nefarious cruelty as soon as he recognizes it. He recognizes it, however, through the thin and squalid form of **Achaemenides** <note>original: "achemenide"; a character in the Aeneid abandoned by Ulysses in the cave of the Cyclops</note>, through whom the wise poet has secretly signified all the calamities that come upon the human race from tyranny: when citizens, who shortly before were graced with all the ranks of most ample honors, are driven to a lack of resources and extreme hunger; when they are forced to hide in fear of an illustrious death. For, having left behind their most dear fatherland, they live as exiles in the most ignoble and obscure places. This is a misery that, even if it falls upon a Greek man and an enemy of Aeneas, nevertheless cannot fail to drive one who is—or strives to be—a good man toward the greatest hatred of tyranny.

Who, therefore, would not rightly admire the wisdom of **Maro** <note>Virgil</note>? He commands the man who is to come into Italy to cautiously circumnavigate the seas and adjacent shores besieged by such horrendous monsters, so that, having avoided them all, he may arrive safely in **Sicily**, from whence there is the shortest course into **Italy**. For it is easy for a man who has already extricated himself from every appearance of avarice, and has cast off all injustice and savagery, to be raised up to the knowledge of great things—especially if he has already come into Sicily. 

Now, Sicily is an island, but formerly it was joined to Italy and was part of the continent; but the sea came between them and with its waves cut off the Hesperian side from the Sicilian side, and it washes between the fields and cities separated by the shore with a narrow tide <note>a paraphrase of Aeneid 3.414–419</note>. For the divinity of our souls was so created by the immortal God from the beginning that the lower part of reason—about which we must soon dispute more distinctly—was one and the same with the upper part. But since reason itself is occupied in the doing of things, dealing with those matters circumscribed by place and time which receive constant change, it happens that through the intervention of various disturbances—by which prudence is often deceived into choosing evil things instead of good—that lower reason, struck by those disturbances as if by the most violent waves, is at last torn away from Italy <note>representing the higher state</note> and falls away from the upper reason toward appetite. 

Although all these things are so, you will find no shorter course to Italy (that is, to **contemplation**, which is situated in that upper reason itself) than from the lower reason which is signified by Sicily—especially once the parent is buried, whom we have interpreted as a certain enervated softness in man arising from the senses. For we shall never arrive at true contemplation unless that **sensuality** <note>original: "sensualitas"; the author notes this is a "Christian term" for the lower, sensory nature</note> has been not only extinguished but also deeply buried within us. 

For this reason, if you notice correctly, the poet mentions the death of **Anchises** <note>Aeneas's father</note> but does not mention the burial here, for he is not yet about to enter Italy. However, in the **Fifth Book**, the funeral is celebrated so that, once Anchises is finally buried, Aeneas may be permitted to hasten into Italy. Thus, with all things prepared, Aeneas sets sail from Sicily and hopes shortly after to enter the ports of Italy. Nor would he have been deceived in his hope if he had previously appeased **Juno**, the goddess most hostile to him, according to the precept of **Helenus**. We showed a little while ago that Juno expresses the desire for honors and power. Even if Aeneas conducts himself in such a way that he will act with nothing unjust or cruel in the administration of affairs—which he indicated by his flight from **Polyphemus**—nevertheless, when he is already in sight of Italy and standing almost on the threshold of contemplation, let him realize that one cannot arrive at the knowledge of divine things unless he despises all these human concerns; yet he still struggles...

<summary>The author provides a Neoplatonic allegory of Aeneid Book Three, casting Sicily as "lower reason" and Italy as "divine contemplation." The death and burial of Anchises are interpreted as the necessary suppression of the physical senses (sensuality) required to achieve higher spiritual knowledge.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Virgil's Aeneid. Having just discussed the dangers of tyranny (the Cyclopes) and the need to seek a higher path, the dialogue moves to the character of Juno as a representation of worldly ambition and the psychological struggle of the soul leaving the active life for the contemplative life.</meta>

### Book Three

<margin>Lorenzo</margin>
...to finish the task. But the appetite, which is not yet entirely subject to reason, fights back completely; and through false arguments, it persuades one that either honors should not be neglected, or that one's former unholy ways should not be abandoned. Everything you have said, **LORENZO** <note>Lorenzo de' Medici, one of the interlocutors in Landino's dialogue</note> replied, is very convenient and extremely relevant to the matter. But there is one thing about which, even if I perhaps suspect a consistent answer, I would nevertheless greatly desire your opinion. For what, I ask, does that difficult and especially moody goddess Juno want for herself? For if she was angry while the Trojans remained in Troy, why does she subsequently oppose those same people with such a hostile spirit as they sail to Italy? Is it perhaps because both lives <note>the life of pleasure and the life of contemplation</note> are adverse to an ambitious man and one greedy for power?

<margin>Baptista</margin>
That is exactly it, **BAPTISTA** <note>Leon Battista Alberti, the famous architect and humanist who serves as the primary teacher in this dialogue</note> said. For the goddess of Ambition was formerly angry at Aeneas when, charmed by pleasures, he sought nothing honorable; but now she is angry again when she sees him raised toward certain higher things and utterly despising those things which other mortals hold in admiration. For, leaving behind those things which hold the first rank in civil life, he no longer seeks mortal things, but immortal ones. The poet therefore puts it wonderfully:

<margin>The Poet</margin>
> Scarcely out of sight of the Sicilian land, they were
> happily giving their sails to the deep, and were plowing the foam of the salt sea with their bronze prows,
> when Juno, nursing an eternal wound deep in her heart... <note>original: "Vix e conspectu Siculae telluris..." Aeneid 1.34-36</note>

and what follows next. For the way of life <note>the "active" or "civil" life</note> which serves honors, when it notices that it is being deserted by Aeneas—by whom it had also formerly been neglected when he loved pleasure and leisure—grieves vehemently. For she <note>Ambition/Juno</note> recognizes that if the Roman Empire is established, her own Carthage <note>representing the worldly city or the peak of earthly ambition</note> will surely fall. For who would not understand that if men of excellent talent turn toward <term>contemplation</term> <note>the highest form of life in Platonic philosophy, dedicated to the study of divine truths</note>, they will desert civil actions? She grieves, therefore, and being reminded by the present injury, she remembers the past:

> For there remains stored deep in her mind
> the judgment of Paris and the injury to her slighted beauty,
> and the hated race, and the honors of the kidnapped Ganymede. <note>original: "Manet enim alta mente repostum..." Aeneid 1.26-28</note>

Indeed, if these myths are considered more diligently, they will relate nothing else to us besides a life given over to pleasures. For the judgment of Paris, in which Venus is preferred to Juno—what else do you think this is but a soft, weakened life addicted to pleasures being preferred to a life greedy for honors? Furthermore, the "hated race"—that is, the adultery of Jupiter and Electra <note>the parents of Dardanus, the founder of the Trojan race</note>—and finally the kidnapping of Ganymede <note>a Trojan prince taken by Jupiter to be his cupbearer, replacing Juno's daughter Hebe</note>, no one who is even moderately learned would interpret as anything else. Inflamed by these things, therefore, Juno attempts to destroy the Trojans in a shipwreck. But so that those things which are hidden under the fiction of this kind of storm do not escape us in any part, and lest what Juno, what Aeolus, and what Neptune signify remains unknown, we must recount a few things about the power and nature of our souls. I will, however, give this forewarning: it happens that the same principles must be applied to explain many passages. If I express these once and then pass over them in silence in subsequent places as if already known, I see that I will serve the brevity which I so greatly desire. But then again, if we act in this way, I would be imposing a double burden on you at the same time: for you would have to both remember what was said elsewhere and, in the meantime, perceive what is being woven together in the continuous thread of the discourse without any gap. I prefer to be more talkative than to risk the mind being distracted by many things at the same moment, or being forced to skip over even the smallest part of the discussion as unknown. Since, therefore, regarding that which...

<summary>Lorenzo and Baptista discuss the goddess Juno as an allegory for worldly ambition, explaining her hatred for Aeneas as the conflict between the soul's desire for higher contemplation and the demands of the active civil life. The text introduces the famous opening of the Aeneid, interpreting the storm and Juno's grievances (Paris, Ganymede) as psychological hurdles.</summary>
