<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Virgil's Aeneid, specifically focusing on the psychological and philosophical meanings behind the characters. Having established Juno as a symbol of worldly ambition, the text now explores the division of the human soul into higher and lower faculties, using the figures of Aeolus, Neptune, and Jove to explain how reason governs or succumbs to the passions.</meta>

# On the Allegories of Publius Virgilius Maro

<margin>
Understanding.
Mind.
Soul.
Wisdom.
Prudence.
</margin>

This is our own and what we have drawn from the divinity of our origin: that is, we are raised up to reason, to contemplate, and to understand. We employ that part of the soul which the Greeks call <term>**ΝΟΥΝ** (Nous)</term> <note>The highest part of the intellect or intuitive mind, often distinguished from discursive reasoning.</note> and we call the **mind**. Truly, many—but especially the Platonic and Christian philosophers—have willed this to be twofold: one lower part, which they call *reason*; the other more divine and superior, which they call *understanding*. Therefore, Avicenna <note>A Persian polymath (c. 980–1037) whose philosophical works were central to Medieval and Renaissance thought.</note> wisely said that our souls are marked by two faces, like another Janus: so that with this face looking upward, we may look upon the highest principles through *wisdom*. With that other face, however, we may manage mortal things and human actions through *prudence*. The mind is thus divided into two: upward into wisdom, and downward into prudence—which is the right method of doing things—just like into a man and a woman: so that the man is superior and rules, and the woman is inferior and is ruled.

<margin>
Man: Understanding/Wisdom.
Woman: Reason/Prudence.
</margin>

Wherefore that saying is excellent: <term>Better is the iniquity of a man than a woman doing good</term> <note>original: "Melior est iniquitas uiri: quā mulier benefaciens" — a quote from the biblical book of Ecclesiasticus (42:14). The author uses this potentially jarring phrase to make a philosophical point about the hierarchy of the soul.</note>. For the iniquity of a man is not preferred over the equity of a woman in a literal sense; but it is aptly expressed that he acts more rightly who, seized by the desire for heavenly things, neglects many comforts of the body and social life—which seems "iniquitous" to the world—than he who, so as not to abandon any duty of civil life, neglects the care for heavenly things. Since these things are so, let us see what is said by Maro <note>Virgil's cognomen, or family name.</note>: Namely, that Aeolus, at the prayers of Juno, released the winds—which he ought to rule by the command of Jove—into the sea; in which storm the Trojans could have been overwhelmed had they not been succored by Neptune. In this passage, if Juno represents the desire for civil life, we will aptly interpret Aeolus as the *lower reason* and Neptune as the *higher reason* of man. It is no wonder, then, if that lower reason is bent by the most burning desire for honors and power, and is cast down from its rank. The myths report that Aeolus was placed over the winds by Jove so that, by his command, he might be able to both restrain them within their prison and let them out from there by a certain law. Wherefore *Aeolus sits in a high citadel holding his scepter, and he softens their spirits and tempers their rages;* and also: *Unless he did so, they would surely carry the seas and lands and deep heaven with them in their rapid flight, and sweep them through the air.* <note>Quoting Aeneid 1.56-59.</note>

And indeed, our souls were so established by the supreme architect of all things that, since there is in us that part which rises up to seek or flee things, that little part of reason is placed over it; which, though it is lower, moves the appetite to do all things rightly. However, the reason implanted in mortals themselves is not from the body, but from Jove. Therefore, while this reason obeys its Creator, it "sits in a high citadel" because it thinks of nothing humble, but meditates on everything excellent and lofty; and it "holds the scepter." For it has the administration of the whole life; it "softens their spirits and tempers their rages" when it restrains the appetite from excessive desires and keeps it within the bounds of modesty. But if it should be so caressed by the flatteries of Juno <note>Worldly ambition or the active civil life.</note> that, forgetful of its own nature and origin, it is ensnared by the desires for earthly things, it gives itself entirely to her. And at her command—and not Jove’s—it sends out the winds; for these are the <term>perturbations</term> <note>original: "perturbationes" — the Stoic term for passions or emotions that disturb the soul's rational calm.</note>. They truly strike the sea—which we said earlier was the appetite—from different directions, and though it was previously calm, they stir up horrendous storms. For the desires for honors dull the edge of reason, which, as if covered by clouds, does not discern the true good from the false. And it not only fails to extinguish the burning appetite which takes its origin from the senses, but even further inflames it; and the race hostile to Juno—that is the mind...

<summary>This page provides a Neo-Platonic allegorical reading of Aeolus and the winds in the Aeneid, defining the soul's division into "higher" wisdom and "lower" prudence. It explains that when the lower reason (Aeolus) listens to ambition (Juno) instead of divine law (Jove), the passions (winds) create a storm in the human appetite (the sea).</summary>

## Book Three

...disdainful of human affairs, yet greedy for divine things, it strives to overwhelm the soul with insane disturbances. But I return to Juno: for she, when a recent injury had rubbed her old wound raw, full of anger, she heads to Aeolia:

->The homeland of storms, places teeming with the raging south winds.<- 
<note>original: "Nimborum in patriam loca fœta furentibus auſtris." A quote from Virgil’s Aeneid, Book 1, line 51.</note>

She is an entirely clever goddess who did not fail to choose for herself the region best suited to fulfilling her desires. For when the love of human things strives to pull us away from the knowledge of divine things, it must necessarily come to Aeolia, the homeland of the winds—for that is the place exposed to the disturbances of the **appetite** <note>In this context, "appetite" refers to the "appetitive" part of the soul: the source of our drives, desires, and emotions.</note>. Truly, by the command of Jove, this kingdom was entrusted to Aeolus; for if we obey God, the appetite will always obey **reason**. For that Platonic maxim is most correct: *God is the law for the good man, but lust is the law for the wicked.* Wherefore Juno attempts to deprave this sort of reason. And just like those who lack confidence in their own case and prepare to persuade judges with false reasons or corrupt them with bribes, so she approaches Aeolus. She tries to show that it is just and useful that he should prohibit the nation hostile to her from reaching Italy. Aeolus is persuaded, and he undertakes to do everything according to Juno’s command. Indeed, whatever power of rule he has, he acknowledges it all as coming from Juno. For unless the appetite is inflamed by the desire for earthly things, and unless that appetite—like the sea—is troubled by winds, our life would seem to have very little need for the **rule of reason** <note>The OCR reads "impio rationis," likely a transcription error for "imperio rationis," the governance of reason.</note>.

In this way, therefore, reason confesses that it owes everything to Juno—as if (unless disturbances are present) it has nothing upon which to exercise its rule. Deceived by the desire for those things which it considers great, reason lets go of the reins of the winds and disturbs the sea, for it stirs up the appetite with most turbulent desires. By these, **Aeneas**—who strives toward the contemplation of heavenly things—is so broken by the magnitude of his labors and dangers that he is cast down from his purpose. And indeed, when the appetite (by which alone the soul is moved and by which we ought to be led to the highest good) drags us elsewhere, that most atrocious storm arises, in which "clouds suddenly snatch away the sky and the day from the eyes of the Trojans." For those who just a moment before were carried toward contemplation with a calm appetite are so blinded by the rising disturbances that whatever light proceeded from reason is entirely taken away. Whence it happens that "black night broods upon the deep." The appetite, which until now was illuminated by the light of reason, now, having lost that light, dwells in darkness. 

And this sea seethes so much that some are struck by the north wind—for this is a kind of elation that flows from prosperity. Others "hang upon the crest of the wave": for those attempting difficult and lofty things beyond their strength hang there, anxious and troubled by perpetual expectation. Others, touching the earth among the waves, are cast down by fortune itself and overwhelmed by a mountain of miseries. Finally, there are those who are twisted onto hidden rocks. For many are carried blindly by the force of their disturbances to fulfill these sorts of desires, and they fall into various dangers unforeseen by them. Lastly, there are those whom greed draws into the quicksands. For who does not see that there are also those whose ship is submerged? Whence it is entirely true that "rare swimmers appear in the vast deep" <note>original: "rari nantes in gurgite uasto." A famous line from Aeneid 1.118, describing the shipwrecked sailors.</note>. For out of the innumerable crowd of mortals whom the storm of disturbances submerges, few are able to emerge. Indeed, having formed a habit [of virtue], few can swim to the harbor—especially when the pilot himself is torn from the tiller and, cast down from his instructions, rushes into the deep. For when that part of the soul which is appointed to rule life is cast from its seat, who would not think the entire business is lost? When all these things had happened through the fault of Juno and Aeolus, meanwhile **Neptune**, moved by a grave...

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of the storm in Aeneid Book One, casting Aeolus as Reason and the winds as Passions. It describes how worldly ambition (Juno) can corrupt reason, leading to a spiritual shipwreck where the soul is blinded to divine light.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Virgil’s Aeneid, moving from the depiction of Aeolus (the lower reason) and the winds (the passions) to the intervention of Neptune, who represents the higher, governing reason of the human soul.</meta>

### On the Allegories of Publius Virgil Maro

...tossed by the storm, **he lifted his calm head from the surface of the wave**. <note>original: "placidum caput ex summa unda extulit." A reference to Neptune's first appearance in Aeneid, Book 1, line 127.</note> The poets imagined Neptune to be the supreme god of the sea. I say "supreme" because other water deities also exist; and indeed, many powers preside over the **appetite** <term>appetite: in Renaissance philosophy, the "appetitive" part of the soul is where desires and emotions originate.</term>. For the appetite is moved by the judgment of the senses, and it is moved by the lower reason; however, the highest authority is reserved for the **higher reason** <term>higher reason: the part of the human mind capable of divine contemplation and moral certainty, distinguished from "lower reason" which handles practical, worldly logic.</term>. Therefore, when this reason—which the poet now signifies by the name of Neptune—sees the soul being snatched away and harassed by all these disturbances, it lifts its head above the waves as if from a watchtower. From there, observing the waves and tempests of the appetite itself, reason recognizes that the mind is being swept into an abyss by that fury. 

He does not only perceive the storm, but he also sees that it has been stirred up by the deceits of Juno. For right reason knows that a mind so affected has fallen into these miseries because the lower reason, deceived by a false hope of "goods" (worldly benefits), not only failed to restrain the winds but actually let them out. Therefore, to bring a sudden remedy to such a great evil, he calls the East and West winds <note>Eurus and Zephyrus</note> and the rest of the winds to himself and rebukes them sternly, for they—born of the impious blood of the Titans—dare to infest the kingdom of the gods. 

The myths relate that the winds were the sons of Astreus, and they say Astreus was one of those Titans who dared to take up a reckless war against the immortal gods. You will find these things in the fables. But if we listen to Cicero and other most learned men, what else shall we interpret "waging war with the gods" to mean than resisting our own nature? Indeed, I find nothing that could be more reckless than this; for just as we say and feel that those men are wise who follow nature as the best guide, so we shall deem those fools and reckless who desert her entirely. 

Rightly, therefore, did they imagine the winds to be born from Titans, since disturbances always arise from a reckless judgment that fights against nature. Thus, the disturbances commit a bold crime when, through human folly and recklessness, they dare to disturb the appetite, which should be subject to the command of our divinity—that is, our reason. For this reason, the winds are justly rebuked by Neptune, and Neptune justly claims the "rule of the sea" for himself; for in a well-ordered soul, it is necessary that the appetite be moved by the judgment of the mind alone. 

I thought that Aeolus and Neptune could be conveniently applied to this sort of meaning. But if you are not satisfied in any part, or if something comes to mind that fits the passage more aptly, you may bring it forward. There is nothing for you to fear or be hindered by shame. For you will find no one among all living men who allows himself to be refuted with a more level head than I, or who more greedily learns what he did not know. Nor should you say, "I am a youth and he is an old man." For I have seen many of those who are considered (and are) most learned come to the knowledge of things—through the reminder of even the most unlearned man—which they had never reached by their own talent in so long a time. 

"I do not know what has happened to others," said **Lawrence** <note>Laurentius, likely referring to Lorenzo de' Medici, the author's patron and student.</note>, "but I will never claim so much for myself. However, because it often happens to the most learned men, amidst such a great abundance and variety of things, that many thoughts offer themselves to the mind at the same time, some things—even if not fully explained—can easily be perceived by their similarity to the rest. There are also other things which, although they are explained clearly and in detail, those of a duller wit can hardly follow. I will use the liberty you allow me; and since nothing was said by you regarding the marriage of Aeolus and **Deiopea** <note>The nymph Juno offered to Aeolus as a bribe to release the winds.</note>, I ask—unless you consider it entirely useless—that if there is anything in that little story that bears upon our subject, you explain it to us. But you will say: has it not occurred to you yet...?"

<summary>This page explains the allegory of Neptune as the "higher reason" that intervenes to calm the "winds" of human passion stirred up by worldly ambition (Juno). It concludes with a dialogue between the teacher and his student, Lawrence, regarding the hidden meaning of Aeolus's marriage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the allegorical meaning of Virgil's Aeneid. While the previous page discussed Neptune as the "higher reason" calming the storm of the passions, this page shifts to the speech of the character Baptista (representing the philosopher Leon Battista Alberti), who explains the different layers of meaning—fabulous, physical, and civil—found in poetry, specifically focusing on Juno’s role as the "air."</meta>

# Book Three

<margin>
Fabulous
Natural
Civil
</margin>
...you wear me out with these minor details. It is indeed appropriate. For when reason must be led away from divine things toward human ones, almost no greater reward can be proposed than a beautiful and chaste marriage; for from that, the natural desire for the union of male and female is fulfilled. From this also comes the propagation of offspring, which is not only for our pleasure and use but also looks out for posterity, so that even when we are dead, we may live on in them in some way. At this very point, **BAPTISTA** said: "You should not only have a reason for the question proposed, but for whatever else is to be desired. For I will reveal here what would have to be revealed a little later elsewhere." 

The ancients, therefore, who wrote about the nature of the gods, established three types of theology:
1. One is **fabulous** <term>fabulous: from "fable," referring to the mythological stories used by poets.</term>, which the Greeks call *mythikon*; they used this to entertain idle people in the theater. 
2. The second is **natural** (*physikon*), through which they aptly express the power of nature. For example, when they signify "time" to us through the image of **Saturn** devouring all his children except for those four—for if you except the four elements, time consumes all things with its voracity. 
3. The third they call **civil**, because from it are brought forth precepts for living well and happily. 

The poets—than whom you will find no one more learned—were accustomed to blend all these together and mix them into one, so that with a certain excellent temperament, they might soothe the ears with the greatest pleasure and at the same time nourish the mind with hidden doctrine, leading us toward what is right and honorable, and to the **Highest Good** itself <note>The "Highest Good" (Summum Bonum) refers to the ultimate goal of human existence, often identified with God or virtue.</note>. 

However, since it seemed a too laborious task to follow all these levels exactly in **Maro** <note>original: "Maro," the cognomen of the poet Virgil.</note>, we have omitted the first two types and confined our discussion within the boundaries of the **civil type**. Therefore, that little bit you just plucked from the fable will serve our work; the rest pertains to the "physical" [natural] type. 

For they say there were fourteen daughters of Phoebus [the Sun] and Aurora [the Dawn], and these were assigned as nymphs to **Juno**. According to the interpretation of the ancients, Juno is the **air**. Seven attributes are assigned to the air, and likewise, seven things are born in the air. All these things occur by the work of the Sun, especially when it is active in our hemisphere. 

But to speak of the first group: the first attribute of air is that it is light; second, mobile; third, hot; fourth, humid; fifth, serene; sixth, quiet; and seventh, breathable. The poets fashioned these as if they were seven nymphs. 

Among those things which are born in the air, they place first the one called **Iris** [the rainbow]. To her, they also attribute three "servants": rain, hail, and snow. In these, it happens that a cloud is placed opposite the sun; but the "body" of the clouds is such (so to speak) that it is dense in one part, denser in another, and densest in a third. Because of this, a reddish color comes from the first, a sky-blue from the second, and a black color from the third. Conversely, the parts of the cloud that are pure reflect a saffron color; those that are purer, green; and finally, the purest parts reflect white. This, therefore, is the first nymph among the other seven. Six others follow her: the **python** <note>original: "python." In Renaissance meteorology, this refers to a specific type of fiery atmospheric vapor or meteor, not the mythological serpent.</note>, the comet, lightning, thunder, exhalation, and the earthquake. We would not be burdened to discuss these in their order, were it not for the fact that we decided to follow only the **civil** type among those three I mentioned. 

But when you wish to know properly what these are and by what reason they are produced, you will easily learn it. For they are written about with great sharp-wittedness by **Aristotle** in the works called the *Meteorology*, and also clearly explained by **Albert** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar famous for his scientific commentaries.</note>, who is surnamed "the Great." 

The fact that Juno promises she will give **Deiopea**, the most beautiful of all, to Aeolus is not without reason. For she represents that "face" in the air which is called **serenity** [clear weather]. And what thing could draw Aeolus more into the desire for human affairs than the serene face of the sky? F...

i ii

<summary>Baptista explains that poets like Virgil hide deep philosophical truths within myths, classifying these meanings into fabulous, natural, and civil theology. He identifies Juno as an allegory for the air and explains meteorological phenomena like the rainbow (Iris) and serenity (Deiopea) through this lens.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Laurentius (Lorenzo de' Medici) and Baptista (Leon Battista Alberti) regarding the allegorical interpretation of Virgil's Aeneid. Baptista is explaining how characters and events in the poem represent the internal struggle between human reason and the passions.</meta>

->### On the Allegories of Publius Virgilius Maro<-

Indeed, he intended this to signify a prosperous and happy outcome in all affairs; it is through the hope of such an outcome that we endure all the labors and difficulties of social life.

**LAURENTIUS** said: "These things please me greatly; and they please me so much that I desire nothing more in them. What you said about reason and appetite also pleases me; but see that you do not speak contradictions. For if I remember correctly, a little earlier you wanted **Aeolus** to be the 'lower reason' <note>original: *inferiorem rationem*. This refers to practical or "civil" reason that deals with everyday actions and emotions.</note>; now you establish **Neptune** as the 'higher reason' <note>original: *superiorem*. This refers to the higher intellect or contemplative mind.</note>. Both are correct; but since Neptune says the empire was given to him and not to the other [Aeolus], I do not see why it is not also granted to Aeolus to either raise or settle the sea, whether by sending out or restraining the winds."

"I do not deny," said **Baptista**, "that it pertains to this lower reason to exercise judgment over actions, and to impel the appetite toward those things that should be embraced and call it away from those things that should be fled. But just as in a well-ordered republic a certain supreme magistrate is created, by whose judgment everything is carried out—even if other minor magistrates are present to handle individual tasks—so the command of the whole life consists in the **Mind** <note>original: *mente*</note>. However, this is done in such a way that the lower reason is set over the appetite with the law that it must judge nothing except what is right.

But if, deceived by the allurements of human things, it does not perceive correctly and its judgments are false, that supreme magistrate is present to whom one may appeal. For this reason, it is correctly written that **Aeolus** reigns only within a closed prison <note>A reference to Aeneid 1.52-54, where Aeolus keeps the winds in a cave.</note>, because in this common and civil life, the appetite is restrained by a certain force of reason rather than being made quiet and tranquil; for the passions are not converted into good affections, but are rather restrained by moderation.

However, the **higher reason**—when it has 'raised its head above the waves' <note>A famous image from the Aeneid where Neptune looks out over the storm.</note> and recognized the storm sent out by **Juno**—reduces all things to tranquility. It 'raises its head from the waves' when it rouses itself, reclaiming itself from the corporeal mass by which it was previously overwhelmed and oppressed, and calls itself away from the senses. At that time, it not only recognizes the storm that oppresses **Aeneas** to prevent him from reaching **Italy**, but it also understands the cause of such great evils to be **Juno**—that is, the desire for human things. 

Therefore, it removes the winds from the sea as quickly as possible; for an appetite free from disturbances is rendered more obedient to reason. And to deter them, it threatens that they will pay greater penalties to him than they received from **Aeneas**—and not without justice. For an appetite invaded by disturbances is vexed for a time. But if that supreme intelligence claims empire for itself—since the soul, illuminated by the highest light, is never thereafter deceived or slips—it is necessary that the disturbances, whose parent was false opinion, be buried deep within us. 

For this reason, the winds will not pay a similar penalty to **Neptune** for what they committed. Instead, the waves follow. With the winds removed, **Neptune** collects the scattered clouds and puts them to flight. For it is the role of intelligence to pursue individual false opinions from the beginning, gather them into one, and finally refute them; once these are refuted, then at last all things are illuminated by the light of the sun—for that is the knowledge of the truth.

Furthermore, **Cimothoe** and **Triton** lead the ships away from the rocks. 'Cimothoe,' running through the waves, is easily interpreted if you observe the Greek word <note>From Greek *kyma* (wave) and *thoos* (swift).</note>. **Triton**, moreover, is considered Neptune's trumpeter. Therefore, these two deities lead the ships back from the rocks of desires because when we know what is right, we relinquish vain things. We attain knowledge either by our own talent <note>original: *ingenio*</note>, when it runs through all things with its own velocity, or by doctrine received from elsewhere..."

<summary>This page features a dialogue between Laurentius and Baptista explaining Neptune as an allegory for "higher reason" or Intelligence. They argue that while practical reason (Aeolus) merely restrains passions, the higher intellect (Neptune) disperses the "clouds" of false opinion to reveal the sun of truth.</summary>

### Book Three

The speed of **Cymothoe** <note>A sea nymph; in this allegory, she represents the swiftness of thought or execution.</note> expresses that first point. **Triton**, however, signifies this: for just as trumpeters manifest the commands of a prince by their proclamation, so does doctrine <note>original: *doctrina*; here meaning "teaching" or "learning"</note> reveal what truth desires. That **Neptune** enjoys a prosperous course through a peaceful sea is easily proven. For when the appetite <note>The "appetite" or *appetitus* refers to our desires and emotions.</note> is calmed of all disturbances, reason glides through it in such a way that it never stumbles. We have spoken of the storm; now let us proceed to the rest.

The **Trojans**, snatched from such manifest danger by the benefit of Neptune, are weary and broken. Despairing that they can reach Italy (since it is a far-off land), they direct their sails toward the nearby coast of the **Carthaginians** with an extemporaneous and little-premeditated plan. I think you remember that **Italy** has the form of **contemplation** <note>original: *speculationis*; the pursuit of theoretical or divine truth.</note>, while **Carthage** has the form of **action** <note>The "active life" of politics and civic duty.</note>. 

Therefore, the poet now expresses what we often see happen in human life. For there are many who, since they find the **Highest Good** <note>original: *summum bonum*; the ultimate goal of human existence.</note> neither in pleasure, nor in riches, nor finally in honors, betake themselves to the knowledge of truth. Truly, when they think they can easily despise all human things and devote themselves to contemplation apart from the company of men, they have scarcely begun the task and left the world behind when so great a desire for the things they abandoned arises. They are so weakened <note>original: *effeminantur*; literally "made soft," a common philosophical term for losing mental discipline.</note> by the memory of such great allurements that they fall again into the greatest disturbances. Although these are finally settled by supreme reason, our minds are nonetheless left so weary and exhausted—having escaped such a horrendous storm only with great difficulty—that they think they have done more than enough if, inhabiting human society, they escape those vices which are monstrous and destructive to the human race. 

As for the virtues, if they do not retain them in an exact and perfect state, they at least hold onto them in their beginning stages. Being deterred with difficulty from that life which is placed in the contemplation of truth, they notice that this kind of life almost exceeds human weakness. Since **Aristotle** affirms that one who can do these things is something greater than a man <note>A reference to the *Nicomachean Ethics*, where Aristotle suggests the contemplative life is "divine" compared to the merely human active life.</note>, they reason with themselves thus:

"It will be no small thing to have escaped the fires of pleasures, to have avoided the robberies of the **Thracians**, to have fled the filth of the **Harpies** and the monstrosity of the **Cyclopes**. Now, if we cannot achieve that which seems to belong to divinity rather than humanity, who will find fault if we live prudently, justly, bravely, and finally temperately within the society of men—for whose cultivation, protection, and increase we were born? Let us be prepared to avoid no labor and no danger for our fatherland and our parents. Let us observe piety toward all who are joined to us by blood; let us liberally aid our fellow citizens who are in need, show the right way to those who wander, or lift up those oppressed by injury through our counsel, effort, influence, and authority."

They cast aside the contemplation of great things to a more mature age and to old age itself, which is usually freer from the many disturbances by which this kind of life is most hindered. Indeed, they trust they can defend this opinion by what is said of **Isaac**, the son of the great **Abraham**. For what is read of that patriarch—that he "went out to meditate in the field as the day was already declining" <note>Genesis 24:63. The author uses this bibical passage as a metaphor for starting a contemplative life in old age.</note>—they interpret to mean that he went out from the bodily senses to meditate in the "field" (as if apart from human crowds) when the day was "declining"—that is, around old age, when the heat of the blood is already ceasing. Moreover, they attempt to strengthen their cause by the testimony of the gravest men, who [prefer] the use...

<summary>This page explains the allegory of the Trojans diverting to Carthage as the soul's retreat from the difficult "contemplative life" (Italy) to the "active life" of civic virtue. It justifies focusing on social and moral duties when divine contemplation proves too taxing for human nature until old age.</summary>
<keywords>Neptune, Triton, Trojans, Italy vs. Carthage, Contemplation vs. Action, Aristotle, Civic Virtue, Isaac, Old Age, Allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of Virgil’s Aeneid. Having established that Carthage represents the active life of civic duty and Italy represents the contemplative life of higher truth, the author now justifies why one might choose the active life first, using examples from classical history and philosophy.</meta>

### On Publius Virgilius Maro's Allegory

...the matter. For those who consider the source from which the "good" arises, they often place **action** before **contemplation**. This is especially true in one's "greener years" <note>original: *uiridiori ætate*; referring to youth and the prime of life.</note>, a time in which they are commanded to act as philosophers, to manage the republic, to serve in the military, and to lead. They point out that **Plato** himself, in his more vigorous years, undertook voyages to Sicily and devoted his efforts to **Dion** <note>Dion of Syracuse, a disciple of Plato who attempted to implement Platonic political reforms.</note>. Yet, as he grew old, he found rest in the **Academy** <note>The school founded by Plato in Athens, dedicated to philosophical research.</note> concerning the investigation of truth. 

They also greatly praise the young **Xenophon** for his conduct in practical affairs, but they admire him as an old man for his **speculation** <note>original: *speculatione*; here meaning the contemplative study of divine or natural truths.</note>, and they consider him blessed because of his leisure. Furthermore, they show that many men traveled through numerous nations to become wiser. For this reason, **Homer** calls **Odysseus** wise because he "knew the cities and customs of many men." 

Gathering these and many other such points into one—which you, with the highest skill and prudence, were enumerating just the day before yesterday while extolling this kind of life—they cast aside the goal of contemplation until a later age and, in the meantime, turn themselves toward the conduct of civil affairs. Indeed, if anyone disparages this kind of life, they will surely give everyone a just reason to disparage them in return. For the active life has its own duties, and very many distinguished ones at that. If these duties are performed correctly, it will undoubtedly produce both utility for others and glory and tranquility for oneself, as far as human frailty allows.

Wherefore, not without the highest reason, a safe and tranquil harbor on the **Carthaginian** shore is described. I ask you to inspect its form and location more diligently. *For there is a place in a deep recess, which an island makes into a harbor.* <note>A translation of Virgil’s Aeneid, Book 1, lines 159-160.</note> The "mainland" represents the life of mortals, for that land is less exposed to the waves of the sea. However, the "island" is struck on all sides by the constantly surging and raging waves. Yet, it holds firm by its own mass so that it remains forever immovable against the onslaught of every wind and wave. 

For these things which are momentary—and which human folly nevertheless considers "goods"—are subject to the recklessness of **Fortune**. Since these things set human minds on fire with self-love, they would surely consume us unless we were like an "island" in the middle of the sea: which, although hammered on all sides by the ocean, does not sink because it is stabilized by **virtues**. 

The harbor is in a "deep recess" because a mind fortified by virtues against the attacks of Fortune is set far apart from disturbances. These disturbances are repelled by the "projection of the sides" of the harbor. On one side, **Fortitude** is opposed to adversity; on the other, **Temperance** is opposed to prosperity. These are rightly called "vast cliffs," for **Virtue** is placed in a difficult and high location. Add to this that it holds the middle ground so that, whichever way you move from there, you arrive at the extremes. It is from there, as if from a <term>precipice</term> <note>original: *pcipiti* (praecipiti); a steep drop representing a fall from virtue into vice.</note> on a cliff, that you might slip, and "twin rocks threaten toward the sky." 

For we do not undertake great things relying only on our own **Prudence**. Rather, we do so much more by trusting in **Divine Counsel**. Nor was it said rashly that "under the peak of the cliffs the safe waters are silent." For the **appetite** <note>The emotional or desiring part of the soul.</note>, when illuminated by this double light <note>The "double light" refers to human prudence and divine guidance.</note>, is always free from all disturbance. 

Furthermore, there is a reason why "a backdrop of shimmering woods" and "a black grove with a shuddering shadow" looms from above. You will find no man so prudent that he can fully foresee the outcomes of those things which Fortune turns by her own recklessness. We are exposed to so many and such diverse accidents that we often seek with our highest prayers things that will eventually harm us; and we flee with all our industry from those things which, if they happened, would be for our safety and use, as if they were harmful. Finally, "sweet waters" are found at the "opposing face." For when one is far from the waves of various desires...

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of the harbor at Carthage in Virgil's Aeneid, arguing that the "active life" of civic duty (represented by Carthage) is a necessary stage of life that prepares the soul for virtue before reaching the "contemplative life." The physical features of the harbor—the island, cliffs, and woods—are explained as symbols of the mind's protection against Fortune through virtues like Fortitude and Temperance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Virgil's Aeneid. While the previous page discussed the merits of the active life in youth, this page focuses on how the "social life" (civic duty) serves as a necessary but temporary harbor on the way to the higher, "divine" contemplative life, using Aeneas’s arrival in North Africa as a metaphor.</meta>

<header>Book Three</header>

### Book Three

We have come to a halt: a life of this sort arises; though it may not be entirely blessed and quiet, it is nevertheless tranquil. Those who have long pursued the highest goals, but were finally deterred by difficulty and betook themselves to the **social life**, enter a harbor of this kind. In this life, as they are refined by civic virtues, they win no small measure of praise; yet they are far from that divinity which we seek.

Now, the fact that he [Aeneas] entered here with seven ships, and that he looked out for the rest from a cliff, and finally relieved the need of his companions with wine—all this pertains to our understanding that he who takes up the administration of a republic must undergo all labors and all inconveniences to preserve the safety and well-being of those committed to his care. 

For this reason, **Achates** <term>Acates</term> <note>Aeneas's loyal companion, whose name the author etymologically links to the Greek word 'achos' (care or concern), representing the diligence of a leader.</note> (for that is the care of a prince) will strike a fire—that is, he will ignite the idle toward taking action—so that the things necessary for sustenance are not lacking. And climbing the cliffs, he will seek those who are absent; and if he cannot protect them, he will consult for those who are present. When he has relieved their need, he will also console them with a speech. He will imbue them with the best **precepts** <term>pceptis</term> <note>Latin: *praeceptis*; moral instructions or rules of conduct.</note> so as to warn them that this kind of life is not such that we should wish to fix our permanent seat in it. Rather, all labors and difficulties must be overcome so that we may arrive in **Italy**, where at last they will find quiet dwellings and where **Troy** <term>Troia</term> shall rise again. 

For when a life of pleasure was sought there [in the old Troy], that pleasure was present which, proceeding from the senses, was corporeal and fleeting, and which was immediately followed by repentance. In Italy, however, the highest pleasure will come from the **speculation** <note>original: *speculatione*; here referring to the Neoplatonic concept of the soul’s intellectual vision of divine truth.</note> of divine things. This is the true and simple pleasure, which is perpetual and eternal, followed by no sorrow. 

For this is the administration of the best prince: when he sees that civic action serves human necessity, but not the divinity that is within us, he will conduct himself in it in such a way that, while he provides for the needs of mortals, he nevertheless raises the souls of his people to divine things. He will command them to endure against the accidents of fortune and to preserve themselves for the favorable circumstances they will find in **Latium**. 

O divine genius! O man, utterly excellent among the rarest of men, and truly worthy of the name of poet! Though he was not a Christian, he nevertheless brings forth all things most similar to the truest Christian doctrine. Read the **Apostle Paul**—for I wish to name him here from among all as the head of our religion—who directs human life toward a norm of this sort: that the necessities of the body are not to be withdrawn, and yet one must always be free for investigating the truth. 

For what does Paul describe at such length and breadth that this poet does not compress into poetic constraints? A truly marvelous thing: that almost every single word is able to embrace the longest sentiments from the Platonic and Aristotelica Republics. 

But I do not wish—something I have nowhere done until now—to follow this passage so exactly that the remaining parts must then either be omitted or passed over with such speed that the same thing happens to us as usually happens to those viewing a long painting while running at a very rapid pace. For in a single point of time, even when they strive greatly, they can scarcely note the colors, let alone the outlines, the likenesses of bodies, and what the Greeks call **symmetry**. Therefore, let the rest be deferred to a time of greater leisure.

<margin>The speech of Venus</margin>

It is now pleasing for the speech of **Venus** to **Jove**, and in turn Jove to Venus, to contain the pure sequence of the text. For all things serve the poetic fiction; nevertheless, Maro [Virgil] plucks something from mathematics and finally praises the Julian family, especially **Augustus**. For I judge that those things we wish to refer to allegory should only be summoned from where they follow their leader of their own accord.

<summary>This page explains Aeneas's arrival in Africa as an allegory for the "social life" of a civic leader, who must provide for his people's physical needs while guiding their souls toward the "Italy" of divine contemplation. The author compares Virgil's philosophical depth to the teachings of the Apostle Paul and the political theories of Plato and Aristotle.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Virgil's Aeneid. Having established that Aeneas has entered the "harbor" of the active social life (civic duty), the author now explains Aeneas’s first actions on the Libyan coast as a metaphor for how a wise leader must evaluate the character of his citizens—distinguishing between those who are truly "human" and those who have become "beasts" through vice.</meta>

# On the Allegory of P. Virgilius Maro

If, however, by the force of genius they are unearthed in life, they lose so much of their weightiness that they are rendered almost ridiculous. Therefore, let us omit anxious interpretations and assume only those which do not hide in concealment but offer themselves freely to those who seek them. What I said a little while ago regarding mathematical matters <note>In the Renaissance, "mathematics" often included astrology or the study of the celestial order, which the author likely touched upon in the preceding section as part of a higher "contemplative" knowledge.</note> was explained at the beginning briefly, but as the constraints of time allowed, not entirely obscurely. Therefore, I return to Aeneas. 

For he, recalling many things in his mind through the night, decided as soon as it dawned to explore the nature of the places more carefully, and to investigate whether men or wild beasts occupy them. In these verses, [Virgil] excellently expresses what sort of person an administrator of a **republic** <term>rei publicæ</term> <note>Latin: *res publica*; literally "the public thing," referring here to the state or the common good.</note> ought to be. And first of all, he approves of that Homeric sentiment: 

->For who, to whom the care of so many mortals has been entrusted, would spend the entire night in sleep?<- <note>original: "Quis enim cui tot mortalium cura commissa sit universam noctem somno impendet." This is a Latin translation of a famous line from Homer’s *Iliad* (2.24), often used to describe the "sleepless watch" of a responsible ruler.</note>

Everyone will admit this was said with the highest wisdom. For it is the primary duty of the best prince, when he sees uncultivated places, to propose to himself to discover whether men or wild beasts inhabit them. For whoever looks carefully at civil life will find varied human geniuses, varied pursuits, and varied customs. There are those who always lean toward the right and the honorable, favor civil concord, desire liberty to be safe, and want no one to be more powerful than the laws in the republic. They relieve those oppressed by injury. They desire the pride of seditious citizens to be cast down. They increase the public majesty to the best of their strength. Finally, they prefer religion and justice above all things. These, therefore, can rightly be called **men**, since they do not abandon the duties of human nature. 

On the contrary, however, you may find many whose most wanton lust leaves nothing sacred, nothing modest; many who, kindled by the greatest greed, hold everything for sale, and either like little foxes <note>A common Renaissance trope comparing the "fox-like" cunning of the deceitful to the "lion-like" strength of the violent.</note> deceive the unwary with tricks and snares, or—since they are superior in strength—wish also to surpass in wealth and honors those to whom they are far inferior in wisdom and **virtue** <term>virtute</term>. Men depraved by vices of this kind, although they retain the human form and limbs, nevertheless, since they have put on the manners of beasts, are no longer to be thought of as men but as most cruel wild beasts. 

Therefore, in human gatherings, those things which bristle like uncultivated places with the briars and brambles of vice are far more numerous than those which shine, cultivated by noble arts and distinguished virtues. Aeneas therefore proceeds to explore each thing carefully, yet not rashly; but he takes with him **Achates** <term>Acates</term>, his most faithful companion, and walks equipped with arms. For who has ever correctly administered a republic whose mind was either void of care and diligence or lacked fortitude? For by those [cares] we foresee long before what must be done; by this [fortitude] we resist, so that nothing we have foreseen as great and noble remains undone because of fear. 

Therefore, since the purpose of contemplation has been deferred to another time and Aeneas departs for civil life—and since much labor is required in it—it is necessary that he reach it with his mother as leader. For unless the mind grows warm with the **love** <note>Here, Venus (Aeneas's mother and the goddess of Love) is allegorized as the "love of virtue" or the "passion for duty" that drives a leader to endure the hardships of governing.</note> of those things which must be done, it must necessarily grow numb against such great and varied labors. His mother therefore meets him, yet not as a confessed goddess, such as she is accustomed to appear to the inhabitants of heaven and in such greatness. For she showed herself in that way when she led her son away from pleasure with the plan that he should strive for the highest; at which time it was necessary for him to be inflamed with the love of divine things, which, being itself divine, is far removed from all matter and body. But here he is kindled with the love of those things which are corporeal and largely submerged in matter...

<summary>This page provides an allegorical reading of Aeneas exploring Libya, interpreting it as a statesman's duty to distinguish between virtuous citizens ("men") and those ruled by vice ("beasts"). It also introduces Venus not as the goddess of lust, but as the necessary "love of duty" required for the active civic life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical interpretation of Virgil's Aeneid. Following the previous discussion of Aeneas as a leader, the author now interprets Aeneas’s encounter with his mother, the goddess Venus, who appears in disguise. This leads into a deep Neoplatonic and Hermetic lecture on the nature of the "Forest" (Sylva/Matter) and the hierarchy of the universe.</meta>

# Book Three

k

...[not revealed] <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's description of Venus appearing to Aeneas.</note>. For this reason, she did not reveal herself as a goddess; instead, having disguised herself in human form, she offered herself to her son and appeared to him in the forest as a huntress. I shall explain this place to you more clearly if I first lay out a few things—and only those which are necessary—concerning the **forest** <term>sylva</term> <note>The author is playing on the double meaning of the Latin word *sylva*: literally "a forest" or "woods," but philosophically used (following the translator Calcidius) to represent the Greek word *hyle*, meaning "prime matter" or the raw material of the universe.</note>.

**Hermes Trismegistus**, **Homer**, and **Plato** have shown that a certain right order of all existing things exists. And so that they might more clearly express what they felt, they imagined a **golden chain** <note>The *Catena Aurea* or Golden Chain of Being, a concept originating in Homer’s *Iliad* where Zeus claims he could pull up the earth and sea with a chain, later adapted by Neoplatonists to describe the hierarchy of existence.</note> let down from the fountain of nature to the very lowest dregs, by which all things are connected in their various steps. Since its origin is the essence of God, the order proceeds such that **Power** is placed in the second rank, **Wisdom** in the third, and **Will** in the fourth. After these follows **Fate**, and then the **World Soul**; next are the celestial **spirits** <term>demones</term> <note>The author uses *demones* in the classical sense of *daemons*—intermediary spirits or divine powers that inhabit different realms—rather than the modern sense of "demons."</note>; then there are the ethereal, the aerial, the watery, and finally the earthly spirits. 

However, the very last of all things, **matter** <note>original: "hyle"</note> (which we call the **forest**), resides at the bottom. I could pursue each of these points in my discourse, not without the greatest use and pleasure; but since they are not necessary for our dispute, I will favor brevity. Therefore, omitting the rest, we shall place **God** as the beginning and the **forest** as the extreme end in the chain. Thus, nothing is higher than God, and nothing is lower than the forest. Nothing is more excellent than Him; nothing is viler than it. Indeed, the middle things, while surpassing the lower, are conquered by the higher.

<margin>God
Matter</margin>

Therefore, there is God and the forest; the latter, however, is the **matter** from which all bodies are made. For just as a woodworker has the **wood** <note>original: "sylvam"</note> as the material from which he makes all things—for that wood contains what is still crude and unformed, but nevertheless, by its own innate capacity, is able to receive all forms—and the craftsman transforms it into whatever forms he wishes; by the same reasoning, matter is to God. For God has embraced all **forms** from eternity. Matter, however, if you look at its nature, has no certain or expressed form. But by its innate capacity for receiving, and (if I may speak thus) it seems to contain all of them confusedly. 

Indeed, matter is so called because it becomes a **mother** <note>A Latin etymological pun: *materia* (matter) derives from *mater* (mother).</note>. God, however, is the **Father**, and **Form** is the offspring. For God gives; the forest receives; form is born. For this reason, **Trismegistus** rightly calls the Father and Mother eternal, but the offspring mortal. Matter is the Mother because she provides the womb. God begets and creates, and does so by His own power; she, however, conceives from the mixing in of another. Furthermore, she conceives by the infusion of the **divine spirit**, which Trismegistus calls the **World Soul**. This fact moves him to attribute the office of Father to God, because He infuses; and that of Mother to the forest, because she conceives from God. Finally, he says the World Soul has the power of seed, because it is inspired by God into the lap of the forest. 

I pass over many names by which he expresses the various properties of the forest, for they pertain nothing to what we are doing. Yet he often calls the whole of matter "**malignity**," and not without justice. For she alone is the cause for things falling into destruction. For that which is separated from matter never perishes; it never receives what is contrary to itself, but always flees and turns away from it. But that which is contained in the lap of the forest falls into destruction for this reason: because the forest, since it stands indifferent to all those things they call "qualities," it happens that—just like a second **Helen**—she calls **Menelaus** into the house and opens the threshold <note>The author compares prime matter to Helen of Troy, who betrayed her husband Menelaus. In this allegory, matter is "unfaithful" because it constantly abandons its current "form" (husband) to welcome in a new, often "hostile" form, leading to the decay of the original object.</note>. For while she admits forms contrary to those she had previously received, it easily happens that the external forms rushing in extinguish the domestic ones. For this reason, who would not call her malignant? She betrays familiar forms, admits unknown ones, and, as if deserting the cause of a client already accepted into her protection and taking up the cause of the adversary, she commits the highest treachery. The forest also slows down and disturbs our minds...

<summary>The author explains the Neoplatonic "Golden Chain of Being," positioning God at the top and "Sylva" (Prime Matter) at the bottom. He uses the metaphor of a craftsman and parents to describe how God (the Father) imposes Form on Matter (the Mother) to create the world.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Chain, Prime Matter, Neoplatonism, Hermes Trismegistus, World Soul, Form and Matter, Allegory of Helen, Divine Hierarchy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical interpretation of Virgil's *Aeneid*. The previous page discussed the hierarchy of the universe and the role of the World Soul. It concluded by discussing how human vices and folly cloud the soul. The text here picks up mid-word, completing "stultitia" (foolishness/stupidity).</meta>

## On the Allegory of Virgil

...[stupidit]y; indeed, every vice flows from it. For ignorance and lack of knowledge give birth to vices. Moreover, Plato and most of those who are considered the most serious philosophers are the authorities for the view that ignorance itself arises from the thickness and darkness of the body. 

Moved by this kind of reasoning, the divine Maro <note>A common medieval and Renaissance name for the poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro).</note>—since he who desires to be a leader in the state <term>res publica</term> is inflamed by a love for human affairs and for those things which are not free from the body, and because of this, they wander in various errors—introduces Venus in a mortal form. He places her in the forest itself; he does this because all the things we do are immersed in **matter** <term>sylva</term> <note>The author uses the Latin *sylva* (forest) to represent the Greek *hyle* (matter), a standard Neoplatonic metaphor where the "woods" represent the chaotic material world.</note>. Nor does he dress her in the habit of a huntress by chance: for he who intends to consult correctly for his fellow citizens proposes that those **wild beasts**—of which we spoke a little earlier—must be hunted down <note>The "wild beasts" are the passions and vices that a leader must subdue within himself and his people.</note>. 

Aeneas, however, recognizes something of divinity in her even though she is thus disguised. For even if the governors of nations are occupied with human actions, nevertheless, because they protect what is honorable and right with that same love by which we desire these fleeting things, we feel that our origin is divine. For when we love religion, when we love justice, and when we love greatness of soul, these things are certainly concerned with actions. But yet, who does not see that those things proceed from divinity? 

Nevertheless, the speech of Venus is not as a goddess, but as a human; and yet, it reflects a certain divinity. For when she persuades him to go to Carthage, she uses arguments derived from human prudence. For those things she draws from the history of Dido all bring hope of safety; and when she predicts something that will happen, she predicts it not as a goddess, but as an **augur** <note>A Roman religious official who observed natural signs, especially the flight of birds, to determine the will of the gods.</note> from the flight of swans. 

However, the poet devised this with the highest wisdom: that at the end of the speech, Venus reveals herself as a goddess. For when we have long cultivated those things which are right and honorable in civil life, we are raised up by their beauty to the divine things of which these are, as it were, images. 

Persuaded by these reasons from his mother, he heads toward Carthage, yet shrouded in darkness <note>In the *Aeneid*, Venus wraps Aeneas in a mist so he can enter the city unseen.</note>, so that no one might hinder his attempt. And indeed, this is how things are. For men endowed with great prudence lead the rest of the multitude—whom they have undertaken to govern—toward what is right and honorable in such a way that they very often conceal their plans. Indeed, if they made them public, they would be hindered either by the envy of rivals or the ignorance of fools, and they would hardly bring those plans to a conclusion. For just as prudent doctors often deceive the sick (whose desires seek nothing healthy), so the best princes hide their plans by pretending or disguising. 

For, to omit other things, did not those who brought forth laws attribute their plans to some god, so that there might be greater authority in them? Numa Pompilius <note>The legendary second king of Rome, who claimed his laws were dictated by the nymph Egeria.</note> pretended to do everything according to the commands of the nymph Egeria; Lycurgus <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta.</note> feigned that he had written the civil law of the Spartans according to the opinion of Apollo; whatever Zautraftes <note>Zoroaster.</note> established among the Arimaspians, he said he had received from a good deity. Moreover, Zamolxis <note>A legendary social and religious reformer among the Getae/Scythians.</note> referred whatever he handed down to the Scythians to Vesta. For how many and how difficult things did Quintus Sertorius <note>A Roman general who claimed a white doe brought him messages from the goddess Diana.</note> manage correctly amid military turmoils while he kept saying that he received them from Diana through a doe? 

But there are too many examples regarding a matter that is especially manifest. Truly, they look upon Carthage from a higher place; because, as was also discussed the day before yesterday, the republic will never be tempered by the best institutions and laws unless those who are in charge direct everything—whatever they either prescribe or prohibit—according to the rule and norm of those things which they have seen through the **contemplation** of great matters. When he describes the effort and industry of the Carthaginians in building the city, does he not collect in a very few verses everything which is done by those who...

<summary>This page interprets Aeneas's encounter with Venus as an allegory for the divine inspiration required for political leadership and the necessity of the "noble lie" or strategic secrecy in governance. It lists several historical lawgivers who claimed divine sanction to ensure the cooperation of the ignorant masses.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the state, shifting from the individual soul's vices (concluding "stupidit-y" from the previous page) to the structural requirements of a functioning commonwealth. The author uses Virgil's description of Carthage in Book 1 of the *Aeneid* as a blueprint for political theory.</meta>

### Book Three
k ii

...[stupidit]y; even those who have written most wisely concerning **the state** <term>res publica</term>: are expressed in nothing less than many books. For first, they prepare those things by which they may be safe against hostile attacks: <margin>Action</margin> <note>original: *Praxis*. In a philosophical context, this refers to the practical application of theories.</note> by which they defend themselves against the hardships of the weather with private buildings. For these two things provide for the possibility of a city existing. But after these, they turn themselves toward laws and magistrates: so that they may be able to exist not only in a basic sense, but in the way proper to a human being: to exist rightly and honorably. 

Furthermore, since public resources are primarily useful for magnificence, for generosity, and for repelling force: the method for creating excellent ports is considered. Finally, the care of the stage and the theater is not neglected: where bodies may be exercised for health and strength, and minds exhausted by public and private business may be relaxed by the most honorable games. 

Now, with what mind and with what plan he compared those people to **bees**: I pray you, observe quite diligently. For if you consider the nature of this insect, you will find nothing sharper than it in industry and skill, or more untiring in constant labor. They have, foremost, a leader whom they follow: whose command they never despise. They distribute labors among themselves with the greatest fairness; with the highest harmony, they both perform their own works and keep enemies away. Whatever is sought: it is all sought for the common good. Indeed, if you transfer all these qualities into any state, you will establish a **Platonic city** <note>A reference to Plato’s *Republic*, which describes an ideal state governed by justice and shared goals.</note>. 

Moreover, there was in the middle of the city a temple sacred to **Juno**: to show that nothing in the state ought to be more ancient <note>In this sense, "ancient" means "venerated" or "foundational."</note> than religion. And since the first prayers in civil life are that the empire might not only be preserved, but also increased: it was not without reason to consecrate the temple itself with every cult to Juno, who is considered the goddess of empires. I would be too long, and even more detailed than is fitting for such a great matter, if I were to relate more distinctly the individual things which were depicted in the temple: which were administered by the queen, and which were produced by the craftsmen. 

Many things are also contained in the speeches of **Ilioneus** and **Dido**: you may find even more in the meeting of Aeneas, and the most in the banquet and the joining of hospitality: by which the life and status of a citizen are expressed. Truly, because the most wise poet knew that the origins of states and empires rely on virtues—and that the saying of **Sallust** <margin>Sallust</margin> <note>Gaius Sallustius Crispus, a Roman historian. The quote refers to his idea that a state is maintained by the same virtues that created it.</note> is true: that if empires were retained by the same skills by which they were acquired, human affairs would not have so many changes—for that reason, he wishes the first meeting of the king and queen to proceed from religion, from generosity, and from every kind of virtue. 

But they slip little by little into the worse, so that she who had been a most modest woman, and most vigilant in administering the state: overcome by a shameful love, slips into idleness and lasciviousness. By all these things, it is shown how easily the minds of humans decline from labor into lust during prosperous times. However, since virtues in social life are commenced rather than perfected: and here civil life is treated in such a way that he wishes to express—as I was saying a little earlier—the **foundations of states** <term>res publica</term>, which grow from small things and have better beginnings than endings; for that reason, he placed the queen in the beginning as temperate in every matter. But a little later, as love rises, she slips little by little from **temperance** into **continence** <note>In Aristotelian ethics, "continence" is a state where one still has bad desires but resists them, whereas "temperance" is the state where one no longer desires the wrong things.</note>: finally, overcome by love, she is rendered **incontinent** in such a way that at last she falls into the greatest **intemperance**. 

Dido is moved from the beginning to love Aeneas, not only by virtue—which is the one thing we look upon in a life devoted to contemplation—but by those things which are considered not only good but the "highest goods" in human gatherings. For who would not count among the highest goods nobility of birth, dignity and excellence of form, and finally, a speech distinguished by much ornament? When in the forum, when in the sen[ate...]

<summary>This page interprets the building of Carthage in Virgil's Aeneid as an allegory for the creation of an ideal state, emphasizing that a republic requires defense, law, religion, and industry (likened to bees). It also begins a moral analysis of Dido's decline from a virtuous, temperate ruler to a victim of passion, illustrating how prosperity can lead to the erosion of civic virtue.</summary>

<keywords>political philosophy, Carthage, Dido, Aeneas, Juno, Sallust, temperance, continence, Plato's Republic, allegory, statecraft, virtue</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of Virgil’s Aeneid. While the previous page discussed the construction of a city and the virtues of the "bees" (the citizens), this page shifts to the moral downfall of Dido, interpreting her story as a warning about how bad counsel and earthly passions can derail even the most virtuous leaders and states.</meta>

# On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro

...are these things not weighed by the balance of the wise, but on the common scale? 

Since indeed in common life it is of great importance what advisors you use. For many, moved either by a bad example or driven by the improper persuasions of those they hold dear, are moved toward depravity. It was not unfitting, therefore, for the poet to show Dido made unchaste by the encouragement of her sister. For a woman miserably in love, now losing much of that strength of mind which she had shown until now, shows herself continent <note>Self-controlled, especially regarding sexual desire.</note> in the first words she speaks to her sister. For whoever is pressed by love resists it, though with difficulty; but the sister's speech draws everything from common life. For she does not use arguments taken from philosophy, but tries to lead her to her own opinion by proposing pleasure, or by injecting fear of things that should not be so greatly feared, or by offering a hope that is neither firm nor solid, so that at last she might give hope to a wavering mind and release her from her sense of shame. 

By this very thing it happened that, once conquered, she slid into incontinence. While she was occupied in this, she was gradually led by unchaste habit to the point where, with shame no longer standing in the way, she no longer thinks of a secret love; rather, having become most shameless, she calls her shameful lust by an honorable name <note>Referring to Dido calling her affair with Aeneas a "marriage."</note>. In all of this, what else does the poet hold or attempt more divinely than to set Dido before us as a most serious example of how much harm comes to those who are under authority when luxury and sloth creep into the minds of princes in place of industry and labor? 

For she who, a little while before, would not speak to outsiders and strangers except briefly and with a downcast gaze—whose supreme religion toward the gods, generosity toward guests, counsel in building the city, and justice toward her own people was being carried almost to the heavens; she who thought it a wicked crime to be seen in public except for the sake of divine or public business, and who, protected by her modesty, remained free from all disturbance—now she is driven by such madness that she wanders through the whole city like a lover. Or, if she stays at home without her lover, she wastes away in the greatest sorrow as if she were alone and deserted by everyone. She so neglects public works that the things which until now were being erected with the greatest speed through her care, her expense, and the labor and zeal of her citizens, now hang unfinished and interrupted. 

Aeneas, meanwhile, whose plan had set Italy before him, but who had turned aside to Carthage out of exhaustion from the difficulty of affairs—not to establish a home there, but to refit his fleet—is enticed by Dido’s charms and casts aside his purpose of setting out. Nor is Juno <margin>Action</margin> absent. To prevent the rise of Roman power, she sees to it that the marriage of Aeneas and Dido is performed at Carthage. But since this could not be accomplished without the work of Venus, and Venus desired her son not to linger in Carthage but to sail for Italy, Juno approaches this goddess with tricks, so that all the things she did for the sake of the Carthaginians might seem to be done for the benefit of Aeneas. 

When Maro <note>original: "Maro." A common name for the poet Virgil (Publius Virgilius Maro).</note> says these things, he depicts social life with almost divine wisdom. In this life, some people conduct themselves with such a high mind that, despising human things, they are soon to move from this first kind of virtues into those which they call **purifying** <term>purgatorias</term> <note>original: "purgatorias." In Neoplatonic philosophy, these are virtues that cleanse the soul of earthly passions to prepare it for divine contemplation.</note>, and from there they strive finally to reach those which belong to the purified mind; yet they are so softened by the charms of earthly things that they almost forget the heavenly things they had set as their only goal. For the **lust for ruling** <term>libido imperandi</term> joins Aeneas to Dido; that is, it desires to place an excellent man in charge of a kingdom. But it lacks the power to accomplish the task unless his love consents. Love, however, notices that a union of this kind does not look out for Aeneas, but for Dido; for it is not conducive to minds born for greater things, but to the unpious soul itself. For it is better for us to set out toward true wisdom than to be occupied in actions; but

<summary>This page analyzes Dido’s transition from a virtuous, industrious ruler to a neglected lover as an allegory for how "common" advice and earthly lusts can destroy both personal character and the stability of a state. It frames Aeneas's delay in Carthage as a conflict between the "lust for ruling" and the higher pursuit of wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Dido, Aeneas, Anna, Juno, Venus, purifying virtues, political philosophy, Carthage, allegory of Virgil, social life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Virgil's Aeneid, moving from Dido’s moral decline to the symbolic meaning of her "marriage" to Aeneas in the cave, interpreted here as the soul's entrapment by political ambition and earthly luxury.</meta>

# Book Three

If the administration of affairs is abandoned by the wise, human affairs are surely doomed. Therefore, although Aeneas knows that the things which the lust for ruling <term>libido regnandi: the desire or passion for power</term> persuades him of are false, he nevertheless assents to them—either because he is already ensnared by them, or because he is moved by pity for those he must counsel. 

Now, a marriage of this kind is celebrated during a hunt, regarding which I believe I explained my feelings to you clearly enough a little while ago. But as for their meeting in a cave, a subterranean place: what else should I believe this indicates, except that those who seek honors, wealth, and power carry a soul enclosed within bodily and fleeting things? You see that no divinity was present at this "wedding" except for Earth, Juno, and the nymphs who inhabit the groves. All these things seem to fit perfectly with what I was saying about the cave. 

Therefore, ensnared by his love for Dido, Aeneas casts aside his plan to depart; he is not ashamed to waste the winter, however long it may be, in the highest luxury. Truly, what else does this mean except that even distinguished men are sometimes turned away from the right course by ambition? Charmed by the pleasure of honors and command, they dread the harshness of winter and the difficulty of sailing to Italy <note>In this allegory, "Italy" represents the destination of the soul toward higher virtue or the "highest good."</note>.

Wherefore, unless help is provided by divine intervention, the most excellent and immortal virtues of men perish through such a persistent plague. They say that Circe possessed such a character and power that she transformed the companions of Ulysses <note>The Greek hero Odysseus</note> into various monsters. Yet we hear that Ulysses brought her so far under his own power that their original form was restored to his companions. I would not be surprised at this. For those who possess a high mind easily despise bodily lusts; indeed, by giving right counsel, they claim back those who are addicted to such things from great servitude into liberty. 

But very few mortals have been able to overcome Juno <note>In this commentary, Juno represents the goddess of earthly power and political ambition, often seen as an obstacle to the "heavenly" goal.</note>. For if someone is not touched by the desire for command, I would believe that he has already surpassed all humanity and come closest to divinity. This is why we see even those whom we hold in the highest admiration broken by this kind of desire: so much so that, having abandoned true virtue, they pursue the emblems of virtue like a shadow. For it is easy to despise the soft delights and luxury of a Sardanapalus or a Heliogabalus <note>Sardanapalus and Heliogabalus were ancient rulers synonymous with extreme decadence and physical indulgence.</note>, and even to hate them. But when we place before ourselves Alexander the Macedonian or Julius Caesar and their achievements—in which we see both of them often depart from what is true and right—we admire them for that desire for glory. Thus, we sometimes do not hesitate to approve that impious saying of Euripides <note>original: "Euripide" - likely referring to a quote from his play *The Phoenician Women*: "If one must do wrong, it is best to do it for the sake of power; in all other things, be God-fearing."</note>, which is entirely worthy of the king by whom it is uttered; we think it might benefit a man to violate the law for the sake of ruling. 

<margin>Hercules</margin> This one thing moved the poets to imagine that even Hercules—whom they say was wise and who, they wish us to believe, reached for heaven through his most famous deeds—tamed all monsters before he could overcome the cruelty of Juno. She is rightly called not the mother, but the most unjust stepmother of great men. For it is not the desire for mortal things (as most believe) but for heavenly things that produces those virtues by which one may reach the highest good. This, indeed, Hercules could not achieve unless Juno was first appeased—by which we mean, unless his ambition was settled. 

Who, then, would not pardon Aeneas for this? Or rather, who would not pity him? If he is not yet present in Italy (for that is the state of the highest virtues), but is still positioned in the very race to get there, he is so captured by the deceits of Juno that he prefers a marriage entered into with Dido over the fates granted to him. Having cast away the plan of departing, he sets out to found the citadels of Carthage and to renew the roofs; he rejoices to wear purple and a sword adorned with jewels—all of which are the emblems of empire.

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of Aeneas’s stay in Carthage, viewing his relationship with Dido and the "cave marriage" as a soul trapped by political ambition and material luxury. The author argues that while physical lust is easy for a wise man to overcome, the "Juno-like" urge for power and worldly glory can derail even the greatest heroes and historical figures.</summary>

<keywords>Aeneas, Dido, Juno, Hercules, ambition, political power, virtue, Carthage, Alexander the Great, Julius Caesar, allegory, Virgil</keywords>
