<meta>This page marks the transition between Book Three and Book Four of Landino’s Disputations. The previous book concluded the day's discussion on Aeneas's moral trials in Carthage. Here, the narrative frame of the dialogue closes for the evening, and Landino begins a new book addressed to Federico da Montefeltro, shifting the focus to a defense of his specific allegorical method.</meta>

->On the Allegories of P. Virgil Maro<-

"It is not, O LORENZO, it is not, I say, a matter of human weakness, but rather of some greater faculty—by which, however, our condition is sometimes strengthened for so arduous a task—to be able to reach such a high and lofty peak of things." While these things were being said by BAPTISTA <note>Battista Alberti (1404–1472), a famous Renaissance polymath and architect who appears as a character in these dialogues.</note>, and it seemed that our most pleasant host Mariotto was about to dispute in his turn about the remaining matters, Mariotto—gathering that whatever light remained would be consumed in that dispute, both because of the time of day spent on what had been said so far and the multitude of things that still remained to be said—intervened: "I will not allow, most pleasant men, that our comfort and health be compromised—matters which I have proposed to consult with all diligence as long as you are with me—by prolonging today’s disputation any further." "And yet," said LORENZO <note>Lorenzo de' Medici (1449–1492), the "Magnificent" ruler of Florence.</note>, "I was of the very same opinion; but I was hindered by a sense of shame, lest I should interrupt the speech of such a great man too bothersomely, especially since I did not doubt, O Mariotto, that you would play your part in your own time." "I shall indeed," said Mariotto; and at the same time, taking BAPTISTA by the hand with his usual cheerfulness, he led us back to the small rooms where the tables were prepared.

# THE FOURTH BOOK OF THE CAMALDOLESE DISPUTATIONS OF CRISTOFORO LANDINO OF FLORENCE TO THE ILLUSTRIOUS FEDERICO, PRINCE OF URBINO, ON THE ALLEGORIES OF P. VIRGIL MARO, BEGINS HAPPILY.

The previous book, Illustrious and Most Invincible Federico <note>Federico da Montefeltro (1422–1482), the Duke of Urbino and a great patron of arts and letters.</note>, had already reached the hands of certain men who, although they are learned, also wish to be considered and called the most learned. Although these men previously thought and spoke very differently about Maro's <note>Virgil's full name was Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> *Aeneid*, they have been convinced, I think, by those arguments which we set forth in the prologue of that book. Thus, they do not dare to deny that many things in that book were written through allegory. However, they contend that these matters are of such a kind that they should be referred not to ethics <term>ethicen: the branch of philosophy dealing with human morals and conduct</term>, as we have disputed in a long oration, but to physics <term>physicē: the study of the natural world and the laws of the universe</term>. 

To prove what they desire to defend, they bring forward writers who lived shortly before our age—men by no means unlettered—who, having interpreted some passages there both acutely and learnedly, contend that the nature of things <note>original: "naturam rerum"</note> is being explained. Against these men, we think the response should be that we do not deny that many things can be conveniently and truly transferred to the meaning they describe. For I remember that we also said in the conversation of the previous day that Virgil—because he had found several kinds of gods among the ancient theologians—deliberately wrote in such a way, and very often narrated certain things, so that those same things might be referred to life and morals, to the power of nature, and to the pleasure of the ears. 

Nevertheless, when we wish to know the purpose and mind of the writer <note>original: "consilium mentemque scriptoris"</note> by which the entire work was undertaken, I believe we must always put forward that goal for the sake of which all other things were undertaken—that which is written for its own sake and not for the sake of something else. For if we believe anything of the Platonists or the Aristotelians, there is surely no thing that can be more clearly discerned and distinguished from other things than by its very end <term>fine: the 'telos' or ultimate purpose of a thing</term>. Just as an archer directs all his missiles toward his target <note>original: "signum"</note>, so the writer, whatever he narrates, whatever he composes...

<summary>This page concludes a day of philosophical dialogue between Lorenzo de' Medici and Battista Alberti and introduces Book Four, where Landino defends his moral (ethical) interpretation of Virgil against critics who prefer a scientific (physical) reading.</summary>
<keywords>Cristoforo Landino, Virgil, Aeneid, Allegory, Ethics vs Physics, Battista Alberti, Lorenzo de' Medici, Federico da Montefeltro, Humanism, Aristotelians, Platonists</keywords>

<header>Book Four</header>

# Book Four

...derives or reports <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing the argument that Virgil’s poetry is a vehicle for deeper truths.</note>. This being the case, who would not see that the things he wrote concerning both the conduct of life and the acquisition of the <term>highest good</term> <note>original: "summum bonum"; the ultimate goal of human happiness and virtue in ancient philosophy.</note> were not written merely to express the force of nature? On the contrary, since he pursues with a continuous and perpetual discourse those matters in which he condemns vices, extols the beauty of virtue, and leads us toward the investigation of truth, he has added certain elements for the sake of ornament and delight which are drawn from <term>natural philosophy</term> <note>original: "physice"; the study of the physical world and natural sciences.</note> itself. Since he composed all these things not for their own sake, but for the sake of the goals I have mentioned, who does not see that this is the primary—or, if you prefer, the ultimate—purpose of the work he undertook? This is what we wove together in yesterday’s conversation with a continuous thread, so that you could find nothing interrupted in it. For everything is led back to that which was proposed from the beginning, and the subsequent parts cohere strongly with those that preceded. 

Therefore, I by no means disparage the things related to natural philosophy by those on whose authority they rely. For although those points are neither numerous, nor do they depend on one another in such a way as to seem a whole body rather than severed limbs, they are nevertheless not inconveniently translated, nor do they repugn our own opinion. But even if they did repugn it, right reason will prevail more with me than their authority—especially since, if we must contend by authority, we can bring forward those by whose brilliance these others are dazed, like owls in the sunlight. 

For I shall omit those whom Servius <note>Maurus Servius Honoratus, a famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil.</note>, the most diligent of all grammarians, commemorates in individual books on individual passages of this poet; I shall pass over what was said by Macrobius <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist whose "Saturnalia" treated Virgil as a source of all knowledge.</note>, an excellent philosopher among the Platonists; I shall say nothing of those things interpreted in this sense within the works of Virgil by Saint Jerome and Saint Augustine <note>Early Christian Church Fathers who often cited Virgil.</note>. Do we not have from among our own people Dante <note>Dante Alighieri (1265–1321), author of the "Divine Comedy."</note>, a man polished in every doctrine and a most weighty authority? In that journey where he traverses the entire world from the lowest depths of hell to the highest heaven, he fashions for himself that man <note>Virgil</note> as a guide. In seeking the <term>highest good of man</term>, he proposes the *Aeneid* as the sole work to be imitated with a wonderful genius. Although he may seem to excerpt only a few things from it, nevertheless, if we look more closely, he never departs from it. For is it not true that from the very beginning, the things he wrote concerning the midpoint of life’s journey, the dark wood, the three beasts, and the mountain peak already illuminated by the sun's rays—are all from this source? I leave aside the rest, which are so hidden in Dante’s poem that they can be grasped only by a few of the most learned. 

He therefore places Virgil before himself as a guide in that matter which looks toward the <term>highest good</term>, and not toward <term>natural philosophy</term>. But I see I have been too curious in a matter that could be refuted with very little effort. Therefore, I shall return to my purpose.

You, however, illustrious and most invincible Federico <note>Federico da Montefeltro (1422–1482), the Duke of Urbino and a celebrated Renaissance patron and soldier.</note>, will read most diligently both the preceding parts and these things contained in the final, fourth day of the disputation. For you will find many things there on account of which you will greatly rejoice to be the man you are now and have always been. You will recognize that your counsel came from God: you who, burning with the love of wisdom from your earliest years, so devoted yourself to the study of the liberal arts that the more your age matures day by day, the more ardently you apply yourself to them. For while other princes consider it especially kingly to waste away in much leisure and vain games, or spend all their time in fowling and hunting, you have judged such things unworthy even of a free man, except occasionally for the sake of relaxing the mind. Instead, you believed that he who is to command others must render his mind cultivated with every doctrine, whereby not only for himself, but also for those entrusted to his faith... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the catchword "cō" suggests the next word is "communitos" or "commissos" (those committed to his care).</note>

<summary>In this opening to Book Four, Landino defends his allegorical reading of Virgil by citing authorities like Dante, Augustine, and Macrobius, arguing the Aeneid is a guide to moral virtue rather than just natural science. He then addresses his patron, Federico da Montefeltro, praising the Duke's dedication to scholarly study over typical princely distractions like hunting.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Landino’s dedication of Book Four to Federico da Montefeltro, Duke of Urbino. Having established Virgil’s poetry as a philosophical work, Landino now discusses the qualities of a virtuous ruler and transitions into a historical comparison between Federico and the famous King Ptolemy Philadelphus of Egypt.</meta>

->On the Allegory of P. Virgilius Maro<-
<meta>Digitized by Google watermark in footer</meta>

...were sent: and while he acts rightly by example, and while he advises wisely by precept, he can be of the greatest possible benefit. Such a man would consider it the duty of a king not to be fed by the labor of others like a **drone** <note>original: "fucus"; a male bee that does not gather nectar, often used as a metaphor for a person who lives off the work of others.</note> among bees, but to labor for the safety of others. He takes care to prohibit the innocent from injuries and to suppress the insolence of the wicked, and strives to show himself fair to all. 

This, however, philosophy alone provides to us. For from philosophy we have it that we may live piously, that we may loathe all impiety, and abstain from every crime. Wherefore **Aristotle** used to truly claim that he had attained this from philosophy: that he did willingly and with pleasure those things which wicked men are compelled to do by fear of the laws. For to good men (as **Plato** says), the law is a god; but to the wicked, it is **lust** <note>original: "libido"; referring here to unrestrained desire or whim rather than just sexual passion.</note>. Therefore, studies of this kind have rendered you so cultivated, so polished in every part, that although you are surpassed by many in the boundaries of your empire (which is a **fault of fortune** <note>original: "crimen fortunae"; Landino is diplomatically suggesting that Federico's smaller territory is due to luck, not merit.</note>), you nevertheless exceed all men in those virtues without which no one has ever ruled rightly. 

But for the present, I omit all the other things by which you have rendered yourself an immortal leader from a mortal **clod of earth** <note>original: "humunculo"; literally "little man of earth."</note>. However, I cannot in any way pass over in silence your exceptional kindness toward the cultivators of the Muses and philosophy, since I observe that in this matter you can be preferred to all men. We know that **Ptolemy Philadelphus** <note>Ptolemy II, a Greek King of Egypt (3rd century BCE) famous for his patronage of the arts and the Library of Alexandria.</note> was held in such great admiration among the ancients that his fame is celebrated by the praises of the most distinguished writers as most flourishing even after so many centuries. And indeed, there was in that king a singular justice and a wonderful clemency. In military matters, neither courage nor fortune ever failed him. But nothing in all his actions is more extolled than the fact that he wished his kingdom to be a most liberal guest-house for all literate men. 

Furthermore, he thought he owed so much to those who had written something that he gave the task to **Demetrius of Phalerum** <note>A Greek orator and statesman who helped establish the Library of Alexandria.</note>—not only a most serious philosopher but also a most eloquent orator—to take care to collect at least five thousand books for his library. In which matter, we will best be able to guess what expenses he incurred when we see how much he labored on the **Mosaic law** <note>original: "mosayca lege"; the Torah or the first five books of the Hebrew Bible.</note> alone, so that it might be interpreted and converted into the Greek language by Hebrew interpreters. 

For first, he commanded that all the Jews who had been captured in previous wars and were serving in his kingdom should be diligently sought out, and that each should be redeemed for twenty drachmas and sent back unharmed to their fatherland. The number of these was so huge that six hundred and sixty **talents** <note>A "talent" was a massive unit of weight for gold or silver, representing a vast sum of money.</note> were paid out by the king. Then he sent envoys of the highest authority to **Eleazar**, the High Priest of the Jews: **Aristeas** (of whom I spoke a little earlier) and **Andreas**, his own prefect. He also sent a golden table, mixing bowls, and vials as gifts to be placed in the **Temple in Jerusalem**. Indeed, the material of these vessels consisted of fifty talents of gold and seventy talents of silver; moreover, he used about five thousand gems and precious stones with which the vessels were adorned and distinguished, all of which were of wonderful size. 

This liberality was so accepted and pleasing to Eleazar that he immediately sent seventy-two men to the king. These were not common people, but chosen from the most learned leaders in such a way that he took six from each of the twelve tribes, so that they might convert the law of God into the Greek language for Ptolemy. <note>This refers to the creation of the Septuagint, the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> To what purpose, then, is all this? Surely so that those who consider the matter more diligently may understand that your **Magnificence** toward learning in our time is no less than that of Ptolemy in the past. For this will appear clearer than the light of the sun if Empire is compared to Empire, and Expense to Expense.

<summary>Landino praises Federico da Montefeltro's philosophical virtue and compares his patronage of scholars to that of Ptolemy Philadelphus, specifically citing the King's role in commissioning the Septuagint translation.</summary>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues Landino’s dedication to Federico da Montefeltro, contrasting the Duke's genuine virtue with the wealth-driven patronage of the ancient King Ptolemy. It then transitions into the dialogue itself, marking the start of the fourth day of the Camaldolese Disputations, where Leon Battista Alberti begins an allegorical interpretation of Virgil’s Aeneid.</meta>

# Book Four

For he <note>Referring to Ptolemy II Philadelphus, the King of Egypt famous for founding the Library of Alexandria.</note> possessed not only the most opulent kingdom in all of Egypt—from which an incredible abundance of gold and silver flowed—but he also held the greatest part of Syria and Phoenicia as tributaries. Everyone, however, knows the extent of your own borders. Add to this that during the time Ptolemy reigned, he had many princes in Asia and Europe who honored poets, philosophers, orators, and historians with both respect and wealth. Thus, even if he acted out of his own natural passion for those studies, he nevertheless seemed to be spurred on in large part by a certain **rivalry** <note>original: "emulatione"; the idea that ancient rulers competed to be the most cultured.</note>, lest he be surpassed in this kind of glory by those whom he already defeated in wealth.

But your own kindness has fallen upon such times that, unless it were of the most burning intensity, it would easily be extinguished by the **avarice of other princes** <note>A common Humanist complaint that contemporary rulers were less generous toward the arts than those of antiquity.</note>. Therefore, in our age, no thanks at all are owed for those gifts which you bestow upon the Muses, for you find no one among those who rule today whose example might inspire you. But whatever you are—and you are indeed something entirely magnificent—is due entirely to your own genius and your innate humanity. For departing far from the character of others, you offer yourself as a **unique exemplar** for others to follow, both toward the highest liberality and all other right actions, and toward the investigation of truth. For you so love virtue that you embrace her not led by glory, but enticed by love for her. 

Yet it happens that, just as a shadow always follows the body even if the body does not seek it, so too a solid and distinct glory follows your pious, just, merciful, and brave deeds—not as some shadowy and empty thing. But the matter at hand demands that we now return to our hero <note>Aeneas, the protagonist of Virgil's epic.</note>, in whose actions you will easily recognize your own character and way of life.

We had gathered, therefore, in the same place very early on the **fourth day of this disputation**. And when I indicated by my face and gesture a wonderful desire to hear the speech of **Battista** <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous architect and polymath who serves as the main speaker in this part of the dialogue.</note>, he—so that he might more conveniently connect what he was about to explain with what had already been said—began his discourse in this manner:

"We have seen thus far, most learned men, with what prudence and greatness of soul Aeneas extricated himself from all those **impurities which proceed from the body**. For so that he would not perish at Troy and be utterly overwhelmed by bodily pleasures, he did not hesitate to be carried as an exile into the deep, 'uncertain where the fates might lead' <note>A quote from Virgil's Aeneid, Book III, line 7.</note>. After this, as soon as he recognized the Thracian robberies, he escaped them with wonderful speed. And soon, in doubtful matters, he took counsel from wisdom; yet he was deceived by the interpretation of **Anchises** <note>In Landino's allegory, Anchises often represents the soul's previous experiences or the ancestral traditions that can sometimes mislead the developing mind.</note>. For those things which belong to the body easily follow bodily things; he proposed for himself a **civil life in Crete** <note>Landino interprets Crete as a stage of human development focused on earthly governance before reaching spiritual maturity.</note>. 

But he did not regret, once his error was known, giving his sails to the winds for a third time. Carried to the Strophades, he fought unconquered against the **avarice of the Harpies**. Nor did he dread sailing through the midst of enemies to reach Helenus. I pass over the prudence and excellence of mind with which—having been made more learned by Helenus—he turned away from the savagery of the **Cyclops**; the industry and speed with which he avoided the monsters of Scylla and Charybdis; and the zeal and mental fire with which, his father having died in Sicily, he took up the voyage to Italy. 

Truly, when he could not be a match for the deceptions of Juno and the forces of Aeolus and the winds, he indeed yielded; and having deferred his plan for the investigation of truth to another time, he turned aside into Africa with the intent that he should head for Italy as soon as the storm of the sea—that is, of **appetite** <note>original: "appetitus"; Landino allegorizes the storm at sea as the turbulence of human passions and irrational desires.</note>—allowed. But having been established in the domain of a most hostile goddess, and soothed by the love of **Dido**—see what ambition can do, and how much power it has to overturn the minds of even the greatest men—soothed, I say, by the **desire for reigning**, he who had already overcome all other perturbations and vices..."

<summary>Landino praises Federico da Montefeltro's innate virtue before transitioning to the dialogue's fourth day, where Leon Battista Alberti begins an allegorical reading of Aeneas's journey as the soul's escape from vice.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from Landino's introductory praise of Duke Federico into the core of his allegorical analysis of Virgil's Aeneid. While the previous page discussed patronage and virtue, we are now deep into the interpretation of Aeneas's stay in Carthage, viewing it as a symbolic struggle between worldly duties and spiritual contemplation.</meta>

# On the Allegory of Virgil

<note>Landino interprets the Aeneid not just as a story of a hero, but as a map of the human soul's journey toward God.</note>

He abandoned the **most divine plan** of sailing to Italy and had given himself over entirely to the goal of stabilizing the kingdom of the Carthaginians. He would have persisted in this error had he not received word from **Mercury** <note>In Landino’s allegory, Mercury represents reason or the divine word that awakens the soul.</note> that it did not please Jove for him to build a beautiful city as a husband subservient to a wife. He was told to forget his kingdom and his own affairs. Indeed, the supreme God commands man to desire to return to his own origin. These commands are easily handed down to us by that **learning** <note>original: "doctrina"; refers here to philosophy and classical education.</note> which we receive from the speeches or books of the most highly lettered men. 

Rightly, therefore, is Aeneas rebuked because he undertook the administration of a "wife's city"—for that represents a life situated in **action** <note>original: "uita in actione"; the "active life" of politics and business, which Landino contrasts with the "contemplative life" of the philosopher.</note>. He had become forgetful of his own kingdom and of that total **contemplation** through which alone human minds truly reign. This is urged most strongly: that if the glory of such great things does not move him, he should at least look out for **Ascanius** <note>Aeneas's son.</note>, his heir and successor, to whom the kingdom of Italy and the Roman land are owed. In this context, what else should we understand by Ascanius but the **future and eternal life**, which succeeds this brief and momentary one? 

For if our souls, while they are occupied within these **frail little bodies** <note>original: "corpuſcula"; a diminutive used to emphasize the insignificance of the physical body compared to the soul.</note>, are soothed by such great allurements of earthly things that they desert the contemplation of heavenly things, they must remember this: if they migrate into the future life stained by the filth of vices and uncultivated by any learning, it will happen that they are never illustrated by any light of truth. Therefore, Ascanius shall reign, and there will be no end to his empire—unless he is defrauded of it by his father. For the future life is rightly said to be "born" from this life we live in, according to the reasoning I have shown; and by that same reasoning, if this life is filthy and wrapped in the darkness of vice, the soul is defrauded of such a great good. 

If, on the contrary, the soul remains happy and eternal, then:

->**The house of Aeneas shall rule over all shores,**<-
<note>original: "Hic domus æneæ totis dominabitur oris." Virgil, Aeneid 3.97.</note>
->**And the children of his children, and those who shall be born from them.**<-
<note>original: "Et nati natorum & qui naſcentur ab illo." Virgil, Aeneid 3.98.</note>

When Aeneas had received these commands, is it any wonder he was vehemently moved? For there was in him a mind that always looked up toward high things. Thus, finally rousing himself, he desires to depart as soon as possible and to leave those lands, however sweet they may be. For a man accustomed to power and command, once captured by sweetness, does not depart without difficulty. But when he is able to discern the **true good** from that which false opinion thinks is good, he prefers the former. 

Indeed, after long deliberation, he decides to depart without greeting Dido and without her knowledge. For he knew she would not allow him to leave if she knew. This excellently warns us that when we wish to withdraw the mind from things of this sort, we should not consult the **softer parts of the soul** <note>Landino views Dido as a symbol of the lower, passionate faculties of the soul.</note>, but must secretly set sail for "Italy" <note>Italy represents the goal of the soul: wisdom and divine contemplation.</note>. For these earthly things are such that the more flatteringly we address them, and the more familiarly we greet them, the more stubbornly they oppose us. 

The Queen, however, senses the trickery; she bears it with a bitter mind that the **civil life** <note>original: "uita ciuilis"; the life of a citizen and ruler.</note> is being deserted by an excellent man—especially if there is no other wise man to succeed him. Hence those complaints that "no offspring remained to her from King Aeneas." For this reason, the **lower reason** <note>term: "ratio inferior"; the part of the human intellect that manages worldly, practical affairs, as opposed to "ratio superior" which contemplates God.</note>—which we have said is called "woman"—strives with arguments of this sort to retain the excellent man in the civil life and turn him away from the purpose of speculation. First, she urges him by asking how he can endure to desert her, by whom he is so ardently loved. For the civil life vehemently loves an excellent man; for by his counsels, empires are not only obtained and preserved, but even increased. Nor does she hold back from reminding him that he is not keeping the faith he gave. For Aeneas had already given himself entirely to administration because of the **sweetness of ruling**. Indeed, she even informs him that she will die if she is deserted. Nor...

<summary>Landino interprets Aeneas’s departure from Carthage as the soul’s struggle to leave the "active life" of worldly politics (Dido) for the "contemplative life" of spiritual truth (Italy). He identifies Ascanius as a symbol for the eternal life that depends on the soul's moral choices in the present.</summary>

Having established that Mercury represents the divine reason calling Aeneas away from the "active life" of Carthage, Landino now analyzes Aeneas’s internal struggle and Dido’s desperate pleas. He contrasts the demands of human society with the soul’s higher calling toward contemplation.</meta>

# Book Four

->I ii<-

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark visible in bottom right. Partial text from a previous page visible on the left margin. Text contains various typographical abbreviations common in Renaissance printing (nasalization tildes, long s, ligatures).</meta>

...it usually happens otherwise: for the power of acting, if destitute of virtue, must necessarily perish. He is deterred by the difficulty of winter navigation. By this, the greatest labors are signified, which we must undergo if we wish to reach Italy <note>In this allegory, "Italy" represents the heights of divine contemplation or the soul's true home.</note>. Finally, she insists vehemently on this: if he were to return to Troy and to a life of pleasure, he still should not be allowed to abandon his honors—much less so when he seeks unknown places, for he did not yet know the **life of contemplation** <note>original: "speculandi uitam"; the philosopher’s life of study and prayer.</note>. At last, turned to pity, she pours out tears and brings to memory the marriage they had begun. She reproaches him with whatever sweetness he once received from her, and implores him not to desert her falling house. For even gentler natures can be vehemently moved when parents, children, and others joined by blood—as well as friends and country—beg that one not desert them, or leave a partnership once begun. This is especially true when it is feared that an empire, destitute of good men, might be invaded either by the greed of **Pygmalion** <note>Dido’s brother and the king of Tyre, who murdered her husband for gold.</note> or the tyranny of **Iarbas** <note>A local African king and suitor of Dido.</note>. We revere this duty even more when no other wise man is left to succeed us.

Indeed, when the requirement of conducting worldly affairs presents all these things to our minds, we cannot help but be vehemently moved. For that Platonic saying occurs to us, which most gravely warns us how much we owe to the human race: that we would seem to strip ourselves of our very humanity if we were to desert human society. But when the great man perceives that the **mind** alone is what makes us human, and that it is perfected not by **acting** but by **knowing**, and that this is the command of Jove <note>Representing the supreme divine will.</note>, he remains unmoved and, struggling, presses his cares deep beneath his heart. 

Yet he has a way to best maintain his purpose. For he does not deny that the queen has deserved well of him. Who, indeed, would not see that great aids to human weakness come from this **kind of life** <note>The "active life" of politics and civic duty.</note>? Furthermore, he promises that he will remember her "as long as breath rules these limbs." For we shall, in the end, call him the most perfect man who, while he lives, so engages in contemplation that he acts in turn when the situation demands it. Therefore, he does not flee from the **life of action** <note>original: "uita agendi"</note>, but rather recedes from it because he had not contracted a true marriage with it. For we are not born to be occupied with mortal things or to be wedded to them. Rather, we must apply ourselves to them out of necessity, bestowing only as much labor as is sufficient to preserve society. Therefore, if Dido delights in Carthage—that is, if the lower reason dwells willingly in action—let it be so; but let him also be allowed to delight in the higher "Italy." I could translate many parts of this same speech toward this same sentiment.

***

But let something be said from the perspective of mere history. The remaining things, which are told over many verses, have this force: they detest lustful and corrupt love. They warn us by the most grave example of so great a woman to flee such a shameful and pernicious plague. We might conveniently transfer to this place all those things said by **Pausanias** in Plato’s *Symposium* concerning base love. If you recall the few things we said when we spoke of **Paris** <note>Paris of Troy, often used as the archetype of the man destroyed by lust.</note>, you will understand that saying of **Propertius** to be most true:
> Nothing on earth lives more harshly than a lover.
<note>original: "Durius in terris nihil est quod uiuat amante."</note>

That Aeneas felt these cares in his "great heart" and yet his "mind remained unmoved" shows that the man who has decided to obey the commands of the gods has already returned from the **incontinence** <note>Lack of self-control or moral weakness.</note> into which he had fallen through Dido’s allurements, back to **continence**. And though he was pressed by love, he nevertheless placed honor before pleasure. Indeed, the death of Dido clearly shows us that those republics which are deserted by wise men must necessarily perish. Yet we shall not go astray if we think the fury and extreme desperation of lovers and madmen is shown by a slaughter of this kind. Aeneas, therefore, warned by the gods—

Landino uses Platonic philosophy to argue that while civic duty is important, the soul's ultimate perfection lies in the pursuit of divine truth.</summary>

# Allegories on P. Virgil Maro

...he sails out toward Italy. But as a wind rises, Palinurus <note>Aeneas's pilot, whom Landino interprets as the "appetitive" or impulsive part of the soul.</note>, the pilot of the ship, declares that Italy cannot be sought in such weather. Aeneas agrees to turn aside to Sicily, the island where his deceased father <note>Anchises, who died at the end of Book Three.</note> lay, not yet honored with the required funeral rites.

In this place, you will be able to understand without difficulty what Palinurus signifies from those things I explained about him a little further above. For since an appetite of this kind is easily overwhelmed by disturbances, it not only turns one away from the right course but very often draws Aeneas (and this represents the mind of an excellent man) into its own opinion—namely, to return to his father. We have already said that this weakness, which the soul contracts from the body, is the reason why one returns to a father who is already dead. If you recall those things we have already said concerning Sicily, it is not unreasonable for the Trojans to return to that island, from which there is the shortest passage to Italy <note>In this allegory, Italy represents the "homeland" of the soul or the state of divine contemplation.</note>.

However, the Poet <note>Virgil</note>, whose plan is not only to present the weightiest matters but also to sprinkle them with varied delights so that he might completely remove the boredom and sadness that accompany deep subjects, interjects various kinds of games. These things were thus managed by Aeneas so that, after all matters were finished, he would soon set sail for Italy.

But Juno <note>The goddess Juno represents the lower world of the senses and material fortune, which opposes the soul's ascent.</note>, whose hatred toward the Trojans neither calamity nor the length of time could satisfy—because the closer she saw them to Italy, the more she was inflamed—did not miss the opportunity offered. For when the weak race of women, separated from the men, was sadly contemplating their desire to finally rest, she persuaded them through Iris to set fire to the ships.

You should understand these things in this way: the desire for earthly things does not persuade the "men"—for the higher part of reason is not easily broken by these things—but it persuades the "lower reason" itself when it is separated from the "higher." It does so so that, having rejected the purpose of great things, it may flee the boredom of a long voyage and settle down where it currently is. Therefore, the women—to whom inglorious leisure is far dearer than honorable labor—at first hesitated miserably between their love for the present land and the kingdoms calling them by fate, and they looked upon the sea with spiteful eyes. 

For when the lower reason, which follows the higher reason toward every excellence when persuaded by it, is now gradually weakened by the allurements of the senses in its absence, it finally yields, conquered, to their power. Thus, the women set about consuming the ships with fire. When the poet says this, he shows that the will, which was being carried toward great things, perishes by the fire of desires. 

The entire fleet would have perished had not Eumelus immediately reported the danger to Aeneas. For Eumelus was the man left to guard the women. Indeed, for this lower part of the mind, a certain very sharp remorse of conscience—which always cares for what is good and right—is always present. This is called <term>synderesis</term> <note>original: "synderēsis"; a term in scholastic philosophy for the innate spark of conscience that provides the mind with basic moral principles.</note> in Greek; it is an inborn power by which the soul is both raised toward honorable things and flees from base ones. The name of the man himself clearly demonstrates this:

<margin>for he will be easily interpreted as "care for the good."</margin>

for he will be easily interpreted as "care for the good" <note>original: "boni cura"; Landino derives the name Eumelus from the Greek 'eu' (good) and 'meletē' (care/attention).</note>. He, therefore, seeing the "woman" <note>the lower reason</note> already fallen into crime, reports the matter to the "man" <note>the higher reason</note>. Upon hearing this news, Ascanius is the first to fly to rescue the ships. 

Ascanius, the son of Aeneas, is swift, robust, and endowed with great spirit; he may rightly be interpreted as a certain vigor born from the mind itself. He is not held back by any terror from being the first to face the dangers. The others follow, but Aeneas is first. But the women, once the men are recognized, repent of their undertaking. For a woman <note>the lower reason</note> separated from a man <note>the higher reason</note> hardly resists appetite. But if she is joined to the man again, she becomes stronger, and as if rescued from darkness, she finally sees what has been created; she grieves and is ashamed at having been deluded by Juno. However, the fire is not easily extinguished. For the appetite, captured by Juno, persists in its goal to destroy the will, which leads us to the highest...

He introduces Eumelus as a personification of "synderesis" or conscience, who alerts the mind to moral danger.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Cristoforo Landino’s Neoplatonic analysis of the Aeneid. While the header reads "Book Four," this refers to the fourth book of Landino’s own commentary, which provides an allegorical reading of Virgil's Book Five. # Book Four

...the [supreme] good should be carried out, lest he lose it entirely. Otherwise, there would be a wretched distraction in man: while reason holds one way, appetite drags him another. In this situation, when our mind recognizes itself as unequal to such a great struggle, it strives indeed with its own strength, but at the same time it also implores divine help, which it deserves to obtain. For he who prays to God in such a way that, in the meantime, he does not fail himself as far as he is able, is by no means abandoned. 

For what is written by Sallust <note>Gaius Sallustius Crispus, a Roman historian; Landino is referencing a sentiment from the "Speech of Cato" in the Bellum Catilinae.</note>—that the help of the gods is not obtained by prayers or womanly entreaties <note>original: "nec precibus nec suppliciis muliebribus auxilia deorum parari"</note>—was rightly said. For God does not help the idle or the lazy, nor the one who, having fallen into the depth of despair, prepares nothing against dangers. But he who rises with a great spirit against difficulties—who leaves nothing undared and nothing unattempted, who is neither terrified by dangers nor numbed by labors—he surely makes himself worthy of the pity of both gods and men. 

Therefore, Aeneas wisely turns to prayers when neither the strength of heroes nor the force of poured water was of use. Having obtained help, though four ships had already been consumed <note>In Book Five of the Aeneid, the Trojan women, driven to a frenzy by Iris, set fire to the ships to stop the endless wandering.</note>, the rest are saved from the fire. Moreover, since there were not enough ships to carry the entire crowd, there was the advice of the aged sailor <note>Nantes, an old and wise companion of Aeneas.</note> that the weaker crowd should be left behind in Sicily, and a city should be founded for them to inhabit. This counsel was made firm and certain from its previous uncertainty by a paternal oracle—for his father <note>Anchises, appearing as a ghost.</note> spoke by the command of Jove. 

This passage, unless you think otherwise, we shall interpret thus: Only the mind, now supported by the full strength of the virtues, approaches the contemplation of divine things. For the remaining powers of the soul, which are the "weaker ships"—for those represent the **will** by which we are carried—were lost in the fire. Therefore, the mind must be called away from the senses. This counsel originates from that man to whom Pallas <note>Athena/Minerva, goddess of wisdom.</note> was a teacher, for he is instructed by wisdom. 

It is approved, however, by Anchises, but one who is already buried. For the senses that have now been subdued by reason easily yield to its authority, especially when Jove so commands. They are converted into reason in this order: reason itself, ascending to a higher place, is affected by the **intellect**. That intellect, in turn, emerging into a higher grade, becomes **intelligence**. But intelligence is transformed into God. 

Thus Aeneas uses counsel and an oracle of this kind. Yet he does not set sail from Sicily before he performs the sacred rites piously and according to custom; for he knew how laborious and how full of dangers a voyage of this kind is. He knew "what a great task it was to found the Roman race." <note>original: "tantae molis erat Romanam condere gentem" — one of Virgil’s most famous lines, here used to represent the difficulty of the soul's perfection.</note> Meanwhile, **Venus** relaxes nothing, but looks about at everything, being vehemently anxious for her son’s safety. Above all, she asks **Neptune** to make the sea calm. 

For the love by which we are carried toward the supreme good encourages the highest reason in man to keep the appetite within its own power. Neptune promises everything most kindly; for the mind itself denies nothing to the love that rouses it toward what is right. Nor is it far from reason <note>Meaning, it is logically consistent.</note> that he shows Venus to have been born from his kingdom. For the sea is Neptune's kingdom; and as long as it is ruled by him, it is calm. Into this sea, the virile parts of Heaven <note>original: "caeli uirilia" — referring to the myth of Uranus (Caelus) being castrated, with his genitals falling into the sea to create Venus.</note> were thrown, and while they were tossed about, they produced the foam from which Venus was born. 

Therefore, we say that the higher reason, containing the appetite within itself, is the place where the "parts of heaven" fall, because a certain power falls from heaven into the appetite governed by reason; this power, stirred within it, procreates the love of divine things. Moreover, as for his promise that all except one, **Palinurus**, would reach Italy safely—is this not drawn from the very heart (as the Greeks say) of philosophy? For as the fleet heads toward Italy...

<summary>Landino interprets the burning of the Trojan ships and the subsequent departure from Sicily as a metaphor for the soul discarding its weaker, sensory-driven "will" to pursue divine contemplation. He outlines a Neoplatonic hierarchy where the mind ascends from reason through intellect and intelligence to eventually reach God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Cristoforo Landino’s Neoplatonic interpretation of the Aeneid. On the previous page, Landino discussed Aeneas's prayer for divine aid. Now, the commentary moves from the end of Virgil’s Book Five to the beginning of Book Six, focusing on the death of the pilot Palinurus and the arrival in Italy. In Landino’s allegory, Italy represents the "Highest Good" or the state of divine contemplation.</meta>

### On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro

The base appetite <note>The Latin *appetitus* refers to physical desires or instincts driven by the senses rather than reason.</note> is the greatest adversary; because it arises from the senses alone, it looks toward nothing lofty. For this reason, as long as [the pilot Palinurus] was in charge of the fleet, the Trojans were never able to reach Italy. But at last, overcome by sleep, he is extinguished in the sea. For once reason turns most sharply toward contemplation and leaves behind the care for fleeting, perishable things, nothing from among those things that can charm the senses is desired. Whence that entire base appetite gradually loses its savor, and then, lulled to sleep, it is extinguished. 

The fleet, however, even without its pilot, is carried safely by the promises of Neptune until it reaches the rocks of the Sirens <note>In the Aeneid, the Sirens represent the temptation of worldly pleasures.</note>. When it began to drift there, Aeneas, taking the helm, steered the ship through the nocturnal waves. For our soul, once it has set Italy as its goal, is carried along securely until it happens upon the rocks of pleasures. Then, it is necessary for the rational appetite—which stands against opposing pleasures—to take the helm. 

Therefore, with Palinurus extinguished, Aeneas finally arrives at the Euboean shores <note>The coast of Cumae in Italy.</note> after his long wanderings. For they have come into Italy, a place Aeneas would never have reached with Palinurus as his pilot. Now they have entered the harbor. In this harbor, the same thing does not happen as happened in Carthage, as the poet shows. There, even if the ships seemed to be in calm water, far from the fury of the waves, they were nonetheless not secured by any anchor. For that reason, although they were not entirely tossed about, they were still in some motion. Here, however, "the anchor held the ships firm" <note>original: *anchora fundabat naues*; quoting Aeneid 6.3</note>, which shows that they must remain stable, resting as if on a foundation. 

For that highest good, which is placed by philosophers in the busy and civic life <note>The "active life" of politics and social duty.</note>—even if it is placed in a sort of retreat so that it is far from the storms of fortune by the benefit of the virtues—is nonetheless not so firmly established that it cannot be shaken. But he who has proposed the contemplation of divine things <unclear>divinarum</unclear> to himself as his ultimate end has placed his reasoning in such a safe and solid place that no force can move it. 

For the arrival in Italy shows that a "habit of the virtues" has now been formed, so that Aeneas is not intended to depart from the life he has proposed—not yet the virtues of a soul already purified (for nothing would then be difficult for him), but those virtues which they call "purgative" <note>In Neoplatonism, "purgative virtues" are the steps taken to cleanse the soul of earthly attachments before reaching a state of pure contemplation.</note>. This established purpose is not abandoned by fortitude or strength of mind, nor by the ardor for the task at hand. 

The poet expresses this when he says: "A band of youths flashes burning onto the Hesperian shore" <note>original: *iuuenum manus emicat ardens Littus in hesperium*; Aeneid 6.5</note>. The "hand" or "band" indicates that all the powers of the soul are running together; having now removed Palinurus from their midst, they have voluntarily subjected themselves to the mind. The fact that the gathering is "burning" indicates its intensity. Nor is it without reason that it is a "band of youths." For it shows that the powers of a well-ordered soul are never affected by any old age, in which weariness, lethargy, and laziness are customary. Therefore, he does not move with a slow step, but "flashes" forth. 

Truly, because the soul is an exile while in the body—even if it gives itself entirely to speculation—it cannot help but care for necessities. The poet enumerates those things which look not toward the pleasure of the senses, but toward the preservation of life. For "one part seeks the seeds of flame hidden in veins of flint, part ravages the dense woods, the coverts of wild beasts, and points out discovered rivers" <note>original: *quærit pars semina flāmis Obstrusa inuenis filicū pars dēsa ferarū Tecta rapit siluas inuētāq; flumina mōstrat*; quoting Aeneid 6.6-8</note>. 

Therefore, the lower powers of the soul perform these tasks. But Aeneas, by whom our mind is represented, seeks "the peaks over which high Apollo presides, and the vast cave, the fearful retreat of the Sibyl" <note>original: *Arces quibus altus Apollo præsidet: Horrēdaq; procul secreta sybillæ: Antrū immane petit*; Aeneid 6.9-11</note>. When he does this, he raises himself up to contemplate divine things. For those who write using the "veils of figures" <note>Landino refers to allegorical or symbolic writing.</note> express these types of speculations through a high place. Whence that line from the Psalm: "Who shall ascend the mountain of the Lord?" <note>original: *Quis ascēdet in mōtē dñi?*; Psalm 24:3</note>. Add also that: <note>The text breaks off here.</note>

<summary>Landino explains the arrival in Italy as the soul's transition from the active life to the contemplative life. The death of the pilot Palinurus symbolizes the silencing of physical desires, allowing the rational mind (Aeneas) to lead the soul toward divine truth.</summary>
<keywords>Aeneid allegory, Neoplatonism, Palinurus, contemplation, purgative virtues, Apollo, Sibyl, Cristoforo Landino, rational appetite, Hesperian shore</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from Book 5 to Book 6 of the Aeneid. Having reached the shores of Italy (symbolizing the state of divine contemplation), Aeneas now approaches the temple of Apollo and the cave of the Sibyl. Landino begins to explain the allegorical meaning of these locations, drawing parallels between Virgil’s imagery and the works of Dante Alighieri.</meta>

# Book Four
->A<-

Since the goal of the virtues placed in contemplation is the Truth, by which we are made wise, he [Virgil] rightly said that the sun was set before this speculation <term>speculation: from the Latin *speculatio*, meaning deep theoretical study or contemplation</term>. For just as night and darkness represent ignorance, so the sun, the giver of light, signifies Truth. Following this example, our fellow citizen Dante, when he describes the progress from ignorance of things to the knowledge of truth, testifies that having emerged from the night and the forest, he ascends the mountain whose peaks are illuminated by the sun. 

He adds, furthermore, that the cave of the Sibyl is there, into whom the Delian prophet <note>Apollo, so called because he was born on the island of Delos.</note> inspires a great mind and soul and reveals things to come. To express the nature of this place more clearly, I will first touch upon a few things regarding the Sibyl, and soon I shall return to the matter at hand. It is well established that among the Greeks, those women were called Sibyls who, inspired by divine frenzy, predicted future events. The word "Sibyl" is as it were <gloss>divine counsel</gloss> <note>original: *sioboule*</note>; for that means the judgment of God, because the counsel of God is known to her. For the Aeolians <note>A group of ancient Greeks with a specific dialect.</note> say <gloss>god</gloss> <note>original: *sios*</note> for the one whom the rest of the Greeks call <gloss>god</gloss> <note>original: *theos*</note>. 

However, there are those who maintain that a prophetic woman at Delphi was first called by this name, and from her, others aware of future events were subsequently named. Marcus Varro <note>A celebrated Roman scholar and writer (116–27 BC).</note> gathered that there were ten such women from various regions. If I thought it pertained to our subject, I would not be burdened to follow them all in their proper order; but as you see, it has nothing to do with what we are currently discussing. Therefore, it shall be enough to see that the Sibyl easily interprets the doctrine of divine things. She predicts for us those things known to Apollo without falsehood, for she opens the way to wisdom and truth. 

The fact that he places her in a cave expresses that truth lies hidden in the dark. Nor does he haphazardly join the groves of Trivia <note>An epithet for Diana or the Moon, referring to her role as the goddess of the "three ways" or crossroads.</note> to the temple of Apollo. For the moon is exceedingly corporeal and lower than the other stars. For this reason, it rightly serves as a figure for human affairs, which are far inferior to divine ones. The sun is set over light, but human affairs are buried in a forest <term>forest: Landino uses the Latin *sylva* (forest) to represent the Greek *hyle*, a philosophical term for the dense, chaotic nature of physical matter</term>; for they are not without bodies. And just as the moon receives light from the sun, so do human affairs receive whatever they possess from divine things. 

Understand, therefore, that since wisdom is the knowledge not only of divine things but also of human ones, the grove of Diana is rightly joined to the temple of Apollo. The temple is the place of divine things; the forest denotes matter. We know the temple is a building sacred to God in which we perform only divine acts and abstain from all else; for when we enter it, we cease from business and devote ourselves to contemplation alone. 

He [Virgil] posits that the temple was built by Daedalus. What else is building a temple to Apollo than making oneself fit to receive wisdom? This we finally do only when we transfer a soul, purified from every bodily stain, to the contemplation of divine things. This can be achieved by Daedalus, a man instructed in all the finest arts, in whom there was such genius that poets call Circe "daedala" and the earth "daedala" <gloss>ingenious or skillfully made</gloss> precisely when they wish to show the greatest genius. 

Furthermore, he came neither by sea nor by land, nor to the south (the lowest part of the world to us), but through the high air toward the north. For let nothing lowly, nothing earthly remain in that mind which is carried toward speculation; rather, let it be raised to sublime and celestial heights. The first entrance into speculation, however, begins with turning away from vices. For the first knowledge must concern the nature of evil, so that we may be able to abstain from it. For unless we have been expiated from our vices, we shall never touch divine things. To repeat what I have often said, David <note>Refers to King David in the Psalms (e.g., Psalm 24).</note> denies that anyone can ascend the mountain of the Lord unless...

<summary>Landino provides a Neoplatonic interpretation of Aeneas reaching the Sibyl's cave, equating the Sun with Truth and the Moon with human/material affairs. He explains that the temple built by Daedalus represents the human mind being purified and prepared for divine wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Apollo, Diana, Sibyl, Daedalus, Dante, allegory, contemplation, Truth, matter, virtue, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Landino’s allegorical analysis of Book 6 of the Aeneid. Having established that the sun represents Truth and the Sibyl represents divine counsel, Landino now explores the requirements for entering the temple of wisdom and the symbolic meaning of the artistic depictions on its doors.</meta>

### On the Allegories of P. Virgilius Maro

...him who is **innocent of hands and pure of heart** <note>This is a direct reference to Psalm 24, suggesting that only the virtuous can approach the divine.</note>. For this reason, at the doors through which there was an entrance into the temple, he [Virgil] places the murder of Androgeus, the adultery of Pasiphae, and the pride of Icarus <note>These figures from Greek mythology represent various sins: violence, lust, and intellectual pride.</note>. Aeneas gazes upon these things from the beginning. However, one must not linger too long in the recognition of vices. For it is necessary to know them instantly—and to depart from them just as quickly.

Rightly, therefore, is Aeneas warned by the Sibyl—whom Achates, having been sent ahead, had already summoned—lest he waste more time on such a spectacle. For even the most excellent men, enticed by the various lures of pleasure, would fall, unless through their care and study they had attained that **doctrine** <term>doctrine: from the Latin *doctrina*, referring to the systematic philosophical or moral education that guides the soul</term> by which we are warned to consider that the small amount of life and time granted to the human lot should be spent on nothing but great and noble things.

This, moreover, is the difference between an excellent man and a fool. For the one, if he should fall once, does not easily rise; the other, even if his soul—weighed down by the body—should occasionally turn a little from the path, the Sibyl is immediately present, summoned by Achates, to lead him back to the right way.

But truly wonderful is the poet’s genius, who teaches us wisdom itself through his own wisdom. The first teaching, therefore, is that we should enter the temple purified and clean. Next, he shows that although our mind is able to know certain things by its own power implanted in it by the supreme God, nevertheless the knowledge of divine things is of such a kind that we cannot conceive of it unless we are **illustrated by divine light from heaven**. Since this is the case, who does not see that this matter must be sought from God through prayers and sacrifices?

He chose seven victims <note>In the poem, Aeneas sacrifices seven young bulls and seven ewes.</note>, because many philosophers have considered the number **seven** to be the most perfect; for that reason it is attributed to wisdom, and is called both <gloss>virgin</gloss> and <gloss>Pallas</gloss> <note>original: *uirgo ac pallas*. In Pythagorean thought, the number seven was called "the virgin" because it is the only number in the first ten that is not a product of others and produces no others. Pallas Athena is the virgin goddess of wisdom.</note>. He sacrifices seven, therefore, because he desires wisdom.

Nor was it said without purpose that there are **a hundred wide entrances** and **a hundred victims**; for by the "entrances" he expresses the manifold and various doctrine by which we are led to wisdom. The "victims," indeed, which oppose those who enter, show no small difficulty in the matter. These entrances will not open before we have obtained it by prayers poured out from the bottom of the heart. For wisdom is acquired with the highest ardor of the spirit and a mind deeply devoted to it.

Moreover, so that we may attain wisdom, he promises that he will build a temple to Phoebus <note>Apollo, representing divine light.</note> and Diana <note>The moon, representing human knowledge or reflected truth.</note>. I have spoken about the temple a little above; now I shall briefly explain why he promises to build it of **solid marble**. Marble is a hard thing, and a marvelous whiteness and splendor appear in it. From this, it took its name <margin>The Greeks say the name comes from "to shine." <note>original: *marmairein*, a Greek verb meaning to glisten.</note></margin> because all these qualities must be present in the mind that is raised toward speculation. It shall be **solid**, so that just as a cliff resists the insane waves by its own hardness—remaining whole while the waves are dashed against it and fall away broken—so the mind may not be broken by any **disturbances** <term>disturbances: known as *perturbationes* in Latin, these are the emotional storms of the soul like fear, desire, and grief</term>, but should break them instead.

Furthermore, we say something is of "solid marble" when it is not merely decorated on the outside with marble veneers, but consists entirely of marble. For this reason, the mind must be of such a kind that it does not merely display certain signs by which it might seem to desire wisdom, but, burning entirely, it should always lean upon it. Likewise, it will be shining with the highest **purity** <note>original: *candore* (whiteness/brightness)</note>, so that it is polluted by no bodily stain. For in what way could that mind lack splendor when it is never to perceive wisdom unless it is first illustrated by many teachings? He places the temple to Phoebus and Diana because (as I was just saying) wisdom is the knowledge of both divine and human things. He establishes festival days for Apollo alone, because true worship is owed to the divine sun. He also promises a sanctuary for the Sibyl, in which her lots and secrets may be stored; for unless in a deep mind...

<summary>Landino explains that Aeneas must look past images of vice and use "Achates" (diligent care) to find the "Sibyl" (wisdom). He interprets the temple's solid marble and the number seven as symbols of the purity and perfection required for divine contemplation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Landino’s allegorical commentary on the Aeneid, specifically Book 6. Having discussed the symbolic warnings at the temple entrance, Landino now moves into the necessity of mental discipline and the transition from the "active life" to the "contemplative life" as Aeneas reaches Italy.</meta>

# Book Four

If the things we acquire through learning do not remain stored within us, we will labor like the daughters of Danaus <note>In Greek mythology, the Danaids were cursed in the underworld to eternally carry water in sieves or perforated jars; Landino uses this as a metaphor for a mind that cannot retain knowledge.</note>: nor will we ever be able to fill a perforated vessel. For this reason, chosen men <note>original: "uiri lecti," referring to those with refined intellects.</note> are to be preferred over the merely strong. For the excellences of the mind must be applied, by which the things we have learned may be best preserved. 

We must take care, first of all, that the responses given to us are not "written on leaves," but are pronounced by the mouth. For the things we learn are not to be entrusted to little books and commentaries, but to the mind. Nor, again, should we seek learning in trivial and foolish matters, like those who waste nearly all their time on the superfluous quibbles of logicians, vain ambiguities, or empty fables. 

It was truly said that at the beginning, the Sibyl was "not yet enduring Apollo." <note>A reference to Aeneid 6.77–80, where the Sibyl struggles against the god's inspiration. Landino interprets Apollo as "Truth."</note> <note>The following phrase is repeated in the original text, likely a typesetting error.</note> For our little minds are not able to grasp those things which are most difficult to know until Apollo (for he is Truth) settles us; for once we are instructed, all those things are made easy. But hear what the Sibyl says:

> O you who have at last finished with the great dangers of the sea:
> But more grievous things remain on land.

Nothing is weightier, nothing truer. For he who, having left behind the civic life, arrives at that life which is placed in the contemplation of things, has left the sea and retreated to the mainland. For the life that is occupied with actions is very much like the fluctuating sea. We see, indeed, that everything done in that life is exposed to the storms of fortune. 

Contemplation, however, since it turns toward those things which always remain the same and do not fall into decay, stands on solid ground. Therefore, Aeneas was tossed by the great dangers of the sea before he could avoid the various and horrendous monsters of the deep through his long wanderings. It was difficult to escape the Trojan fire unharmed; it was laborious to depart from Thrace, deterred by its cruelty and greed; it was troublesome to fall into the Sicilian <note>original: "trinacrenſem," referring to Trinacria, an ancient name for Sicily.</note> plague, deceived by the ambiguity of an oracle. 

Who would not loathe the foul filth of the Harpies? Even if he does not fear the journey to Helenus through the midst of enemies. Who is not struck with terror by the monstrosity of the Cyclopes? To traverse the Sicilian waters so cautiously that one sees neither Scylla nor Charybdis, and to resist the storm stirred up by Juno and Aeolus so as not to suffer shipwreck—this is the work not of a mere man, but of a hero. 

I pass over what he suffered among his comrades on the African shore, what deceits Juno prepared for him, and with what bond of love Dido bound him. I pass over the losses he accepted from the burning of the ships in Sicily. When he has endured all these things—heavy and full of danger as they were—how could he be about to suffer "harder things" in Italy? 

Yet the Sibyl was not far from the truth. For when you have claimed yourself for solitude, away from the common life and the company of men, then the memory of the things you left behind applies certain sharper torches, as it were; and the most piercing bites of desire arise from the longing for them. And just when we think we have consigned those things to oblivion, then the cares of them redouble most intensely, and "raging love rises up again." Thus, unless he secures his ships with the firmest anchor, he will seem ready to sail back to Africa <note>In Landino's allegory, returning to "Africa" or "Dido" represents a soul falling back into earthly passions and the active life after attempting to reach the contemplative state.</note>. 

For even if the resolve not to depart from there is firm, the struggle does not cease when the allurements of the life once led suggest one thing, and the present counsel suggests another. Aeneas comes to Italy, but he does so through that class of virtues which are called <term>purgatory virtues</term> <note>These are the "purgative" virtues in Neoplatonic philosophy, which cleanse the soul of bodily passions to prepare it for divine contemplation.</note>. Before the mind is thoroughly purified by these, it is necessary that it wage a most bitter war—just as our people say—of the "spirit against the flesh." For the more these things are above human weakness, the greater the danger with which we approach them. For...

m
<meta>catchword: For (Hu...)</meta>

<summary>Landino explains that the journey from the "sea" (the active life of passion and politics) to the "land" (the contemplative life of wisdom) is difficult because the soul is haunted by its past desires. He introduces the Neoplatonic concept of "purgatory virtues" as the means by which Aeneas—and the human soul—must wage an internal war to reach spiritual purity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of Virgil’s Aeneid. Having just discussed the Sibyl’s cave and the entry into the underworld, the text now explores the transition into the "contemplative life" and the psychological challenges of leaving common society for higher truth.</meta>

### Allegories on P. Virgil Maro

...human society. For when we leave it, we either degenerate into a beastly life through madness and **black bile** <note>original: "atram bilem," referring to the ancient medical theory of melancholy which, in excess, was thought to cause insanity or isolation.</note>, or we are raised above humanity with heroic strength. For this reason, someone was once asked why he was wandering alone on the shore; when he spoke, he replied, "I am speaking with myself." To which the other said, "See to it, then, that you are speaking with a good man." And rightly so: for one does not easily find a **Scipio** <note>Scipio Africanus the Elder (236–183 BCE), a Roman general famous for his self-discipline, of whom it was said he was "never less alone than when alone."</note> who was "never less alone than when he was alone."

It is because of these kinds of difficulties that the **Sibyl** says that when the **Dardanids** <note>A name for the Trojans, descendants of Dardanus.</note> (for they are excellent men) come to Italy, they will wish they had not come. For they will find another **Achilles** in Latium; they will also find **Juno**, by whom they must be harassed in no small measure. 

For **Ambition**—which is expressed in Juno just as it is in a warlike man—just as she opposed Troy and **Pleasure**, so too will she oppose **Contemplation** <note>original: "speculationi," referring to the highest form of philosophical study or the "contemplative life."</note>, for she cannot bear to see it preferred over herself. Now, **Achilles** is born of a goddess because a certain divine nobility is implanted in our minds, which wishes to obey no one, but to command everyone. If this quality is cultivated by right reason, it brings forth true **Fortitude**; but if it is carried away against reason, attempting to turn everything toward its own lust, it creates **Ambition** and a desire for dominion. Therefore, even if it degenerates greatly from the goddess, it nevertheless takes its origin from that divine force of the mind.

The Sibyl does not only predict what is to come, but she also brings forth the cause of such great evils. For she says that just as the marriage to a **foreign woman** <note>Helen of Troy.</note> overturned Troy, so too will a marriage with **Lavinia** stir up a most bitter war in Italy. Our soul is joined to a "foreign woman" when, having abandoned **Virtue**, it delights in fleeting things. For this reason, the pleasure of **Paris** overturned Troy. In Italy, however, since the soul is not yet able to lay aside its desire for human affairs, bitter wars are stirred up—wars, indeed, in which the soul does not succumb as it did at Troy, but from which it may return as a victor and a triumpher once the kingdom has been won. 

"All these things seem to be fitly explained," said **LAURENTIUS**. "But I do not understand why it was said: 'they will wish they had not come.' For if a man who has already reached the state of contemplation ought to be firm in his purpose, I do not see why repentance <note>original: "poenitentia," which can mean regret or a change of mind.</note> should follow him. For I do not deny that even a grave man in such a stable purpose is often affected by a sharp sting; yet I do not think he is so greatly affected that he is driven to repentance—unless perhaps this was said as an <term>hyperbole</term> <note>original: "hiperbolem," from the Greek word for exaggeration.</note> (for so the Greeks call 'exaggeration'), so that by those words indicating regret, she intended to show not actual regret, but the **greatest difficulty**."

"That is exactly it," said **BAPTISTA**. "But let us see what **Aeneas** responds: truly, he observes that which those imbued with true doctrine always follow. For he says that he has come so **premeditated** that he has already revolved all things within his mind. For those things that are foreseen by us in advance provide a space so that, before they arrive, they can either be avoided or at least provided for so they do not cause such harm. When the mind itself gathers its strength, it is made more robust against difficulties. 

For just as if we attack enemies who are off-guard and fearing no such thing, we easily overcome them even if they are superior in position and number; but on the other hand, even a small force, if they have time to prepare those things which are conducive to battle, are often found to be a match for a very strong army. So it is with us: if we reflect within ourselves—although many things can happen through the concerns of the body—nevertheless, minds born of celestial seed, unless they give themselves over to sluggishness and cowardice, [will succumb] to no labors, to no difficulties...

<summary>Landino explores the allegorical meaning of the Sibyl's prophecy in Aeneid Book 6, interpreting Achilles as the soul's innate nobility and the "foreign woman" as the soul's attachment to fleeting pleasures. The dialogue between Laurentius and Baptista clarifies that Aeneas's "regret" is actually a rhetorical exaggeration of the immense difficulty of the contemplative path.</summary>
<keywords>Aeneid allegory, Cristoforo Landino, Sibyl, Achilles as nobility, Juno as ambition, Paris and pleasure, Lavinia, contemplation vs action, foresight and fortitude</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of the Aeneid, moving from Aeneas's arrival in Italy to the philosophical significance of his descent into the underworld (the katabasis). The author transitions from historical/poetic definitions of Hell to a Platonic interpretation where "Hell" is the physical body.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>Book Four</header>

...provided we are willing, no injury of fortune can hinder us from being able to return to our origin; we shall emerge undefeated from every battle of disturbances. Since **Aeneas** had meditated on these things within himself for a long time, he does not ask to be taught those things only now. Rather, standing at the threshold of the things to be contemplated <note>The "threshold of contemplation" marks the transition from active life to the deep study of divine or philosophical truths.</note>, he prays to be led down to the underworld. In this place, I will attempt to explain in a few words what Aeneas's descent to the underworld signifies, provided I first briefly demonstrate what "Hell" <note>original: "infernum," meaning the lower places or the world below.</note> is and in how many ways one descends to it. 

The concept of Hell was established many centuries before the Christian name, not only by the Hebrews but also by the Egyptians. Whatever our religion affirms after the birth of Christ without any doubt regarding Hell and the punishments that the souls of the guilty suffer there, we have received all these, if I am not mistaken, from the Hebrews. But those things entrusted to the monuments of the Egyptians, **Orpheus** first transferred to the Greeks. Most Greek poets then expanded these with their fictions, the leaders of whom we see are **Homer**, **Hesiod**, **Euripides**, and **Aristophanes**. Following them among our own writers, besides **Maro** <note>Publius Virgilius Maro, the author of the Aeneid.</note>, were **Ovid** of Sulmona, and from Spain, **Statius Papinius** and **Lucan**, and **Claudian** (whom many believe to have been Florentine).

And all of these believed the seats of the underworld to be subterranean and reaching even to the center—which place is the lowest in the sphere <note>In the Aristotelian and Ptolemaic cosmos, the center of the Earth was the lowest point in the entire universe.</note>. Therefore, they fabled that certain caves and gaps in the earth—especially if they vomit fire or smoke—are the entrance to the underworld, with the full agreement of old women <note>original: "muliercularum," a dismissive term for unlearned women or folk superstitions.</note> and the entire common folk. 

For in the region of Laconia, there is Mount **Taenarus** near the end of the Malean promontory; because a noise is heard from its deepest cave (driven by the movement of air), it was easy to persuade the common people that one descends to the underworld from there. Furthermore, the **Acherusian** marsh in Epirus, not far from Heraclea, named (as they say) by the Argive **Hercules**, has a cave through which antiquity believed **Cerberus**, the three-headed dog of **Pluto**, was led out by Hercules. As for Lake **Avernus**, there is nothing I need to report; for it is a well-known matter and sung of by many. 

And so much for the poets. **Plato**, however, imbued with the same teaching as Orpheus, follows the details in such a way that he intends the "place of the underworld" to be nothing else for our souls than the body itself, in which they are enclosed as if in a prison <note>This is the famous Platonic concept of *soma sema*—the body as a tomb or prison for the soul.</note>. For he posits that souls were created by the highest God; these, led by their own nature, turn toward God their parent. Nor is it a wonder. For there is nothing that does not return to its origin when it can. 

For we see (to use this as an example) that this "earthly" fire (so to speak), because it was generated by the power and seed of a higher fire, is raised toward the higher regions by the impulse of its nature. Souls turned toward God are so illuminated by His rays that where their essence had previously been, as it were, formless by itself, it is now shaped by that brilliance. And it happens in a wondrous way that the brightness received within the soul's essence does not reside there as something external and accidental; but drawn to the soul's capacity, it is indeed rendered dimmer and degenerates from itself, yet becomes the mind's own natural light. Therefore, with this as a guide, the soul comes to the knowledge of itself and of all things that exist below it (for those are bodies). 

But God and the other things that appear above it, the soul does not see with this light. For how will it reach those things which are above its nature, if that light has now become natural to it? Yet it performs this service for our mind: for through this first "spark," as it were, having become closer to God, the mind receives another and indeed clearer light, by which it now perceives celestial and heavenly things...

<summary>The author discusses the historical and poetic origins of the concept of Hell, attributing it to Hebrew and Egyptian sources before exploring Plato’s philosophical view that Hell represents the soul's imprisonment within the physical body.</summary>
<keywords>Aeneas, Underworld, Orpheus, Plato, Katabasis, Soul and Body, Neoplatonism, Avernus, Taenarus, Divine Light</keywords>
