<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the nature of the soul's descent. In the previous page, the author began to move from a poetic definition of "Hell" to a Platonic one, where the physical world and the body are seen as the "underworld" for the divine soul. We now see the characters of Lorenzo de' Medici and Leon Battista Alberti discussing Marsilio Ficino’s theories on the soul's "twin lights."</meta>

# Allegory on P. Virgilius Maro

...to receive the knowledge of these things. But I think these things are by no means hidden from you, **Lorenzo**: for they were explained not only learnedly and precisely, but with total clarity by our friend **Marsilio** <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), the central figure of the Florentine Renaissance who translated Plato into Latin.</note> in those dialogues which he wrote as a commentary on Plato’s *Symposium* <note>original: "Platonis symposium," referring to Ficino's "De Amore," a commentary on Plato's dialogue about love.</note> and published under your name. I desire for these works to be most familiar to you, both because they are addressed to you and especially because they abound with many choice insights.

"They are indeed familiar," said **Lorenzo**. "But that particular passage sticks in my mind especially, in which he demonstrates that a double light shines within us: one natural and innate, as you were saying, and the other divine and infused. Supported by these two together, as if by twin wings, our souls can fly through this entire upper world. He adds that if they always use that divine light, they will always cling to divine things. However, it would seem that this lowest place of the earth would be lacking in a living being possessed of reason. Lest that happen, it was ordained by divine providence that the soul should be entirely its own master: it has the power, at its own discretion, either to use both lights together when it pleases, or to use only one. Therefore, it happens that with nature as its guide, the soul—having turned toward its native light—considers only itself and its own powers which look toward the fabrication of the body, setting aside the divine light for the present, and desires to exercise those powers in constructing the body."

"You remember correctly and well," said **Baptista** <note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), the famous architect and polymath, who appears as a character in these dialogues.</note>. "Our soul, therefore, dwells in heaven—as we see is the view of Plato, whom the poet follows—overflowing with the contemplation of divine nature itself. But, infected by that desire you spoke of and weighed down by the very mass of its thought, it begins to descend into the lower regions. However, since there is no single opinion among the followers of that philosopher when inquiring into the boundaries of the underworld according to the mind of Plato, we will follow the one at this time which we think is more consistent with the reason of souls and which we see is more pleasing to the more learned."

These men, therefore, propose a bipartite world. For they wished the highest heaven, which is called the **Aplanes** <term>Aplanes: a Greek term for the "unwandering" or fixed stars, the outermost sphere of the universe.</term>—fitted with burning stars (as the poet says)—to be the region of the upper beings; and they named those the **Elysian Fields** and the **Isles of the Blessed**. But the sphere of **Saturn** and the six remaining spheres which are below it, and again whatever space lies between the moon and the earth, and the earth itself, they attributed to the underworld.

Therefore, that highest part by which divine things are either sustained or consist is called **Nectar**, and it is believed to be the drink of the gods. But they call the lower part **Lethean** <note>referring to Lethe, the river of forgetfulness.</note> and the drink of men. For when the soul has fallen into this part from the upper heaven through **Cancer** <note>In Platonic myth, the constellation of Cancer was called the "Gate of Men" through which souls descend to earth, while Capricorn was the "Gate of Gods" through which they ascend.</note> (for that is called the gate of men), it falls into the tumult of **Hyle** <term>Hyle: a Greek word meaning "wood" or "timber," used philosophically to refer to raw, chaotic matter.</term> itself, which is the matter of the elements. In this place, weighed down by the intoxication of this new drink and made as if drunken, it forgets divine things. Enticed by the desire for earthly things, it glides down through the underlying spheres in such a way that it acquires from each of the celestial ranks one of those motions which it will later use in the body.

For from that which they call the star of **Saturn**, it draws the power of reasoning and understanding; from **Jupiter**, the power of acting; from **Mars**, the power of daring. The **Sun**, however, grants it the ability to know and even to form opinions. Soon, received by **Venus**, it borrows the motion of desire. Then, descending through the heavens of **Mercury** and the **Moon**, it acquires from the former the powers of proclaiming and interpreting, and from the latter the powers of planting and growing. And finally, having been carried down to the earth as if to the center—whither all heavy things are borne—and having entered the body as if into a prison, or rather a tomb, it is rightly said to be banished among the inhabitants of the underworld. For the soul dies in the body as if submerged in a tomb—not in such a way, however, that it is extinguished by a death of this kind, but so that it is buried for a time.

<summary>This page provides a Platonic allegory of the soul's descent from the fixed stars through the planetary spheres to Earth. It explains how the soul acquires different human faculties (like reason, desire, and growth) from each planet before being "imprisoned" in the physical body.</summary>
<keywords>Platonism, Marsilio Ficino, Leon Battista Alberti, soul's descent, planetary spheres, Elysian Fields, Hyle, Lethe, Aeneid allegory, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical views of the Platonists—who see the physical body as a "hell" for the divine soul—to the Christian theological perspective. The previous page introduced Marsilio Ficino’s theories; here, the text explains the allegorical meaning of the rivers of the underworld before defining the physical location of Hell in Christian doctrine.</meta>

# Book Four

<meta>image quality: clear; primary language: Latin with Greek terms; catchword: none</meta>

Since harmful bodies slow down the soul's divinity, and earthly limbs and dying members dull it: you have (albeit briefly) what the Platonists think the underworld is, and what descent of souls they posit toward it. For it is clear that all those things which the poets tell stories about regarding the inhabitants of Tartarus, the soul suffers while in the body. For once it is dragged into matter, it drinks a new "drunkenness of the forest" <note>The Latin *sylva* (forest/wood) is a translation of the Greek *hyle*, a technical term for the chaotic "raw material" of the physical world. The "drunkenness" refers to the soul becoming dazed and forgetful of its divine origin upon birth.</note> when it is submerged in it as if by a river. 

Moreover, not without precise reasoning, they lead that very river into four rivers and the Stygian marsh: Lethe, Acheron, Styx, Cocytus, and Phlegethon. For the soul, mixed with matter, forgets all things it had seen in the heavens. Therefore, they rightly devised the name "Lethe" <note>original: *Lethæum* from the Greek *lethe*, meaning "forgetfulness."</note> from the fact of what it is; for so the Greeks say "to forget" <note>original: *λήθη*</note>, and thus they imagined the soul drinks from it. From this, moreover, flows Acheron, a name which denotes a privation of joy, as if from *achos* <note>original: *αχός*, meaning "pain" or "distress."</note>. For the joy which the soul, existing pure in the contemplation of God, used to receive, it loses entirely through forgetfulness. 

Once that joy is lost, Styx—which you will easily interpret as "sadness"—must necessarily arise. Finally, the last estuaries of Styx form Cocytus <note>The OCR reads *coitum*, likely a transcription error for *Cocytum*.</note>. For who does not fall from sadness into mourning? It does not escape you that the Greeks call this *Cocytus*, which the Latins interpret as "to mourn." From long-lasting mourning, we are accustomed to fall into a heat of fury and madness, which they call Phlegethon <note>From the Greek *phlegethein*, "to burn."</note>. Therefore, from *hyle* <note>Matter.</note> as from a single river, all these evils come about. 

Wherefore, not without the highest learning, they imagined the remaining streams to be derived from Lethe. But these things are explained more broadly by Socrates in the *Phaedo* <note>A dialogue by Plato regarding the immortality of the soul.</note>. For us, however, a few things out of many had to be brought to this place, and only those by which the descent to the underworld would be made clear according to Plato’s opinion.

Our [Christian] thinkers, however, who most rightly feel that souls are so created by God that they are created and infused into their bodies at the same moment, did not wish them to dwell in this lower world merely to purge committed sins. For how could they have sinned outside the body if they had not existed before the body? Rather, they were placed here so that through their right actions—which they perform with total free will—they might eventually deserve to enjoy heaven. 

For God granted to us that we might freely use our judgment, not so we would fall through wickedness, but so we might acquire the "Supreme Good" through religion and justice. But when, neglecting that through the greatest folly, enticed by bodily and foul pleasures, we are defiled by the greatest and most varied crimes, it was necessary for there to be a place where souls of this kind, having departed from the body, would pay the most deserved penalties for their deeds. 

They willed this place to be located especially around the center of the earth. For since both the prophet said, "God rescued my soul from the lower hell," <note>Referring to Psalm 86:13.</note> and the Savior of the human race himself declared he would be in the "heart of the earth" for three days, it is easily proven to be at the center. For nothing is lower than the center; indeed, it stands in the middle of the earth just as we see the heart is in the middle of a living being. In which part no one would deny there is "outer darkness" (since it is farthest from the light) and "gnashing of teeth" (since no power of the sun descends there). 

Therefore, Hell will be in the center of the earth, but it will be such that—according to the things most wisely gathered by Gregory <note>Likely Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD), whose "Dialogues" shaped the medieval understanding of Purgatory and Hell.</note>—it extends all the way to this air, which is made thicker by the mist of earth and water. 

And so much for the underworld; almost the entire human race has said that souls descend to it. But still, some think one thing, others another. For that plunging of souls from the highest heaven into these bodies is believed by Plato to be the "descent to the underworld"; Christians, however, believe the souls of the wicked [descend] from their bodies...

<summary>This page contrasts the Platonic allegory of the five rivers of the underworld (representing the soul's descent into the body and forgetfulness) with the Christian view of Hell as a physical place of punishment at the center of the earth.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Socrates, Gregory the Great, Hell, Lethe, Acheron, Styx, Cocytus, Phlegethon, soul, hyle, center of the earth, descent to the underworld</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the various types of "descents" to the underworld. Having previously discussed physical death and the soul's entrapment in matter, the speaker now transitions into the moral and philosophical allegory of Aeneas’s journey, interpreting it as an intellectual descent into the nature of vice.</meta>

->### On Virgil’s Allegories<-

<meta>Catchword: descend</meta>

...they warn that souls are dragged to the underworld. We likewise say that living men, when they fall into vice, rush to the underworld. There are also those who believe that through magic arts and incantations, a kind of descent is made so that souls can be summoned from there. 

But besides these four descents, there is a fifth which it seems should not be omitted: for we also head toward the underworld when we cast down the light of our reason and our diligence to observe the nature of evil and all vices. I therefore gladly inquire of you, O **Lorenzo**, since you have perceived all these things: what do you think **Virgil** <note>original: "Virgilium." The text refers to the Roman poet Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> wished to express by this descent of **Aeneas**? 

"I have seen what you have been doing for a long time, O **Baptista**," <note>Likely Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472), a famous polymath and architect who appears as a character in these dialogues.</note> said **Lorenzo**, "and I thank you most greatly for it. For who does not see that this entire discussion was given not only at my request, but also worked out by me for the instruction of myself and my brother? For even if all the others who are present are wonderfully delighted by your speech, yet their age and learning are such that even without a guide they can understand all these things by themselves. 

Since you have undertaken to educate these two, and because you wish to know whether we hold correctly or otherwise what you have discussed so far, I will answer whatever you ask without any hesitation. For in this way you will be able to easily correct errors, and if I hold anything correctly, it will stick more firmly, being confirmed by your judgment. 

**Aeneas** therefore asks of the **Sibyl**—whom you have already interpreted as 'learning' or 'doctrine'—to lead him to the underworld and to his father. When he asks this, he shows that the mind, with doctrine itself leading the way, descends into sensuality. For he wishes to thoroughly know the vices that come from it. 

But see how much I owe you from this discussion: for you have not only made me grasp these things divinely said by **Virgil**, but, prompted by the similarity of things, I now easily guess what our **Dante** <note>original: "Danthes." A reference to Dante Alighieri and his own journey through Hell in the *Divine Comedy*.</note> also intended. But of this, another time; you, indeed, if it pleases you, proceed to the rest."

"You indeed interpret correctly," said **Baptista**. "Moreover, that talent and judgment of yours delight me exceedingly. But hear what is answered to him by the prophetess. For first, she demonstrates that the descent to the underworld is no trouble at all, since the door stands open nights and days. Which indeed is too true, even more than I would wish. For since we are prone—as even the old man in **Terence** <note>The Roman playwright Terence; the "old man" likely refers to a character in his plays who laments the moral decline of youth.</note> complains—to move from labor to lust, we easily slide into vice. 

And most rightly is that saying from **Hesiod**; <note>An early Greek poet. The reference is likely to the "two paths" of virtue and vice mentioned in *Works and Days*.</note> and most rightly also that one can descend there even with closed eyes. For whether we slide into vices by failing, since that happens through foolishness—and very few are without foolishness—what, I ask you, will you find easier? Or—but I now let the remaining descents go, the steepness of which is clearly apparent—who does not see the matter now at hand? 

The mind itself and reason easily descend into the knowledge of the senses. But the greatest danger arises lest, while we are occupied with individual pleasures of the body, we are so soothed by their allurements that we remain stuck, entangled. Therefore the mind easily descends into the senses, but is not easily recalled from them. For that is what it means to 'return from the underworld.' 

For only 'a few, whom impartial Jupiter loved, or whom glowing virtue carried to the heavens, born of gods, were able to do it.' <note>A translation of *Aeneid* 6.129-131: "pauci quos aequus amavit / Iuppiter aut ardens evexit ad aethera virtus / dis geniti potuere."</note> As you see, he posits three kinds of men who are permitted to return to the upper world. But we will speak first of the last two: **Plato**—as we demonstrated a little more fully above—holds that our souls, weighed down by desire for earthly things, descend into bodies...

<summary>This page explores the allegorical meaning of Aeneas's descent into Hades, interpreting it as the human mind's descent into the world of the senses to study vice. The dialogue between Lorenzo de' Medici and Baptista links Virgil's poetry to Dante's Inferno and Platonic philosophy regarding the soul's entrapment in the body.</summary>

# Book Four

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark in bottom right corner. Foxing and staining present on the right margin. Text on the left edge is bleed-through or from a facing page and is ignored.</meta>

Therefore, those who previously fed upon ambrosia and nectar—for that is to say, they enjoyed God—and took wondrous joy from that source, have now, having drunk from the **Lethe** <note>In Greek mythology, the river of forgetfulness in the underworld.</note>, been submerged in the forgetting of all things. But if the soul, established within the body, is defiled by the body's thoughts and filth, it is covered by such thick darkness that there is no hope thereafter of returning to the higher light. 

If, however, gathering itself into itself, it lives purely and chastely, and avoids the company of the body as much as possible, it gradually awakens from that forgetfulness—by which it was lulled to sleep as if by drunkenness and wine—and is able to prepare for itself those twin wings which I have already named so many times. Supported by these, it easily leaps back from the lower world, and returning to the heavens, flies back to its own region. 

By these two wings, therefore, we understand two kinds of virtues: those that correct the actions of life, which he calls by the single name **justice**; and those by which we are led to the knowledge of truth, which he most rightly calls **religion**. Therefore, that phrase—*the few whom burning virtue has carried up to the ether* <note>original: "pauci quos ardens euexit ad æthera uirtus." A quote from Virgil's Aeneid, Book VI, line 129.</note>—expresses the first wing and refers to the virtues concerning life and character. When he then adds, *the sons of the gods were able* <note>original: "diis geniti potuere." Aeneid VI, line 131.</note>, he signifies the second wing: that very religion which <term>Plato</term> <note>The Greek philosopher (c. 427–347 BCE) whose ideas on the soul's immortality and "wings" of virtue are central to this text.</note> wishes to consist of those virtues that lead to the truth. 

Thus, <term>Maro</term> <note>Publius Vergilius Maro, better known as the poet Virgil.</note> borrows these things from such a great philosopher. To this doctrine, he added something from the opinion of the mathematicians <note>The "mathematicians" here refers to astrologers or those who study the movements of the heavens.</note> in such a way that he neither took away the right and liberty of souls, nor deprived the celestial bodies of their own power. For if we were to say that the stars bring force and necessity upon our souls, not only would that be impious in our religion, but it would also be shrinking away from the doctrine of the greatest philosophers. 

But so you may understand that this is not foreign to Platonic dogma, he relates in the <term>Timaeus</term> <note>Plato's famous dialogue concerning the creation of the universe and the nature of the physical world.</note> that the creation of the rational soul is to be attributed to no one but God, since He Himself creates the essence and reason of our souls. However, the body and the other parts of the soul—such as those which feel desire or anger—we borrow from the "soul of the world." 

Wherefore, even if our mind itself is subject to no command of the stars, nevertheless, because it exercises no action from which virtues and vices flow except through the senses and appetite—and because the stars bring various affections toward virtues or inclinations toward vices into those senses (since they are of the body)—it seems to matter a great deal under which star we are born. 

This applies not only to those things which we said pertain to life and character, but also to those which look toward speculation and the knowledge of truth. For if all the souls of all people are of the same nature, from where else but the body does it arise that some exist who are most excellent in talent, judgment, and memory, while in others these things do not appear at all? Since all our knowledge takes its beginning from those things which are produced (effects) to the producing cause, and from those things circumscribed by place and time to the infinite, much difficulty is cast before our souls in attaining intelligence, science, and wisdom. This happens when those powers—which, as I said a little while ago, use the limbs as instruments—grow dull through the depravity of those instruments and cannot unfold themselves. 

Therefore, when <term>Maro</term> had read in his <term>Plato</term> that one cannot return to the gods above unless we recover those twin wings, and had likewise noticed that the recovery of those wings is hindered by the stars (for the reason I have shown), he showed that there was a need for us to be loved by *just <term>Jove</term>.* <note>original: "Ioue æquo." A reference to Virgil’s line "quos aequus amavit Iuppiter" (whom just Jupiter has loved). In this context, it refers to receiving favorable divine or celestial influence.</note> This is nothing other than that we should receive, through the kindness of the stars, inclinations toward right actions. For since the power of the <term>planets</term> is varied...

<summary>This page explains the Neo-Platonic allegory of the soul's "wings"—Justice and Religion—required to escape the material world and return to the divine. It reconciles Virgil's poetry with Plato's Timaeus, arguing that while the soul is free, the stars influence the physical body and senses.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, Virgil, Timaeus, Justice, Religion, Astrology, Wings of the Soul, Lethe, Jupiter, Soul of the World</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion from the previous section, moving from the necessity of "wings" for the soul's ascent to an exploration of how the "Soul of the World"—often identified with Jupiter—assists in this return. The dialogue features references to Marsilio Ficino and Plato's *Timaeus*.</meta>

->**On the Allegories of P. Virgilius Maro**<-

We see that the nature of Jupiter is of this kind: those whom he has looked upon kindly at their birth are rendered inclined toward justice and religion. This inclination impels them toward recovering those wings which we have discussed. Let us conclude, therefore, that no one returns from the lower regions unless they recover their wings; however, this is not easy except for those who are raised to the heights by the kindness of the stars. But what did you, looking at **L. Marsilius** <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), the famous Florentine philosopher and head of the Platonic Academy.</note>, whisper secretly? **L.** replied that it was only because **Marsilius** had spoken to him about the "soul of the world" <note>The *Anima Mundi*, a concept from Plato's *Timaeus* describing a spirit that connects all living things.</note> a few days ago while he had Plato’s ***Timaeus*** in his hands. **B.** said: I see that I must proceed more cautiously, since our business is not only with a learned man, but also with one who has a great memory. But what you say about the soul of the world is both true and fits this passage perfectly. For **Plato** believes that the seeds of reason were produced by God, and that our souls were created by Him, and then handed over to the soul of the world to be clothed in a body. Thus, they were both clothed in a body and shaped by the soul's attendant powers. 

It was fitting that, since the desires of the flesh and the impulses of anger exist for the sake of the body’s safety, these should be given to us by the same one who had enclosed us in bodies. But because these parts are slippery, it was right that the one who had given us those parts—which easily slide toward the worse—should himself, in some part, protect us from errors and support us as we fall. For this reason, the Highest Father commanded that, since He himself had provided the cause of forgetfulness for our souls by surrounding them with a "muddy" body <note>A reference to the physical, earthly body as a vessel of clay or mud that obscures the soul's divine origins.</note>, He should also infuse the brightness of truth into mankind. Therefore, obeying instructions of this kind, the soul of the world leads all those to whom it is favorable—either through dreams, oracles, and omens, or by a certain internal motion—toward both the provision of the future and the knowledge of divine law, so that with it as our leader, we may recover our wings. 

Ancient theologians, and the Platonists who followed them, often call this soul of the world **Jupiter**. Hence **Orpheus** says: "Jupiter is the first-born; Jupiter is the last; Jupiter is the head; Jupiter is the middle. All things are born from Jupiter." From this also comes that saying: "Jupiter is whatever you see, whatever you move." Indeed, even **Maro** <note>The poet Virgil's family name.</note> himself says: *From Jupiter is the beginning of the Muse; all things are full of Jupiter.* <note>original: "A ioue principium musæ iouis omnia plena." From Virgil's Eclogue III.</note> For all things are full of the soul of the world; since it is so entirely within the whole world, and entirely within any part of it, it flourishes everywhere just as our souls do in our small bodies. Finally, this soul, holding the heaven like a lute, creates harmony from the sounds of the different heavens; since they call these harmonies "Muses," the Muses are rightly said to be the daughters of **Jupiter**. 

Therefore, I can more easily conceive in my mind than express in words how much teaching **Maro** included in those three verses. Rightly, then: *the few whom impartial Jupiter has loved, or whom blazing virtue has carried up to the heavens.* <note>original: "pauci quos æquus amauit Iupiter: aut ardens euexit ad æthera uirtus." From Aeneid VI.129–130.</note> And that other phrase is also right: *the forests hold all things.* <note>original: "tenent omnia siluæ." From Aeneid VI.131.</note> For from **hyle** <term>hyle: A Greek term for "matter," often viewed by Platonists as the source of chaos and physical limitation.</term> (as we demonstrated above) comes all our foolishness and the infection in all souls, by which they are hindered from returning to the heights. 

But enough regarding the difficulties of returning. Next, he explains those methods by which we may descend so safely that the return is open to us. The **Golden Bough** <note>The "Aureus Ramus" from the Aeneid, which Aeneas must find to enter the Underworld.</note> indicates wisdom to us, without which there is no contemplation to act as a judge in choosing and doing things. Nor should you wonder that gold holds the symbol of wisdom with this poet, since many writers have done the same. Whence comes that saying: *Wisdom is gold, and a multitude of gems, and the lips of knowledge are a precious vessel.* <note>original: "sapiens aurum & multitudo gemmarum & uas pretiosum labia scientiæ." A paraphrase of Proverbs 20:15.</note> For gold is the vigor and the brilliance of wisdom. No metal is more precious than gold. Nothing in human affairs is to be valued more than wisdom. Gold shines the most. Nothing is more splendid than wisdom.

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic interpretation of Virgil, identifying Jupiter as the 'Soul of the World' who assists the soul in overcoming the 'muddy' body to regain its divine wings. It also establishes the Golden Bough as a symbol of divine wisdom necessary for the soul's safe passage.</summary>
<keywords>Jupiter, Soul of the World, Marsilio Ficino, Plato, Timaeus, Golden Bough, wisdom, hyle, Muses, Virgil, Aeneid, allegory, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on Virgil’s Aeneid, specifically focusing on the allegory of the Golden Bough as a symbol for wisdom and the soul's journey through the material world.</meta>

# Book Four

n

...Gold is consumed by no rust; nothing diminishes wisdom. Gold is defiled by no filth; wisdom is disfigured by no stains. But it lies hidden in a shaded tree; for truth is so overwhelmed by the many and varied shadows of ignorance, and so covered by the grove of the body’s <note>The author uses the phrase "the grove of the body" to describe the physical form as a dark, dense place that obscures spiritual sight.</note> dullness (if I may put it that way), that it is entirely difficult to unearth it. For it was skillfully observed by **Democritus** <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 460–370 BCE) often associated with the idea that truth is hidden or difficult to find.</note> that nature has submerged truth in the depths. Yet we are not able to descend into this contemplation before we have plucked the golden bough <term>Golden Bough (ramus aureus): In Virgil’s Aeneid, this is the magical branch Aeneas must find to enter the Underworld; here, it represents the light of wisdom.</term>. For **Proserpina** <note>The Queen of the Underworld; in this allegory, she represents the lower, sensory part of the soul.</note> forbids anyone to come to her without a gift of this kind. Indeed, Proserpina herself is that part of the soul which contains nothing except the senses; if we were to approach her without wisdom, no other remedy would be given to prevent our ruin. Entangled by her, there would never be any hope of returning. 

That saying is also correct: *when the first is torn away, another golden one is not lacking* <note>original: "primo avulso non deficit alter aureus" — a famous line from Virgil’s Aeneid, Book 6, line 143.</note>. For wisdom is nourished by itself, and it happens in the process of investigation that one truth opens up another. Nothing is perceived which, once understood, does not lead to the perception of something else. Who would not see that this was most truly said about the truth? For it must be investigated deeply; if we wish to find the truth, we must look at divine and heavenly things, not these lowest and decaying things. For all teaching from science comes from those things which are circumscribed by no boundaries and do not fall into destruction. 

Furthermore, she commands that what is already found be plucked by us in the proper rite—and she commands this rightly. For unless we proceed in a certain fixed order, we will never make progress. He adds finally that *it will follow you easily* if you are called by the fates; but if you are not called, you can be pulled away neither by strength nor by hard iron. For the virtues which correct morals and respect what is right and fair are so powerful at purging the soul from filth that they may migrate from the body of the world. 

However, to come to that supreme knowledge of things is granted to very few, and only to those who are called by the fates. Therefore, he rightly says: *if the fates call you*. To express this more clearly, the **Platonists** <note>Followers of Plato's philosophy, particularly those in the Renaissance like Ficino who blended it with theology.</note> want God to know Himself first. Then, He knows all other things. In the third place, He brings to effect all that He has known. Therefore, this last stage depends on the second, and the second depends on the first. For He produces all things because He knows them; and He knows them for no other reason than because He contemplates Himself, in whom all things exist. 

In this kind of order, they place these three things in God: the first they name **Wisdom**, the second **Providence**, and the third **Fate**. **Christians**, however, although they feel the same way (unless I am mistaken), hardly dare to use the name of **Fate**. This is not because they are angry with Plato, but because they saw that there were certain families in philosophy <note>Likely referring to the Stoics, who believed in a rigid, inescapable destiny.</note> that imposed such necessity upon fate that they left us no freedom of choice in our actions; thus, they seem to loathe the name of fate. But let us, following the philosopher I mentioned a little while ago, say that God considers the causes of things—that is, Himself. Then, He gazes upon the origin, order, and finally the governance of the things He encompasses. He subsequently executes all these things in such a way that He can be hindered in no way. This very thing they call "fate." 

If this is so, those who say that the reason and order of things—which is called **Providence** in the mind of God—is called **Fate** in things that are movable and circumscribed by place and time, are not in error. Therefore, if the fates allow it, the golden bough will follow you willingly and easily. Thus, it is granted to a few, and that by a certain divine gift beyond one's lot, from the very providence of God, whose counsel it is forbidden for man to scrutinize. For the Lord is righteous and his [judgments are] right...

<summary>This page explores the allegory of the Golden Bough as a symbol for Divine Wisdom, explaining that truth is hidden by the "grove" of the physical body. The author reconciles Platonic hierarchies of Wisdom, Providence, and Fate with Christian theology, emphasizing that "Fate" is simply God's Providence enacted in the material world.</summary>

<keywords>Wisdom, Golden Bough, Proserpina, Democritus, Platonism, Providence, Fate, Free Will, Allegory, Virgil, Soul's Journey</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Virgil's Aeneid, moving from the symbolism of the Golden Bough (wisdom) to the character of Misenus, who represents the hollow pursuit of worldly fame.</meta>

## On Virgil’s Allegories

...his plans; but those which cannot be comprehended by mortal intellect. For who is so reckless as to claim to know why the Lord revealed to the apostles **John** and **Paul** things which he refused to uncover for many most holy men, even those enlightened by great learning? This example applies widely and can be transferred to all who have labored in any kind of learning: though many may burn with the same zeal and expend the same effort and labor, some reach the highest point in that art, while others are scarcely permitted to stand among the last. 

You have my judgment on what the **Golden Bough** <term>Golden Bough (ramus aureus): In the Aeneid, a magical branch that grants Aeneas passage to the Underworld; here, it is an allegory for the divine gift of wisdom.</term> signifies. As for what pertains to the funeral of the wretched man, understand it thus. The name **Misenus** <note>Aeneas’s trumpeter. The author uses a common Renaissance technique of "etymological allegory," claiming the name Misenus derives from the Greek *misos* (hatred) and *ainos* (praise), thus "hateful praise."</note> is correctly interpreted as "hateful praise." Therefore, it represents a certain empty glory. For those who neglect virtue—from which solid and distinct glory flows—and instead chase after honors and the other trappings of virtue, are worthy of the greatest hatred. Those who undertake great deeds in civic life are especially accustomed to seeking this. These men labor not out of love for what is right and honorable, but from a lust for glory. While they desire to attain this, they often destroy the state and incur the extreme hatred of the citizens. 

Therefore, **Juvenal** <note>A Roman satirical poet known for criticizing the corruption of Roman society.</note> said excellently: *The thirst for fame is so much greater than for virtue.* <note>original: "Tanto maior famae sitis est quam virtutis"</note> Thus, men of this kind utterly corrupt the excellence of the mind, whether it was implanted by nature or acquired through industry and practice. For they do not love virtue, but the insignia of virtue, which are more often displayed by the wicked than by the good. Empty and shadowed glory, therefore, always clings to those who are more prominent in the administration of public affairs. 

For this reason, Virgil imagines that Misenus was also a companion of **Hector** <note>The greatest hero of Troy. Misenus serving Hector before Aeneas suggests that the pursuit of fame is a carry-over from one's worldly, "Trojan" life.</note>. If men of this kind were moved more by love of country than by a lust for glory, the cities they governed would be altogether most blessed. He, therefore, who moves toward the observation of vices for the sake of disentangling himself from them, must first of all cast away glory of this kind. This is why Misenus is rightly extinguished at the very time when **Aeneas** receives instructions from the **Sibyl** <note>The priestess and guide of Aeneas, representing spiritual or philosophical guidance.</note>. For the beginning of investigating the truth is best taken from the death of Misenus. Yet it was not enough for him to be extinguished, unless he were also buried, so that no trace of him might appear and he might never come back to life. 

Moreover, the fact that Virgil says he was a trumpeter fits perfectly. For it is the nature of such men to broadcast their own deeds as widely as possible and display them to everyone through their own heraldry. Furthermore, he is the son of **Aeolus**, the king of the winds. <note>In mythology, Aeolus controls the winds; allegorically, this means Misenus's "fame" is nothing but "hot air" or vanity.</note> For nothing is more "windy" than one who, having neglected solid and distinct virtue, hunts after a certain shadowed and utterly empty glory; hence they are called "swollen," "inflated," and "windy." 

Also correct is that line: *none was more outstanding at rousing men with the trumpet and kindling war with song.* <note>original: "quo non praestantior alter aere ciere viros martemque accendere cantu" — Aeneid 6.164-5.</note> For what else—to mention only these from countless Asian kings—impelled **Ninus**, **Cyrus**, or **Xerxes** so that, not content with their ancestral borders, they brought war upon many peoples and nations? What, among the Greeks, roused the **Spartans** or **Athenians** to join a great part of Asia to their empire? What persuaded **Hannibal** that, having subdued the Spanish and the Gauls, he should seek Rome, the head of the world? Among our own people, what kindled **Lucius Sulla** first, then **Gaius Marius**, then **Julius Caesar**, **Gnaeus Pompey**, and finally **Octavian** and **Mark Antony** with such fury that everything was filled with the blood of citizens, if not a certain insane desire for fame? For they sought glory in those things which compel the foolish masses to be struck with awe, but provoke the wise to the most righteous indignation and greatest hatred; but not even...

The author cites numerous historical conquerors as examples of those driven by this "windy" and destructive lust for fame.</summary>

# Book Four

...provided he does not become evil himself, he might call such men good. But what if even the best men in human society—those most ready to die for the republic—rushed into manifest death not only out of love for their country but also because they bore their final fate with a calmer mind out of a desire for glory? For who could persuade themselves that **Themistocles** <note>An Athenian politician and general whose naval strategy saved Greece from the Persian invasion at the Battle of Salamis in 480 BCE.</note> of Athens, in the naval battle fought at **Salamis**, or **Epaminondas** <note>A Theban general who broke Spartan military supremacy at the Battle of Leuctra.</note> in that victory by which he overcame the Spartans, or the Spartan **Leonidas** <note>The king of Sparta who famously led three hundred men in a heroic stand against the Persians at the pass of Thermopylae.</note> fighting manfully at **Thermopylae**, thought nothing of glory? Indeed, I do not believe that **Brutus** <note>Lucius Junius Brutus, a founder of the Roman Republic, who killed and was killed by the son of the exiled king Tarquin in single combat.</note> in his single combat against the son of the exiled king, nor **Scaevola** <note>Mucius Scaevola, who demonstrated Roman resolve to the enemy king Porsenna by thrusting his right hand into a fire and letting it burn without flinching.</note> burning his right hand with such constancy of spirit, nor those famous **Decii** <note>A father, son, and grandson from the same Roman family who each "devoted" themselves to death in battle to ensure a Roman victory.</note> rushing into the thickest of the enemy, nor the countless others who preferred the liberty of their country to their own lives, ever did anything without considering the fame they would leave to posterity.

But perhaps we linger too long on a matter manifest to everyone. Therefore, I return to **Misenus**, who, since he dared to challenge the god **Triton** <note>A sea god, son of Poseidon and Amphitrite; in Virgil's poem, he drowns Misenus for his arrogance in a musical contest.</note>, might rightly be called "insane." For what is more foolish than if this empty glory, arising from frail and quickly perishing things, should dare with such extreme rashness to equal itself to that glory which is true and proceeds from divine things? For this reason, he is easily overwhelmed by it. But let these points suffice for our purpose; the rest of the details described concerning the funeral may be granted to the pleasure of the history and the ears of the reader.

Furthermore, understand the "twin doves" to be those two wings of the soul which we mentioned a little earlier. For these lead the guides toward the contemplation of things. They are the birds of **Venus** <note>The goddess of love and mother of Aeneas; in the allegory, she represents the "ardent love" or desire for truth that motivates the seeker.</note> because it is necessary for them to spring from an ardent love. Nor does he invoke his mother without cause; for unless love seizes them, men easily flee from such great difficulty. Moreover, the doves lead him to the place not all at once or in a single rush, but by flying little by little. For it is not in the nature of man to see all things in a moment, but rather to come from the known to the unknown by reasoning step by step. But see what follows: "from there, when they came to the jaws of foul-smelling **Avernus** <note>A volcanic crater lake near Naples, believed by the ancients to be the entrance to the Underworld due to its sulfurous vapors.</note>":

->Swiftly they raise themselves and, gliding through the clear air,<-
->they settle on the desired seats atop the twin-tree.<-
<note>original: "Tollunt se celeres liquidumque per aera lapsae: Sedibus optatis geminae super arbore sidunt" (Aeneid 6.202-203).</note>

For those things which offer themselves as guides to the knowledge of such great matters must escape the contagion of earthly things—for that is what the foul odor of Avernus represents—with the swiftest flight. Therefore, a double kind of virtue leads us to the truth, which **Aeneas** seizes without delay, so that by its light he might be able to discern those things which are most obscure throughout the underworld.

The description of Avernus demonstrates the natural layout of the place. Nor is there any reason for us to labor in arranging the sacred rites performed by Aeneas into the sequence of our interpretation. For the poet, serving his own plot, explains those rituals which the ancients used for **necromancy** <note>The practice of communicating with the dead to predict the future.</note>. As for Aeneas being ordered to take up his journey with a naked sword—even if they were accustomed to observe this in those same rites—he is nevertheless warned himself to approach the arduous task with a robust spirit. Thus, Aeneas "matches his guide with no timid steps." For who does not see that whatever learning reveals to us must be snatched up as quickly and as diligently as possible? The journey was through obscure places, either because, as I said, nature has hidden the truth in shadow, or because the seats of the vices are far from the light. For he who is illuminated by the light of reason both knows the truth and acts rightly; but those who have lost that light, being ignorant of their own nature, fall into vice. Furthermore, he calls the houses of **Pluto** <note>The god of the Underworld.</note> "empty" and his realms "hollow." What could be said more truly than this? For it is...

->### On Publius Virgil Maro, Allegories<-

The day before yesterday, it was proved by clear reasoning that evils and vices are absolutely nothing, since they bring nothing of substance but instead drive away the good. When the wise and intense poet **Persius** <note>A Roman satirist (34–62 CE) known for his difficult style and Stoic moralizing.</note> understood this, he broke out most gravely into that exclamation: 

> O, the cares of men! O, how much emptiness there is in things! <note>original: "O curas hominum / O quantum est in rebus inane"</note> 

To more clearly set forth the nature of the beginning of vice, he showed that in such great darkness some light nevertheless appeared. For even if we slip into vice through a blindness of the mind, yet at the beginning not all light is utterly removed: for we become incontinent <note>Lacking self-control, particularly regarding physical desires.</note> before we fall into total intemperance. 

With wonderful judgment, Virgil places what follows at the entrance to the underworld. For if, according to the opinion of **Plato** (whom he follows), he is describing the descent of souls into their bodies, it is manifest that the soul—which until now had been free of all these evils—now falls into all of them through the contagion of the body. From that point, it feels every **disturbance** <term>perturbatio: a technical Stoic term for irrational or excessive emotions that "disturb" the soul's natural calm.</term>. It is tormented by grief and cares; it fears looming threats; it experiences diseases and labors; it is pressed by hunger and poverty; finally, it is weighed down by all the calamities the poet enumerates—none of which it had ever experienced when free from the body. 

But if we wish to interpret the descent of souls into vice by this kind of descent into the underworld, the reasoning will not be much different. For what thing follows a committed crime with such speed as repentance for the deed? And he who repents cannot be without grief. Add to this that he must be constantly purged by the stings of conscience: for constant cares press upon him, which, like avenging furies, exact punishment for his outrages most severely. I ask you to remember how learnedly, elegantly, and expressively **Juvenal** <note>A famous Roman satirist (late 1st/early 2nd century CE) who wrote about the corruption of Roman society.</note> put this. For he says by way of example: 

> Whatever evil is committed displeases the author himself; this is the first vengeance: that with himself as judge, no guilty man is acquitted. 

And a little later: 

> For whoever meditates any crime within himself has the guilt of the deed. Tell me, if I have carried out the attempt, perpetual anxiety does not cease even at mealtime. 

Rightly, therefore, are "avenging Cares" placed in the vestibule of the underworld. Nor will we wonder if "pale Diseases" dwell there when we understand the very sharp arguments of the **Stoics** <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy that taught that virtue is based on knowledge and that the wise live in harmony with Divine Reason.</note>. For they say that just as temperance settles desires and makes them obey right reason, and preserves the deliberate judgments of the mind, so intemperance is hostile to this, and inflames, disturbs, and incites every state of the soul; and in that way, all disturbances are born from it. 

For just as when blood in the body is corrupted, or phlegmatic humors truly overflow, diseases and illnesses are born, so the dissonance of wicked disturbances robs the soul of its health and violently perturbs it. Indeed, from these disturbances, "diseases" are formed which they call **passions** <note>original: pāthē (the Greek word πάθη, referring to irrational emotions)</note>; then follow the "illnesses" which are called **maladies** <note>original: nosēmata (the Greek word νοσήματα, referring to chronic spiritual sickness)</note>. Therefore, a disturbance is always in motion because opinions toss themselves about inconstantly and turbulently. But when a madness and agitation of the mind of this kind has become chronic, and has settled as if in the veins and marrow, then disease and illness arise. For when we are inflamed with a desire for money from a certain false opinion—which grants more to riches than should be granted—and if some **Socratic medicine** <note>The use of philosophical questioning and logic to "cure" the soul of its errors.</note> is not immediately applied to extinguish that desire, it flows into the veins and brings about that disease and illness which we call avarice. You are right...

# Book Four

->### On Publius Virgil Maro, Allegories<-

"Indeed, concerning these diseases, you seem to me," said **Laurentius**, "to have spoken quite appropriately to explain the passage. For not only philosophers (as you have rightly shown), but also orators and poets very often speak of diseases not just of the body, but also of the soul."

"Therefore," said **Baptista**, "just as we refer diseases to the soul, so too shall we rightly refer old age to it. For since the mind itself possesses a certain sharpness and strength of youth, when it wastes away with a certain idleness and lethargy as if by the decay of old age, we see it easily fall into vices; and this is the death of souls. Now, what else does 'hunger, the persuader of evil' <note>original: "malesuada fames." A reference to Virgil, Aeneid VI.276, describing the personifications at the gates of Hell.</note> designate than greed, by which men are driven to every crime? For what else persuades us to provoke innocent peoples with most unjust wars, or to carry out slaughters and robberies, or to go about in banditry, or to prepare poisons, or to break faith, or to betray one's country and citizens, if not the 'cursed hunger for gold'? <note>original: "auri sacra fames." From Aeneid III.57.</note>

If this is indeed the case, 'foul poverty' <note>Aeneid VI.276: "turpis egestas."</note> must also be placed in the same category. For since men think poverty—which no wise man ever considered foul—is most foul, and they dread it as the greatest evil, nothing resists and no shame stands in the way of their holding everything up for sale in order to flee it. Nor are 'forms terrible to behold,' 'death,' and 'toil' absent. For those who, exiled from the light, dwell in these shadows, seek nothing except idle leisure. Nor do they remember that men were born for action and contemplation, and that no labor which is joined to virtue should be shunned. 

Regarding 'death,' understand it thus: Philosophers who are occupied with the acquisition of wisdom notice that if the body is taken as a partner in acting, it is the greatest hindrance to them. For the senses, which come from the body, contain in themselves no truth and nothing pure that would enable them to judge these matters rightly. From this it follows that if the soul uses them for investigation, it is often deceived, and, drunk with their allurements, foresees nothing. Therefore, they call the mind away from the senses and the body as much as possible. For **Plato**, in the dialogue titled ***Phaedo***, says that we shall only then be happy if we are drawn away from bodily things and made like unto God. And what else shall we say this is but 'to die'? 

Therefore, if men of this sort meditate on dying while they live, do you think they will tremble at coming death? But fools, who know nothing except the body, will suffer its dissolution with the greatest bitterness of soul. Rightly, therefore, does he whom I name so often, **Plato**, call the former 'philosophers' and the latter 'body-lovers' <note>original: "philosophomatos," a Latinized Greek term for those who love the material body over the soul.</note>. Since **Maro** <note>The family name of the poet Virgil.</note> knew all these things well, he did not say those forms *were* terrible, but that they *seemed* terrible. 

As for the remaining things enumerated—'sleep,' and the 'evil joys of the mind,' and finally 'war,' 'furies,' and 'discord'—any man of even moderate talent will easily refer them to the same reasoning. For he who is in vice grows numb, as if sleepy, to every honorable reason; nor does he take any pleasure except from shameful things. Moreover, he wages war and discord not only with others, but with himself, since lust wants one thing and greed another. Sloth calls him to leisure, while ambition calls him to labors. He is hounded by this distraction of the soul as if by Furies. In the description of the last thing, he now depicts with a most beautiful and entirely poetic fiction the same point he had shown a little earlier. For it, placed in the middle, occupies a great space; it yields no fruit, but delights us with its shadow alone; thus it shows us that shameful deed which has nothing of substance, and which, though it seems great, is nothing...

<summary>This page interprets Virgil's underworld monsters as psychological allegories for the soul's corruption by the body, framing greed and poverty as the drivers of vice. Drawing on Plato's Phaedo, it argues that true philosophy is a "meditation on death" that separates the mind from the deceptive senses.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Virgil’s Aeneid, Book VI. # On Publius Virgil Maro, Allegories

...the example of Aesop <note>Referencing Aesop’s fable of the dog and its reflection: by reaching for a shadow, it loses the real substance.</note>, let us not abandon the body only to chase a shadow. Virgil notes this even more expressively when he adds that a single dream sits upon every single leaf, and that these dreams are indeed empty. Nothing is lighter or more changeable than leaves. Those things in which fools place their "highest good"—and for the sake of which they commit robberies, frauds, and many other outrages to attain honors, riches, and the like—are placed in the recklessness of Fortune. Everyone knows how easily these things change and fall away. They are most truly compared to empty dreams. 

In that same place, many monsters are positioned, and not by chance. For if we call "monsters" those things that occur contrary to the laws of nature, then all outrages against morality can be called monsters by their true name, since they arise contrary to the law of reason, which is the only thing that makes us human. 

The Centaurs are rightly thought to be the sons of Ixion <note>Ixion attempted to seduce Hera but was tricked by a cloud-clone (Nephele); their offspring were the Centaurs, symbolizing lawlessness.</note>; for he, despising justice and breaking the bond of humanity, oppressed free peoples with the yoke of tyranny. Therefore, his thoughts—which at the beginning might show some hint of humanity—eventually degenerate into a kind of cruelty and wildness. Not unskilfully, therefore, does Plutarch demonstrate that men of this sort, clinging to a mere image of virtue, do nothing sincere or right, but make everything a confused and notorious mess. Since each man follows his own pleasure, he is carried away to his own impulses by the greatest disturbances of the soul. 

I would be more wordy than the subject requires if I were to pursue both Scyllas <note>Referring to the mythical sea-monsters; here they likely represent different forms of excessive desire.</note> and show that they express excessive desires; for the Hydra can easily be related to deceptions and frauds. Indeed, the Hydra, as Plato testifies, was a most clever sophist <note>A "sophist" was a teacher of rhetoric often accused of using clever but fallacious arguments to deceive.</note>. Since there are two ways of investigating the truth—one through true reasons and the other through sophisticated ones—we equate the Hydra with those quibbling and deceptive arguments. When one of its heads is cut off, many more are born again; for when one argument is refuted, the sophist subjects many more people to his clever tricks. However, Hercules extinguishes this monster with fire—that is, with the fervor of the intellect. 

Nor is there any reason you should deny that this, too, should be numbered among the monsters. For just as true logic has always been highly approved by all the most learned men, so have the weightiest men always detested this deceptive kind. One may see that the Chimaera refers to anger, and the Gorgons to the allurements of pleasures. Fools are said to be turned into stone by the Gorgons for this reason: because they are utterly paralyzed by them. The prudent, however, easily slay them with the shield of Pallas <note>Athena/Minerva, goddess of wisdom.</note> and the sword of Mercury <note>The god of eloquence and reason.</note>. 

As for Briareus and the others who waged war against the gods, Cicero interprets the fable most correctly: it is nothing other than resisting nature when she gives good counsel. Geryon <note>A three-bodied giant slain by Hercules.</note>, moreover, if you interpret the Greek name, expresses "strife of the earth" <note>original: "terræ litem." The author suggests a Greek etymology from "gē" (earth) and "eris" (strife).</note>. This strife is the eternal war of the earth—that is, the body—against the spirit. Geryon, therefore, represents the more effeminate part of the soul proceeding from the senses, which, in a corrupted man, rules over the entire soul. Because there are three parts of the soul, they fable that he ruled over three islands. His dog has two heads for this reason: because he suffers from both desire and fear simultaneously. 

Aeneas, terrified by these monsters, prepared to use force. But the Sibyl, restraining the man, showed him that all those things were empty images. She commands that they are not to be overcome by force, but understood by reason; once understood, they must be fled. After these monsters, one comes to Acheron and Cocytus <note>The rivers of the Greek Underworld.</note>. Although these rivers were discussed a little earlier, let us follow them with another explanation. Water flows from our "concupiscence" <note>Earthly or carnal desire.</note> as if from a fountain...

<summary>This page provides a moral and psychological allegory of the monsters at the gates of Virgil's Underworld, interpreting them as symbols of sophistry, tyranny, and the conflict between the body and the spirit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Virgil’s Aeneid, Book VI, moving from the monsters at the entrance of the Underworld to the specific symbolism of the infernal rivers and the figure of Charon, interpreted through the lens of moral psychology and the struggle between reason and desire.</meta>

# Book Four

...which makes the Stygian marsh. Thought does not first come from lust, then the action by which we sin; after these, Acheron is a swifter river, for through it is expressed the movement toward shameful acts. This, however, is stirred up after the thought. Nor is it without reason that Seneca says it is carried with a huge tumult; for the soul cannot be carried into a crime without the noise of a struggling conscience. Moreover, since by this kind of deliberation on sinning the will passes into vice, they place a small boat and a sailor in this river. After this kind of passage—that is, after the sin—sorrow follows, which the Styx itself represents; finally, a greater lamentation, which is Cocytus. So that this (if I may call it so) gradation may be placed before your eyes: in the first place is the movement of conscience; in the second, the deliberation of undertaking a crime; after this, sorrow; and finally, greater lamentation. Therefore, the Styx signifies the first and the third; Acheron the second; Cocytus the fourth.

These things please me exceedingly, said **LAURENTIUS**. Nor does it offend me that you transfer the same rivers not to one, but to several interpretations. For we see even the most serious points in our theology interpreted in various ways by the most learned men.

You have, then, the interpretation of the rivers, said **BAPTISTA**. Now I think we must consider what Charon means for himself. For "Charon, the ferryman, guards these horrendous waters and rivers in terrible squalor: on whose chin a mass of unkempt gray hair lies." <note>Quoting Virgil, *Aeneid* VI, 298-300.</note> But so that the matter may proceed in its proper order, we will interpret not only the sailor, but also the ship at the same time. Let the ship, therefore, be the will; let the sailor be the judgment of free will <term>liberum arbitrium: the human capacity to choose between different courses of action</term>. Again, the ship is directed on its course by a pole. This expresses choice; for by that very choice, free will directs the will. Furthermore, it will not be absurd to understand the celestial inclinations through the sails; for toward what the heavens incline, we choose that thing willingly—unless reason opposes them, the power of which is so great that it even dominates the stars. <note>This refers to the common Renaissance maxim "Sapiens dominabitur astris," suggesting that while the stars/astrology might incline a person toward a behavior, reason allows a person to overcome it.</note>

These things you say are very pleasing, said **LAURENTIUS**. For I see that you retain the Christian dogma, yet in such a way that you do not entirely mock the mathematicians. <note>original: "mathematicos"; in this context, it refers to astrologers who calculate the movements and "inclinations" of the celestial bodies.</note> But continue, I pray.

Charon is an old man, said **BAPTISTA**, because the soul—if not in time, as the Platonists <note>Followers of Plato who believed the soul existed before the body.</note> (whom the poet follows) believe—is prior to the body at least in dignity and origin. Add that it is eternal; and nothing is more ancient than eternity. Wherefore, free will in those eternal things is also eternal. But "the god's old age is fresh and green," <note>Quoting Virgil, *Aeneid* VI, 304, describing Charon's divine vitality despite his aged appearance.</note> because it never fails. He is, however, in "terrible squalor," and a "filthy garment hangs from his shoulders." All these things, if I am not mistaken, are most rightly referred to the body; for the body is like a garment for the soul, which is constantly changed, grows old, and finally wastes away. He adds a double flame <note>Referring to Virgil's description of Charon's eyes as "standing out in flame."</note> because it belongs to free will to be bent toward whichever it wishes: both to the brightness of reason and to the heat of desires.

Moreover, it is not rashly nor without a certain most exact reason that Charon is the son of Erebus <term>Erebus: in Greek mythology, the personification of deep darkness or shadow</term> and Night. For if the night within us were to cease—which is nothing else but the very darkness that comes from ignorance—there would be no need for deliberation; for the mind would clearly know the highest good and would be carried toward it without any hesitation. For we never "choose" necessary things, and once doubt is removed, all deliberation ceases. For this reason, those who are already in the third class of virtues—which they call "purgative" <note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, these are the virtues of a soul that is actively purifying itself of worldly attachments to reach a state of divine contemplation.</note>—do not use prudence in the selection of things, but they know nothing except those things which are true goods, and they behold those alone. Erebus, therefore (which word derives its origin from "obscurity" in Greek), opposes himself to reason in such a way that there is a need for deliberation. Since truly (as I was just saying) no one concerning necessary things [deliberates]...

<summary>This page provides an allegorical reading of the rivers of the Underworld and the ferryman Charon, interpreting them as stages of moral failure and the mechanics of the human will. Charon represents the eternal soul's capacity for choice, operating between the "squalor" of the physical body and the "light" of reason.</summary>

This page transitions to the figure of Charon and the boat, interpreting them as symbols of Free Will and the process of intellectual inquiry or contemplation.</meta>

# On the Allegories of P. Virgil M.

...it was necessary for this power to be endowed with such liberty: that it decides according to its own judgment whether to act as one from many, or as one alone. If this is so, then **Charon** is named from "grace" <note>The author is likely drawing on a Greek etymology, linking the name Charon to *charis*, meaning grace or joy.</note>. For nothing is more free than grace, since it comes forth of its own accord and is not owed to anyone’s merit. For this reason, it refuses to be compelled in any way; and for that cause, when he sees **Aeneas** coming through the silent grove, he is the first to speak: *Whoever you are, armed, who come to our thresholds, tell me now why you come from there, and check your step.* <note>Original: "Quisquis es armatus qui nostra ad limina tedis / Fare age quid uenias isthinc & comprime gressum." *Aeneid* VI, 388-389.</note> For when <term>Free Will</term> <note>original: *liberum arbitrium*</note> sees even Reason coming toward it, it does not wish to admit it until it has examined more diligently what must be done.

Therefore he adds: *Truly, I did not rejoice to receive Hercules as he went across the lake* <note>original: "Nec uero alcidem me sum lætatus euntem accepisse lacu." Hercules is referred to here by his patronymic, "Alcides."</note> because <term>Free Will</term> does not wish to be dragged even toward virtue. Instead, it deliberates beforehand, and after deliberation, it applies a choice. Observing this very thing, the **Sibyl** immediately adds: *There are no ambushes here, nor do these weapons bring violence.* <note>original: "Nullæ hic insidiæ nec uim tela ferunt." *Aeneid* VI, 399.</note> And so that it may appear that he is not compelled, but persuaded through deliberation, she shows the **Golden Bough**. He, however, turns gladly toward the wisdom he has seen; and so much for his nature.

The ship, moreover, is made of a dark blue <note>original: *caeruleo*</note> color. Now, that color is composed of white and black. For the one who <term>contemplates</term> <note>original: *contemplator*</note> moves between ignorance and knowledge. For no one is moved to investigate unless they see something; on the other hand, when they have seen everything in that matter, they cease their observation. For nearly the same reason, the boat is fashioned as "flimsy" <note>original: *futilis*</note>; for it joins things to be perceived to those already perceived. Furthermore, it is flimsy and "leaky" <note>original: *rimosa*</note>. For before a perfect knowledge of things is obtained, the series of things will not be so continuous as to leave no gap between them. As for the souls whom he drives out of the ship so that he may receive **Aeneas**, I believe these are to be interpreted as all the passions of the soul that oppose reason.

But perhaps you will think this kind of interpretation is too meticulous and too foolish, since I follow even the smallest details so minutely.

Do you call yourself meticulous, or call those things "details," said **LAURENTIUS**, which, even if they had to be drawn out by great ingenuity, are nonetheless worthy of your labor? Now, since they offer themselves freely, who would reject them? Therefore, do continue; and do not skip those things that fit our discussion. And first of all, open to us what **Cerberus** signifies. For as to why the boat groaned, and why, being leaky, it took in the muddy marsh—unless you feel otherwise—I take it this way: in the one, he wished to express the difficulty of <term>contemplation</term> <note>original: *speculationis*</note>, and in the other, the allurements of earthly pleasures which flow in secretly while we are observing vices.

I see, by the immortal God, that there is in you, O **LAURENTIUS**, a ready wit and one versatile in all things, said **BAPTISTA**. I confess I could not have interpreted those things more aptly myself. As for **Cerberus**, about whom you desire to hear, I shall come to him a little later. In the meantime, let us run through a few things that were omitted. All the souls flock to the ferryman; the first are eager to be carried across the stream, and they stretch out their hands in love for the farther shore. This gathering, then, signifies this, as I think: we all desire by nature to know; but nature does not admit everyone, because the <term>Free Will</term> of the mind does not admit everyone to <term>contemplation</term>. For as to why the souls of the unburied wander for a hundred years, both **Servius** and **Seneca** affirm this was drawn from the custom of the Egyptians. **Orpheus** then transferred this matter to the Underworld. It is indeed very fitting that **Palinurus** is severely rebuked by the **Sibyl**; for it is a sin to bring that desire to the investigation of truth which is rebellious against reason. But I return to **Aeneas**, whom...

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of Charon as Free Will, the Sibyl’s Golden Bough as Wisdom, and the leaky boat as the imperfect process of human knowledge. The dialogue between Laurentius and Baptista explores how the soul must set aside passions and handle the difficulties of intellectual and moral inquiry.</summary>

# Book Four

At last, Charon leads him safely across to the other bank. For [Aeneas] himself, after a long-lasting struggle between <term>Reason</term> and <term>Appetite</term>, is led across into the state of <term>Speculation</term> <note>In this context, "speculation" refers to the highest form of intellectual contemplation or philosophical vision.</note>. In this place, he feels a new war being stirred up against him. For *huge Cerberus makes these kingdoms resound with his three-throated barking, reclining massive in the cave opposite.* <note>Original: "Cerberus hæc ingens latratu regna trifauci personat aduerso recubans immanis in antro." *Aeneid* VI, 417-418.</note> But observe with how little effort everything is pacified by the **Sibyl**: she offers a morsel <note>original: "offam". Often described as a honey-cake drugged with herbs.</note> to the barking dog, and once it is swallowed, he falls into a deep sleep. Thus, Aeneas seizes the entrance once the guard is buried in slumber.

Perhaps the poets handed down the tradition that **Cerberus** has three heads for this reason: they interpret him as the "Earth," which is divided into three parts. In Greek, the word for earth is *ge*, which sounds like *kerean* <note>The author is likely playing on the Greek *ge* (earth) and *keraizein* (to ravage or destroy) or *ker* (death/fate). He suggests the earth is a "devourer" of bodies.</note>. For the earth devours all bodies, since all things eventually fall back into it. If, therefore, Cerberus is the earth, who cannot see that our poet intended to express the needs of our body through the barking of Cerberus? 

For when we are raised up toward the knowledge of great things, we truly do this: we call the mind away from the senses as much as possible, and we learn how to "die." <note>This refers to the Platonic idea that philosophy is a "practice for death," meaning the separation of the soul from the distractions of the body.</note> Yet, you should not think it necessary for the body to perish from this kind of meditative death; rather, one must take account of it. For the body cries out lest you withdraw its necessities, and it rises up with a "three-throated barking." It requires three things: **food**, **drink**, and **sleep**. Unless we satisfy these needs, the body will make such a clamor that it permits us to meditate on nothing excellent. 

For this reason, the care of the body must by no means be neglected; yet it must be refreshed simply, modestly, and entirely soberly, so that it may be able to endure labors, yet not be made rebellious against the soul through excessive luxury. Nature is content with few things, and these are of such a kind that they are easily obtained without great labor or expense. 

Lest you think I am calling you back to those things with which a **Cynic** might be content—for whom raw vegetables seasoned without even salt might provide a most delicious feast—listen to what **Epicurus**, the patron of pleasures, agreed upon. Did he not think he was defended from the cold by the cheapest rag just as well as by a purple or scarlet garment? Did he think thirst could not be quenched unless by wine from Chios or Crete? Or that hunger could only be settled by the most exquisite and royal banquets? 

**Epicurus**, I say, who placed the "highest good" in the pleasure of the body, sought no other seasoning for his dinner besides hunger and thirst. We even read that he was accustomed to add almost nothing to his bread except for a little cheese. As for beccaficos <note>Small songbirds eaten as a delicacy.</note>, peacocks, and those other outrages which **Macrobius** detested in the priestly banquets of his own time—and which we in our own time cannot remember without the greatest indignation and groaning when looking at the feasts of Roman prelates—Epicurus dreaded them like the most deadly monsters. 

In this matter, my dear **LAURENTIUS**, I think you are worthy of the highest praise, just as you are in the other parts of temperance. For besides the fact that for many years now you have not drunk wine in order to avoid the pains of the joints and limbs <note>A reference to Lorenzo de' Medici’s struggle with gout, a common ailment in his family.</note>, can it not be considered a miracle that you—amidst such an abundance of all things, such luxury in our city, and such frequent and lavish banquets in your own home due to constant hospitality and the frequent gatherings of your associates—take nothing for your own nourishment except simple and common food?

As I think on these things, there also come to my memory those things which I not only heard regarding **Federico, Prince of Urbino**, but which I also very often saw for myself on account of our long-standing guest-friendship and old bond. In this Duke, virtues of such magnitude shine forth (even if many others do as well) that, just as the lesser stars vanish before the rays of the sun, so these other virtues are overwhelmed by the brilliance of his greatness. Yet who would not be stunned...

<summary>This page interprets Cerberus as an allegory for the body's three basic needs (food, drink, sleep) and argues that while the philosopher must satisfy these to achieve contemplation, they must do so with extreme temperance. The author praises Lorenzo de' Medici and Federico da Montefeltro for their personal frugality despite their immense wealth and social obligations.</summary>
