<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Book VI of the Aeneid. Having described Cerberus as the "devourer" or the physical needs of the body, the text now discusses how these needs must be managed through temperance to allow for intellectual and spiritual growth.</meta>

### On the Allegory of P. Virgilius Maro

...the man's restraint and sobriety: I will not speak of military camps where life is usually harsher, and where the lack of resources often allows no distinction between the highest general and the lowest <term>sutlers</term> <note>original: "lyxas & calones"; camp followers and servants who performed menial tasks for the Roman army.</note> and camp servants; but also at home and in the court, where all the luxuries suited to his fortune present themselves, and where neither generosity nor magnificence is ever lacking. Yet, when he dines, no place is left for a <term>Pantalabus</term> or a <term>Nomentanus</term> <note>These are characters from the satires of the Roman poet Horace, used here as archetypes for gluttons and spendthrifts.</note>, but rather for both the philosopher and the orator. 

For he himself, from his earliest years, was most sparing with wine; by gradually reducing its use, he progressed so far that he has now given it up for a long time. There is no one who eats more common meals, no one who eats simpler ones; while the body's strength is restored by these, the soul is meanwhile nourished by the sweetest disputations of learned men. Thus it happens very often that when I turn to him each year to escape the Roman autumns for the sake of health and leisure, I seem to myself to have fallen from the feasts of <term>Sardanapalus</term> <note>An ancient Assyrian king who was a legendary symbol of extreme luxury and decadence.</note> into the banquet of <term>Alcinous</term> <note>The king of the Phaeacians in the *Odyssey*, known for his noble and generous hospitality.</note>, and to have arrived at the home of some Socratic host. In him, the vestiges of ancient restraint delight me as much as the luxury and decadence of our own men—who profess the doctrine of all holiness with their red and black hats and snowy veils—disturb me. 

Indeed, I am ashamed, my **LAURENTIUS**, and I am weary of our customs when I see the way open daily to those men into the court of the entire Christian republic—in which I would not deny that there are some excellent fathers of untainted religion and great learning worthy of such a great senate—whom I would only judge to rightfully hold the senatorial rank in Rome if <term>Heliogabalus</term> <note>A Roman emperor infamous for his extreme depravity and disregard for tradition.</note> were brought back from the underworld to rule again. But since I remember having lamented these things elsewhere, let us deal with what is at hand.

And let us understand that our nature is content with very little. When **Maro** <note>The family name of the poet Virgil.</note> wished to express this, he introduced the **Sibyl** (who is True Doctrine) offering a morsel <note>original: "offam".</note>—in which there is both dry and moist nourishment together—to **Cerberus**, by which he easily settles both hunger and thirst and falls into sleep. This is surely a golden precept. For he who indulges the body with the most refined feasts, or seeks out various wines, makes his body rebellious through that very gluttony and drunkenness, and so blunts the edge of the soul that it can look up to nothing high. 

"Those things you say are indeed excellent," said **LAURENTIUS**. "But I see that not all poets feel the same about Cerberus. For **Maro** introduces him as a barking dog that opposes those wanting to enter, not those wanting to leave; I understand that the reason for this was excellently explained by you. For this kind of bodily need does not bark at those who return to care for the body, but at those who, having neglected it, turn toward the knowledge of truth. Therefore, as I said, I seem to clearly grasp what **Maro** intends for himself. 

But since I have read in the most noble poet **Hesiod** (as it does not escape you) that Cerberus fawns with his tail and ears upon those arriving, but allows no one to leave—for, lurking in ambush, whomever he finds outside the gate, he immediately tears with his bite—I do not understand how these accounts are not entirely contradictory, unless perhaps **Hesiod** wished to express one descent to the underworld and **Maro** another." 

"You are indeed ingenious," said **BAPTISTA**. "For **Aeneas** descends to the underworld because he strives for the knowledge of vices. If that is the case, Cerberus will oppose him as he enters, for the body hates these things. But imagine another person seeking the underworld not to know the nature of evil, but to fall into the vices themselves; Cerberus will fawn on him with ears and tail as he desires to enter. For what else do ruined men strive for, what else do they attempt, except to not only satisfy the needs of the three-headed beast, but to fulfill all its pleasures? But if those same men should ever wish to leave a ruined life—for that is what it means to 'exit' the underworld..."

<summary>The text uses Cerberus as an allegory for bodily needs, arguing that physical temperance is necessary for intellectual pursuits while criticizing the decadence of the contemporary clergy. It resolves a contradiction between Virgil and Hesiod by suggesting Cerberus barks at those seeking truth but welcomes those seeking vice.</summary>
<keywords>temperance, sobriety, Cerberus, Virgil, Hesiod, Aeneid, allegory, philosophy vs vice, Medici circle, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Book VI of Virgil's Aeneid. Following the discussion of Cerberus as a symbol of bodily appetite, the author now transitions into Aeneas’s psychological and spiritual journey through the Underworld, specifically focusing on the liberation from the fear of death and the transition from vice to the contemplation of the divine.</meta>

# Book Four
->o ii<-

...they hold us; then the dog barks, then he bites. You were right, then, to doubt; but even more right in how you resolved that doubt. But to return to <term>Maro’s</term> <note>original: "Maronis"; refers to Publius Virgilius Maro, the poet Virgil.</note> Cerberus, he was easily appeased by the simplest of offerings. Aeneas, however, quickly escapes the riverbank; for these matters must not be approached slowly or with such sluggishness, but must be pursued with all one's effort and fire. 

Since the order in such matters is that vices must first be recognized; once recognized, they must then be shunned; so that finally, having been purified of them, we might be made fit observers of divine things, in which the highest good consists. The course of his entire human life had to be revisited in his mind, so that he might clearly understand not only the magnitude of the crime committed by those who—neglecting their own nobler part—place all their hope in a single body and in those pleasures which arise from the body. 

Indeed, he had to see the great misery by which they are overwhelmed. For, having been completely stripped of those weapons of virtue—the only means by which they could have emerged as victors—they expose themselves naked to the cruelest blows of fortune. To say nothing of other adversities which occur daily in countless numbers, they dread death itself, which hangs over us at every moment of every hour, as if it were the greatest of all evils. This fear alone, even if you are afflicted by no other disturbance, never allows us to catch our breath. 

For this reason, the poet shows that death often seeks out infants themselves at the very threshold of life; he teaches that it is brought by violence at the very springs <note>The author is referring to the "Mourning Fields" in Aeneid Book VI, where those who died in infancy or by suicide are found.</note>; and he demonstrates that it comes to those who are so weak in spirit that they cannot bear up under certain heavy disturbances. Observing all these things diligently, Aeneas finally decides that a wise man’s primary duty is to remain free of blame, and to count death itself among the gifts of nature—since from it no evil comes to us (that is, to our souls), but on the contrary, the highest good, because being released from such a <term>foul prison</term> <note>original: "tetro carcere"; a common Platonic metaphor for the physical body.</note>, we return to our true nature.

By this reasoning, we are forced to admit that those who were snatched from such miseries before they could fall into them were treated kindly and indulgently; and that the gift bestowed by Juno upon <term>Cleobis and Biton</term> <note>In Greek mythology, these two brothers were granted the "best gift for a mortal"—a peaceful death in their sleep—after performing an act of great devotion to their mother and the goddess Juno.</note> was entirely divine. 

Furthermore, we shall consider the <term>Trausi</term> <note>original: "trauſos"; a Thracian tribe mentioned by the historian Herodotus, known for their custom of mourning births and celebrating deaths.</note> to be wiser than all other nations and peoples; for these are a people in <term>Thracia</term> who greet a birth with many tears and lamentations, counting out how many evils will happen to them in life. Yet they follow a death with every kind of joy, reflecting on how many sorrows and various heavy accidents of fortune they have been freed from by death. 

Through these sorts of reasons, Aeneas gradually lays aside the fear of death. Indeed, if he sees himself or any good man targeted by punishment or death through the greatest injustice, he will not say—as foolishly as that <term>Xanthippe</term> <note>The wife of the philosopher Socrates. According to tradition, she wept that her husband was dying "innocently," to which he replied, "Would you have me die guilty?"</note> did regarding Socrates—that "at least it happened to one who did not deserve it." Rather, he affirms what the wise know to be true but the foolish deny: that no one can be defiled by anyone except himself. Nor will he count anything that lacks moral shame among evils. 

Moreover, through <term>Socratic reasoning</term>, he will demonstrate that whoever acts unjustly or cruelly toward another does not injure the victim, but himself. However, he will judge those to be pursued with all disdain who have so weakened the immortal soul—which is by its own nature robust enough to despise all human things—through their own foolishness and corrupted it by wicked habit, that, inflamed by "womanly" love <note>A reference to Dido’s suicide in Book IV of the Aeneid, viewed here through a Stoic lens as a failure of the soul.</note>, they gradually collapsed into such madness that they laid hands upon themselves. Thus, they attempted to escape the evil that pressed upon them through death—which they mistakenly believed to be the greatest evil. In this matter, he first condemns their cowardice and laziness, because by their own fault...

<summary>This page presents a Stoic and Neoplatonic interpretation of Aeneas’s descent into the Underworld, framing it as a philosophical journey where Aeneas learns that death is a natural gift and liberation from the "prison" of the body rather than an evil. The text uses the examples of Socrates and the Trausi people to illustrate that the only true harm is moral vice, not physical death or suffering.</summary>

Having discussed the distraction of bodily appetites (Cerberus), the author now explores the philosophical causes of human suffering, the "branching path" of moral choice, and the symbolic architecture of Tartarus.</meta>

### Allegory on P. Virgilius Maro
->On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro<-

...they fall into a dreadful love <note>original: "dirum amorem"; the OCR reads "dinofum," likely a transcription error for "dirum," referring to destructive passion</note> which Plato affirms is born from human diseases. This is because they are affected only by those things which are devoted to the belly and to sleep; and having utterly forgotten their own divinity—which they allowed to be overwhelmed by the thick shadows of error—they look upon nothing except the fleeting form of the body, which is destined to perish quickly through either disease or old age. For this reason, they sin twice. First, it is from their own lazy idleness and lustful decadence that they fall into a state full of anxiety. Then, when they can no longer endure the disease contracted by their own fault, they bind themselves with the highest impiety; for though they were sent into the body by the supreme God as if into a prison <note>original: "custodiam"; the Platonic idea of the body as a "guard-house" or "jail" for the soul</note>, they desert it without His command. 

He observes after this the extreme madness of those men who, while they could spend their lives in tranquil and secure leisure through the highest justice, nevertheless preferred to disturb the peace through the greatest injury and impiety, throwing all things into confusion. For either they themselves, affected by no injury, were driven by greed or ambition to provoke those deserving no such thing with sword, fire, and fraud; or, having been provoked themselves, they abandoned legal debate—which is proper to man—and turned to force, which belongs to wild beasts. From this we see the human race, which could have lived in the greatest peace through concord, being constantly thrown into turmoil. From this we observe the devastation of many regions and the slaughter of infinite mortals to arise. Thus, when we gather all the calamities that happen to us from every side, no plague is found to be more bitter to man than man himself. 

You see, therefore, with what exact wisdom all these things are set forth through poetic fictions. Since he [Virgil] noticed that things of this kind occur both through criminal intent and sometimes without actual vice, he decided that there should be one simple, common path. However, when <term>Deiphobus</term> <note>In the Aeneid, Deiphobus is a Trojan hero whose mutilated ghost represents the horrors and passions of war.</note> has been left behind—because those things now offer themselves for contemplation which are either utterly shameful or, being far from all crime, contain only virtue—it is necessary that the path now be twofold: so that one turns to the left toward vice, while the other reaches toward the right toward virtue. 

I would believe, then, said **LORENZO** <note>original: "LAVRENTIVS"; referring to Lorenzo de' Medici, a participant in this philosophical dialogue.</note>, that the famous Pythagoras wished to express this same thing when he invented the letter **Y**. This did not escape the poet Persius, who wrote: *“And error, ignorant of life, led trembling minds into the branching crossroads.”* <note>original: "Et vitæ nescius error Deduxit trepidas ramosa incompita mentes."</note> 

**BATTISTA** <note>original: "BAPTISTA"; referring to Leon Battista Alberti, the famous polymath and architect.</note> said, “That is exactly the point. But let us see what follows.” 

*Aeneas sees broad walls under a cliff on the left, surrounded by a triple wall.* 

This is indeed a wonderful description of <term>Tartarus</term> <note>The deep abyss in Roman and Greek mythology used as a dungeon of torment for the wicked.</note>. For the place must now be described in which various crimes are punished. The Greeks call this Tartarus from the word *tarattein*, which means “to disturb.” For vices arise from disturbances, and these same disturbances always hold the troubled mind of the sinner. Furthermore, they are enclosed by a triple wall because we do not sin in one simple way, but in a triple way. First, we conceive a crime through a certain movement of the soul alone, arising from a depraved will. Second, in the next stage, the act itself is added. Finally, when the act is repeated again and again many times, it forms a habit. Therefore, the poet rightly expressed these three things in Tartarus, which the prophet of sacred songs <note>Referring to King David, the traditional author of the Psalms.</note> testifies are far from the blessed man. For he begins his work at the very start in this way:

> Blessed is the man who does not walk in the counsel of the 
> ungodly.

You see here the first movement of the soul toward crime. Then: *“And has not stood in the way of sinners.”* For what else is a "way" if not the action itself? When an action is repeated often, it is no longer just a movement; it establishes a seat for itself and resides there as a firmly established habit. Rightly, therefore, he adds:

> and has not sat in the seat of pestilence.

Furthermore, when he says that the river <term>Phlegethon</term> <note>The "river of fire" in the underworld.</note> surrounds Tartarus with its flaming stream, he spoke with no small reason. For it shows the fire of wrath and the heat of the soul by which that kind of man is constantly scorched.

<meta>catchword: rem</meta>

<summary>This page provides an allegorical reading of Aeneas’s arrival at the crossroads of the Underworld, interpreting the "triple wall" of Tartarus as the three stages of sin—will, act, and habit—and linking Virgil's poetry to the Pythagorean 'Y' and the first Psalm.</summary>
<keywords>Virgil, Aeneid, Tartarus, Pythagoras, Lorenzo de' Medici, Leon Battista Alberti, Phlegethon, Psalm 1, Allegory, Three Stages of Sin, Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Book VI of Virgil's Aeneid. Following the description of the entrance to the underworld, the author moves into a philosophical discussion of Tartarus—the place of punishment—interpreting its physical features as symbols for the psychological state of the "lost" soul and the mechanics of habitual sin.</meta>

# Book Four

For so great is the hatred of vices that even those who once took pleasure in them are eventually led to repentance, condemning their past life and hating it vehemently; indeed, they are most bitterly angry with themselves. For you find at last that your "thick days and marshy light" have passed. <note>original: "crassos transisse dies lucemque palustrem"; a quote from the Roman satirist Persius (Satire 5.60), referring to a life spent in the "fog" of ignorance or spiritual darkness.</note> Yet, held captive by a single habit, they are dragged along against their will and consumed by anger and fury.

> Therefore the Tartarean Phlegethon surrounds everything with rapid, burning torrents. <note>In Greek mythology, the Phlegethon is the river of fire. Here, it symbolizes the burning remorse or agitation of the sinful mind.</note>

For no furnace, no smith’s workshop, seethes more than the mind of the wicked. The stones tossed and turned by a river show how heavy and distressing such movements and agitations are. To this, the poet adds a gate fortified with columns of solid adamant <note>a legendary stone of impenetrable hardness</note>; he makes the place so secure that neither the strength of men nor of the gods who dwell in heaven can break through. What, then, does this most learned man mean? Surely this, as I see it: that when shameful and ruined men descend into Tartarus—that is, when they have become slaves to crimes through long-ingrained habit—they cannot be rescued by any warnings of men, any divine commands, or finally, any mercy from the stars. For this reason, such men are rightly called "lost" <note>original: "perditos"</note> by the Latins and "profligate" <note>original: "asotos"; in Greek, this term implies someone who is "beyond saving" or "wasteful" of their own life.</note> by the Greeks. In these men, says **LAURENTIUS**, the free judgment of the mind will be lost, so that even if they wish to return to virtue, they cannot.

I see the sharpness of your talent in this, says **BAPTISTA**. For with that brief little question, you have stirred up everything that the most serious philosophers usually ask regarding the <term>voluntary</term> <note>actions taken by choice and with knowledge</note> and the <term>involuntary</term> <note>actions taken under compulsion or through ignorance</note>. In this matter, I not only praise your wit but also strongly approve of your judgment. For although many things offer themselves which you could solve on your own without anyone's help, you prefer to have them spoken by another, so that you may receive praise both for your modesty (since you claim nothing for yourself) and for your intelligence (since you question so prudently). 

However, this point can be easily addressed if we say that the poet did not wish to demonstrate an unavoidable necessity, but rather a difficulty that is very close to impossibility. But even if we assume (if it pleases you) that all power of escaping is taken away, we still will not say that the crimes they commit are <term>involuntary</term>, since their beginning was <term>voluntary</term>. For the person lacking self-control knows they are sinning when they commit adultery, and they can abstain if they wish. Therefore, they sin willingly until, through constant depraved actions of this kind, they reach a point where—having now contracted the disease of intemperance—they cannot abstain even if they wish to. Yet they will not be said to have sinned against their will, even if they are unwilling at that moment, because it was permitted from the beginning; they only had to wish not to fall into that firm habit of intemperance. 

For this reason, a person will be said to have sinned "unwillingly" no more than one who throws a stone at someone of their own accord, and then, led by repentance, would call the stone back if they could as it flies through the air because they no longer wish to strike the person. Therefore, they strike while willing it, because the beginning came from their own will. But you will find these matters discussed more extensively in Aristotle’s book on ethics. <note>original: "libro de moribus"; a reference to the Nicomachean Ethics, Book III, where Aristotle discusses the responsibility for one's character and habits.</note>

And so I return to Aeneas, who, as you see, does not enter the city itself. For those who study vices do not live among them. He understands their power and nature deeply through the Sibyl (for doctrine teaches all things). Nevertheless, he sees Tisiphone <note>one of the Furies, whose name means "avenging murder"</note> at the threshold. The poet places the Furies at the threshold of Tartarus; I will omit most of the things the poets invent about them, as they are well known. However, it is clearly established that it pleased the ancient writers that whoever bound themselves by a greater crime would be harassed by the Furies, as we see in the cases of the mother-killers Orestes and Alcmaeon. In this context, what else do the Furies represent but the restlessness and indeed the torment of a turbulent...

o iii <margin>signature mark</margin>

<summary>The author interprets the geography of Tartarus as an allegory for the psychological imprisonment of habitual sin, arguing that while a habit may become unbreakable, the soul remains responsible because the initial choice was voluntary. The Furies are presented as symbols of the internal torment and restless conscience that follow great crimes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Tartarus from Book VI of Virgil's Aeneid. The previous page ended mid-sentence with "rightly call...", which this text completes as "the innermost part of our mind." The author here shifts from a physical description of the underworld to a psychological one, explaining how the mythological torments represent the internal struggles of the human soul.</meta>

->### On the Allegory of Publius Virgilius Maro<-

<sig>iii o</sig>

[...the most inward part] of our mind, which, through the memory of committed crimes, continually surrenders itself to suffering. For this is the first revenge: that with himself as judge, no guilty man is acquitted. <note>original: "se iudice nemo nocens absoluitur"; a famous line from the Roman satirist Juvenal (Satire 13.2), suggesting that the heaviest punishment for sin is the criminal's own guilty conscience.</note> For though you may escape laws, judgments, disgrace, or infamy—though no one has seen, and no one has heard—there is still conscience herself who can be summoned as a witness. She is the one who urges you, hunts you down, drags you to judgment, gives the heaviest testimony, and finally convicts you. Indeed, to stay with Juvenal: *no guilty man is acquitted by his own judgment.* These, therefore, are those whips; these are the serpent bites with which the Furies drive us.

You have heard regarding the Furies. Now, when the various crimes and shames had to be explained, the poet enumerates from the beginning those which stand out as the gravest of all: impiety toward the gods and pride toward men. For we understand this to mean the Titan offspring, the twin sons of Aloeus <note>Otus and Ephialtes, giants who attempted to reach heaven by piling mountains on top of each other</note>, and Salmoneus—all of whom were despisers of gods and men. 

The more learned interpret the vulture of Tityus—whom I spoke of a little while ago—as the torture of conscience. <note>In myth, the giant Tityus was punished in the underworld by having a vulture eternally feast upon his liver, which grew back as quickly as it was consumed. Here, the author treats the liver as the "seat" of the soul's desires and the vulture as the "gnawing" of regret.</note> For that bird, like a most savage avenger of crimes, continually searches the inner parts <note>original: "intestina"</note> that are liable to shame. While it brings committed acts back to mind, it gnaws at the innermost heart and continually stirs up cares, providing no room for breathing. 

He also intends the Lapiths and Ixion to be examples of tyranny. Above them, a black rock, just about to slip and fall, hangs as if dropping. <note>original: "atra silex iam, iam lapsura cadenti imminet assimilis"; quoting Virgil's description of the terror felt by the damned who live in constant fear of a collapsing ceiling.</note> For such men never live without the expectation of impending evil. Dionysius of Syracuse clearly showed this to his friend Damocles, who thought the tyrant was the most blessed of men. Dionysius placed him among the most lavish banquets and precious ointments, yet Damocles could feel no pleasure due to the fear of a sword hanging from the ceiling above his head by a single horsehair. 

Truly, men know these psychological truths much better than they know how to avoid the pursuit of power, provided only that the opportunity for acquiring it is given. Nor is it difficult to understand what the feasts prepared before their faces in royal luxury mean, or why the greatest of the Furies reclines nearby and forbids their hands from touching the tables. For the poet could not have marked avarice more truly or more clearly, which drags men into such madness that, though they have accumulated much, they prefer to die of hunger rather than diminish their pile. 

Wherefore Horace also beautifully compares them to Tantalus, who, placed in the underworld amidst the greatest abundance of water and fruit, is tortured by thirst and hunger. Beautiful also is this: "You sleep, gaping over bags piled up from everywhere, and you are compelled to spare them as if they were sacred objects, or to enjoy them as if they were merely painted pictures." <note>original: "Congestis undique saccis indormis inhians & tanquam parcere sacris cogeris, pictis tanquam gaudere tabellis"; quoted from Horace, Satires 1.1.70–71.</note> Great indeed is the foolishness of men who do not know that the possession of these things is sought not for itself, but for their use. Therefore, we do not rightly call those things "goods," but rather "useful things." 

But we have said too much, since we have spoken of avarice in many places already; thus let us see the rest. For those roll a massive stone <note>A reference to Sisyphus, though here applied generally to the ambitious</note> whose lives are spent in the greatest misery, always seeking and attempting to achieve things that are set above their nature or their fortune, so that all their efforts are vain and ineffective. And are they not rightly said to hang fastened to the spokes of wheels who—foreseeing nothing with reason and counsel, and explaining nothing with virtue—commit themselves so far to Fortune that they are tossed by her accidents as if among the waves of the Euripus <note>The Euripus Strait in Greece was famous in antiquity for its violent and unpredictable currents that changed direction many times a day.</note>, spinning continually? Nor do they have any virtue into which they might retreat as if into a safe and tranquil harbor. Thus, vices of this kind and others which [more...]

# Book Four

[...the crimes] are various and diverse, as the Sibyl <note>The prophetess who guides Aeneas through the underworld</note> teaches Aeneas. She immediately warns him, now that he is instructed, how he might expiate himself from them, so that he may enter the Elysian Fields <note>The final resting place for the souls of the heroic and virtuous in Greek and Roman mythology</note> in a state of purity. Who, then, would say that Maro <note>original: "Maronem"; a common name for the poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro)</note> departs from the dogma of Plato? For since Plato placed the highest good in the knowledge of divine matters, he believed that anyone who is to be blessed must excel in every kind of virtue. Nevertheless, he shows that one must begin with those virtues that are rooted in daily life and morality. Since no one who is impure can reach divine things—which are most pure and unpolluted by any bodily stain—it is necessary that we be purified through these types of virtues. For through them, we both recognize our vices and, once recognized, we loathe them. Thus made pure, we are able to go forth toward celestial and immortal things. Driven by this reasoning, Maro, wishing to lead man to the highest good, ensures that Aeneas is instructed in this way: first, he teaches him all the vices, and then, once Aeneas is purified of them, he leads him to the Elysian Fields. For once the foulness of vice is recognized, a hatred for it immediately creeps into us; this is truly the very beginning of wisdom. Indeed, the first step toward wisdom is to be free from folly. 

But you, I know not what you are pondering in your mind with such wonder, said **BAPTISTA**.

That very thing, **LORENZO** replied. But see how much I owe you for your discourse. While you are attempting to make Virgil’s meaning clear to me—and you are succeeding—at the same time, you are introducing me to the divine poem of our fellow citizen. For only now do I finally perceive what **Dante** means, who first descends to the underworld, and emerging from there finds no other way to heaven except through the places of Purgatory. 

Bravo for your excellence, young man, said **BAPTISTA**. You not only easily grasp what I say, even if the matters are difficult, but led by a certain similarity, you move from one point to another. Just as Dante achieved the greatest praise from a most diligent imitation full of intellectual craftsmanship, you too deserve no small praise for recognizing all these things in that poet, even though they are quite heavily disguised.

Indeed, said **LORENZO**, you yourself shall judge how much I deserve for this; though you must be careful not to value my talent far more than is proper because of your excessive love for me. Nevertheless, even if it seems irrelevant to our current conversation, I will not omit this thought. I would like you to believe that what I say is spoken for this reason: not to set down my own "decree" in such a great matter, but to draw out your judgment (which I value above the judgment of all others) through my words. 

From almost my earliest childhood, by my parents' instruction, I have made the entire work of the Florentine poet <note>Referring to Dante Alighieri</note> so familiar to myself that there are very few passages in it which I could not easily recite word-for-word if I required that kind of amusement. But what could I, as a boy, perceive from such a divine bard beyond the mere words? Now, however, when I run through the entire argument of the work in my mind, I follow that man’s genius with the greatest admiration. For although in weaving his work he seems to borrow only a few threads from the Virgilian loom, almost everything is drawn from there. For this reason, I finally understand what **Landino** <note>Cristoforo Landino, the author of this text, referring to himself as a teacher</note> is often accustomed to remind us based on the precept of Cicero: that in imitating someone, diligent reason must be applied. For we should not act so as to be the same as those we imitate, but rather be similar to them in such a way that the similarity itself is hardly understood, except by the learned. But please, return to what you began. 

Since, therefore, said **BAPTISTA**...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Leon Battista Alberti (Baptista) and Lorenzo de' Medici (Laurentius) in Landino’s Disputationes Camaldulenses. Following the discussion of purification and the underworld, the text now transitions to the rewards of the virtuous in the Elysian Fields, interpreted as a Platonic metaphor for the celestial spheres.</meta>

### On the Allegory of P. Virgilius Maro <note>The poet Virgil</note>

Now that Aeneas has been expiated of all vices, Maro <note>Virgil</note> is about to lead him to the knowledge of those things that are in the heavens; however, he names them not "heaven," but the Elysian Fields <note>The final resting place of the heroic and virtuous in Virgil's underworld</note>. Indeed, the poet possesses a marvelous genius, for he serves both his own poetic fiction and the truth at the same time. For even if he had relegated the heroes to the underworld in the manner of poets, he nevertheless grants them a more ample atmosphere <note>original: "largiorem ætherem"; a reference to Aeneid 6.640, describing the bright air of Elysium</note> and their own sun and stars, so that we may realize he is thinking of heaven. In this way, while never departing from his fiction, he still pursues the truth of philosophy.

If we consider more carefully which men he places in the heavens, we will notice that he has most acutely embraced all those things that were set forth by us on the first day of this discussion regarding both types of life <note>The "active" and "contemplative" lives, a central theme of Renaissance philosophy</note>. He shows that those who have been religiously occupied in the pursuit of knowledge, as well as those who have lived justly in their actions and civil life, are entirely worthy of returning to heaven as if to their own origin. 

Therefore, he commemorates Orpheus and Musaeus <note>Mythical poets and musicians associated with the founding of religious mysteries</note> and the rest of the priests who remained chaste—those who spoke things worthy of Phoebus <note>Apollo, the god of prophecy and music</note> and were able to reveal the truth to others, and those who made life more refined through the invention of various arts—as if they were watchers or scouts. Nor does he omit those who defended the state <note>original: "rem publicam"</note> either by pious arms or by counsel, labor, industry, and authority, and who were engaged in civil and social life. 

When he notices that souls of this kind—expiated from all bodily contagion, being of a most simple and entirely incorporeal nature, and capable of the greatest things—are circumscribed by no narrowness of place and included within no boundaries of regions, but are seen to wander most freely through all the borders of the world, he introduces Musaeus speaking in such a way as to show that no one has a fixed home. Indeed, when he writes:
> The same joy in chariots and arms they had when living, the same care to pasture sleek horses, follows them now that they are laid in the earth. <note>Virgil, Aeneid VI.653-655</note>

He demonstrates that he is not unmindful of what the divine Plato and the Platonic Cicero felt concerning our souls. For they believe that the administrators of public affairs, when they have been received into heaven, do not set aside their care for ruling men. Nor is it only those who lived justly and piously who are held by the same pursuits once stripped of the body, according to the same author <note>Virgil</note>, which they delighted in while life remained; but even the souls of the shameful, since they carry away with them much of the filth with which they defiled themselves within their bodies, are unable to depart from their former cares. 

You have seen, unless I am mistaken, a journey that is indeed long and full of difficulties and errors, but one by which at last the man who is a lover of virtue attains his long-desired end. For through various misfortunes and through so many perils of circumstances <note>A paraphrase of Aeneas's famous line in Aeneid I.204</note>, Aeneas strives toward Italy and arrives at the quiet seats. If we imitate him, we too—freed from bodily plagues and watered by the shining fountain of the virtues—shall rejoice in that same kind of life while our souls are engaged within these bodies; and when they wish to go forth from there, having returned to our origin, we shall enjoy eternal ages.

When these things had been said by **BAPTISTA** <note>Leon Battista Alberti</note> in such a way that he seemed to have brought the discussion to an end, **LAVRENTIVS** <note>Lorenzo de' Medici</note> said: "I can find nothing lacking in so long a discourse. For from the beginning even to this place, the discussion has been conducted with such a continuous flow that nothing is interrupted, or torn apart, or hurried; nor is there anything left in the middle or omitted that I can see. For all things are linked together in a wonderful series and woven in such an order that nothing can be taken away from them without the remainder being left crippled, and nothing can be added that..."

<summary>Alberti concludes his allegorical interpretation of Virgil's Aeneid, arguing that the Elysian Fields represent the celestial reward for both contemplative thinkers and active statesmen. Lorenzo de' Medici praises the lecture for its seamless logical structure and philosophical depth.</summary>
<keywords>Virgil, Aeneid, Elysian Fields, Plato, Cicero, Contemplative Life, Active Life, Leon Battista Alberti, Lorenzo de' Medici, Soul's Immortality, Allegory</keywords>

Lorenzo de' Medici (Laurentius) is speaking to Leon Battista Alberti (Baptista), pressing him for a deeper explanation of the "Song of Anchises" from Virgil's Aeneid. This section marks a shift from the moral allegory of the underworld to the physical and metaphysical nature of the universe.</meta>

# Book Four

<meta>Catchword: indeed</meta>

Indeed, if it were absent, it would seem greatly to be required. Forgive my excessive desire, however, if I ask for this now: because although I vehemently desire it to be made clear to me, I do not understand it to have been explained by you so far, nor does a place seem to be left in which it might be explained hereafter. For I was waiting with not only a suspended but even an anxious mind to hear what you felt about those things which Anchises, looking upward, reveals in their proper order. But you, while distributing each of the remaining points to their own places during the discussion, removed those matters far from your discourse—not as if they were familiar things unjustly cast out, but as if they were foreign things excluded without any injury. Therefore, I am uncertain what to do: for I neither dare to ask anything further of you, exhausted as you are by long speaking, nor can I with a calm mind lack those things which I desire to know.

At this, **BAPTISTA**, smiling, said: You ought to remember, O Lorenzo, that we circumscribed the entire question within these boundaries: that we would propose for opening those things which were obscured by certain ambiguities and fictions of allegory. You, however, ask for those things which are told in their own words without any fiction. Nevertheless, I will not deal with you by such an exacting rule that I count out only what is owed by agreement; but beyond that, I want something to be added for free in this liberality.

That passage, therefore, which Maro <note>The poet Virgil</note> writes—*In the beginning, the spirit within nourishes the heavens and the earth, the watery plains, the shining globe of the moon, and the Titan stars* <note>original: "Principio caelum ac terras: camposque liquentes: Lucentemque globum lunae titaniaque astra: Spiritus intus alit"; from Aeneid 6.724–726</note>—is of such a kind that it refers to the opinion of the Stoics <note>A school of philosophy that taught that God/Reason is a physical force immanent in the universe</note> concerning the gods. It would be a long task if I were now to relate the opinions of all the ancient philosophers regarding the immortal gods; these are indeed so diverse and so contrary to one another that almost as many are found as there are heads of those who have written. For not only have individual "families" <note>philosophical schools</note> devised individual opinions, but often men of the same sect disagree vehemently among themselves about the matter itself.

But to let the rest go for the present and come to those things which are consistent with the present inquiry: most of the Stoics, but especially their leader Zeno, believed the entire globe of the world possessed a mind, reason, and supreme wisdom, and that this mind was a certain most pure and thin fire. And just as our souls are diffused through all the particles of our body, so that mind penetrates through all the members of the world like a "generative seed" <note>original: "genitale semen"; the Stoic concept of *logos spermatikos*</note> from which all things are procreated; for it is the vigor and seed of all things to be brought forth.

Virgil, therefore, although he almost never departs from his master Plato in other matters, nevertheless, when he had seen that Chrysippus <note>A famous Stoic philosopher</note>—in that book which he wrote about the nature of the gods—interpreted the fables of Orpheus, Musaeus, Hesiod, and Homer in such a way that he tried to show the ancient poets once felt the same thing that the Stoics felt many years later, Virgil decided in this matter not to be thought unlike those poets whom he desired to resemble, and he himself supported the "Porch" <note>The *Stoa*, the painted porch in Athens from which the Stoics took their name</note> and adhered to the Stoics.

For the opinion of Plato is far different. He posits that God is entirely incorporeal and outside of all matter and the whole world, existing on the very "back of heaven." For this reason, he calls Him "hypercosmic" <note>original: "hypercosmion"; Greek for "above the world" or transcendent</note>, since His essence remains above the summit of heaven, though by His power and providence He is never absent, but looking upon all things, He cares even for the smallest. For in the *Phaedrus*, he says: *Great Jupiter, driving a winged chariot in heaven, proceeds first, adorning all things.* And in the same book, he demonstrates that no poet has yet praised that place, nor ever will praise it worthily. Therefore, since the Platonists place God outside the world—with whom the Aristotelians also agree—while the Stoics diffuse Him through the whole world as I have said, who does not see that Virgil...

<summary>Lorenzo asks Baptista to explain Virgil's description of the "spirit within" the universe. Baptista explains that Virgil is temporarily adopting Stoic pantheism (God as a fire within the world) to align with poetic tradition, rather than Plato's view of a transcendent God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Leon Battista Alberti as he explains the philosophical underpinnings of Virgil’s poetry to Lorenzo de' Medici. Alberti is currently contrasting the views of various ancient philosophical schools—Stoics, Epicureans, Peripatetics, and Platonists—regarding divine providence and the fate of the soul.</meta>

### On the Allegories of Publius Virgilius Maro

...[Alberti continues] that he had described that god whom he had seen in the Portico <note>The "Portico" refers to the Stoa, the school of Stoic philosophy. Stoics believed God was an immanent, rational force pervading the universe.</note>: and that we received from Chrysippus <note>A leader of the Stoic school</note> the idea that our souls are little particles of Him. But when he follows the providence of God in many places, he never departs from Plato. For not everyone thinks the same.

You might ask perhaps what Plato thinks about the world. He believes indeed that the world has a soul from which the souls of other living beings come. However, he intends that the minds of men are created by that god I mentioned a little while ago and are adorned with reason. But the body and all the other powers which we know to be in them beyond reason, he writes come to man from the World Soul <note>The *Anima Mundi*, a concept where the universe itself is a living being with a rational soul.</note>. For that soul is the vicar of God, to whom the whole "forest" <note>The Latin *sylua* here translates the Greek *hyle*, meaning raw matter or substance.</note> is entrusted like its own province. Therefore it provides life and motion to it, not by its own power or faculty, but whatever it does, it does as the instrument of God.

So Maro <note>The poet Virgil</note> turns slightly from the path and departs from his Plato. But when he follows the providence of God in most places, he adheres to him entirely. For not everyone thinks the same. For there are those who place everything in the accidents of fortune and believe the world is moved by no ruler. In this opinion you will find Leucippus of Abdera and his fellow citizen Democritus, also Protagoras, and Theodorus, and Epicurus <note>These are Atomist and Skeptic philosophers who generally argued for a universe governed by chance or material laws rather than divine intent.</note>. There are likewise those who, following Aristotle, do not posit God as so idle that they say He cares for nothing at all. Yet they do not believe His providence descends below the sphere of the moon <note>A common medieval and Renaissance interpretation of Aristotle: that God’s direct providence governs the eternal heavens, but earthly "sublunary" events are left to nature.</note>. Finally, there are a third group who, even if they want providence to reach the whole universe, believe it cares only for the greatest things, while entirely neglecting the smallest.

But Plato, just as he thinks all things were made by God, so he believes He cares for all things. And so much for God. As for the cycle by which Virgil writes that our souls pass in turn from the underworld to the body, and from there back to the underworld, we do not deny that this comes from the Academy <note>The school founded by Plato.</note>. But if we had proposed to discuss a matter of this kind more broadly, there are much more divine things which we could bring forward from such a great philosopher about the departure of souls from the body. But it is difficult altogether to include such arduous matters—which require a long and diligent speech to be explained—within these narrow limits. Therefore, let us do what alone is pressing.

You will find therefore among the Platonists that when the souls of men have been in the underworld for a thousand years, they return to bodies, and from there return in turn to the underworld. And they do this until twelve thousand years have passed. For they consider this a "perfect cycle." For they believe that in that space of time souls are thoroughly purged, and therefore they can then, finally purified, return to their origin and to their celestial seats. But if there be someone who gives himself to philosophy, such an easy purgation falls to him that he is allowed to fly away to the world above after three thousand years. They add also that if someone has lived altogether religiously, he is both purged before a thousand years and, once purged, returns immediately to his origin.

There is besides what they call the "Great Year," which they believe is finished when the sun, together with the moon and the five remaining wandering stars <note>The five planets known to the ancients: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>, return to the same part of the zodiac. Once this circuit of time is completed—which, although the opinion of learned men about it varies, most have believed is finished in thirty-six thousand years—Plotinus believes the souls of all men will return to the same state of life.

Therefore, you will easily perceive what these things are and how much they are to be valued from that book which our friend **MARSILIO** <note>Marsilio Ficino, the leading Platonic philosopher of the Medici circle and a close friend of Landino.</note> has here in his hands, not yet polished nor yet published. But when I had stayed with him at his villa in Figline, happening upon it by chance, I opened it and ran through certain passages with the greatest pleasure. The matter is altogether great, **LORENZO**, and handled with great powers of intellect, and utterly worthy of your labors. For it will be able to provide not only the greatest and most beautiful things to a man desiring to know himself...

<summary>Alberti compares different philosophical views on divine providence (Stoic, Epicurean, Aristotelian, and Platonic) and explains the Platonic theory of metempsychosis, the "Great Year," and the 12,000-year cycle of the soul. He references a yet-unpublished work by Marsilio Ficino on these subjects.</summary>
<keywords>Providence, World Soul, Plato, Epicurus, Aristotle, Metempsychosis, Great Year, Marsilio Ficino, Lorenzo de' Medici, purgation of souls</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Book Four of Cristoforo Landino’s "Camaldolese Disputations." Leon Battista Alberti (referred to here as Baptista) is finishing his lengthy allegorical interpretation of Virgil’s *Aeneid*, transitioning from the philosophical secrets of the poem to a concluding dialogue with the gathered Florentine noblemen.</meta>

# Book Four

...to teach you necessary things, but also to sweep you into the highest admiration. For he writes not physically <note>In the sense of "natural philosophy" or physics, dealing with the material world.</note> (as most are accustomed) but **metaphysically** about the immortality of our souls, so that he can clearly remove all doubt concerning that matter. When you read that book, you will easily recognize both these things you ask regarding Maro <note>The poet Virgil, whose full name was Publius Virgilius Maro.</note> and many other things which we did not lie about being most divine a little while ago. This fact makes me perhaps briefer than you would have wished in the things you requested. Yet there is no reason to be angry at my brevity. For since you required things which were included in no boundaries of the discussion we had pledged, I could by my own right have passed over them in silence. Therefore, I will easily wash away that charge if it is accidentally objected to.

But among you, most learned men, I do not find how I may justify myself. For I see that while I deny nothing to the requesting **LORENZO**, I have fallen into the fault of a double error. For what could be imagined more talkative than me, who for four days now does not cease to instill into your most learned ears those things which, because of your own learning, are better known to you than to me? Or what could be thought of as more audacious than me, who have come unprepared to discuss those things which even by the most learned and long-prepared men are hardly explained elegantly enough for their dignity?

"On the contrary," **Alamanus** <note>Alamanus Rinuccini, a prominent Florentine humanist.</note> replied, "what could be more humane, what more worthy of your easy grace, than to discuss those things for us while we are at leisure—things which we would not hesitate to prefer over even great and vehemently urgent occupations?"

"But we," said **Piero Acciaiuoli**, "for I wish to respond for my brother as well, would not have even dared to hope for what fortune, smiling upon us of its own accord, has brought: that you, a man endowed with such wisdom and eloquence, should briefly, clearly, and absolutely gather into one place during these first two days those things concerning the double question <note>Refers to the two main topics of the first two books: the active vs. contemplative life and the "Summum Bonum" (the Highest Good).</note> which cannot be extracted from many and various writers except through the highest labor and industry. For your interpretation of Maro, filled with the most diligent and multifaceted doctrine, in which you have been engaged for the third and fourth day now, has affected us with no less amazement than delight, both because it is most beautiful and because it was unheard of."

"The things said by you cannot be praised enough for their merit," said **Antonio** <note>Antonio Canigiani.</note>. "But I wish, **Baptista**, since you spent the rest of your life in Rome, that you would finally be willing to grant this old age of yours to your fatherland—either as it hopes for or as it demands from its citizen—so that your fellow citizens, hearing you frequently discussing great things, may be rendered daily more learned and better."

"Indeed, I think these are the duties of **Marco** <note>Marco Parenti.</note> here," he continued. "For I trust that you will easily be led over to his opinion through the close bond that exists between him and you."

<margin>'</margin> "Indeed, I have been working at that for a long time now," said **Marco**. "Nor shall I cease until I have obtained it by reason, or won him over with prayers, or extorted it by wearing him out. But as I trust, the talent and study of **LORENZO** and **Giuliano** <note>Lorenzo and Giuliano de' Medici.</note> will help me much in that matter. For since both have already made much progress in literature, and there is the most ardent desire for learning many things, and since other aids from both nature and fortune are abundantly present for them to perfect their goal, he will not long suffer his labor to be missed by these youths whom he holds most dear."

"As long as it is permitted," said **BAPTISTA**, "I will never be lacking to such youths. But let us rise now; and since you must return to the city <note>Florence.</note> early in the morning—for you understood a little while ago that **Lorenzo** was summoned by public letters <note>Official state business or diplomatic correspondence.</note>—let us spend what remains of the day on our health."

->The Camaldolese Disputations of Cristoforo Landino of Florence<-
->End of the fourth and last book. With Privilege.<-

# Book Four

**Severe bleed-through (ghosting) from the reverse side of the page, combined with significant staining and foxing, makes the text highly illegible in many sections. The transcription below reflects the visible foreground text.**

Furthermore, those individuals of whom I have spoken do not wish to be sent forth into the light merely for the sake of the highest admiration—though that is by no means surprising. It is not a matter of "fantasy" (as they say) but of **metaphysics** <note>In the Renaissance, metaphysics was the study of things beyond the physical world, specifically the nature of being and the divine.</note> which, by its nature, openly promises immortality; these things bring the highest resolution to every doubt. Since these laws of ours—the subjects we are discussing—are free, it does not pertain to the enmity of others to perform whatever they might, provided they act in their own interest. For these things are not their own, but uniquely absolute; because where it may be then, no one may recognize. We investigate the method of measuring this through five principal chapters. For those things in that immensity are like points on a line, or stars in the sky: in their wandering, no one can ask where either one is or is not. 

But among us, who exist in a specific place and consist of a certain mass of extension, it is said to be everywhere: for while we are absent from it, we do not go to a distant place. Hence, when I was thinking to myself for a long time: what is it that makes us, who dwell in these lands for some months, fail to approach that measurement which is in the sun's rays themselves? It is not yet possible for what we are accustomed to say about lines to be said about real bodies. All these things which seem so close to us are also so distant from us that we can in no way reach them. Nor is there any other doctrine known to us than the one where we are. Then, indeed, that question begins which proceeds to the premeditated degrees of difference regarding things brought forth. What of the fact that any other thing is almost more elegant than one of these, and that nothing is so small compared to such a great mass <gloss>soft</gloss> as whatever is in the infinite? But it is by no means so small that it does not become huge, and so small that it remains small: and this is not sufficiently explained to us. 

Therefore, what is at hand, which we have proposed to explain clearly in this book: let not others withdraw themselves far from us while we dwell on these matters. For we have said that the questions of "where" and "presently" exist: since although there is one place, it is nevertheless one certain place; although we may doubt where it is presently, there is nevertheless presently a single place where it is. Nor is it because they act within us—even if they act far from us—that they are able to exist; then also because in one thing which exists, it is both a part and not a part. We are not so much distant from ourselves as from others, and we who are so absent from ourselves: not so much because nothing can be done besides those things, but because it ought not to be done presently. And here a certain consideration occurs regarding those who do not act and are not acted upon by others: since this presence is that which is in the intellect and the sense; but presently that which is in being: regarding which presence there is no reason for you to be troubled. Indeed, that matter pertains not so much to a certain dimension as to the highest totality. 

Therefore, in those things which are outside of us: we are not yet said to be in them unless we approach closer. Nor, because they are in one and the same species which is where anyone is, are they therefore understood to be there where each person is. But these things which we will presently explain—even if this place is where it is then—are certainly not so much because we are there: nor because the thing itself does not pertain to us, because they certainly do not act then in the species in which we exist: because in them there is presently that certainty which is in the totality itself, even if not so in the parts: where those things in which we exist act, and those in which we do not exist do not act: as we shall also presently explain. These very things which I am about to treat presently: and that for the sake of those things which will be said presently, because those things which act in the highest, certainly act presently in all things; but those things which act in all things do not act presently in the highest: since this is that ratio <note>Original: "ratio." This can mean reason, proportion, or governing principle.</note> which is the same in every part and in the whole. 

Wherefore, regarding this, he said again: I have long inquired whether it is true that I am alone, or one with the Great <note>Refers to the Neoplatonic concept of the "Great Soul" or "Universal Intellect."</note>: or certainly, I am alone because I am myself: or I am one with the Great because He is both great Himself in all things, and I am in Him. And hence again, a consideration of those who do nothing at all: regarding whom there is no reason for us to enter presently into much care in those things which pertain to the highest. But those who do act, both presently in those and presently in those things which are highest, it is certain that they act again. For those who do not act in the smallest things, do not act presently in the highest. And hence there is an approach from one thing to another again. Furthermore, it will presently happen that in those same places again and regarding those same things, others may succeed one another presently: as we propose to act presently regarding those things of nature and those things which approach nature. But what has been said up to this point, here again in the same places for the same things, others may succeed one another presently: as we propose to act presently regarding those things of nature and those things which approach nature.
